## Publisher’s Preface

Anyone with an interest in culture and intellectual life will know the historic importance of the publication of Husayn Muruwwa’s *Materialist Tendencies in Arab-Islamic Philosophy*. They will also know how important a gap the book filled in the Arabic library, especially for democratic and progressive readers. Muruwwa intended it to be an encyclopedic work, and it became an important resource not only for progressive intellectuals, but also for university students and anyone with a serious interest in Arab cultural and intellectual history.

The problem was the sheer size of the book. Dar al-Farabi originally published it in only two volumes. The first ran to more than a thousand pages, while the second was around eight hundred pages long.

Edition followed edition, and the book gradually became a standard reference for intellectuals, researchers and students at Arab universities. But many readers today are interested in only one particular part of it. Some may be studying the pre-Islamic period, for example, while others are interested only in Muruwwa’s method, or in his materialist analysis of Ibn Sīnā’s philosophy. They may have little need for the other sections. Buying one part of the work is also less expensive than buying the entire set.

For these reasons, we decided to publish a new edition in four volumes:

**Volume I:** The book’s methodological introduction, followed by studies of the pre-Islamic period, the rise of Islam, and the early Islamic period.

**Volume II:** *Kalām*—Islamic theology—focusing on the Muʿtazila and the Ashʿarites, followed by Aristotelian formal logic, since the Muʿtazila were the first to make use of it.

**Volume III:** Sufism, examined through two main examples, Ibn ʿArabī and al-Suhrawardī; the *Epistles of the Brethren of Purity*; and a brief account of the development of the sciences among the Arabs.

**Volume IV:** The period in which philosophy reached maturity: al-Kindī, al-Fārābī and Ibn Sīnā.

**The Publisher**

# The Philosophical Stage

> “Philosophers do not spring from the ground like mushrooms. They are products of their age and their people—the finest, most precious, and least visible essence of both, expressing itself in philosophical ideas. The same spirit that builds philosophical systems in the minds of philosophers builds railways with the hands of workers. Philosophy does not stand outside the world, just as the brain, though it is not in the stomach, does not stand outside the human being.”[^V3-1]
>
> **Karl Marx**

# Chapter One

## The Independence of Philosophy

### First — The Decisive Conditions for Philosophy to Become Independent

By the middle of the third century AH, or the ninth century CE, more than a century and a quarter had passed since Muʿtazilite theological debate had begun to play its great historical role: providing a setting in which the seeds of philosophical thought could grow. This development took place as Arab-Islamic society changed according to the general laws of historical development, but always under its own particular conditions. As we saw in Volume II, the role played by the Muʿtazilite theologians had two closely connected sides: one theoretical and intellectual, the other ideological.

During the first half of the third century AH, as ideological conflict in society became increasingly intense, philosophical thought appeared ready to move beyond the stage in which Muʿtazilite *kalām* had sheltered its early development. Philosophy was beginning to follow a path of its own, separate from theology.

Philosophical thought had developed as far as it could within the framework of *kalām*. If it had remained there, that very framework would inevitably have become an obstacle to further growth. It might even have strangled philosophy altogether.

The reason is that *kalām*, in its basic structure, is a theological philosophy. It rests on the idea that religious truth is one, embodied in the religious law and tied to a single divine source. Philosophy, by contrast, tends to search for truth through human reason, without depending on supernatural sources.

That is one side of the question.

The other is that social and ideological conflict in Abbasid society had become extraordinarily complex during the third century AH—a century filled with some of the sharpest contradictions and historical tensions of the period. These conditions demanded a higher form of thought than theological *kalām* could provide, both for theoretical understanding and for ideological struggle.

The third century AH saw a number of mass uprisings and revolutionary movements. They broke out in Iraq, Egypt, parts of the Arabian Peninsula, and the distant Asian provinces that then belonged to the caliphal state.

Large sections of the working and oppressed population took part, especially peasants. These people came from many different ethnic groups and peoples within Arab-Islamic society.

Some of these revolts and uprisings took on religious, sectarian, or philosophical forms. But a scientific historical study shows that social pressures were the main driving force behind most of the revolts of the third century, some of which continued into the fourth.

Three examples are enough to show this clearly: the Babakite Khurramite revolt, the Zanj revolt, and the Qarmatian movement.

The social importance of these three movements was as great as the threat they posed to the very foundations of the caliphal social order. At the height of their power, they created a level of tension that may have had few parallels even in the most turbulent periods of Islamic history—and Islamic history had no shortage of such periods.

It cannot have been mere coincidence that all three appeared during the same century.

The economic and social conditions of the time had prepared the ground. At the beginning of the century came the revolt of the Babakites, from 201 to 223 AH[^V3-2] / 817 to 838 CE,[^V3-3] in the Qarabagh mountains on the borders of Azerbaijan.[^V3-4] Then, near the beginning of the century’s second half, the Zanj revolt broke out around Basra, from 255 to 270 AH / 869 to 883 CE.[^V3-5] Barely ten years after the Zanj revolt began, the Qarmatian movement started planting the seeds of revolt in the Sawād of Kufa in 264 AH / 887 CE.[^V3-6] It then grew and spread through Iraq, Syria, and Bahrain during the rest of the century, and its influence extended well into the fourth century AH and beyond.

The economic and social conditions we are talking about were the accumulated effects of the social system on which the Islamic caliphate had rested under both the Umayyads and the Abbasids.

Since the rise of the Umayyad state, this system had been marked by an economy in which agriculture and trade were closely connected. Its basic production relations were feudal: land was held through feudal ownership and exploited through feudal forms of production. At the very top stood the ruling elite, which exercised absolute power in the name of Islam.

The effects of this system accumulated throughout the first two centuries AH. By the third century, that long accumulation had reached the point where it began to produce major social explosions. The Babakite revolt, the Zanj revolt, and the Qarmatian movement were the clearest examples in that relatively short century.

Feudal relations of production began to develop vigorously in the Islamic state especially under Muʿāwiya I, first in Syria and then in Iraq. They represented a younger and more energetic form of the Roman and Sasanian feudal systems, which before the Islamic state had already entered a period of decline and growing conflict with the human forces of production, both slaves and free workers.

But the Muslim ruling elite became directly involved in agricultural production.

It did not enter this process only as a political authority representing the landowning class. It also became the largest landowner and the largest feudal exploiter.

Over time, this had increasingly harmful effects on both feudal relations and the people who actually worked the land.

The state combined economic and political power. It burdened peasants and agricultural workers with hard labor, growing taxes, and harsh methods of tax collection. This slowed their development, weakened their ability to work productively, and eventually drove many of them to abandon the land and move to the cities in search of work—or simply into unemployment.

Another factor entered agricultural production when trade flourished during the early Abbasid period. Merchants began investing their commercial profits in land.

Large merchants acquired the lands of small peasants, leaving those peasants poor and without property.

A third factor also had a major effect on agricultural workers. Many landowners, trying to escape heavy taxes and brutal tax collectors, registered their lands in the tax records under the names of powerful government officials. In return, the original owner gave part of the land’s produce to the official who had “agreed” to let the property be registered in his name.

In the end, however, much of this land passed into the hands of the ruling elite itself.[^V3-7]

The peasants who had originally owned it were reduced to laborers tied to the land, or they left altogether and looked elsewhere for the basic means of survival.

So the very forces that concentrated and strengthened feudal relations also weakened them and held back production. At the same time, they impoverished large numbers of agricultural workers and prevented them from making full use of the wealth of the land.

Yet because the social order represented and supported by the caliphal state was fundamentally feudal, the ruling landed aristocracy still regarded merchants—even wealthy ones, let alone smaller traders—as part of the “common people.”[^V3-8]

This was despite the fact that merchants occupied a central position in the economy. As we have already seen, this was fundamentally an agricultural and commercial society in which the two sectors were closely linked.

Large merchants were also active in politics. Their wealth gave them enough influence to affect the decisions of rulers at different levels.

Conflict therefore often arose between the landed aristocracy and the great merchants.

But it remained a conflict between two sections of the same economically dominant class. As a whole, the interests of that class stood against those of the peasants, other rural workers, small craftsmen, and small traders.

---

All these economic and social conditions were common features of the caliphal state across its different provinces and peoples.

But every region also had its own local conditions.

This is why the uprisings and revolutions of the third century differed from one another even though they shared the same broad social causes and emerged from the same general economic and social conditions.

1 — **The Babakite revolt:** Much has been said about its supposed religious[^V3-9] or “national” causes. But there is strong evidence that this was fundamentally a social revolt with a clear class basis.

Economic and social pressures explain the enormous number of peasants who rallied to it across a wide area of Asia and among many different peoples. Persian, Kurdish, Armenian, Byzantine, and other peasants from communities beyond the Caucasus all took part.[^V3-10]

The old forms of feudal production inherited from the Sasanian period were still dominant in Azerbaijan and the neighboring Persian regions. The right to own and exploit land was concentrated in the hands of a small group of *dehqans* and khans,[^V3-11] who exploited peasants and agricultural workers harshly, whether they were slaves or free.[^V3-12]

The Islamic caliphal state made no basic change either to these relations or to the condition of the oppressed people who worked the land.

The Babakite revolt also put forward a revolutionary economic and social program. Its most important and radical demand was that the large estates held by landlords who had previously seized them from peasants or from the state should be redistributed to farmers who needed land.[^V3-13]

When we place this program alongside the backward Sasanian forms of feudal production that still dominated the region, the class character of the revolt becomes unmistakable.

There is another reason for this conclusion. In the historical works that discuss the revolt, we have found nothing showing that it had a clearly defined religious program based on a particular philosophy, religious doctrine, or sectarian belief that would support the claim that it belonged to Mazdakism or Zoroastrianism.

This is so despite the many reports that tried to associate the Babakite revolt with ideas that would discredit its revolutionary social aims.

2 — **The Zanj revolt:** This revolt was directly connected with one of the worst forms of feudal exploitation of the people who worked the land.

Here the confrontation was direct: agricultural workers stood against the owners of the land.

But southern Iraq, especially the Basra region, had its own particular conditions.

Under the weight of taxation and the brutality of tax collectors, local peasants had increasingly abandoned agricultural work. Many had already lost all ownership of land after the caliphal state began granting estates to wealthy merchants in order to rid itself of the burden of managing them. Those merchants, in turn, invested their commercial profits in acquiring even more land from small peasants.

The first result was that vast numbers of peasants lost their small holdings.

The second was the spread of large agricultural estates.[^V3-14]

These estates required large numbers of laborers. Yet at exactly the same time, more and more peasants were leaving the land because the new landowners forced them to work harder while giving them too little to live on.

The owners of these estates therefore began looking for another source of cheap labor.

They found it among the Zanj of East Africa.

Huge numbers were brought to the Basra region, where their revolt eventually broke out.[^V3-15]

They were forced to work the land under extremely harsh conditions and to live in terrible circumstances. Poor food, exhausting labor, and widespread disease killed many of them. They were also cut off from their homelands and families.

This oppressed group of agricultural workers therefore had every reason to explode in revolt, and every reason for deep and justified social anger.

When they found in ʿAlī ibn Muḥammad[^V3-16] someone who gave expression to those feelings and could organize them under his leadership, they responded quickly.

The Zanj revolt shook the caliphal state violently.

Over fifteen years it swept through cities, villages, and productive lands. It defeated government armies, threatened the existing social order, disrupted agriculture and trade, and imposed enormous costs on a state treasury that was already in no condition to bear them.

It is true that the Zanj revolt had no program clearly defining its aims.

But this did not reflect any lack of revolutionary social content.

The real problem was the ideological gap between the masses who made the revolution and the leadership that directed it.

When ʿAlī ibn Muḥammad took up the leadership of the Zanj revolt, neither his class position nor his intellectual outlook equipped him to express the ideology of this crushed social group. He therefore could not turn their outlook into a clear program defining the aims of their revolution.

The Zanj themselves, meanwhile, had been kept in conditions of such social and intellectual deprivation that they could not formulate such a program on their own.

It should also be noted that this was the third Zanj revolt of its kind in Basra,[^V3-17] although it was by far the largest.[^V3-18]

3 — **The Qarmatian movement:**[^V3-19] This movement had a revolutionary side and possessed both a theory and an ideology. It also developed a plan and a program that matched them.

Yet this may be precisely why so much about its history and ideology remains unclear.

Books on the history of Islamic sects and religious groups usually describe the Qarmatians from only one side: that of their opponents. Their own direct sources—the works written by the Qarmatians themselves—largely disappeared under equally obscure circumstances.

That fact itself suggests that the movement stood in opposition to the state’s official ideology, to the landowning interests represented by the state, and to conservative religious authorities.

There is considerable disagreement among historians of Islamic sects about the relationship between the Qarmatians and the Ismāʿīlīs.

Without entering into all the details of that debate, we are inclined toward the view that the Qarmatian movement originally emerged from the Ismāʿīlī movement, and that later differences between them arose more from political conduct and practical action than from major intellectual, ideological, or doctrinal disagreements.

What matters for us here is something else.

We need to understand how the Qarmatian movement entered the lives of large numbers of small peasants, rural workers, craftsmen, and other poor and dispossessed people in the cities. It reached a wide area between Basra and Kufa in Iraq, as well as Bahrain, parts of Syria, and even some areas of Yemen in the south of the Arabian Peninsula.

The movement drew in these oppressed and disadvantaged people.[^V3-20]

From among them it formed an armed force that fought the armies of the Abbasid caliphate with great determination in different regions.

In Bahrain, it established a republic that gave expression to their still-vague class hopes.

That republic remained independent for decades and represented an unusual historical experiment, both for its place and time and for its revolutionary social content.

Al-Maqrīzī,[^V3-21] for example, tells us that the Qarmatian movement in the Kufa region spread among several Arab tribes living there at the time, as well as among the Nabataeans[^V3-22] who worked the agricultural lands of the Sawād of Kufa.

The movement had a highly organized underground structure. Its missionaries stayed in regular contact with members and local organizations and followed coordinated plans for monitoring their situation and the progress of the movement among them.

Ḥamdān Qarmaṭ showed considerable organizational skill. He was assisted by twelve *naqībs*, or senior representatives, whom he chose from among the missionaries in a system similar to that used by the Ismāʿīlīs.

This careful organization was supported by a financial system that helped the movement spread among poor peasants, rural workers, and small craftsmen in Iraq.

First, members paid certain contributions.

Second, the money collected through these contributions, together with property donated to the movement by its owners, was redistributed among members according to their needs.

These resources also made it possible to build a fortified base near Kufa in 277 AH. It was called the “House of Migration,” or *Dār al-Hijra*. The movement also used its resources to arm its followers, strengthen its position, command respect, and prepare for revolt.[^V3-23]

The caliphal state paid relatively little attention to the movement until 287 AH / 900 CE, during the reign of the Abbasid caliph al-Muʿtaḍid, who ruled from 279 to 289 AH / 892 to 901 CE.

From that point on, the state began pursuing the Qarmatians much more aggressively. It had begun to recognize the movement as a social and ideological threat, as well as a political threat to the authority of the caliphate itself.

Ibn al-Athīr[^V3-24] gives us a striking example of how deep and widespread this threat had become.

In 287 AH, one of al-Muʿtaḍid’s commanders surprised the Qarmatians at one of their centers in the Kufa region. He killed their leaders and captured the others. Yet he then had to release all the prisoners because they were peasants. Killing them, or even keeping them imprisoned, would have left the surrounding land without enough people to cultivate it.

This tells us two things.

First, it reveals the class dimension of the movement.

Second, it shows just how many peasants had joined the Qarmatian cause. Their absence from the land alone was enough to bring agriculture in the region close to a halt.

From that point on, armed Qarmatian activity continued in Iraq, and the armies of the Abbasid caliphate were unable to destroy the movement.

During this growing struggle between the state and the Qarmatians, one of the movement’s leaders reached Syria in 289 AH / 901 CE. He quickly began spreading the call for armed Qarmatian revolt there.

This expanded the movement geographically and prolonged a conflict that was already weakening the caliphate’s control over many provinces.

The threat to the social order grew still further when one of the Qarmatian missionaries[^V3-25] sent by Ḥamdān Qarmaṭ succeeded in Bahrain.

Abū Saʿīd al-Jannābī found a wide audience there among both the settled population and the people of the desert.

He spread the idea of universal brotherhood among human beings, regardless of religious, ethnic, or geographical differences.

He also awakened hopes of happiness in this life, on this earth, rather than asking people to wait for the happiness promised to them in the afterlife by religion.

From the end of the third century AH and into the beginning of the fourth, the main military confrontation between the Qarmatians and the caliphal state shifted toward the Qarmatians of Bahrain.

Their strength continued to grow, and the armies of the caliphate could not subdue them.

Eventually Bahrain broke away from caliphal authority.

During the 320s AH, under Sulaymān Abū Ṭāhir, one of the sons of Abū Saʿīd al-Jannābī, the Qarmatian state of Bahrain became independent.

At that point, the first Arab republic of the medieval period—and indeed, in Muruwwa’s view, the first in all Arab history—came into being.

It had its own theory, ideology, and social, economic, and political program.

For its time it represented an entirely new kind of state.

It was not based on religion and was much closer to what we would now call a secular state.

It is true that during its struggle in Iraq, the Qarmatian movement had used slogans with a religious and sectarian form, especially the call to obey the hidden and awaited Imam.

But after the establishment of the Bahraini republic, it became clear that the idea of the Imam had been less a purely religious belief than a social idea expressed in religious form.

The Qarmatian republic nevertheless committed brutal acts of destruction and bloodshed against the Kaaba and Muslim pilgrims.

On one level, these acts were an unfortunate expression of the Qarmatians’ struggle against what they regarded as “idolatry.”

On another, they were an attempt to weaken the prestige of the caliphal state by showing the Muslim public that it could not protect Islam’s holiest places and rituals, or even guarantee the safety of pilgrims visiting them.

The same applies to the terrible attacks carried out by the Qarmatians of Bahrain against Kufa and Basra on more than one occasion.

We do not believe that looting and killing were pursued simply for their own sake.

Rather, they were used as political means by the Qarmatian republic. Conservatives and defenders of the official ideology then exploited these actions to portray the Qarmatian movement itself as barbaric.

All we can say with confidence about the beliefs of the founders of the Qarmatian republic in Bahrain is that they had a philosophy, but not a religious creed in the usual sense.

That philosophy centered on the idea of the “deification of reason,” or the “rationalization of God.”

They spoke of “the Supreme Intellect, which is God, or the Supreme Wisdom.”

They abolished religious rites and rituals altogether.

Yet when the Persian traveler Nāṣir-i Khusraw visited the Qarmatian republic in 1052 CE, he found a mosque in al-Aḥsāʾ that had been built by a Sunni Persian.

He also reported that the Qarmatians “do not prevent anyone from praying, although they themselves do not pray.”[^V3-26]

Al-Baghdādī was therefore right when he said that they “reject the prophets and all religious laws,”[^V3-27] provided that by “religious laws” we understand the theological systems of the religions.

The political system of the Bahraini republic also differed fundamentally from the oligarchies[^V3-28] that dominated the medieval world.

Its leadership was collective.

The government was headed by six people who formed what was known as the Council of the “ʿAqdāniyya.”

The citizens of the republic elected its members from the family of Sulaymān Abū Ṭāhir, from among his close associates, or from other senior figures in the movement whom they trusted.

There were also six deputies to the members of the Council. They sat on a raised platform behind them and took their places whenever a member was absent.

The Council made its decisions on matters concerning the republic unanimously.[^V3-29]

This political structure appears to have remained in place until at least 1052 CE, when Nāṣir-i Khusraw visited the republic.[^V3-30]

Among the early measures attributed to the ʿAqdāniyya were the abolition of land taxes and fees, along with the creation of a tax system designed not to place a heavy burden on ordinary citizens.

It included a tax on ships passing through the Gulf, a tax on the people of Oman, a yearly tax on pilgrims traveling to Mecca and Medina, and a tax on pearl divers in the waters of Bahrain and the Gulf.

Other state income came from yearly payments imposed on some towns and villages in Iraq, as well as from Bahrain and Oman’s own agricultural wealth. These were among the most fertile lands in the Arabian Peninsula.[^V3-31]

Thanks to this organized financial policy, it has been said that Bahrain’s finances under the Qarmatians reached a level they had not reached “at any other period of its history.”[^V3-32]

As for land ownership, the sources available to us do not give a clear picture.

But Nāṣir-i Khusraw provides information that sheds some light on the issue.

He tells us that thirty thousand Black workers were employed in fields and gardens on behalf of the ʿAqdāniyya, and that these fields and gardens had been bought with public money.

He also says that the people paid neither taxes nor tithes to the government.

If someone fell into poverty, or became trapped in debt that he could not repay, the ʿAqdāniyya lent him what he needed without charging interest.

If a foreign craftsman arrived in al-Aḥsāʾ, the government lent him enough money to buy his tools. He could then work at his trade, earn his living, and repay the loan without interest.

If the owner of a house or mill suffered a disaster and lacked the means to repair it, the ʿAqdāniyya supplied workers to rebuild the house or mill.[^V3-33]

Nāṣir-i Khusraw also says that the government owned mills in al-Aḥsāʾ where people could have their wheat ground free of charge, and that there were no poor people left in the country.[^V3-34]

Internal and external trade was also in the hands of the government, and its profits were spent on public needs and on improving the conditions of farmers and workers.[^V3-35]

These facts show that something resembling a “public sector” was the dominant feature of the Bahraini republic’s economic and social institutions.

This was the most fundamentally revolutionary side of this unusual experiment.

It also helps explain the strength that allowed the republic to survive for decades, despite being surrounded by forces that were hostile to it both in class terms and ideologically.

Those forces did everything they could to destroy the republic before it could become a dangerous example for the wider social order of the caliphate and, later, the smaller states that emerged from it.

When they failed to destroy it, they distorted its history, erased its achievements, and left later generations with little more than its failures and mistakes—mistakes that were, in fact, serious.

4 — **The weakening of the caliphate’s ideology:** Our discussion of these three revolts and movements, which confronted the caliphal state throughout the third century AH and continued to affect it afterward, has not taken us away from our main subject.

We stopped to examine them deliberately.

We needed to understand their real nature because that helps us see the instability and breakdown the caliphal state was experiencing at this stage.

The economic and social system on which its political, religious, and ideological institutions rested was itself beginning to shake and crack.

This weakness also appeared in the military and financial systems of the state.

They were increasingly unable to perform their role in maintaining the existing order, and eventually even became unable to protect that order from social movements and from armed and unarmed popular revolts.

The growing strength of the three movements we have discussed, along with others,[^V3-36] did not arise mainly because the state’s armies and finances had become weak.

The reverse is closer to the truth.

The armies and finances were weak because the social system itself was suffering from deep problems. It had become increasingly unable to function or defend itself.

Historians often discuss the harmful effects of growing Turkish influence throughout the institutions of the state.

They also describe popular anger as Turkish military power expanded to the point where Turkish commanders could determine not only which caliph would take power, but even whether a caliph would remain alive.[^V3-37]

Yet these historians rarely look behind this visible political development to the material conditions that made it possible.

The deeper problem began with the rise of what we might call “decentralized feudalism.”

By this we mean the very wide independence enjoyed by representatives of the central government in the different provinces of the Abbasid state.

Their freedom of action allowed them to become major feudal landowners in the regions they governed, often ranking first among local landowners.

At the same time, they sent large amounts of money back to the center of power in the caliphal capital in order to preserve their positions and estates.

To raise this money, they imposed heavier and heavier taxes on peasants and other working people and used increasingly brutal methods to collect them.

These revenues helped support the almost legendary luxury enjoyed by the inhabitants of the caliphal palaces at the center.

The result was weakness on two levels.

First, the feudal relations of production themselves began to deteriorate, because the people who worked the land were exhausted and prevented from developing their productive capacity. Even the land itself suffered.

Second, the political institution of the state began to fragment.

Those at its head became increasingly unable to supervise the enforcement of laws, regulations, administrative systems, and government orders.

At the same time, they could no longer provide even the most basic social services that could reasonably have been expected in that period.

This was the background to what some researchers[^V3-38] describe as the Abbasid caliphs’ “loss of confidence,” beginning with the reign of al-Muʿtaṣim, in Arabs and Persians.

They therefore brought Turks first into the army and later into the administration, from its lowest ranks to its highest.

The Turks then became another source of instability.

They turned against caliphs who refused to satisfy their ambitions and demands, and sometimes killed them.

They also became major landholders themselves, causing even greater damage to the feudal relations of production than those who had come before them.

But the real issue was not whether the caliphs “trusted” one people or another.

The problem was an economic and social system that had reached a stage where it could no longer develop itself.

It was also losing its ability to protect its institutions from collapse, or from the anger of the exploited, oppressed, and impoverished population.

The system began to stagger under the weight of its own problems.

The gap between rulers and the mass of ordinary people continued to widen, while resentment grew among those people to the point where they were ready to respond to almost any call for revolt, any rebellion against the state, and any movement based on ideas that challenged those of the ruling power.

Nor was this fundamentally a question of Persians, Arabs, Turks, Daylamites, and so on.

In other words, it was not basically a question of “nationalities.”[^V3-39]

This does not mean that feelings of ethnic or communal loyalty played no part in some movements, especially those led by figures who themselves came from non-Arab peoples within the caliphal state.

But where such motives existed, they generally belonged to the individual leader or leadership of a movement.

Historically, no leader of a revolt or uprising against the Abbasid caliphate in a non-Arab region openly raised an explicitly nationalist program.

What we actually find is that revolutionary leaders, if they wanted to win popular support, had to raise slogans or announce programs connected with the economic and social demands of ordinary people.

Sometimes they did this directly.

Sometimes those demands were expressed through religious or philosophical ideas.

But the religious or philosophical form did not hide the real social content underneath.

We can see this, for example, in movements built around the idea of an awaited Imam.

This is a historical fact, not a theoretical assumption.

And it tells us two important things.

First, ordinary people were never completely cut off[^V3-40] from the political events taking place within the ruling order. Nor did they simply submit without resistance to whatever those events brought.

The form of their resistance could vary, but resistance existed.

Second, these people did not mobilize to defend a cause or ideology that had no connection with their own class interests.

Of course, we are not claiming that they possessed class consciousness in the modern scientific sense.

Such a claim would make no sense in the historical conditions of the medieval period.

What we mean is that a spontaneous class impulse existed among them.

It moved them even when traditional or theological ideas prevented them from clearly understanding the class boundaries created by their own place in the production of society’s material wealth.

The historian al-Baghdādī[^V3-41] came close to recognizing this fact, although he did not fully grasp it.

He tried to explain why the peasants of the Sawād of Kufa[^V3-42] responded so quickly and so widely to the Qarmatian call.

His explanation was that they were ignorant of Islamic religious law, and that this ignorance led them to follow their personal interests whenever those interests came into conflict with the requirements of the law.

This explanation is naive in the way it connects their behavior to ignorance of Islamic law.

Yet it contains the beginnings of a realistic insight.

It almost recognizes the decisive role of material interests in social movements.

But al-Baghdādī, writing in his own age, could not call class interests—economic and social interests—by their real name.

So he described them in a simpler and less accurate way as “personal interests.”

---

This overview of the main economic, social, and political conditions within Abbasid society during the third century AH brings us to an important conclusion.

That conclusion is what we have been trying to reach through these historical facts.

Taken together, these conditions—and the uprisings, revolutions, and other forms of resistance they produced—began to shake the foundations of the existing social order.

They also began to weaken some of its most important intellectual and religious assumptions.

In other words, the crisis was spreading into the ideology of the system itself.

Religious ideology had been the main legal and ideological foundation of caliphal political authority.

That authority belonged, in practice, to those who benefited from the existing social system and from its relations of production.

They used the spiritual idea of the caliphate and its religious ideology as both a fortress and a weapon against ideas, movements, and uprisings with revolutionary social content.

5 — **Kalām reaches the limits of its historical role:** As we explained in Volume II, Muʿtazilite *kalām* had helped prepare the intellectual ground for weakening certain parts of the state’s ideology, even at a time when that ideology was at the height of its strength.

It was therefore natural that these early intellectual challenges could only appear inside the same general framework as the ideology of the caliphal state itself.

That framework was Islamic theology: religious thought based on Islam and its basic principles.

But by the third century AH, the accumulated effects of new historical forces had become much greater.

These forces were increasingly obstructing, and in some cases stopping, the development of the growing feudal relations of production and of the productive forces working within them.

Earlier in this chapter we saw how this long accumulation began to erupt throughout the third century in different forms: uprisings, revolutions, and other social movements.

One result was that the social order itself began to shake, weaken, and crack at the foundations on which the ideology of the state had been built.

By the middle of the third century, this ideology had begun to fall from the height of its power.

It was becoming unable to impose its own authority, or even to force new ideas to remain inside its theological framework.

These were the ideas of an age of revolutions, mass uprisings, and revolutionary social movements.

At the same time, the creative interaction between a growing and developing Arabic culture and the “sciences of the ancients”[^V3-43]—both the natural sciences and philosophy—was becoming much deeper during the third century AH.

Its effects were beginning to produce major changes across intellectual life in Arab-Islamic society, as we will examine in detail in a later chapter.

So two developments were taking place at once.

On one side, the ideology of the caliphal state was beginning to crack.

On the other, scientific culture—both natural science and philosophy—was being absorbed more deeply into Arab intellectual life.

As a result, rational thought began to take clearer form among a number of scholars and thinkers.

It was moving toward a way of thinking that tried to go beyond pure speculation and beyond the hidden or open control of theology.

This was the wider setting: explosive economic, social, political, and cultural conditions of the kind we have just described.

In such a climate, philosophical thought could no longer remain under the guardianship of Muʿtazilite *kalām*.

It had been growing there since the time of Wāṣil ibn ʿAṭāʾ, who died in 131 AH / 748 CE.

But now the official ideology itself was weakening and could no longer control the wider cultural movement of society through its theological framework.

At the same time, rational thought was becoming increasingly capable of moving beyond supernatural forms of knowledge.

We believe that these historical conditions were the decisive material conditions that made philosophy’s independence possible.

They allowed philosophy to break away from the Muʿtazilite theological framework of *kalām* that had sheltered it for roughly a century and a third.

That development followed the general laws of historical change, but always in a form shaped by the particular conditions of Arab-Islamic society during the second and third centuries AH—the eighth and ninth centuries CE.

### Second — A Relative Independence

Before asking whether philosophy became completely or only relatively independent from *kalām* in the third century AH, we first need to explain what we mean by “independence.”

Only then can we judge how far that independence actually went.

It does not mean that philosophy had to deal with completely different questions from those discussed by *kalām*.

Both existed in the same period, in the same society, and under the same social relations.

If we insisted that each had to discuss entirely different subjects, we would be treating both philosophy and *kalām* in an idealist way.

We would, in effect, be imagining that the questions they discussed existed outside society.

Here we should remember Marx’s words at the beginning of this section:

> “Philosophers do not spring from the ground like mushrooms. They are products of their age and their people...”

The difference between Muʿtazilite *kalām* and the philosophy that later separated from it was not the kind of absolute boundary that exists, for example, between the natural sciences and the social sciences.

The *kalām* developed by the Muʿtazilites was, in Muruwwa’s view, essentially philosophy taking Islamic beliefs as its particular form.

That form suited the social conditions of the time because those conditions were themselves interacting with Islamic beliefs and presenting the problems of society through them.

If the third century AH created the conditions for philosophy to become independent from *kalām*, this simply meant that new historical developments had removed the earlier necessity for philosophy to appear in the form of Islamic religious doctrine.

That religious form had once been the way theoretical thought expressed a particular stage in the development of the new Arab-Islamic society.

But the basic problems of that society had not suddenly disappeared.

The broad framework of social relations was still the same.

The changes taking place in the third century were changes within that framework.

So when philosophy became independent from *kalām*, it did not suddenly have to begin discussing completely different questions.

The same social conditions still placed the same basic problems before theoretical and philosophical thought.

If we demanded that philosophy abandon those questions simply because it had become independent, we would once again be separating philosophy from the society and historical conditions that produced it.

The real meaning of philosophy’s independence at this stage was different.

Philosophy could now approach those same questions through its own logic rather than beginning from accepted religious premises.

Its method came from examining existence itself, in the broadest sense, without first placing that investigation inside theological categories.

Instead, it relied on concepts that reason drew from the dialectical relations found in real existence.

We can put the same point another way.

During an earlier stage in the development of Arab-Islamic society, philosophy had approached a problem such as knowledge through a mixed rational and theological framework.

Once philosophy began to separate from *kalām*, it gained the right to treat such a problem as a purely philosophical question, free from the theological outlook that had necessarily shaped *kalām*.

That is what the independence of philosophy meant at the time.

But was philosophy actually able to achieve this kind of independence in full?

Historical reality answers: no.

Philosophy could not immediately free itself completely from its structural relationship with *kalām*.

Its independence therefore remained relative, perhaps until the beginning of the Avicennan stage of philosophy.[^V3-44]

During the early period after philosophy began to separate from theology, a theological tendency continued to influence some philosophical conclusions from beneath the surface.

We can see this, for example, in arguments about whether the world is eternal, whether it was created from nothing, and in other questions that we will examine in later chapters.

It would not have been reasonable to expect this theological influence to disappear suddenly or within a short period.

Nor could philosophy have passed instantly and completely from one stage to another without a period in which the two overlapped.

There was another reason as well.

Arabic culture at the time had no earlier philosophical tradition that existed completely outside Islamic theology and *kalām*.

A substantial period therefore had to pass after philosophical thought became independent before new philosophical traditions could form and become firmly established.

Only when those traditions had developed into something qualitatively new could philosophy’s independence become complete—that is, fully free from the earlier theological control.

## Conclusions from Chapter One

### 1

By the middle of the third century AH, more than a century had passed since Muʿtazilite thought had begun playing its major historical role: nurturing philosophical thought among the Arabs after the rise of Islam. But during the first half of the third century, it became clear that philosophy was about to move beyond this stage and follow a path of its own, separate from Muʿtazilite *kalām*. One reason was the growing intensity of ideological conflict under the Abbasid state.

At this point in history, *kalām* had begun to hold philosophy back rather than help it grow. In its basic form, *kalām* is a theological philosophy built around the idea of a single religious truth. Philosophy in the strict sense, by contrast, seeks a single truth through rational inquiry into questions of existence, without relying on supernatural sources of knowledge.

There was another reason as well. As social and ideological conflict developed within Abbasid society during the third century AH, it required a more advanced intellectual form than *kalām* could provide. A different kind of thought was needed to carry the theoretical and ideological struggles of that society.

### 2

The third century AH saw popular uprisings and revolutions across most regions of the Abbasid state. Historical study of these movements shows that social conflict was usually their main driving force.

Three cases stand out historically, socially, and ideologically: the Zanj revolt, the Qarmatian revolt, and the Babak Khurramite revolt.[^V3-45] It was not mere coincidence that these three major revolts all appeared during the third century AH within a relatively short period. They were brought together by the economic and social conditions of Arab-Islamic society under the Abbasids.

These conditions were simply the further development, both in scale and in character, of forces and patterns produced by the social system on which the Islamic caliphate had been built. This system combined an agricultural and commercial economy. Its production relations were defined above all by feudal ownership and feudal exploitation. Politically, these were represented by the power of the caliphal state and by a theocratic[^V3-46] form of absolute rule exercised in the name of Islam.

From the time of Muʿāwiya I, feudal relations in the Islamic state initially helped revive the productive forces after Roman and Sasanian feudalism had exhausted much of their energy. But new problems arose when the Arab ruling group itself became directly involved in agricultural production and feudal exploitation, since it owned most of the productive land.

Over time, this had damaging effects both on the productive forces themselves and on the working population in different regions and among different peoples. The state placed increasingly heavy burdens on the people who worked the land, demanding more labor, raising taxes, and collecting them harshly.

At the same time, wealthy merchants began investing some of their commercial profits in land. They bought land from small farmers, who saw this as a way to escape the heavy demands of labor, taxation, and tax collection. Many therefore left the countryside and moved to the cities in search of other ways to live.

This meant that the same forces that had originally helped revive feudal production relations eventually became sources of weakness and stagnation.

A second contradiction also appeared within the economically dominant groups themselves. Wealthy merchants played an important part in the economy, yet the ruling feudal aristocracy still regarded them socially as members of the “common people.”

Although these features were common to the society of the Islamic caliphate as a whole during the second century AH, we must also take account of the local features of each region. Once we do that, we can see that alongside the shared economic and social causes of the revolts and uprisings, there were also differences from one movement to another because local conditions differed.

**1 — The Babak revolt:** This was a social revolt with a clear class basis, despite the religious or “national” labels that historians later attached to it. Peasant masses from Azerbaijan took part in it between 817 and 838 CE. They included Arabs, Persians, Kurds, Armenians, Romans, and members of other peoples of the Caucasus.

The revolt had an economic and social program that reflected its class basis. At the heart of this program was the demand to “take large estates away from the owners who had previously seized them from peasants or from the state, and distribute them among farmers in need.”

We have found nothing in the specialist literature to show that this revolt had either a religious or a “national” program.

**2 — The Zanj revolt:** This took place around Basra between 869 and 883 CE. Its class basis is even clearer. Here the confrontation was direct: between those who worked the land and those who exploited their labor.

The rebels were originally Zanj from East Africa. New landowners brought them into the countryside around Basra after the original peasant owners had abandoned their land to escape state taxes, harsh tax collection, and feudal oppression.

These African laborers were forced to work under extremely harsh conditions and to live in terrible circumstances. Disease spread among them because of poor nutrition, exhausting labor, and the absence of basic sanitary conditions.

Their class hatred, though not yet politically conscious, pushed them spontaneously toward revolt. They then found in ʿAlī ibn Muḥammad a leader with great skill in organization, command, and mobilization.

The Zanj revolt shook the caliphal state severely. It lasted for fifteen years, swept across many towns, villages, and productive areas of Iraq, defeated several armies of the caliphate, disrupted major agricultural and commercial activity, and cost the state treasury enormous sums.

This was the third Zanj revolt in Basra, and by far the largest and most dangerous.

The revolt had no program clearly stating its goals. Its leadership did not express the ideology of the mass forces at its base, while those forces themselves remained intellectually undeveloped and were therefore unable to formulate either an ideology of their own or a clear set of revolutionary aims.

**3 — The Qarmatian revolt:** The first seeds of this movement began to grow in the Sawād of Kufa in 264 AH/877 CE.

Unlike the two previous revolts, this was a revolutionary movement with an explicit ideological character. It developed a plan and a program connected to a theory and ideology that directly opposed the ideology of the state, as well as the feudal landlords and conservative religious authorities whose interests the state represented.

Historical sources confirm that the Qarmatian message spread among large numbers of small farmers and agricultural laborers in the countryside, as well as craftsmen, other workers, and impoverished people in the towns. Its influence covered a wide area between Basra and Kufa in Iraq, as well as Bahrain, parts of Syria, and some areas of Yemen in the south of the Arabian Peninsula.

The movement had a highly organized secret structure and a strict financial system. These helped it spread among poor farmers, agricultural laborers, and small craftsmen in Iraq.

The caliphal state paid relatively little attention to the movement until 287 AH/900 CE, during the reign of the Abbasid caliph al-Muʿtaḍid. It then began pursuing the Qarmatians more aggressively because it had come to see their movement as both a social and an ideological threat to the state itself. Large numbers of agricultural workers had rallied around it in one of the most fertile farming regions under caliphal control.

But the armies of the state could not destroy the movement. On the contrary, the harder they pursued it, the more it spread into new regions.

Eventually it reached Bahrain under the leadership of Abū Saʿīd al-Jannābī. He had originally been an ordinary laborer who earned a very small wage repairing flour sacks.

Among the people of eastern Arabia along the Gulf, he spread the idea of brotherhood between people regardless of religion, race, or nationality. He also encouraged dreams of happiness in life on earth, rather than waiting for the happiness of the afterlife promised by religions.

The Qarmatian revolt continued fighting the armies of the caliphate until its forces became firmly established in Bahrain. It succeeded in separating the region from state control and, during the 320s AH, established a Qarmatian republic there.

Muruwwa describes it as the first Arab republic in Arab history. It had its own political system, theory, and ideology, based on a philosophy expressed in the idea of the “deification” of reason, or the “rationalization” of God.

The Qarmatians spoke of a “Supreme Intellect,” which they identified with God, or with supreme wisdom. They completely abolished religious rites and rituals.

Their political system was also fundamentally different from the oligarchies that dominated the medieval world. The Qarmatian republic in Bahrain had a form of collective leadership.

Its government was headed by a group forming what was called the Council of the “ʿIqdāniyya.” Citizens of the republic elected its members from the family of Sulaymān Abū Ṭāhir, from his close associates, or from other trusted people who held senior positions within the movement.

Another six people were elected as deputies to the members of this council.

The council made its decisions unanimously.

Among its known measures were the abolition of land taxes and other charges that had weighed heavily on agricultural workers, and the introduction of a tax system that did not place excessive burdens on the population.

### 3

These three examples of social revolution show the instability and growing cracks within the caliphal state during the third century AH.

The problem reached into the economic and social system itself—the same system on which the state’s political, legal, and ideological institutions rested. This weakness could also be seen in the inability of its military and financial institutions either to serve the aims of the system effectively or to protect it from the dangers surrounding it.

The basic cause of this weakness lay in what we can call **decentralized feudalism**.

By this we mean the very wide independence enjoyed by representatives of state power in the different regions of the Abbasid state. This allowed them to change from officials serving the central government into major feudal landlords. They came to occupy the highest ranks among landowners and those who profited from the land.

They paid large sums to the central authorities at the seat of the caliphate in order to preserve their privileges. In turn, they raised this money by imposing arbitrary and heavy taxes on weak peasants and the working population.

This “decentralized feudalism” weakened feudal production relations themselves. It became an obstacle to the development of the productive forces, both natural and human.

At the same time, it created forces of decay and fragmentation within the political institution of the state.

As a result, the state could no longer properly supervise the application of its own laws and regulations. It was also unable to provide even the most basic social services, by the modest standards of that period, to the mass of its “subjects.”

This was the background from which the many revolts and uprisings of the third century AH emerged.

Their main content was therefore social and class-based.

As for “national” motives among the non-Arab peoples then ruled by the caliphate, it seems to us that where such motives existed at all, they were mainly limited to individuals in leadership positions.

Historically, it is striking that no leader of any revolt or uprising against the Abbasid caliphate raised an openly “national” slogan in a non-Arab region.

What we do know is that revolutionary leaders had to raise demands connected to the real economic and social needs of the population. Sometimes these demands were stated directly. At other times they appeared within a religious or philosophical framework. But even then, that framework could not hide the real social demands reflected within it.

The developments we have discussed lead us to the deeper reality behind them.

Together, they shook the foundations of the social system and began to create cracks in some of its most important intellectual and legal supports and assumptions.

This meant that the cracks were spreading into the ideology of the system itself.

Religious ideology had provided the main legal justification for the authority and political rule of the caliphate. In other words, it supported the power of those who benefited from the existing social system and its particular relations of production.

It also supported those who used the spiritual idea of the caliphate, together with its religious ideology, as a weapon against ideas of liberation in whatever form they appeared.

### 4

When Muʿtazilite *kalām* first appeared, it became the first intellectual channel through which cracks began to enter certain parts of the ideology of the state.

This happened even while the state was at the height of its strength and stability, and even though Muʿtazilite *kalām* itself still operated within the same general framework as the ideology of the state: a purely theological framework.

But by the third century AH, the situation had changed.

The developments we have described had weakened this ideology so deeply that it could no longer impose its authority as before, or even impose its theological framework with the same force.

At the same time, there was a growing creative interaction between Arab culture, then in a period of expansion and development, and the sciences inherited from the Greeks, Persians, and Indians—especially Greek philosophy.

In this new economic, social, and cultural climate, Muʿtazilite *kalām* no longer suited the needs of the period.

It had completed its historical task.

There was no longer any reason for philosophical thought to remain under the guardianship of Muʿtazilite theological debates and their broader theological outlook.

We believe that the historical conditions examined in this chapter provided the decisive material foundations for philosophy to become independent.

In other words, they created the conditions for philosophy to break free from the theological framework of *kalām*, which had sheltered it for more than a century, despite the general laws of development and the way those laws corresponded to the particular conditions of Arab-Islamic society during the second and third centuries AH—the eighth and ninth centuries CE.

### 5

When we say that philosophy became independent of *kalām*, we do not mean that it suddenly began discussing entirely different questions and subjects.

That is not what we mean by the independence of philosophy.

As long as both fields existed in the same period, within the same society and under the same social conditions and relationships, both were bound to deal with many of the same questions. Those questions came from the problems of the society and age they shared.

So what does the independence of philosophy mean?

There had never been a sharp or absolute dividing line between Muʿtazilite *kalām* and the philosophy that emerged from it during the third century AH.

Muʿtazilite *kalām* was, in effect, philosophy operating within the framework and language of Islamic doctrine.

But during the third century, new developments appeared that removed the historical need for philosophical thought to remain within this theological form.

The basic problems facing Arab society did not disappear, because the general structure of its social relations had not undergone any fundamental change.

The real meaning of philosophy’s independence at this stage was therefore different.

Philosophy began to deal with the questions of its age and society through its **own logic and its own method**. It began freeing itself from the need to accept theological assumptions in advance and instead relied on independent concepts based on rational principles.

But did philosophy actually achieve full independence in this sense at that stage in the development of Arab philosophical thought?

Our answer is no.

It would not have been realistic for philosophy to become completely independent in a single first step. Nor could it escape all its complex ties with *kalām* in one sudden leap.

For some time, theological tendencies continued to appear in some of its views, conclusions, and starting points, as we will see in the next chapter.

# Chapter Two

## Sufism

### A — Sufism as Practice (Asceticism): A Political Position

#### 1 — Method

As soon as we enter this subject, we encounter a large number of idealist views and assumptions in the writings of scholars and historians of Islamic Sufism.

Most of them revolve around two main questions.

The first is how to explain Sufism itself, understood as what they describe as a universal phenomenon connected with every religion.[^V3-47]

The second is where Islamic Sufism got its basic ideas and theories.

But both questions clearly come from the same starting point: a one-sided way of looking at Sufism and at the ascetic practices that came before it.

The different views and assumptions offered by these scholars are generally built on the idea that Sufism is the expression of a purely inner and subjective process. Any connection it has with something outside the individual is reduced to religion—whether one particular religion or several religious or religiously colored philosophical sources, such as Neoplatonism.

If we look at the debate over the origins of Islamic Sufism and the sources from which it developed, we find this same one-sided idealist outlook dominating almost the entire discussion.

Some scholars trace Sufism, in its various forms and stages, to outside influences: Christianity, Zoroastrianism, Indian religion, ancient Gnosticism, Neoplatonism, or several of these at once.[^V3-48]

Others trace it directly to “Islamic revelation.” They describe Sufism as the “essential aspect” of that revelation, its “heart,” or its “inner and esoteric dimension.”[^V3-49] Others describe it as “a movement aimed at deepening spiritual meanings.”[^V3-50]

From another direction, bourgeois psychologists describe Sufis simply as a type of “psychologically ill person,”[^V3-51] and nothing more.

Empiricists and rationalists, meanwhile, reject Sufism because, they say, it “does not conform to the standards and rules of rational logic.”[^V3-52]

Despite their very different intellectual positions and starting points, all these approaches meet at one point: they look at Sufism from only one side.

They fail to see its other sides, and above all the most important one: the connection between Sufism, both as practice and as theory, and the particular social relations of a particular historical setting.

Those who say that Islamic Sufism comes from Islam itself as a divine religion, and those who say that it comes from outside religious and intellectual influences, share the same basic mistake.

Both ignore the historical, economic, social, and political conditions of which Sufism was one intellectual reflection.

They ignore this part of the question so completely that the Sufis appear to have no connection with anything except the religion they follow or the outside “spiritual” influences that may somehow have reached them.

For this reason, we disagree not only with these two approaches, but also with the empiricists and rationalists who reject Sufism because, as we saw, they believe it does not conform to “the standards of rational logic.”

These empiricists and rationalists also imagine the “standards of rational logic” in a way that completely separates them from their living basis in reality.

In other words, they treat the laws of consciousness and thought as though they were separate from the general objective laws through which society moves and changes, and separate from the relationship between human consciousness and practical social activity.

Historians of Sufism, scholars of comparative religion, and philosophers of religion have argued that “Sufism is a universal phenomenon connected with every religion.”[^V3-53]

We accept only the first half of this claim.

We agree that Sufism is a universal phenomenon. Historically, that is true.

But its “universality” does not come from a necessary connection with “every religion.”

Comparative studies of Sufism have found similarities between known forms of Indian, Christian, and Islamic Sufism.[^V3-54]

But this does not prove that Sufism must necessarily be tied to religion, or that religion is the cause of Sufism.

The similarity between these forms of Sufism is historical.

What produced it was the similarity between certain historical patterns: similar forms of social relations and similar general conditions.

These produced the similarities we can observe between different forms of Sufism—similarities that are relative, not absolute.

At their foundation, Sufi phenomena are products of particular social relations under particular historical conditions.

We therefore do not deny the existence of Sufism, as some empiricists and rationalists do.

From our historical materialist point of view, Sufism is a historical reality that cannot be denied.

We also recognize that it has appeared in many real forms and that several genuinely similar patterns have existed—similar, though not identical.

Even in our own time, somewhat similar patterns can still be found in certain contemporary bourgeois schools and in intellectual, literary, artistic, and behavioral trends.[^V3-55] In our view, these trends sharply reflect some of the lowest symptoms of the economic, social, and ideological crisis faced by the bourgeoisie of the modern age.

What we reject is the idealist and metaphysical explanation of Sufism.

Our method explains it through the changing relationship between the individual and society, or between human consciousness and practical social activity.

We also explain it through the dialectical interaction between the forms of social relations that prevail in a society and the forms of human consciousness—or, put differently, between the laws of the material world and the laws of thought as they interact in human intellectual and spiritual activity.

The conclusion is that Sufism, in its different forms, is fundamentally a product of social reality.

It is not an isolated psychological phenomenon in the idealist and metaphysical sense in which bourgeois psychology understands psychological and spiritual phenomena.

We have seen this truth impose itself even on researchers who begin from idealist and supernatural assumptions when trying to explain the rise of asceticism and Sufism among the Arab Muslims.

The English scholar Nicholson,[^V3-56] for example, acknowledges that in “the first century of Islam (...) there were many factors that encouraged the rise and spread of asceticism, such as the long and bloody civil wars that took place during the period of the Companions and the Umayyads, the violent extremism of political parties, the growing relaxation of moral standards, and the suffering of Muslims under oppressive rulers.”

Modern studies of “Islamic Sufism” contain many similar acknowledgments.

But many researchers fall into a direct contradiction when they deal with this question.

As long as they remain at the level of abstract theoretical analysis, they explain the rise of Sufism through religious factors connected with Islam itself as a divine teaching, or through purely external influences.

But when they turn to the actual history of Sufi movements, they begin to see the deep connection between the rise and development of these movements and the social reality of Arab-Islamic society as it changed over time.

Yet instead of allowing this evidence to make them reconsider their theoretical conclusions, they separate experience from theory and historical description from analysis.[^V3-57]

We should pause here and make sure we do not fall into the same mistake we have criticized in others: looking at the question from only one side.

So, alongside the basic fact that Sufi phenomena were connected to social relations and to the way those relations changed through history, we must keep a second fact in mind. Human mental and spiritual activity—thinking, cultural development, and all their different forms, expressions, and early beginnings—is far too complex to be reduced to the kind of simple explanation we may sometimes seem to be giving it.

It is true that this activity, both in its origins and its development, is basically a product of social conditions. But it also has its own inner logic, which has a certain degree of independence as it develops through its many interactions. At times, this inner movement can take directions that have no obvious connection to the immediate direction of social life. We will see examples of this later in the study.

So we are not looking at these phenomena from only one side. At the same time, however, we do not treat all the different factors that influence them as equally important. The main factor remains the overall direction in which social relations are developing as they continue to change.

#### 2 — The Ascetic Movement

##### Its Social and Political Origins

The Sufi movement first began to take shape in embryonic form with the rise of the ascetic movement during the first century AH, or the seventh century CE.

At this stage, a number of prominent figures in Arab-Islamic society adopted asceticism in what we might call its negative sense. They withdrew from public social and political life and devoted their energies to the worship of God.

This was not simply a personal choice made freely by individual ascetics, cut off from the events of their society and their time. It was itself a product of those events. These ascetic figures were a social phenomenon. Their particular way of life expressed a passive political response to the events shaking Arab society while that society was going through an extraordinarily rapid process of formation and consolidation.

We must, however, distinguish between the asceticism of some of the leading Companions of the Prophet and the kind of conduct that began to appear after the first great social uprising in Arab-Islamic history. That uprising was directed immediately against the third Rightly Guided Caliph, ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān. Its effects then grew until they developed into bloody events and wars that brought the young society some of the most violent forms of political conflict it had yet seen. Behind this political conflict were the first elements of social conflict as well.

The difference between these two forms of asceticism is quite clear.

The first—the asceticism of the Companions—was mainly moral in character. It meant living a simple and modest everyday life. It did not develop into a broad social phenomenon, nor did it express any distinct position on Islamic affairs, on the nature of Islamic “metaphysics,” on the main problems of society, or on knowledge and its sources.

This form of ascetic life reflected the early beginnings of Islam and the simple lives of the first Muslims, before the conquests outside the Arabian Peninsula brought major changes into their way of life.

The second form was very different.

It became a widespread phenomenon and one of the most visible features of the new society. It took a religious form, but its basic content was social and political.

In reality, it was an intense reaction to the series of dangerous and bloody events and political struggles that followed the killing of Caliph ʿUthmān. The distinctive feature of this kind of “asceticism” was precisely that it expressed political opposition to those events and struggles.

At first, however, that opposition was purely negative. A number of prominent Muslim figures rejected the bloodshed that followed ʿUthmān’s death, as well as the political forces behind the conflict between the two sides. But they did not take an active part in the struggle. They did not state clearly where they believed the error and falsehood lay, where truth and justice lay, or stand with what they believed to be right against what they believed to be wrong.

Instead, their rejection of the conflict led them to stand outside it. They withdrew from both sides, remained in their homes, gave up participation in public life, and chose instead to turn inward and devote themselves to the worship of God.

Al-Nawbakhtī tells us about this group. He says that after ʿUthmān was killed, the people pledged allegiance to ʿAlī and were called “the Community.” They then divided into three groups. One remained loyal to ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib. Another withdrew with Saʿd ibn Mālik—that is, Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqāṣ—along with ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, Muḥammad ibn Maslama al-Anṣārī, and Usāma ibn Zayd ibn Ḥāritha al-Kalbī, the freedman of the Messenger of God.[^V3-58]

According to al-Nawbakhtī, these men withdrew from ʿAlī and refused either to fight him or to fight alongside him, even though they had already accepted him and pledged allegiance to him. They were therefore called the Muʿtazila—“those who withdrew”—and, he says, became the ancestors of the later Muʿtazila. Their position was: “It is not lawful to fight ʿAlī, nor is it lawful to fight alongside him.”[^V3-59]

Ḥassān ibn Thābit, the Prophet’s poet, and ʿAbd Allāh ibn Salām also belonged to this group. They followed the position expressed by Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqāṣ:

> “The Messenger of God instructed me that if the people fell into conflict, I should take my sword and strike it against the wall until it broke. Then I was to remain in my house and not leave it until either a mistaken hand reached me or death came for me.”[^V3-60]

But this purely negative position among the ascetics did not last long.

Asceticism soon began to appear and spread in a different form among educated people and among those who had taken part in political or administrative work. It increasingly became a form of opposition to the existing social and political order under Umayyad rule. This was a time when the gap within society was becoming extreme: the rulers were growing enormously wealthy, while broad sections of the population were being driven into poverty. These included educated people[^V3-61] and veterans of the earlier struggles for Islam.[^V3-62]

Take ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar himself. We have just seen that after the killing of ʿUthmān he was among those who withdrew completely from the conflict. Yet during the rule of the tyrant al-Ḥajjāj, he openly declared:

> “I have not eaten my fill since ʿUthmān was killed.”[^V3-63]

When ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar said this, he was not complaining that he personally lacked food. He was still living the life of a strict ascetic. His statement was a public protest against the social injustices of the age of al-Ḥajjāj, just as his ascetic way of life itself expressed that opposition.

Asceticism at this point was therefore no longer simply a refusal to take sides or an escape from political conflict. It had entered the struggle on the side of those who opposed unjust social and political domination.

Among the growing number of ascetics, many now gave their way of life this meaning. From the second half of the first century AH onward, asceticism in Kufa, Basra, and Egypt was no longer simply a retreat from conflict, as it had been immediately after the death of ʿUthmān. It had become a passive protest against the existing social order and the political authorities that represented it, especially against the injustice those authorities produced.

Social injustice was what drove people in Kufa and Basra toward asceticism. It was an asceticism born from events involving “terrible killing, betrayal, support for falsehood, and anguish at being unable to resist injustice.”[^V3-64]

Goldziher was therefore right when he examined reports about the piety and asceticism of men who followed this tendency, including some who had once been leading fighters for Islam. From these reports he concluded that “the tendency toward asceticism was connected with rebellion against the existing authority,” and that many Muslims, “in protest against a government and order they rejected, turned to withdrawal and asceticism.”[^V3-65]

In al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī (22–110 AH/642–728 CE), one of the leading intellectual figures of his time, we find the earliest and clearest example of this type of first-century ascetic in Basra.

We have already seen that his asceticism was marked by deep sadness,[^V3-66] and that his statements help explain why.[^V3-67] They reveal what we might call a strong “guilt complex.”

The same feeling helps explain the sadness and fear found among many ascetics of this period who were contemporaries of al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī.

They were haunted by the feeling that they were guilty. Terrible injustices and abuses were spreading through their society, while they either remained silent and did nothing to prevent them, failed to oppose them openly, or found themselves unable to change them once they had grown too powerful.

They therefore lived in constant sadness and fear that they might share responsibility for what was happening. In asceticism, especially in the harsh disciplining of the self, they found a way to make amends for a guilt that continued to trouble their consciences day and night.

Ibn al-Athīr tells us[^V3-68] that al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī was once asked about his earlier criticism of ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib for going to war against his opponents. Al-Ḥasan replied that it had been “a false word by which I spared blood.”

What he meant, according to the account, was that he had spared his own blood.

His statement therefore means that he had once said something about ʿAlī that he did not truly believe, simply to save his own life.

This is a clear statement. At a time well after the Battles of the Camel and Ṣiffīn, al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī had moved away from hesitation and neutrality—or from silence caused by fear for his own life—and toward fear of the guilt he felt over what he had done. He had come to regret having supported what he believed was falsehood rather than the truth.

This reading is strengthened by the fact that immediately after making this admission, al-Ḥasan described Muʿāwiya, ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib’s greatest opponent, in extremely harsh terms.[^V3-69]
