By this point, al-Ḥasan’s sense of guilt had grown so strong that he openly expressed things he had previously hidden or been reluctant to say.

This also helps explain his position on the question of divine decree, which we discussed earlier.[^V3-70]

We saw that Maʿbad al-Juhanī and ʿAṭāʾ ibn Yasār came to al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī and said:

> “Abū Saʿīd, these kings”—meaning the Umayyad rulers—“shed Muslim blood and take Muslim property, and then say: ‘Our actions simply follow the decree of God Most High.’”

Al-Ḥasan replied:

> “The enemies of God are lying.”[^V3-71]

Here, the leading ascetic of Basra went far beyond merely stating an opinion about divine decree, although even that opinion was itself a direct and serious challenge to the Umayyad rulers.

He explicitly accused the Umayyads of lying, and then called them “the enemies of God.”

Al-Ḥasan’s position here clearly shows how far he had moved away from the caution, hesitation, and accommodation he had shown toward the Umayyads during the uprising against Caliph ʿUthmān and during the Battles of the Camel and Ṣiffīn.

The asceticism that became part of his new position after this change also became the general character of the ascetic movement of the period, especially in Iraq.

Iraq, after all, had been the main center of resistance to Umayyad domination since the caliphate of ʿUthmān. It was there that the caliph’s governor in Kufa, one of the Umayyad princes, had made the arrogant declaration:

> “The Sawād is a garden belonging to Quraysh.”[^V3-72]

After what we have seen, the main features of asceticism in this period can be described as follows.

First, its basic content was a clear rejection of the direction of social and political life under Umayyad rule.

Second, this rejection appeared in a particular way of life. Ascetics withdrew from all forms of social activity and devoted themselves entirely to religious worship. They forced themselves to endure the most difficult forms of worship, denied themselves the pleasures of life, and lived with constant sadness and anxiety over the consequences of their “sins”—sins which, as we have seen, were political at their core.

At times, they also tried to make amends for these “sins” in ways that went beyond strictly religious practice.

Sometimes this meant openly expressing their views on current social and political events, as al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī did in his later years.

Sometimes it meant joining an armed uprising against Umayyad rule, as Saʿīd ibn Jubayr,[^V3-73] one of the ascetics of Kufa, did.

And sometimes it meant remaining courageously loyal to the strongest enemies of the Umayyads, even at the risk of death. Many Shiʿi ascetics did this, especially during the rule of al-Ḥajjāj. Kumayl ibn Ziyād al-Nakhaʿī was one of them.[^V3-74]

These features of the ascetic movement after the middle of the first century AH can be found among the ascetics of Basra and Kufa, as well as among those of Egypt and Syria.

There were, of course, local differences between these places. But the broad historical conditions that produced the ascetic movement, and the personal responses that grew out of those conditions, were largely similar. They were not fundamentally different in Basra and Kufa, or in Iraq and Egypt.[^V3-75]

Syria was somewhat different because the Umayyads had spent decades bringing its people under their political influence and control. Even so, during the period we are discussing, Syria also produced ascetics who shared the general characteristics of the wider movement. Among them were Abū Muslim ʿAbd Allāh ibn Thawwāb al-Khawlānī (d. 65 AH/684 CE)[^V3-76] and Yazīd ibn Marthad al-Hamdānī.[^V3-77]

Persia and Khurasan also became known for ascetic movements with their own special character. But Islamic asceticism did not emerge there in a clearly distinct form until after the end of the first century AH.[^V3-78]

#### 3 — Embryonic Sufism Within the Ascetic Movement

By tracing the social and political roots of the early ascetic movement in Arab-Islamic society during the second half of the first century AH, we do not mean that asceticism was simply a social and political movement in the direct and obvious sense.

That would be a crude and mechanical explanation, and it is not what we mean.

Asceticism at this time was also connected, from another side, to the intellectual life of society.

It was not merely a way of behaving. There was also a set of ideas behind ascetic conduct, even if those ideas themselves grew out of a reaction to social and political conditions.

So if we want to understand the historical development of asceticism, we must also look at its own inner movement. Anything connected to intellectual activity develops partly through an internal process of its own.

During this period, the ascetic movement was already carrying within itself the seeds of new ways of looking at life and the universe.

It also contained the beginnings of new ways of interpreting Islamic texts: new positions toward the religious law, prophecy, the imamate, and other Islamic concepts; new ideas about the source of knowledge; and new ways of understanding the place of human beings in existence.

All of these tendencies, which appear only in embryonic form within asceticism, will later appear in Sufism.

This, then, is the intellectual side of asceticism. It is what connects the ascetic phenomenon of this period to the broader movement of Arab thought.

From this same intellectual side, asceticism would later develop into a major field of Arab philosophical thought: Sufism.

Sufism would go on to play a distinctive role in later ideological conflicts. When it first emerged, the Sufi movement would express an ideological position, just as asceticism had earlier expressed a political one.

The difference between the two in social life is matched by the difference between them in intellectual life.

Sufism would play an important role in the development of Arab thought. The earlier ascetic movement, by contrast, had very little direct influence on that intellectual development during its own period.

Yet asceticism still had enormous importance because it was the embryonic stage from which Sufism grew.

What interests us now is how this embryonic stage appeared and developed within the ascetic movement itself. We need to trace the intellectual seeds we have just mentioned and see how they appeared and grew in the conduct of the ascetics and in their earliest mystical ideas.

##### First Observation

We now come to what may be the earliest example of a mystical vision already present within the first ascetic movement.

Al-Sarrāj al-Ṭūsī reports that ʿĀmir ibn ʿAbd Qays said:

> “If the veil were lifted for me, I would be no more certain than I am now.”[^V3-79]

ʿĀmir was one of the earliest ascetics of Basra. He died around 60 AH, during the caliphate of Muʿāwiya ibn Abī Sufyān, and had therefore lived during the time of ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib.

It is not surprising, then, that there should be some historical confusion over this statement. We also find it attributed to ʿAlī.[^V3-80]

For our purposes, it makes little difference whether the words were originally spoken by ʿAlī, ʿĀmir, or another ascetic from the earliest Islamic period, as long as they were not attributed to someone from outside that period, so far as we know.

What matters is that we find here an early mystical “vision” emerging from within the ascetic movement.

Its content is remarkably advanced for its time, because it points ahead toward one of the theoretical foundations of the Sufi theory of knowledge.

The statement itself does not tell us where this certainty comes from.

The speaker’s certainty is already so complete that he claims he would gain nothing more even if the veil were removed and the truth itself appeared directly before him. Seeing the truth with his own eyes would add nothing to the certainty he already possesses.

It is clear that the “truth” meant here—the object of this certainty—is God.

But the statement does not tell us what produces this certainty.

Does it come from rational proof? From illumination? From mystical intuition? Or is it simply a direct and uncomplicated certainty of faith?

The historical period in which the statement appeared makes it difficult to say that this certainty was based on rational demonstration. Nor can we reasonably say that it came from a developed form of Sufi illumination.

At that time, the necessary intellectual conditions did not yet exist for theoretical thought to develop to the point where the ascetics of the first Islamic period would consciously search for truth through either of these methods. Nor had the methods or tools for such a search yet been fully formed.

We therefore think it is most likely that the “certainty” expressed here was simply a certainty of faith, rooted in the intense emotional devotion that marked the Companions and the Successors[^V3-81] in the earliest period of Islam.

But this interpretation does not reduce the historical importance of the statement. Nor does it reduce the power and precision with which it expresses religious certainty.

Its historical value lies in the fact that it contains an embryonic seed of Sufi thought long before Sufism itself emerged as a developed intellectual movement.

The language of certain knowledge and of “unveiling”—even in a very vague form—anticipates ideas that would become basic concepts of Sufi philosophy roughly two centuries later.

This is not the place to investigate the statement closely enough to decide who truly said it, whether ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib or the Basran ascetic ʿĀmir ibn ʿAbd Qays.

Even so, we think there is good reason to connect it with ʿĀmir.

That connection becomes clearer when we look at the striking change in his state of mind.

At one point, his fear, anxiety, and sadness were so intense that he “writhed like a snake on a hot pan,” then stood and cried:

> “God, the Fire has kept me from sleep. Forgive me.”[^V3-82]

For the same reason, he seemed to rule out any possibility of rest or joy for himself:

> “God, in this world there are worries and sorrows, and in the next there are punishment and reckoning. Where, then, are rest and joy?”[^V3-83]

Yet this same ascetic later changed.

He became a man who found within himself the joy of peace and contentment. We can almost hear that joy in his words:

> “I loved God with a love that made every misfortune easy for me and made me content with every decree.”[^V3-84]

So we must ask: what changed ʿĀmir?

How did he move from the sadness and anxiety that marked the asceticism of his age to a state of contentment, peace, and love—a love so deep that it made every hardship easy and left him content with whatever came?

The earlier statement seems to answer the question.

It appears that, at the height of his inner crisis, ʿĀmir was suddenly overcome by a feeling of complete “certainty.” That certainty changed his state. It replaced sadness with joy and anxiety with peace.

Looking at these two sides of ʿĀmir ibn ʿAbd Qays makes it easier to see a connection between him and the statement:

> “If the veil were lifted for me, I would be no more certain than I am now.”

This connection makes it likely that the statement truly came from him, whether he originated it himself or simply adopted it because it expressed the overwhelming certainty he felt.

In any case, these statements attributed to ʿĀmir ibn ʿAbd Qays give us examples of the embryonic seeds of religious Sufi ideas already present in the asceticism of the first century AH.

Those ideas would develop into the religious Sufism of the second century, which in turn prepared the ground for the philosophical Sufism of the third century AH, or the ninth century CE.

##### Second Observation

The theoretical basis of Sufism as a philosophical movement begins with the idea that knowledge can be reached through “unveiling,” direct “vision,” “illumination,” or inward mystical intuition.

But the practical path toward this knowledge is based on a particular way of life.

Sufis called it **mujāhadat al-nafs**: the struggle to discipline the self. It meant training oneself to endure severe suffering and restraining the desire for sensual and worldly pleasures.

All the later systems and traditions of Sufi life, in their many different orders and schools, were built on this basic principle.

And it is here that we find the real historical link between asceticism and Sufism.

The different forms of discipline later practiced by Sufis as a path toward knowledge began as an extension of the way of life followed by the ascetics of the first century AH, which we have already described.

This meant living austerely, forcing oneself to endure difficult acts of worship, and giving up the pleasures of life.

It could even mean training oneself not merely to refuse such pleasures, but to stop desiring them in the first place, as a way of making amends for sins the ascetics believed they had committed.[^V3-85]

This was the first sap that fed the tree of Sufism.

For that reason, **zuhd**, or asceticism, remained a basic and permanent part of Sufi practice throughout the different stages of Islamic Sufism.

This is also why Sufis counted asceticism among the **stations** (*maqāmāt*) of the Sufi path rather than among its **states** (*aḥwāl*).[^V3-86]

For them, a “station” had the character of a goal, while a “state” had the character of a means. And the goal stood above the means.

But this does not mean that the concept of asceticism remained as simple as it had been at the beginning, when it was mainly a way of life expressing a political position.

As social conditions changed, and as different outside cultural influences entered the picture, the concept itself became more complex. New elements were added to it, with different origins, meanings, forms, and purposes.

##### Third Observation

The original connection between asceticism and Sufism was not limited to the ascetic way of life.

There was another connection that we might reasonably call “theoretical,” and it also created a deep link between the two.

It is true that the earliest ascetic movement had nothing to do with the question of “knowledge” from which, as we have said, the theoretical basis of Sufism as a philosophical movement would later develop.

At the beginning, asceticism recognized no source for knowledge of God and the religious law other than the source established by Islam itself: divine revelation to the Prophet.

That is true.

But the “theoretical” side of asceticism came from somewhere else.

The early ascetic movement was, in origin and basic character, a direct negative reaction to the social and political conditions of its age.

The very word **zuhd**, in its ordinary linguistic meaning,[^V3-87] expressed this negative response to everyday reality. It meant withdrawing from the existing struggle and giving up active involvement in the daily life of society.

From the beginning, however, this negative attitude toward social life produced something more than a political position toward the great events of the time.

It also produced a broader view of life and death.

The religious ideas of “this life” and “the next life” were given a much stronger and more extreme meaning than they had normally carried within Islam.

The ascetics did not simply prefer the next life to this one.

They began to measure the sincerity of asceticism by how deeply a person hated and despised “this world.”

As al-Ṭūsī put it:

> “Love of this world is the root of every sin, and renunciation of this world is the root of every good and every act of obedience.”[^V3-88]

So although the ascetics of this period began from what was basically a political position, they supported that position with a particular view of life and of what lies beyond life.

However simple this view may still have been, it already belonged to the world of ideas and theoretical reflection.

This general “theoretical” position then led the ascetics of the period toward their own interpretations of passages from the Qurʾān and the hadith, in ways that suited the demands of their outlook.[^V3-89]

On the basis of these interpretations, they began to give pure religious worship priority over social activity and earning a living.

They also denied themselves pleasures and forms of enjoyment that Islam itself allowed: good food and drink, attractive clothing and appearance, and other comforts of life. Some of them even came close to giving up sexual relations altogether.[^V3-90]

These early ascetic interpretations became the seed of the method of **interpretation** later adopted by Islamic Sufis.

From this method came the famous Sufi distinction between the **outward** and the **inward**—the *ẓāhir* and the *bāṭin*.

From the third century AH, or the ninth century CE, onward, this distinction became one of the basic ideas used by Sufis of many different orders, schools, and tendencies throughout Arab-Islamic society.

It opened a wide path for Sufi opposition to official religious thought, both in matters of doctrine and in matters of religious law.

On this basis, Sufis argued that the Qurʾān has an “outward” meaning: the direct meaning of its words and expressions as ordinary people understand them.

But they also argued that it has an “inward” meaning.[^V3-91]

For them, this inward meaning was the real meaning, and it could be uncovered only by “those firmly rooted in knowledge”—that is, in **gnosis**, or mystical knowledge. These were the Sufi “knowers.”[^V3-92]

They called the first level **the religious law** (*sharīʿa*) and the second **the truth** (*ḥaqīqa*).

From this they eventually reached the claim that “the religious law falls away once the truth is unveiled.”[^V3-93]

In other words, once the Sufi had reached direct knowledge of the “truth,” he was no longer necessarily bound by the rules of the religious law.

#### 4 — Conclusion, and the Logic Behind It

The historical evidence leads us to the following conclusion.

Asceticism emerged around the middle of the first century AH as one of the deep and powerful results of the conflict that had broken out within Arab-Islamic society while that society was still in the early stages of its formation.

One point deserves special attention.

This conflict broke out immediately after the first signs of class division had begun to appear, though these divisions still took a tribal form. The wealth of the Umayyads who surrounded Caliph ʿUthmān and served as his provincial governors had grown enormously.

The conflict therefore began as a social conflict.

It then found political expression in armed clashes and the formation of rival groups. As the conflict deepened and its social base widened, it also began to find expression in the world of ideas.

At the intellectual level, the conflict developed in several different circles. These circles opposed one another and interacted with one another at the same time.

The ascetic movement was one of them.

It did indeed come into conflict with other tendencies—with the Qadarites, for example, and with the school of the People of Hadith.[^V3-94]

But at the same time, it interacted with each of these tendencies. It influenced them and was influenced by them, all within the wider development of society itself.

This is why asceticism at the time had several different sides: social, political, and intellectual.

Yet whichever side we look at, we can see within it some sign of the new movement that asceticism was carrying in embryonic form—the movement that the same society would later bring into being under new historical conditions.

That movement was Sufism.

There is a logic behind this conclusion, and it leads us to an important result for the present study.

The first form of asceticism in Arab-Islamic society was produced by the particular conditions of a society that was still being formed and was, at the same time, experiencing intense internal conflict.

But by “conflict” here we do not mean that society was simply breaking apart or falling into decay.

In reality, this was the turbulence of growth and vitality.

The older social cells were breaking open so that new ones could develop in their place. The remnants of an earlier, more primitive social order and of a largely self-sufficient economy were giving way to the structures of a new society: a historically more developed, feudal, commercial, class-based society.

In a period of such intense growth, it is natural for social reactions to multiply, differ, and come into conflict with one another.

Some reactions will be negative and others positive. Some will take the form of rejection, while others will take the form of acceptance or integration.

But this process of growth and development is so complex that a negative phenomenon does not necessarily remain negative forever, just as a positive one does not necessarily remain positive forever.

There is no place here for an absolute negative or an absolute positive.

Everything is subject to development and change.

Even rejection and acceptance cannot automatically be described as progressive or reactionary in every case.

Rejection can be progressive in one respect and acceptance reactionary in another, and the reverse can also be true.

This is why we saw the ascetic movement begin as a purely negative withdrawal and then gradually change.

It became a symbol of opposition to political tyranny and social injustice. Ascetics gained a position that drew the sympathy of the oppressed masses and, in Goldziher’s words, “won the hearts of the common people.”[^V3-95]

The ascetic movement then continued to develop until it reached a higher form: Sufism.

We can express the same historical process in another way.

Sufism first existed in embryonic form as an ascetic way of life based on withdrawal.

It then developed into an intellectual position containing two forms of opposition: a religious one, through new interpretations of scripture, and a political one, through the condemnation of social injustice and tyranny.

Later we will see how, at a higher stage of development, Sufism became a new form of philosophical consciousness among the medieval Arabs, with its own concepts and its own inner logic, distinct from philosophy in the narrower sense.

This final change was connected to another change in Sufism itself.

At this later stage, Sufism became the expression of an ideological position, as we will see in the course of the study.

#### 5 — The Development of the Term

The general pattern we have traced in the organic relationship between asceticism and Sufism is also confirmed by the history of the word **Sufism** itself.

The change in content—from asceticism to Sufism—was preceded by a similar change in terminology and naming.

The specialized sources and studies we have on the history of Sufism broadly agree that the term “Sufi” was applied to many ascetics, and that the term “Sufism” was used for the ascetic movement itself, before asceticism in its early and simple form had actually developed into Sufism in the later theoretical and philosophical sense.

In other words, the change in terminology began as part of the wider process that eventually produced the change in content.

The author of *al-Risāla al-Qushayriyya* tells us that the name “Sufism” was applied to “the select among the People of the Sunna... before the year 200 AH.”[^V3-96]

The author of *al-Lumaʿ* dates the use of the name “Sufis” even earlier. He says that “this name was known in the time of al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī,”[^V3-97] meaning the first century AH, or the seventh century CE.

Goldziher supports this early dating when he says that the word “Sufi” began to be used during the caliphate of ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān (65–86 AH/684–705 CE).[^V3-98]

In *al-Fihrist*, Ibn al-Nadīm describes Jābir ibn Ḥayyān as “the one known as the Sufi.”[^V3-99]

Ibn al-Nadīm clearly treats Jābir as a real historical person and gives him the scientific qualities attributed to him by most historical sources and modern studies.[^V3-100]

The author of *Rawḍāt al-Jannāt* follows him in calling Jābir ibn Ḥayyān a “Sufi.”[^V3-101]

If Jābir was born in 120 AH and lived for about eighty years, from 737 to 813 CE,[^V3-102] then the title “Sufi” was clearly known and in ordinary use during the second century AH, or the eighth century CE.

The following evidence confirms this further.

During the first half of that century, one of the ascetics of Kufa, Abū Hāshim al-Kūfī (d. 150 AH), was widely known by the title “the Sufi,” and there is no real dispute about this.[^V3-103]

Muḥammad Kurd ʿAlī goes further and describes him as the first Sunni to be called a Sufi.[^V3-104]

Historians and researchers have disagreed about Abū Hāshim, just as they have disagreed about Jābir ibn Ḥayyān. Was he truly a “Sufi,” and what exactly did his “Sufism” mean?

That disagreement does not concern us here.

What matters is that the disagreement itself confirms that the terms “Sufi” and “Sufism” existed in their period, the second century AH, and were already familiar and widely known.

There were many others in the same period who were known by the title “Sufi.”

One was ʿUbaydak al-Ṣūfī, who is said to have been “the first Kufan to be called a Sufi after moving to Baghdad.”[^V3-105]

This ʿUbaydak was an ascetic known for his distinctive appearance and way of life. He died around 210 AH/825–826 CE.[^V3-106]

There was also ʿAbd al-Wāḥid ibn Zayd, one of the ascetic preachers of Basra, who died in 177 AH/793 CE. Ibn Taymiyya described him as “the first Sufi.”[^V3-107]

We also hear Shaqīq al-Balkhī, one of the ascetics of Khurasan who died in 194 AH/809 CE, say in one of his sermons:

> “In every age, the shepherds are the scholars and the Sufis.”[^V3-108]

The matter-of-fact way this statement is expressed shows that “the Sufis” had already become a familiar term, and that the existence of people known by this name during the second century AH was already taken for granted.

Adam Mez gives us another example. He writes that:

> “In the year 200 AH, a group appeared in Alexandria who were called Sufis. They claimed to command what is right and opposed the ruler in his affairs.”

Their leader was a man called Abū ʿAbd al-Raḥmān al-Ṣūfī.[^V3-109]

This report gives us several historically important facts.

First, by the year 200 AH, people called “Sufis” clearly existed in Alexandria.

Second, they formed a distinct group. In other words, they had become visible enough within Alexandrian society to constitute a broader social phenomenon.

Third, they had some form of organization, since they had a leader, Abū ʿAbd al-Raḥmān al-Ṣūfī.

Fourth, their presence had enough influence within society to lead them into social and political activity.

They “commanded what is right” and opposed the ruler.

This is the fifth fact, and the most important.

If we leave Adam Mez’s account and return to the source from which he took it—al-Kindī, Abū ʿUmar Muḥammad ibn Yūsuf al-Miṣrī the historian, not al-Kindī the philosopher—we find that this same “Sufi group” was invited to take part in one of the uprisings in Alexandria.

They did take part, joining forces with their Andalusian allies.

The alliance was victorious.

The influence of the “Sufi group” in the uprising was great enough that its leader, Abū ʿAbd al-Raḥmān al-Ṣūfī, took control of Alexandria in the name of the alliance itself.[^V3-110]

All of these events took place in the year 200 AH.

But that year obviously cannot have been the year in which the Sufi group itself first came into existence.

For the group to have reached such a visible position in history, it must already have existed for some time before 200 AH.

It had to develop into a distinct “group,” acquire an organizational structure, and become an effective social force before it could play the role we have just described.

---

This brief survey shows that a kind of historical continuity developed between the terms **asceticism** and **Sufism** over a period of almost a century, from the first to the second century AH.

It also shows that this happened at roughly the same time across much of the settled territory ruled by the Arab-Islamic state under the Abbasids.

But this does not mean that the major qualitative change had already taken place during the second century AH.

Asceticism in its early and simple sense had not yet fully developed into Sufism in its later theoretical and philosophical sense merely because the two terms had begun to overlap.

So what did the words “Sufi” and “Sufism” mean during this period if they did not yet refer to Sufism in its fully developed historical and theoretical sense?

We think al-Qushayrī gives the clearest answer when he says:

> “The select among the People of the Sunna, who watched their every breath with God Most High and guarded their hearts against the approach of heedlessness, came to be known by the name of Sufism. This name became famous for these great men before the year 200 AH.”[^V3-111]

According to this statement by al-Qushayrī, before the year 200 AH the term “Sufism” meant little more than what the term “asceticism” had already meant before Sufism developed into a theoretical, philosophical, and ideological movement.

Several points we have already discussed support this conclusion.

The first is al-Qushayrī’s statement that before 200 AH the name “Sufism” was associated with “the select among the People of the Sunna.”

The expression “People of the Sunna” itself points clearly to the traditional Muslim community. It is normally used in contrast to “Sufis” in the later theoretical sense, as well as to rationalist, philosophical, and esoteric schools.

The word “select,” meanwhile, clearly refers to the ascetics who differed from ordinary Sunnis through the special practices of **zuhd**.

Al-Qushayrī summarizes those practices when he describes them as people “who watched their every breath with God” and guarded themselves against heedlessness.

The second indication is what the author of *al-Lumaʿ* says, as we have already noted: that the name “Sufis” was known in the time of al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī.

We also saw Goldziher’s view that the word “Sufi” began to be used during the caliphate of ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān.

No historian or researcher of Islamic Sufism has claimed that Sufism in its developed theoretical sense already existed in the age of al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī or during the caliphate of ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān.

So if the terms “Sufis” and “Sufi” were already being used in that period, their meaning cannot have gone beyond the same basic meaning then carried by the term **asceticism**, with the historical features it had at the time.[^V3-112]

The third indication is something else we have already seen: the use of the title “Sufi” for figures such as Jābir ibn Ḥayyān, Abū Hāshim al-Kūfī, ʿUbaydak al-Ṣūfī, and ʿAbd al-Wāḥid ibn Zayd.

In none of their biographies do we find evidence that they added anything to traditional asceticism that would clearly show it developing into Sufism in the full and later sense.

If that is so—if calling the ascetics of the second Islamic century “Sufis,” and calling their asceticism “Sufism,” does not mean that traditional Islamic asceticism had already undergone a fundamental change into theoretical, philosophical Sufism—then why did people begin calling asceticism “Sufism” and the ascetic a “Sufi”?

We will not reopen the long historical debate over how these two terms arose. Many different views have already been put forward. But taken as a whole, those views, together with an objective historical look at the question itself, suggest a fairly simple origin for the connection between asceticism and the words *Sufi* and *Sufism*.

The connection lay in the material link between asceticism as a particular way of life and the wearing of wool. In ancient times, woolen clothing was associated with poverty and the condition of the poor, just as fine silk was associated with wealth, luxury, and people of high social standing.

The woolen clothing meant here was, of course, completely different from the expensive, finely made woolen fabrics of modern times. Ancient wool was processed only in a rough and basic way, which made it clothing for the poor. Since asceticism in the first Islamic century required ascetics to keep away from every sign of luxury and comfortable living, they adopted rough, primitive woolen clothing as an outward symbol of their ascetic way of life.[^V3-113]

That explains the outward form. But there was also a social and political meaning behind it.

At the time, wearing wool could express a kind of defiance and passive opposition toward the ruling Umayyad aristocracy. In this sense, it carried the same meaning that asceticism itself had carried when it first appeared.

It is no accident that the ascetics of Kufa were among the first ascetics of their age to wear wool.[^V3-114] Kufa was the main center of opposition to the Umayyads. And from the time of Caliph Sulayman ibn ʿAbd al-Malik, whose reign began in 96 AH / 714 CE, the Umayyad caliphs made a point, as al-Masʿudi tells us,[^V3-115] of appearing in richly patterned clothes as a display of refinement and luxury.[^V3-116] Kufa itself was also one of the cities most famous for producing these fine fabrics under Umayyad rule.

The ascetics of Kufa therefore wore rough wool partly as an answer to the rulers’ luxury and as a criticism of their way of life. It became one form of political opposition.

Ibn Khaldun may be pointing to something similar when he chooses to derive the word “Sufi” from *suf*, meaning wool. Speaking of the Sufis, he says: “They were generally known for wearing it, because they went against the way people dressed in luxurious clothes and wore wool instead.”[^V3-117]

#### 6 — And within this “historical continuity”!

In its basic character, then, the ascetic movement of the second Islamic century still remained traditional asceticism. It had not yet reached the point where it would become theoretical Sufism.

Instead, it had entered a kind of stagnation that we can call “historical continuity”—by which we mean that its growth and development had largely stopped.

The question now is: why?

If we look beneath the visible surface of the movement, we can begin to see the social and political reasons why this historical continuity lasted through the second Islamic century, preventing asceticism from moving very far toward what we call theoretical Sufism.

Asceticism had first appeared partly as a political response to bloody political conflict. It then became a response to social oppression and injustice after the rise of Umayyad rule, especially during the tyranny of al-Hajjaj. In this sense, asceticism kept its original position into the early years of the second Islamic century.

After that, however, asceticism gradually began to separate from the conditions that had originally produced it.

Its social and political side started to fade into the background, while its religious side became more visible. This religious side had always been part of its outward form as a way of life. But it now began to dominate the social and political side that had originally been central to its existence.

We believe this happened because of two main factors.

The first was the nature of the ascetic withdrawal itself.

Although asceticism had begun as an expression of social and political rejection of particular conflicts within society, it took the form of withdrawal not only from political struggle but from practical social activity in general.

During this withdrawal, the ascetics adopted a purely religious way of life that became increasingly cut off from social reality. It even became cut off from the practical and active side of Islam itself.

After this isolation had continued for several decades, it produced two results.

First, these ascetics gradually lost sight of society to such an extent that their social awareness almost lost the ability to respond to the events and developments taking place around them.[^V3-118]

Second, they became deeply absorbed in religious rituals. Over time, this intense absorption drew them into a world of their own, with its own closed metaphysics and its own supernatural logic.

It may have been in this almost “alienated” metaphysical world that some of them developed the tendency later called “divine love.” The same tendency would later reappear within Sufism in the form of philosophical ideas such as the “unity of being,” “union,” “indwelling,” and others.

The second factor was the development of social and political conflict within Umayyad society.

This conflict became broader and more complicated as its social base expanded. Eventually it reached a major part of society’s productive population: the masses of poor peasants.

These peasants were crushed by heavy taxes and harsh systems of taxation and daily life.[^V3-119] This drove them into the field of social conflict and gave them the material basis to become, in objective terms, one side of a class conflict, even if they did not yet consciously see themselves in those terms.

This development left the ascetic movement behind.

Asceticism, as a social position, could no longer express a response suited to these new and much more complex conditions.

In fact, the new stage of social conflict demanded more than what we earlier called a “political position,” whether that position took the form of opposition and rejection or support and acceptance.

The struggle had developed beyond that point.

As social conflict became broader and more complex, it needed a deeper form of expression at the level of ideas. When asceticism first emerged in the middle of the first Islamic century, a political position had been enough. Now something more was needed.

What was needed was an **ideological position**.

But asceticism, in the “metaphysical” form it had reached by the beginning of the second Islamic century, was not equipped to provide an ideological position suited to the needs of the new stage.

At the same time, however, another way of expressing social reality through ideas was beginning to fill that need.

This appeared first in the position of the Qadarites, which was now developing into a movement with its own social dimensions and intellectual foundations: the Muʿtazila.

The main distinguishing feature of the Muʿtazila was their rationalist outlook.

In several important ways, this differed sharply from the direction taken by the ascetic movement, which, as we have seen, was moving toward a kind of “alienation”—an alienation even from rational thought itself.

Taken as a whole, then, this means that during the second Islamic century, the eighth century CE, it was the **rationalist current** that met society’s growing need for ideological expression at the intellectual level.

Traditional asceticism had been overtaken by history.

#### 7 — Stones in the stagnant lake

After the middle of the second Islamic century, however, we begin to see a movement taking place within asceticism itself.

It was hidden and slow, but it began to disturb the historical continuity of asceticism and shake the static character it had gradually acquired.

We can see this internal movement in two different examples.

The first is Dawud al-Taʾi, who died in 165 AH / 781 CE. The second is al-Fudayl ibn ʿIyad, who died in 187 AH / 802 CE.

In Dawud al-Taʾi’s life, we already find some features of “Sufism” that went deeper than traditional asceticism. They appeared both in the way he lived and in his teachings.

The first sign was that al-Taʾi allowed ordinary people to seek him out, receive his blessing through meeting him, and treat his words as guidance for their own lives.

This was something new among the ascetics of his time.

Later it would become a characteristic part of the role of Sufi shaykhs. As we will see, this role carried a social meaning and eventually developed an ideological side of its own.[^V3-120] Its full importance would become clear once the conditions had matured for the major transformation we are waiting for: the transformation of asceticism into theoretical Sufism as an ideological position.

A second sign was al-Taʾi’s habit of spending long periods in contemplation while speaking very little.

When he did speak, his words were brief, firm, controlled, and delivered in the tone of a teacher with authority.

He once told one of his visitors:

“Fast from the world—that is, fast throughout your life—and let death be the meal with which you break your fast. Flee from people as you would flee from a lion, but do not attack them and do not leave their community.”[^V3-121]

These two features of Dawud al-Taʾi’s life point to the earliest beginnings of something recognizably Sufi.

During the early Abbasid period, these beginnings were preparing, little by little, to break through the stagnant continuity of traditional asceticism. Traditional asceticism was no longer able to respond to society’s growing need for ideological forms that could express its social conflicts.

But in al-Taʾi’s time, these Sufi beginnings were still only buds.

Al-Fudayl ibn ʿIyad entered the ascetic life in a much more dramatic way—one that was unusual in the ascetic movement of his time.

Al-Qushayri tells us that al-Fudayl “was in love with a young woman. One night, while he was climbing a wall to reach her, he heard someone reciting the Qurʾanic verse: ‘Has the time not come for those who believe that their hearts should humble themselves at the remembrance of God?’[^V3-122] Al-Fudayl said: ‘My Lord, the time has come.’ And he turned back...”[^V3-123]

The dramatic story of his conversion to asceticism set the tone for the rest of his ascetic life. At the time, this style of asceticism was unusual outside the Khurasanian environment.

For example, when al-Fudayl wanted to express his fear of the Day of Resurrection, he used language unlike that of other ascetics:

“If I were given the choice of living as a dog and never seeing the Day of Resurrection, I would choose to live as a dog, die as a dog, and never see the Day of Resurrection.”[^V3-124]

Was it simply a coincidence that al-Fudayl ibn ʿIyad entered the ascetic life in this dramatic way during the same historical period when several other men from Khurasan did the same?

Among them were Ibrahim ibn Adham of Balkh, who died in 161 AH / 777 CE; ʿAbd Allah ibn al-Mubarak, who died in 181 AH / 797 CE; and Shaqiq al-Balkhi, who died in 194 AH / 809 CE.

We are also told that sixty ascetics from Khurasan set out with ʿAbd Allah ibn al-Mubarak and Ibrahim ibn Adham in the name of “seeking knowledge.”[^V3-125] We do not know exactly how they travelled, where they went, or what kind of “knowledge” they were seeking.

But we do not think this was a matter of coincidence.

The reports we have about Ibn Adham, Ibn al-Mubarak, and Shaqiq al-Balkhi, together with those about al-Fudayl ibn ʿIyad, throw some light on the question.

Al-Qushayri tells us[^V3-126] that:

“Ibrahim ibn Adham ibn Mansur, from the district of Balkh”—and Balkh was a city in the region of Khurasan—“was the son of kings. One day he went out hunting and flushed out a fox or a hare. As he chased it, a voice called to him: ‘Ibrahim! Is this what you were created for? Is this what you were commanded to do?’

“Then the voice called to him again, this time from the front of his saddle: ‘By God, this is not what you were created for, and this is not what you were commanded to do.’

“So he dismounted. He came across one of his father’s shepherds, took the shepherd’s woolen cloak and put it on, and gave him his horse and everything he had with him.

“Then he entered the wilderness. Later he went to Mecca, where he became a companion of Sufyan al-Thawri and al-Fudayl ibn ʿIyad. He then went to Syria, where he died.”[^V3-127]

We are also told that Ibn Adham “ate from the work of his own hands, such as harvesting crops and guarding orchards,”[^V3-128] and that he said:

“Make sure what you eat is good”—meaning lawful and permitted—“and there is no harm if you do not spend the night in prayer or the day fasting.”

Ibn al-Jawzi tells a similar story about Shaqiq al-Balkhi.

Shaqiq had been a wealthy merchant. A Turk once saw him in a temple of idols and pointed out the contradiction between his pursuit of profit and his claim to believe in a Creator who had power over everything. Shaqiq then abandoned trade[^V3-129] and entered the ascetic life.

Ibn al-Jawzi also tells us that ʿAbd Allah ibn al-Mubarak had been one of the wealthy men of Khurasan and a prominent figure in society. He left behind both his wealth and his social standing and went to Kufa, where he lived the life of an ascetic because, in his view, **the ascetics were the true kings**.[^V3-130]

Here, then, we have a group of ascetics who came from the same original environment—Khurasan—and entered the ascetic life in strikingly similar ways.

In each case, there was a dramatic element that had previously been unusual in the ascetic movement, even after the middle of the second Islamic century.

This becomes even more interesting when the dramatic conversion also involves abandoning political power, wealth, trade, and high social standing, followed by a departure from Khurasan for other parts of the Arab state closer to the centers of government and more active intellectual life.

If al-Isfahani is correct in reporting, as we mentioned earlier, that sixty ascetics left Khurasan when Ibn Adham and Ibn al-Mubarak left it, and if we add this to everything we have already seen about these two men and about Shaqiq al-Balkhi, then we are justified in seeing these names and events as signs of a new phenomenon entering the world of traditional asceticism.

They were throwing not one stone into its stagnant lake, but many.

What was this new phenomenon? And how far did its meaning extend?

To answer that, we should return to something already mentioned about Ibn Adham.

He “ate from the work of his own hands,” and he said:

“Make sure what you eat is good, and there is no harm if you do not spend the night in prayer or the day fasting.”

This shows that Ibn Adham had a new understanding of asceticism.

For him, asceticism no longer meant withdrawing from work and depending completely on others for the necessities of life. Nor did it mean praying through every night and fasting through every day.

It meant keeping away from what was not “good”—that is, what was not lawful or permitted.

We find Shaqiq al-Balkhi moving in the same direction.

The sources tell us that he gave up a way of life similar to that of the traditional ascetics around him after hearing ʿAbd al-ʿAziz ibn Abi Rawwad, who died in 159 AH / 775 CE, tell him:

“The important thing is not eating barley or wearing wool and haircloth. The important thing is knowledge: worship God and do not associate anything with Him.”[^V3-131]

We do not believe that Shaqiq suddenly abandoned his earlier ascetic way of life through some “magical” effect simply because he heard someone, however important, say these words.

We give more weight to another report, which tells us that Shaqiq al-Balkhi was one of Ibrahim ibn Adham’s best students.[^V3-132]

In Ibn Adham’s approach, then, we find something new: a reduced emphasis on ritual worship as the defining feature of asceticism.

And in the approach of his student Shaqiq al-Balkhi, we find another new element. Alongside this reduced emphasis on ritual worship, Shaqiq also gives **knowledge** a place within his understanding of asceticism.

These two developments deserve our attention.

They are two elements of what we have called the “new phenomenon” now entering traditional asceticism.

But there were other elements too, and some of them were even more important.

We find the first in Ibn al-Mubarak’s description of the ascetics as “kings.”

We find the second in a saying by Shaqiq al-Balkhi that we mentioned earlier,[^V3-133] in which he declared:

“The shepherds of every age are the scholars and the Sufis.”[^V3-134]

And we find the third in the way the story of Ibn Adham’s conversion was constructed.

According to the story, he abandoned the life of princes and chose the life of poor ascetics because God Himself called him to do so. It is as if God had personally chosen him and appointed him to the role of ascetic.

Ibn Adham himself expressed something close to this idea when he described his change from “prince” to “ascetic”:

“A warning came to me from the Lord of the worlds... By God, from this day onward I will not disobey God, so long as my Lord protects me.”[^V3-135]

Apart from the phrase “a warning from the Lord of the worlds,” this statement contains another serious claim: the claim of **divine protection from sin**—“so long as my Lord protects me.”

In Sunni belief, such protection belongs to the Prophet alone, while in Shiʿi belief it belongs to the Prophet and the Imam.

There is further evidence that this claim was in fact associated with Ibn Adham. Al-Sulami says that al-Khidr taught him “the secret of God’s Greatest Name.”[^V3-136]

To all of this we should add what we have already said about the legendary way in which al-Fudayl ibn ʿIyad was said to have entered the ascetic life.

Taken together, these many clear signs give us historical material from which we can identify the elements of the new phenomenon that began disturbing the stillness of traditional asceticism after the middle of the second Islamic century.

They broke up the appearance of its stagnant “historical continuity” and pushed it a stage closer to the major transformation that awaited it: its transformation into Sufism.

At the heart of all these elements was an attempt by the Khurasanian ascetics, during a later phase in the history of Islamic asceticism, to draw the ascetics of their age out of the complete isolation into which they had fallen during the second Islamic century.

They were trying to move them from a passive role to an active one in society.

More than that, they were trying to raise this role to a position of great importance—above the position of caliphs and kings, and for a time almost equal to that of the Prophet or the Imam.[^V3-137]

I say “an attempt by the Khurasanian ascetics” because this places the “new phenomenon” where it belongs in historical reality.

Once we locate it there, its roots become easier to see.

The historical material we have examined makes it clear that Khurasan was the source of the phenomenon that set the history of asceticism moving again.

It almost pushed asceticism beyond its narrow, closed framework and toward the point where it could enter the growing field of ideological struggle.

Why Khurasan in particular?

There were objective reasons for this. Two of them are especially important.

The first was cultural and historical, connected with the historical character of Persian society.

If we return to the elements of this new Khurasanian asceticism that we identified above, we find that not all of them came from Islamic sources.

Its exaggeration of the role of ascetics and “knowers,” for example, does not fit the usual Islamic view.

Nor do these elements seem to come from Christian sources.[^V3-138]

Instead, they appear to contain traces of doctrines and beliefs that had arisen or spread in Persia during its ancient history or during the period closer to the rise of Islam, especially Gnostic traditions.[^V3-139]

The second factor was political and social.

From the beginning of the Arab-Islamic conquest, Khurasan had repeatedly been a center of intense resistance to the Arab conquerors, in many different forms and at many different levels.[^V3-140]

At times this resistance took the form of armed violence.

At other times it took a religious form: rejecting the conquerors’ religion, Islam, or entering Islam while bringing older Persian beliefs and ideas into it.

At still other times it became political and ideological resistance to the Arab state: supporting religious or intellectual movements opposed to the state’s official doctrine[^V3-141] or official ideology, or taking part in revolts against state authority.[^V3-142]

At first, “national” feeling lay at the heart of much of this resistance.

We can say that such “national” feelings continued to feed revolts, uprisings, and other forms of resistance to Arab rule.

But they did not remain the only factor.

Over time, they became mixed with political and factional factors, as well as social forces rooted in class relations.

We can see this from the fact that some forms of resistance in Khurasan resembled resistance elsewhere in the vast Arab state because they grew from similar social conditions.

At times, resistance in Khurasan even resembled resistance found in regions that were Arab or had large Arab populations, such as Iraq, Syria, and Egypt.

There is also no doubt that the harshness and humiliation often imposed by Umayyad authorities on Persians in general, and on the people of Khurasan in particular, directly sharpened “national” feelings on the one hand and deepened the social suffering of ordinary working people on the other.

Here we should remember an important historical exchange between the Umayyad caliph ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAziz, who ruled from 99 to 101 AH / 717 to 719 CE, and his representative in Khurasan, al-Jarrah ibn ʿAbd Allah al-Hakami, who died in 112 AH.

Al-Jarrah wrote to the caliph about Khurasan:

“Its people can be kept in order only by the sword and the whip.”

ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAziz replied:

“No. You lie. Justice and right are what will set them right. Spread these among them.”[^V3-143]

This exchange presents us with two very different ways of looking at popular uprisings.

The first sees them through arrogance, despotism, and “chauvinist” superiority. It is also idealist and superficial.

The second, represented by ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAziz’s reply, looks more realistically beneath the surface and sees the social roots of the problem.

---

These two factors were among the objective forces that shaped the historical relationship between the native population of Khurasan and the Arab social, political, intellectual, and ideological institutions.

They therefore give us a basis for understanding the new form of asceticism that arose in Khurasan.

It was no accident that all the people who represented this new asceticism came from the same geographical environment, Khurasan.

Nor was it an accident that they came from the same kind of social environment: palaces, wealth, and political prestige.

Nor was it accidental that they spread out and travelled between Kufa, Basra, Mecca, Alexandria, and Syria,[^V3-144] or that the companions of Ibrahim ibn Adham later returned from Syria to Balkh in Khurasan.[^V3-145]

All of this gives us a clear picture of the social dimension[^V3-146] of the Khurasanian ascetic movement that emerged after the middle of the second Islamic century.

Its heavy religious and “spiritual” coloring could not erase this social dimension.

Its roots were too deep and its presence too strong.

It therefore had to find expression in the ideological side of the movement as well, because at that time the movement formed part of the wider process through which Sufism was beginning to establish itself as both a philosophical and an ideological movement.

#### 8 — On the threshold of Sufism

By the end of the second Islamic century, asceticism had reached the threshold of Sufism.

Before the century ended, it had been pushed toward that threshold not only by the Khurasanian ascetic movement we have just discussed, but also by the appearance of ideas that would later become important concepts in Sufism.

These ideas appeared around figures from Basra and Baghdad who belonged to the later ascetic movement of the second century and whose names later became prominent in histories of Sufism.

Two examples are Rabiʿa al-ʿAdawiyya, who died in 185 AH / 801 CE, and Maʿruf al-Karkhi, who died in 200 AH / 815 CE.

When discussing these two figures, however, we must first put aside much of what later Sufi books attributed to them.

Many of these stories were created long after their own time in order to surround them with a legendary aura, much as Sufi writers surrounded their “saints” with stories of miracles.

Once we set those legends aside, an important historical fact remains.

Through these two figures, we see the appearance of two ideas that later became central to theoretical Sufism.

The first is **“divine love,”** most closely associated with Rabiʿa al-ʿAdawiyya.

The second is **“knowledge,”** which Maʿruf al-Karkhi is said to have approached in some of his practices.

It would be too early, however, to claim that either idea already carried the full meaning it would later have in Sufi philosophy.

But the fact that both appeared during this period means that we need to locate them within the development of the Sufi movement.

##### First — “Divine love” and Rabiʿa al-ʿAdawiyya

“Divine love”—the phrase itself belongs to later Sufi terminology[^V3-147]—was attributed to several ascetics from Basra who lived around the time of Rabiʿa al-ʿAdawiyya.

Among them were ʿAmir ibn ʿAbd Qays, Khalid ibn ʿUbayd Allah al-ʿAsri, ʿUtayba al-Ghulam, and Habib al-Farisi.[^V3-148]

Yet Rabiʿa became far more famous for it than any of these men.

Her name became closely tied to the idea, probably because Sufis and historians of Sufism took a special interest in her as a woman.

This may also explain why they surrounded her with the legendary holiness we mentioned above.

They added stories of “spiritual” behavior to her life and put sayings into her mouth that seem far more at home in the later age of fully developed Sufism than in the earlier age of asceticism, before Sufism had reached maturity and undergone its major transformation.

Sufi writers did something similar with al-Hasan al-Basri.

They portrayed him as a Sufi, even as one of the great Sufi masters.

In reality, al-Hasan al-Basri was no more than an ascetic whose life was shaped by the enormous political upheavals of his age.

His asceticism became a way of protecting himself from the heat of those events, and it stayed with him throughout his life.

But his understanding of asceticism remained very simple and very close to the traditional Islamic meaning.

Many parts of Rabiʿa al-ʿAdawiyya’s life remain obscure.

The sources that tell us so many stories about the “divine love” attributed to her do little to clear up these mysteries.

On the one hand, they simply ignore some of the main questions.

On the other, when they do try to answer them, they often rely on supernatural stories and miracles.

How did Rabiʿa live in her early years?

How did she become enslaved?

How did she gain her freedom from the “master” whom Sufi accounts describe as harsh and cruel?

Where did she go after she was freed?

What did she do?

And what circumstances led her into asceticism, complete isolation, and devotion to intimate prayer with the “highest Beloved”?[^V3-149]

The modern researcher Dr. ʿAbd al-Rahman Badawi is the only scholar who drew serious attention to these obscure parts of Rabiʿa’s “story” and identified them precisely.[^V3-150]

But he too found nothing in the sources that could solve the problem.

Still, when we bring together everything we have been able to find about Rabiʿa, a certain picture begins to emerge.

Behind her extreme asceticism, behind her retreat deep into herself in the legendary form so vividly described by the “historians of Sufism”—especially Farid al-ʿAttar[^V3-151]—and behind the “divine passion” they attribute to her, there appears to lie an intense personal tragedy.

As we understand it, this was a double tragedy.[^V3-152]

Crushing poverty and broken love seem to have worked together, driving Rabiʿa inward into the deepest “cave” of herself.

There, “divine love” became an imagined substitute for the material deprivation that had consumed her youth.

When we read some of the sayings attributed to her, we can almost feel the tragedy burning inside her, even at the moments of her most intense “spiritual” ecstasy and closeness to the “highest Beloved.”

Indeed, we should go further.

Those moments themselves may have been nothing more than the times when the tragedy inside her reached its greatest intensity.

Perhaps this emotional storm repeatedly returned throughout the years she spent isolated inside that deep inner “cave.”

Let us read these words carefully:

“It is told of Rabiʿa al-ʿAdawiyya, may God have mercy on her, that after performing the evening prayer she would go up onto the roof, tighten her robe and veil around herself, and say:

‘My God! The stars are shining. Eyes have fallen asleep. Kings have shut their doors. Every lover is alone with the beloved. And here I stand before You...’”[^V3-153]

Every word of this trembling prayer makes us feel that there is a human being inside it burning with pain and thirst for ordinary human love—the love of one human being for another.

What the Sufis call “divine love” in Rabiʿa’s life and prayers may have been an emotional sensitivity made extraordinarily intense by material deprivation or by a severe emotional shock.

Under such pressure, imagination becomes unusually delicate and the power of abstraction unusually strong.

Think of those moments she describes:

“The stars are shining. Eyes have fallen asleep. Kings have shut their doors. Every lover is alone with the beloved...”

These are precisely the moments when all the longings of life awaken most sharply in a person left alone in the grip of loneliness and deprivation.

I am convinced that these very moments lay at the heart of Rabiʿa al-ʿAdawiyya’s human suffering.

What we mean by all this is that Rabiʿa’s position should be understood from this **human** point of view.

The time has come for us to reconsider this historical figure from a new angle and with a new human perspective, rather than through the sectarian Sufi viewpoint and its supernatural “spiritual” interpretation.

The reality of Rabiʿa’s life was an earthly reality.

It grew out of particular personal tragedies within a wider society marked by tragedy of its own.

Later Sufis then placed this earthly reality inside a supernatural and legendary frame, turning it into a Sufi origin story on which they could build the idea of “divine love.”

But they did so in a historical period when the conditions had not yet fully matured for simple ascetic ideas to turn into the theoretical and ideological concepts of Sufism.

It is difficult for a scientific researcher to accept that the theoretical positions and ideas later attributed to Rabiʿa al-ʿAdawiyya actually came from her.

For example, it was said that when she made the pilgrimage to Mecca, she pointed to the Kaʿba and said:

“This is the idol worshipped on earth. God has neither entered it nor is He absent from it.”[^V3-154]

The idea contained in this statement belongs to a much more developed stage of Sufi “heresy.”

It did not appear in the history of Islamic Sufism until more than a century after Rabiʿa’s lifetime.

In theoretical Sufism of the third and fourth Islamic centuries—the ninth and tenth centuries CE—the idea rested on a distinction between **religious law** (*shariʿa*) and **inner truth** (*haqiqa*).

According to this view, the religious law belongs to the outward level and is addressed to ordinary people.

The inner truth, by contrast, lies beneath the outward form and is revealed only to people who have reached the highest “stations” of “knowledge”—that is, the great Sufi masters.

In practice, this could mean that the fully realized Sufi “knower” was no longer bound by the ordinary obligations of religious law.

The Kaʿba, in its physical form, could therefore be called an “idol,” to use the expression attributed to Rabiʿa.

A physical pilgrimage to it would then become a kind of “idolatry” unworthy of the true “knower.”

Instead, the Sufi would make a pilgrimage toward the abstract “truth.”

The pilgrimage would become an inward pilgrimage of the heart, free from physical and sensory form.

But how could this transformation in the meaning of pilgrimage and “truth” belong to the age of Rabiʿa al-ʿAdawiyya?

This is the main reason for doubting that such a Sufi position really came from her.

There are other reasons as well.

Most of the stories and sayings attributed to Rabiʿa have never been established through serious historical investigation.

They are a mixture of legendary material, material attributed elsewhere to other people,[^V3-155] and other sayings whose language, content, ideas, and concepts[^V3-156] do not fit the level of social and intellectual development of Rabiʿa’s own time, the second Islamic century, or eighth century CE.

Finally, we do not even know what level of intellectual education Rabiʿa herself had by the standards of her own age.

This is another obscure part of her life.

How, then, can a researcher confidently accept sophisticated positions attributed to a figure whose biography is surrounded by uncertainty and legend from every side?

Despite all these doubts about Rabiʿa al-ʿAdawiyya, we still stand by the point we made earlier.

If we remove the legendary material that later Sufi history added to her life, and if we remove ideas that clearly do not belong to her period, one historical fact still remains.

In the second half of the second Islamic century, one of the most important later concepts of Sufism—**divine love**—appeared above all through the figure of Rabiʿa.

We do not dispute that.

What we dispute is the attempt to load this concept, in Rabiʿa’s own age and in her own person, with more meaning than that age and that generation could historically have carried.

##### Second — “Knowledge” and Maʿruf al-Karkhi

With Maʿruf al-Karkhi, who died in 200 AH / 815 CE, we once again find a figure surrounded by an aura of holiness and miracle stories.

This makes it difficult to see important parts of his real relationship with his own time or to judge how far traditional asceticism had moved, through him, toward theoretical Sufism.

Because of this uncertainty, one modern Arab researcher[^V3-157] has argued that Maʿruf al-Karkhi does not really belong, in terms of doctrine, to the “first phase” of Sufism, even though historically he lived in that phase.

According to this view, his doctrinal place belongs to the “second phase”—the stage after traditional asceticism.

We agree with this argument on one point only, and that point concerns one of the uncertainties we have just mentioned: Maʿruf al-Karkhi’s connection with the Sufi idea of **knowledge**.

In our view, this question marks the decisive difference between what is called the “first phase” and the “second phase” in the history of Sufism.

Earlier in this study, we explained that during the period of “historical continuity” in traditional asceticism—which remained dominant even after the Khurasanian ascetic movement began to appear—it was the rationalist current, first through the Qadarites and later through the Muʿtazila, that moved forward to meet society’s need for an ideological position.

This rationalist current based knowledge on rational inquiry and logical method.

Traditional Islamic asceticism, by contrast, still relied on the same sources of knowledge accepted by Islam itself.

Before 200 AH, the ascetics had not yet begun discussing a specifically Sufi kind of “knowledge” reached through inner revelation or personal spiritual disclosure.

We find no ascetic of that period presenting this question either as a way of life or as a theory.

The only exception we have already mentioned is the use of the word “knowledge” by ʿAbd al-ʿAziz ibn Abi Rawwad when he told Shaqiq al-Balkhi:

“The important thing is not eating barley or wearing wool and haircloth. The important thing is knowledge: worship God and do not associate anything with Him.”

But the sources that preserve this saying do not explain what kind of “knowledge” he meant.

Did he mean knowledge in the ordinary Islamic sense?

Did he mean rational knowledge based on logical reasoning, as among the Muʿtazila?

Or did he mean the Sufi idea of knowledge gained through direct inner experience?

We simply do not know.

This supports the view that the historical Maʿruf al-Karkhi cannot properly be placed within a fully developed Sufi doctrine of knowledge before 200 AH.

The doctrine of Sufi “knowledge” did not appear until the third Islamic century, when Sufism began to establish itself both theoretically and ideologically.

Its basic starting point was then **knowledge in the specifically Sufi sense**, which is why the Sufis described themselves as people of “the science of experiential knowledge.”[^V3-158]

For all these reasons, there is room to doubt the claim that Maʿruf al-Karkhi “spoke about knowledge and was one of the first to address this problem,”[^V3-159] or that he had attained “knowledge bestowed directly by God.”[^V3-160]

We doubt these claims not only because of our own scientific position on the idea of divinely bestowed knowledge, but also because of the historical evidence itself.

The surviving texts do not really support what these researchers claim.

They do not show clearly that Maʿruf discussed “knowledge” in the later Sufi sense or that he possessed divinely bestowed knowledge.

The evidence usually comes down to two texts.

The first is a report in *Qut al-Qulub* saying that Maʿruf al-Karkhi acted as an authority when Ahmad ibn Hanbal and Yahya ibn Maʿin disagreed over certain questions of learning, even though Maʿruf “did not possess the knowledge they possessed.”[^V3-161]

The second is a prayer attributed to al-Karkhi in *Hilyat al-Awliyaʾ*:

“Souls that came forward to the call of duty, longed for the precious hidden treasure, and were spared the weight of ordeal.”[^V3-162]

But are these two texts really enough to show that Maʿruf had attained “knowledge bestowed directly by God”?

Is the fact that he settled disagreements between Ibn Hanbal and Ibn Maʿin, despite knowing less than they did, enough to prove that he possessed special divine knowledge or Sufi knowledge gained through inner experience?

To establish that, one necessary condition would have to be met.

We would need evidence that Ibn Hanbal and Ibn Maʿin themselves accepted this idea of divinely bestowed knowledge and regarded it as a valid source of authority.

We have no basis for saying that they did.

And is it enough that Maʿruf al-Karkhi prayed for “souls that longed for the precious hidden treasure” to conclude that this “hidden treasure” meant knowledge in the full technical Sufi sense?

We doubt that such a conclusion is justified.

Those souls might have reached the “hidden treasure” through rational thought, or through the ordinary textual evidence of Islam, rather than through specifically Sufi inner experience.

Nor is the argument strengthened by the claim that al-Karkhi “grew up in a Shiʿi environment and had contact with the eighth Shiʿi Imam.”[^V3-163]

The story of this contact is itself surrounded by doubt and uncertainty, as the same modern researcher who relies on it admits.[^V3-164]

One final point remains, and it concerns our method.

Earlier we mentioned the claim that Maʿruf al-Karkhi should be counted “historically among the figures of the first phase of Sufism, but in terms of doctrine ... should be placed in the second phase.”[^V3-165]

From a methodological point of view, we cannot accept this separation between two sides of the same human personality: the “historical” side and the “doctrinal” side.

If Maʿruf al-Karkhi really belongs historically to the first phase of Sufism—and the main sources do place him there—how can he realistically belong, in terms of doctrine, to a different phase?

A thinker, or the founder of any doctrine, cannot simply step outside the historical limits of his own age unless something within that age already provides a basis for doing so.

As we have just explained, we do not find a clear basis for the specifically Sufi doctrine of “knowledge” before 200 AH, during Maʿruf al-Karkhi’s lifetime.

This means that neither the development of Arab-Islamic society nor the development of thought within that society had yet matured enough to produce the Sufi concept of knowledge.

At that point, the concept still lacked both the social and ideological conditions and the intellectual conditions needed for its birth.

If the logic of this methodological argument is sound—and we are confident that it is—then it leads us to doubt all claims that Maʿruf al-Karkhi practiced Sufism on the basis of a fully developed Sufi concept of knowledge.

This is quite apart from the supernatural and legendary stories that Sufi books tell about him, stories that have continued into modern times to surround the name “Maʿruf al-Karkhi,” and even his grave in Baghdad, with tales of miracles.

But after saying all this, we do not want to push our doubt further than scientific method allows.

Despite all the uncertainty we have raised about the “doctrinal” picture of Maʿruf al-Karkhi presented in Sufi books and in the writings of later researchers, including modern ones, we cannot doubt that history placed this second-century ascetic on the boundary between traditional Islamic asceticism and Sufism.

And not only historically.

It placed him there intellectually and ideologically as well.

This does not contradict what we said a few lines ago.

What we mean is that, from a strictly historical point of view, there are several signs connected with Maʿruf that we have no reason simply to dismiss.

First, al-Qushayri describes him as one of the “great shaykhs.”[^V3-166]

Second, he was a student of Dawud al-Taʾi.[^V3-167] We have already seen the part al-Taʾi played in the development of asceticism before 200 AH.

Third, al-Karkhi was the teacher of al-Sari al-Saqati, who died in 253 AH / 867 CE.[^V3-168]

Al-Sari played an important role in the rise of the Sufi movement during the first half of the third Islamic century, the ninth century CE.

He became famous as “the first person in Baghdad to speak about the ultimate truths and the unity of God.”[^V3-169]

He was also one of those who entered the ascetic life through the kind of “dramatic” conversion we saw earlier among the Khurasanian ascetics.

We are told that al-Sari al-Saqati “was a merchant. He gave up trade, left the market, stayed at home in worship, and withdrew from people.”[^V3-170]

There is a fourth sign.

Al-Qushayri reports that Maʿruf al-Karkhi once heard a water seller call out:

“May God have mercy on whoever drinks.”

Maʿruf was fasting, but he stepped forward and drank.

Someone asked him:

“Were you not fasting?”

He replied:

“Yes, but I hoped for his prayer.”[^V3-171]

If this report is true, it has a particular meaning.

It may represent one of the earliest signs of the special Sufi interpretations of religious law and of the distinction between the outward and inward meanings of religion.

Later, such interpretations could allow Sufis to set aside some outward obligations of religious law on the grounds that they followed the “inner” truth instead.

The fifth sign is just as important.

Among the definitions of Sufism collected by al-Qushayri, he attributes this definition to Maʿruf al-Karkhi:

“Sufism is to grasp the truths and give up hope of what people possess.”[^V3-172]

Even though this definition is phrased in a way that fits later Sufism, we cannot completely ignore the Sufi direction—or at least the Sufi flavor—of the word “truths.”

These, then, are some of the signs connected with Maʿruf al-Karkhi.

Taken together, they give us a historical, intellectual, and ideological marker.

Through them, we can see how history placed this major ascetic—one of the most important ascetic figures of the second half of the second Islamic century—on the boundary between two stages in the history of Sufism:

the stage of asceticism, and the stage of the “science of experiential knowledge,” or philosophical Sufism.

### B — Theoretical Sufism: An Ideological Position

#### 1 — A decisive age

We now move from the first stage of Sufism to the second.

At the end of the previous section, we left Sufism still in the form of asceticism, but trying, during the final decades of the second Islamic century, to move beyond itself.

It was trying to leave behind a purely practical way of life and enter a stage in which practice and theory interacted with each other.

But, as our earlier discussion showed, the conditions for this change had not yet fully matured.

We will now follow this process into the stage when it did mature—the stage when all the conditions began coming together for a complete qualitative transformation of Sufism.

This transformation would affect two basic sides of Sufism.

The first was its **intellectual side**.

Sufism was one form of social consciousness produced under particular historical conditions.

Its transformation at the intellectual level meant that it would develop its own theoretical foundations, its own concepts, and its own internal logic, with a degree of relative independence.

In this way, Sufism could stand alongside philosophy on the same intellectual level.

Philosophy, for its part, also had its own theoretical foundations, concepts, and relatively independent inner development.

The two would exist alongside each other while remaining in constant and active interaction.

The second was Sufism’s **social side**.

Sufism was also a particular way in which social reality was reflected in human consciousness.

Its transformation on this level meant that it would begin to express an **ideological position**, whereas in the earliest period of asceticism it had expressed only a political position.

With this second transformation, Sufism became an active part of the wider process then under way: the rebuilding of society’s ideology in opposition to the ideology of the theocratic state.

In this second stage, then, Sufism underwent a transformation on both levels, intellectual and social.

It moved from the simple to the complex.

And in doing so, it reached a level that matched the historical development of the first Abbasid age.

Historians usually define this period as the years from 132 to 232 AH / 749 to 846 CE.

It ended with the death of the Abbasid caliph al-Wathiq and the beginning of the reign of al-Mutawakkil.

In our view, historians were not wrong to draw the boundary there, although we are fully aware that periods in the history of human societies cannot really be measured with such mathematical precision.

The accession of al-Mutawakkil was not literally the moment when the first Abbasid age began to end, nor was it the final end of that age.

The ending of one period and the beginning of another overlapped.

Still, after all the necessary qualifications, the century historians call the “first Abbasid age” appears to us almost like a complete historical era extending over several centuries rather than just one.

That is true if we judge historical periods by the depth and lasting effect of what they produced, rather than simply by the number of years they occupied.

When the achievements of an age put down deep roots, that age can remain present in history for centuries.

This standard certainly applies to the first Abbasid age.

The Abbasid state arose from a revolution in which several different forces took part.

There were Alid elements, meaning supporters of the line of ʿAli ibn Abi Talib.

There were Persian political forces.
