And there were large numbers of ordinary people, both Arab and non-Arab.

The revolution benefited, first, from the long-standing conflict between the Umayyads and the Alids.

Second, it benefited from the “national” resentment felt by non-Arab peoples, especially the Persians, toward the Umayyads.

The Umayyads had made Arab tribal partisanship a basic principle of state policy across its different institutions and branches, and applied it to their “subjects” without distinction between Muslims and non-Muslims.

Third, the revolution benefited from the social resentment of broad sections of the population toward Umayyad rule.

That resentment grew from the harshness of the government and from the freedom given to provincial governors to extract ever more material wealth from the population in order to satisfy their own desire for riches.

Two features therefore stood out in the Abbasid state that emerged from this revolution.

First, it abandoned the Umayyad principle of Arab tribal partisanship as the basis of government policy toward the state’s “subjects.”

Second, the Abbasids used the founder of their dynasty’s connection with the Arab Qurayshi Prophet as a basis for strengthening their own “religious” authority.

They gave their political rule a fully theocratic character.

The caliph was presented as a “spiritual” ruler whose authority all his “subjects” were obliged to obey because he represented the power of religious law as “God’s deputy on earth.”[^V3-173]

These two main features left their mark on the first Abbasid age.

Their effects were clearly visible on two levels: the intellectual and the ideological.

##### First — On the intellectual level

Here our discussion will focus on one positive intellectual effect of removing tribal partisanship from the political policy of the Arab state.

We do not mean, of course, that this was the only factor behind the developments we are about to discuss.

We mean only that removing this principle had a real practical effect.

It helped set in motion other forces that were already coming together at the time.

The change in attitude toward non-Arab peoples opened the way for intellectual currents, religious doctrines, and systems of belief from older Eastern civilizations and from cultures contemporary with Islam—especially Persian ones—to become more active within Abbasid Arab society.

But this change did not come from any principled decision by the early Abbasid caliphs.

It followed logically from the important political and military role Persian forces had played in the Abbasid victory over the Umayyads.[^V3-174]

Persian political and administrative influence then gained a broad and deep role in the machinery of the new state, both directly and indirectly.[^V3-175]

At the same time, the struggle between extreme Arab and Persian partisanship reached the point of open confrontation in many different forms.

What matters for us here is, first, that this change really did take place.

Second, we need to see its intellectual side.

The confrontation between the two main forms of ethnic partisanship under the Abbasid state was accompanied—or followed—by a similar confrontation between two cultures: Arab and Persian.

On one side stood the culture that had grown and developed with Islam and whose basic structure had been formed by Islamic teachings in both belief and religious law.

On the other stood Persian culture, with its ancient heritage of beliefs, doctrines, philosophies, literature, and the accumulated influence of other old Eastern civilizations, including India, China, ancient Assyria and Babylonia, as well as Greek and Roman culture.

There is, however, one important fact we must keep in mind in this discussion: the encounter between Arab and Persian culture in this period was fundamentally different from the clash between Arab and Persian group loyalties. The latter involved a certain degree of open conflict. The former was a form of “coexistence” with almost no limits.[^V3-176]

This difference was not accidental. Intellectual coexistence reflected, and grew out of, a much older history of interaction between the Arabs and the peoples around them, both before and after Islam, and especially after the conquests. This interaction was a real historical process that continued even while rival group loyalties were in conflict.

The coexistence of the two cultures did not, of course, prevent conflict between the intellectual and religious currents within them. But this conflict itself became a positive part of the wider process of interaction. It brought together, on one level, the distinctive qualities of the two cultures; on another, their different beliefs, intellectual movements, and tendencies; and on a third, the changing position of the two languages, Arabic and Persian.

This broad cultural coexistence, and the far-reaching interaction that grew out of it, deepened in stages during the “First Abbasid Period.”

Under al-Manṣūr (136–158 AH / 753–774 CE), after he founded the new capital of Baghdad in 762, the first foundations were laid. The earliest translations from Persian into Arabic began to appear in literature, religion, and the sciences, especially medicine, geometry, and astronomy.[^V3-177]

Then a historic relationship developed between Baghdad, the capital of Arab culture, and the Sasanian school of Gundishapur.[^V3-178] This became the first major link not only between Arab thought and Persian culture, but also between Arab thought and Greek culture itself, since Gundishapur already had deep ties to Greek science and philosophy.[^V3-179]

If we move forward from the age of al-Manṣūr to that of al-Maʾmūn (198–218 AH / 813–833 CE), we find the “national” conflict, now intertwined with social conflict, growing more intense than before. Yet at the same time, intellectual coexistence was becoming deeper and wider.

It became deeper because the relationship between Arab-Islamic thought and outside cultures was beginning to change into one of internal fusion and absorption.

It became wider because this process now reached new areas of human knowledge: philosophy, Sufism, the natural sciences, mathematics, and others. It also reached more deeply into the cultures of the ancient Greeks, both Athenian and Alexandrian, as well as the cultures of the Persians, Indians, Syriac Christians, and Sabians.

The appearance of the House of Wisdom in Baghdad under al-Maʾmūn was the organized, practical expression of this intellectual growth in both depth and range. This is exactly what it means to say that the House of Wisdom brought together “a library, an academy, and a translation bureau,”[^V3-180] and that it “was filled with Greek books brought from Asia Minor and Constantinople.”[^V3-181]

---

##### Second — On the Ideological Level

We should not draw an artificial line between intellectual life and ideology. The cultural interactions we have just discussed also had a major ideological effect, because thought and ideology are closely connected.

But another factor was almost decisive in the process that began during the First Abbasid Period of rebuilding the ideology of society on a new basis opposed to the ideology of the state.

That factor came from another defining feature of the Abbasid state: its “theocratic” character.

The Abbasid caliphs were determined to make use of their family connection to the Prophet. They presented themselves as rulers who represented prophecy and the religious law. Their state, they claimed, was simply a continuation of the Prophet’s state. They were the “imams” of the Muslims and the “caliphs of God on His earth.”

That was one side of the matter. But it carried its opposite within it.

These “caliphs of God on His earth” were, first and foremost, a ruling group standing at the head of the class that held economic and political power. The state’s “theocratic” character was simply the historical and ideological form through which that domination appeared.

This forced the caliphs to live their public and private lives through two contradictory identities.

The identity shown in public was that of pious spiritual “imams,” guardians of God’s religion and law.

The hidden identity was that of wealthy kings, driven by their social position and by the conditions around them to enjoy the material wealth placed at their disposal—wealth collected from the labor of others.

To reconcile their spiritual image with their actual social position, they created a barrier between public and private life.

One form this barrier took was the existence of two entourages. On the outside stood jurists and religious figures who benefited from their closeness to power. On the inside were companions, slave women, and jesters. Alongside both stood a political circle tied to the rulers by class interest and sharing with them these two contradictory ways of life.

But no barrier could hide the extraordinary luxury behind it from the wider population, much of which was suffering from poverty and even famine.

The masses themselves exposed the contradiction between the religious and political image of the ruling group and the way that group actually lived. They did so because this contradiction directly affected their own lives and material interests.

The luxury of the rulers, and their enormous spending on private pleasures, placed an ever-growing burden on the population through heavy and continually rising taxes.

The result was not only to exhaust the working population and weaken the development of its productive strength. It also began to obstruct the development of production itself, especially agriculture.[^V3-182]

The effects of this situation became clearly visible after the reign of al-Maʾmūn and grew still more serious from the reign of al-Mutawakkil (232–247 AH / 846–861 CE) onward.

The difference between these two periods in Abbasid history can be explained by the fact that, before al-Mutawakkil, the state covered the oppressive social content of its “theocratic” system with two kinds of protection: one political and one economic.

The political protection came from a highly centralized system of administration. Power was kept firmly in the hands of the central political and administrative institutions connected to the man at the top of the hierarchy, the caliph.

This limited the ability of provincial officials to behave like completely independent feudal lords, serving their own interests at the expense of both the state and the people living in the provinces.

Under al-Mutawakkil, however, this centralization gave way to something we might call “decentralized feudalism.” Provincial officials gained a great deal of independence. This allowed them to rise to the highest ranks of the landowning class.

They bribed powerful figures at the center to keep their offices and their landed grants. The cost of those bribes was then passed on to peasants and agricultural laborers through heavy and varied taxes, collected with great harshness.[^V3-183]

The economic protection came from efforts to improve agriculture and its productive capacity, especially under al-Manṣūr and al-Maʾmūn.

This took two main forms. First came the construction of irrigation systems, including networks of canals, along with the draining of marshes. Second came reductions in taxes on peasants.[^V3-184]

Under al-Mutawakkil and the caliphs who followed him, this concern gradually faded. The forces pushing agriculture backward grew stronger, and the productive capacity of the countryside weakened.[^V3-185]

From this point on, the oppressive reality of the social and political system beneath the “theocratic” state became increasingly clear.

The masses ruled by this system began, through a spontaneous revolutionary impulse, to find different ways of increasing their resistance to it. Some forms of resistance were direct. Some were violent. Some took the form of passive disruption. Others appeared on the intellectual and ideological level.

All of this showed that the Abbasid caliphs’ attempt to strengthen the “theocratic” character of their state did not succeed in securing the ideology of political power.

The opposite happened.

As we have seen, this theocratic character carried its own contradiction within it. It became an almost decisive factor in setting in motion the forces that were pushing society toward a new ideology opposed to the ideology of political authority—that is, opposed to the official ideology of the state.

There is no need to repeat here our earlier discussion of the economic and social forces behind this process, which was of great importance in the history of Arab ideology.[^V3-186]

The purpose of that earlier discussion was to show why the ideology of Abbasid rule began to shake and crack during the third century AH, the ninth century CE, as the social system beneath it also began to shake and crack.

#### 2 — What Was the Ideology of the Caliphate State Built On?

This brings us to the next question: what were the main foundations of the ideology of the caliphate state?

From the time the Islamic caliphate took shape as a “state” in the old legal sense of the word,[^V3-187] it used its Islamic form to present itself as the representative of Islam, a divine religion revealed by God.

Once the state claimed to represent Islam and its law, this shaped the relationship between the state and Islamic society.

But in reality, the relationship came first. The claim to represent Islam came afterward.

In other words, the claim was a reflection of the real relationship between the state and society.

The state presented itself as the representative of Islamic law so that it could use that law as the “theoretical basis” for an absolute monarchical system of rule. This system defined the kind of relationship the state wanted with society.

That relationship found a religious image in the idea of the one and only God, the God of whom it is said that “there is nothing like Him.”[^V3-188]

This picture of God—His absolute oneness and His complete freedom from any likeness or comparison—creates an image of total separation between God and the human being.

That image, in turn, mirrors the separation between the “caliph of God on His earth” and everyone else living on that earth.

And by “everyone else,” we mean everyone outside the ruling group as a whole, not simply everyone outside the individual person of the caliph.

The real relationship between the caliph and society was therefore the basis of his claim to represent Islam, not the result of that claim.

Yet the caliph then used this representative role to impose absolute power on society in the name of the “divine will.”

He ruled people, in other words, through a kind of necessity or determinism supposedly derived from God’s unavoidable decree and destiny.

The direct result was to erase the people’s own will in their relationship with the state.

They had no choice in appointing the caliph or removing him. Nor did they have any choice in any other part of his authority, whether legislative, executive, or judicial.

All these powers were said to be fixed by a religious law that came from one divine source, and that law was “represented” by the caliph.

This was one foundation of the ideology of the caliphate state.

There was another.

Because the state presented itself as the representative of a religion based on “divine revelation,” it also claimed the right to exercise authority over people’s ideas: over how they gained knowledge, over the sources they used to gain it, and even over the kind of knowledge they were allowed to seek.

This part of official ideology, concerned with knowledge, was not separate from the first part. It followed directly from it.

A state that claimed to “represent” divine revelation as the source of religious law had to protect that position by limiting legitimate knowledge to what came through revelation.

Religious law—or the social system defined by that law—was itself presented as something given by revelation.

The state therefore had to limit the source of knowledge to that same divine source: revelation.

The necessary result was that anyone who crossed the boundaries of that knowledge, or looked beyond that source, could be accused of crossing the limits set by an authority that claimed to rule by God’s will.

In other words, such a person could be treated as someone rebelling against the will of God itself.

These were the basic foundations of the ideology of the caliphate state.

If the state wanted to preserve its absolute power, keep it in place, and make it appear permanent, it had to protect these foundations with every means available to it.

This is why the thinkers who tried to create a new ideology for their medieval society played such an important role, both politically and intellectually.

They were opposing the very foundations on which the ideology of the ruling group had been built.

Their attempts appeared at a point when the development of society had created the real conditions needed for this kind of ideological and political change.

Naturally, such attempts could not simply appear because a group of thinkers personally wanted them to. They could not impose this change through sheer will before the conditions for it existed.

We should add one final point.

The growth of conditions favorable to this kind of change was not separate from the intellectual development of society itself. Quite the opposite.

The conditions for ideological change could not mature—and can never mature—unless intellectual life has also reached a level where people can give the new ideology a form suited to those conditions.

The ideological process is therefore directly an intellectual process, and indirectly a social one.

We have already seen how cultural interaction raised Arab thought to a new level. In its relationship with other human cultures, simple contact developed into internal fusion and absorption.

Arab thought moved from a relatively simple condition to one that was richer, more complex, and more developed.

Historically, this made it capable of taking on its new ideological task.

#### 3 — Another Introduction to Sufism

We have laid out these historical and analytical foundations so that the path toward the main purpose of our study of “Islamic Sufism” will be clear.

We are not studying this part of Arab thought simply for historical reasons—that is, merely to study the history of Sufism for its own sake.

Nor are we studying it simply for philosophical reasons, as though our aim were only to examine the philosophical ideas found within Sufism.

Our main aim is to place Sufism within the process through which Arab-Islamic society was building a new ideology under the historical conditions in which the need for such a process was beginning to mature.

But even this aim is not purely historical.

We do not simply want to find the place Sufism occupied in that process in the past.

To stop there would ultimately lead us back to a “metaphysical” view of the past, of cultural heritage, and of the idea of “revival”—the revival of the past and its heritage.

This way of thinking separates the past from the present and treats the past as something fixed and motionless, outside the law of change.

For people who think this way, “reviving” the past becomes the revival of something “dead.”

Our view of the past and of the heritage it carries is exactly the opposite.

For us, the past is living and moving. It is always changing.

It is like a spring carried forward by the movement of history toward the present, bringing all its streams with it, until the present itself becomes another stream flowing toward the future.

On this basis, our main purpose in studying Sufism is also to understand its place in the process taking place in our own present: the effort to rebuild the ideology of contemporary Arab society.

---

#### 4 — Sufism as Philosophy

The historical conditions that, as we have said, were maturing during the third century AH and making ideological change possible were also preparing Arab philosophy to change its position.

Until then, philosophy had remained subordinate to Muʿtazilite *kalām*. It was now beginning to establish an independent place of its own.

As we explained in an earlier chapter,[^V3-189] these historical conditions were marked by increasingly visible forms of social conflict. Beneath them lay real social forces that often expressed themselves spontaneously.

Throughout human history, social conflict has always produced not only movements with a revolutionary character, whether spontaneous or consciously organized, but also particular forms of social consciousness. Ideology is one of these forms.

Philosophy itself is ideological in character, just as it is also one of the highest forms of social consciousness.

It was therefore natural that these historical conditions would affect Arab philosophy and push it out of its subordinate position under Muʿtazilite *kalām*, allowing it to take an independent place among the intellectual movements of the period.

The real meaning of this change was that philosophy was becoming capable of playing a role in the ideological transformation for which the historical conditions were now ready.

Philosophy had become the most mature form of social consciousness available for this task. It could give theoretical expression to a historical movement that was pushing Arab-Islamic society toward a new ideology.

Arab philosophy could not have carried out such an important task if it had remained inside the framework of Muʿtazilite *kalām*.

By the third century AH, the ninth century CE, *kalām* had become an obstacle to the growth and development of philosophy, even though the conditions created by social and ideological conflict were pushing philosophy forward.

The basic structure of *kalām* was still built around a theological philosophy that remained within the general framework of the official ideology—an ideology already showing signs of instability and fracture.[^V3-190]

We have already seen that when Arab philosophy began moving toward independence from *kalām*, its first independent forms appeared in two different ways.

Both belonged to the broader world of philosophy, but in their basic theoretical direction they were different to the point of opposition.

The difference appears most clearly in how they understood knowledge.

The two forms were:

1. **Philosophy in the narrower sense:** a discipline that gained knowledge through rational inquiry and used formal logic as its method. Abū Yūsuf Yaʿqūb ibn Isḥāq al-Kindī was the first major representative of this form.

2. **Theoretical Sufism:** a discipline that sought knowledge through inner unveiling and used a practice of bodily and psychological self-denial—the same basic way of life followed by the ascetics of the period before 200 AH.

Sufism, then, formed part of the independent Arab philosophical movement.

In its theoretical form, it worked with categories and concepts—that is, with abstractions—just as philosophy in the narrower sense did, even though the categories and concepts of Sufism were different from those of rationalist philosophy.[^V3-191]

We now need to identify the theoretical foundations of Sufi philosophy: the main categories and concepts that place it within the philosophical movement.

This will give us the basis we need to understand the role Sufism played in the ideological change that is the main concern of this study.

We are stating this goal now so that we do not lose ourselves in the endless mazes and impossible problems into which the Sufis poured so much effort without result.

From the beginning, then, we must keep our task clearly in mind.

We want, as far as our limited abilities allow, to discover what social reality lay behind the great wave of Sufism that first drove it into these mazes and impossibilities, and later carried it into still deeper forms of the unseen, “alienation,” and loss.

Following this method, we will limit our discussion of the theoretical foundations of Sufi philosophy to the parts that matter most for the purpose we have set.

The main questions are these: the theory of knowledge; outer and inner meaning; the position toward religious law; illumination; and the unity of existence.

##### First — The Theory of Knowledge

The first point to make is that the problem of knowledge forms a common thread running through the different expressions of Arab-Islamic thought: *kalām*, rational philosophy, Sufism, jurisprudence, and the principles of jurisprudence.

Each of these fields also has features of its own, but they share a common framework.

First, the central object of knowledge is God.

Second, the center of this entire question is the oneness of God[^V3-192]—the idea of *tawḥīd*.

Third, the true reality of God, His essence, cannot be grasped by the human mind. What human beings can know is that He exists, that He is one, and that His oneness places Him completely beyond anything similar or comparable to Him.

These three elements form the theological ground from which the problem of knowledge develops throughout Islamic thought, which has stood at the foundation of Arab thought since the rise of Islam.

This theological ground was itself the intellectual expression of a real social ground: a society whose social order was represented by Islamic law.

“Islamic Sufism,” like the other forms of Islamic thought, remained connected to this same theological foundation because they all grew from the same social reality.

So when Sufism first began to develop features that distinguished it from other forms of thought, it still began from this shared foundation. It did not step outside the general framework.

The Sufi problem of knowledge continued to rest on the same three elements that shaped the Islamic idea of knowledge.

What made Sufism distinctive was not that it rejected these elements, but that it developed its own way of “interpreting” them.

As we will see, however, that method of interpretation eventually led to something that was, in substance, a kind of rejection.

The fact that Sufi ideas about knowledge grew from Islamic theological roots gives us a critical position toward theories that treat foreign intellectual or religious influences as the sole foundation of Sufi ideas about knowledge in Islam.[^V3-193]

But this criticism does not mean that we believe Sufi thought was isolated from the wider heritage of human thought.

Quite the opposite.

Our method requires us to reject the idea that such isolation existed, or even that it could have existed.

What we are saying is that Sufism absorbed material from the wider human intellectual heritage while remaining connected to its Islamic theological roots.

This outside material was not simply added from the outside. It was absorbed and transformed from within during a historical period when Arab culture was interacting broadly with other cultures.

The translation movement, which reached the height of its activity during the third and fourth centuries AH, was one of the great historical events that deepened the connection between Arab culture and the accumulated achievements of earlier human knowledge.

But the Sufi “theory of knowledge” in Arab-Islamic thought did not grow only from these theological roots.

As we said a moment ago, those theological roots themselves grew out of a real social setting.

To make this clear, we now need to turn directly to the Sufi “theory of knowledge” itself.

What does this “theory” say?

And what is its relationship to the social reality we have been discussing?

To begin answering the first question, we will return to the original sources of Sufi thought.

The first figure we should mention is Dhū al-Nūn al-Miṣrī (d. 245 AH / 859 CE).

He was one of the earliest figures to express a philosophical form of Sufi thought in the third century AH, and one of the first to define the Sufi idea of “knowledge.”[^V3-194]

We find a clear picture of this idea in a passage where he distinguishes between ordinary religious knowledge, rational knowledge based on proof, and Sufi knowledge.

He says:[^V3-195]

> “Knowledge has three forms. The first is knowledge of God’s oneness, which belongs to ordinary sincere believers. The second is knowledge through proof and explanation, which belongs to sincere wise men, eloquent speakers, and scholars. The third is knowledge of the attributes of divine oneness, which belongs to those sincere people who are specially close to God. They behold God with their hearts, until the Truth reveals to them what He has revealed to no one else in all the worlds.”

Among the earliest philosophical Sufis in Islam, this picture places Sufi knowledge within the broader world of idealist theories of knowledge and within the Islamic theological tradition.

But it also gives Sufi knowledge a feature of its own.

Its only instrument is the heart.

It therefore removes both the senses and reason as ways of reaching this kind of knowledge.

We can also see that the “knowers”—those who “behold God with their hearts”—are raised to the rank of God’s *awliyāʾ*, His specially favored friends or saints.

In Sufi thought, the idea of *walāya*, this special closeness to God, is tied to the idea of *karāmāt*: extraordinary events said to occur through the *awliyāʾ*.

These events break or go beyond the laws of nature because of the special favor these people are believed to enjoy with God.

In this sense, *karāmāt* are effectively the equivalent of the “miracles” that religious belief says appear through prophets.

The Sufis try to draw a distinction between *karāmāt* and prophetic miracles[^V3-196] so that they can protect themselves from accusations of “heresy.”

But the distinction is not convincing.

Nor does it hide the fact that they give the rank of *walāya* a position that may rise above prophecy itself, as well as above the Shiʿi idea of the Imamate.

In Sufi thought, *walāya* means that the “knower” who possesses it has a direct relationship with God, without an intermediary.

This means that the relationship goes beyond the mediation of the Prophet.

And if we take seriously the general wording “all the worlds,” which includes prophets as well, it becomes quite clear that the Sufis are claiming that this special knowledge given to God’s *awliyāʾ* is something that, in their view, even prophets do not receive.

Then comes another major Sufi who lived around the same time as Dhū al-Nūn al-Miṣrī.

The author of *al-Lumaʿ* describes him as “famous for asceticism, worship, learning, and knowledge.”[^V3-197]

This was Abū Yazīd al-Bisṭāmī (d. 261 AH / 874 CE).

Abū Yazīd states the idea with complete openness:

> “By God, my banner is greater than the banner of Muhammad. My banner is made of light. Beneath it are the *jānn*, the jinn, humankind, and all the prophets.”[^V3-198]

It does not make this statement any less significant to describe it as one of “Abū Yazīd’s ecstatic utterances.”

Such ecstatic utterances were accepted as part of Sufi literature. The Sufis did not deny them, even when they tried to reinterpret them.[^V3-199]

We can draw another conclusion from Dhū al-Nūn al-Miṣrī’s account.

Sufi knowledge, in his view, is limited to knowledge of the “attributes of divine oneness”—that is, the attributes of God—not the ultimate reality of God Himself.

We find this conclusion confirmed by Aḥmad ibn ʿAṭāʾ (d. 369 AH / 979 CE), who helped give philosophical form to Sufi theory during the fourth century AH.

He says:

> “There are two kinds of knowledge: knowledge of the Real, and knowledge of Reality. Knowledge of the Real is knowledge of His oneness through the names and attributes He has shown to creation. As for knowledge of Reality, there is no way to reach it, because His absolute self-sufficiency and true lordship make this impossible.”[^V3-200]

Aḥmad ibn ʿAṭāʾ then supports the impossibility of knowing this ultimate reality by quoting the Qurʾānic verse:

> “They do not encompass Him in knowledge.”[^V3-201]

If we return to Dhū al-Nūn al-Miṣrī, we find that the passage quoted above does not tell us what practical method or Sufi discipline leads to knowledge of the “attributes of divine oneness.”

Other statements attributed to him help complete the picture.

Al-Qushayrī reports him as saying:

> “Wisdom does not live in a stomach filled with food.”[^V3-202]

He is also said to have taught that the more the Sufi knows his Lord, the more he denies his own self.[^V3-203]

If we put these two statements together, the practical path to “knowledge” becomes clear.

It requires reducing the needs of the body and suppressing the desires of the self.

This is what the Sufis call *mujāhada*: striving against the self.

Another Sufi from the third century AH, Abū Saʿīd Aḥmad ibn ʿĪsā al-Kharrāz (d. 286 AH / 899 CE), expressed the complete Sufi theory of knowledge—theory and practice together.

He belonged to the generation influenced by Dhū al-Nūn and had heard him directly.[^V3-204]

Al-Kharrāz said:

> “Knowledge comes from two directions: from the fountain of generosity, and from the exertion of effort.”[^V3-205]

In this short phrase, with its unmistakably Sufi style, he brings together both the source of “knowledge” and the practice needed to reach it.

The “fountain of generosity” points to a development in Sufi ideas about knowledge after Dhū al-Nūn.

The general idea of “beholding” God begins to take on a more specific meaning: divine inspiration flowing into the hearts of the “knowers” from the fountain of divine generosity.

We will later see this idea of “inspiration” develop, in al-Suhrawardī, into “divine illumination”—the doctrine of the Illuminationists.

We will return to this doctrine later.[^V3-206]

The “exertion of effort,” meanwhile, means the *mujāhada* of the self: training oneself to endure the severe Sufi experience, including extreme austerity and harsh forms of “spiritual” discipline, in the hope of receiving that divine inspiration.

##### Second — Outer and Inner Meaning (Interpretation)

The distinction between outer and inner meaning follows directly from the Sufi theory of “knowledge.”

Once the Sufis defined knowledge as direct contact with God without an intermediary, and once they claimed this knowledge for themselves on the grounds that they were God’s special *awliyāʾ*,[^V3-207] to whom God “reveals what He has revealed to no one else in all the worlds,”[^V3-208] the logic of their position led naturally to another claim.

They could now argue that the “understanding” flowing directly into them from the divine “fountain of generosity” gave their knowledge through inner experience or “taste”[^V3-209] a unique ability to “see” the “attributes of divine oneness”—the meaning of *tawḥīd*—in a way unavailable to rational philosophers, theologians, jurists, or scholars of legal principles.

Yet the Sufis were still bound by a reality they could not escape.

They were Muslims.

Islam was both the starting point and the framework of their thought and behavior.

So how could they claim a unique “understanding,” “taste,” and “vision” of questions that stood at the foundations of the existing Islamic order, or of its dominant categories, concepts, and accepted truths?

How were they to define their position toward the Qurʾān, which Muslims regarded as divine revelation sent down to the Prophet, who served as the intermediary between God and “the worlds”?

And how were they to deal with the Qurʾān as a body of sacred texts that contained the “documents” of religious law—the “constitution,” in effect, of the social order represented by the caliphate state?

This was a state that presented its own social and political system as a divine order, possessing the same sacred authority as the religious texts themselves and the religious law contained in those texts.

The Sufis had to face these questions in a way that did not force them completely outside the social and religious reality within which they lived.

They found a way to do this through the two ideas of **outer meaning** and **inner meaning**.

These ideas allowed them to remain within the existing order and rebel against it at the same time.

From both an intellectual and ideological point of view, this became the central operation within the whole Sufi experience of the medieval period.

It brought a revolutionary element into the Sufi theory of knowledge, even amid the enormous mass of impossible claims and dead ends found throughout Sufi thought.

One of the strangest contradictions, in fact, is that this revolutionary element came partly from those very impossible claims.

We will return to this point later.[^V3-210]

The Sufis used the distinction between outer and inner meaning when dealing with the sacred texts of Islam, the Qurʾān and the Sunna.

This allowed them to make those texts carry meanings that could conflict with the normal Islamic theory of knowledge.

They argued that the texts had two levels of meaning.

The outer meaning was directed toward ordinary Muslims.

The inner meaning was intended for a special group.

And in the Sufi view, that special group was the Sufis themselves.

Were they not, according to their own claim, “God’s special people, to whom He has given the secrets of the inner knowledge placed within the Qurʾān and the Hadith”?[^V3-211]

This opened a wide door for them.

On the one hand, they could accept the sacred texts in their ordinary form, just as all Muslims did.

On the other hand, they could interpret the outer wording in terms of its supposed “inner” meaning.

And because the possible meanings of that “inner” level had no clear limit, the field of interpretation was almost unlimited, as we will see.

The Sufis were helped here by the fact that the distinction between outer and inner meanings in the Qurʾān and Hadith was not a claim they had simply invented without precedent.

Nor was interpretation a completely new practice created by them.

The foundations already existed within Islamic thought itself, and we find examples of interpretation going back even to the earliest period of Islam.

If the sayings attributed to the Prophet are authentic when they state that the Qurʾān has both an outer and an inner meaning, and that its inner meaning contains seven inner levels, or even seventy,[^V3-212] then this would show that the distinction between outer and inner meanings in the Qurʾān was already present at the beginning of Islam.

Shiʿi sources, like Sufi sources, also attribute to ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib, the first imam of the Shiʿa, the statement that:

> “There is no verse in the Qurʾān that does not have four meanings: an outer meaning, an inner meaning, a limit, and a point of ascent.”[^V3-213]

The distinction between outer and inner meaning also appears in Islamic jurisprudence as something jurists took for granted.

Al-Shāfiʿī (150–204 AH / 767–819 CE), for example, the founder of one of the four main Sunni schools of law, says while defending the use of analogy in deriving legal rulings that knowledge of these rulings comes “in different forms: some includes full knowledge of both the outer and inner meaning, and some gives certainty concerning the outer meaning...”[^V3-214]

We can go further.

The distinction between outer and inner meaning, and the idea of interpretation itself, can be said to have a basis in the Qurʾān.

One verse says:

> “It is He who sent down the Book to you. Some of its verses are clear and decisive; they are the foundation of the Book. Others are open to more than one meaning. Those whose hearts are inclined toward error follow what is unclear in it, seeking disorder and seeking its interpretation. But no one knows its interpretation except God and those firmly grounded in knowledge.”[^V3-215]

The Qurʾān therefore presents two kinds of verses.

The first are the clear and decisive verses, whose meaning is apparent.

The second are the ambiguous verses,[^V3-216] whose meaning is not immediately clear from the text and must instead be uncovered through interpretation.

But first, what exactly is this interpretation?

And second, who has the right to interpret?

We find hints toward an answer to the first question in another Qurʾānic verse:

> “In this way your Lord will choose you, teach you the interpretation of events, and complete His blessing upon you...”[^V3-217]

Here “interpretation” appears to have a special meaning connected with knowledge, rather than simply its ordinary dictionary meaning of explanation.

If it meant no more than explaining the obvious meaning of words, there would have been nothing special about God teaching Joseph how to interpret events.[^V3-218]

Explaining an obvious meaning does not require a special divine teaching described as the completion of God’s blessing.

Interpretation must therefore be a kind of knowledge that is not ordinary or widely shared.

It is not knowledge reached through reason, through the senses, or simply through the apparent meaning of texts.

As for who has the right to interpret, the earlier verse gives the answer:

> “No one knows its interpretation except God and those firmly grounded in knowledge.”

But another question remains.

Who exactly are “those firmly grounded in knowledge”?

One possible answer appears in another verse:

> “Believers, obey God, obey the Messenger, and those among you who hold authority. If you disagree about anything, refer it to God and the Messenger, if you believe in God and the Last Day. That is better and leads to the best interpretation.”[^V3-219]

At first sight, this verse appears to reserve the final authority of interpretation for God and the Messenger.

But the same verse places obedience to “those who hold authority” immediately after obedience to the Messenger.

This means that referring disputed questions to those who hold authority could be treated as equivalent to referring them to the Messenger.

The verse gives them the position of representing him among the Muslims and commands obedience to them alongside obedience to God and the Messenger.

But who were these “people of authority”?

The Shiʿa said they were the imams from the Prophet’s household.

The Sunnis said they were the caliphs standing at the head of a social order that claimed to represent religious law, as the “representatives” of God and His Messenger.

The Sufis, so that this interpretation would not close the door in their own faces, widened the idea of “those who hold authority” until it also included their own people of *walāya*, much as the Shiʿa had developed the idea of the authority of the imams.

This, as we see it, is where the Sufi idea of *walāya* found its basis. See al-Jurjānī, *Kitāb al-Taʿrīfāt*, p. 227.

All of this gave the Sufis an Islamic “theoretical” basis for interpretation without forcing them into an obvious conflict with the religious texts when they used their own method of “inner” interpretation.

As we have seen, these texts do not define with complete precision who “those firmly grounded in knowledge” actually are.

This allowed the Sufis to claim that they themselves belonged to this group.

They could say that they possessed an inner, heartfelt knowledge reached through *mujāhada*, the struggle against the self.

They also found many practical precedents for interpretation before their own time, going all the way back to the early Islamic period.

Many people had already allowed themselves to interpret Qurʾānic verses in ways that served material interests, supported political positions, or strengthened particular religious-political movements in struggles against their rivals.

Historical sources report, for example, that Muʿāwiya, the founder of the Umayyad state, accumulated large amounts of gold and silver and ordered his provincial officials not to distribute these funds among those entitled to receive them, but to send the wealth to him instead.

Abū Dharr al-Ghifārī[^V3-220] challenged him by appealing to the broad and obvious meaning of the verse:

> “Those who hoard gold and silver and do not spend it in the way of God—give them the news of a painful punishment.”[^V3-221]

But Muʿāwiya, wanting to avoid having this verse applied to his own behavior, used the idea of interpretation.

He claimed that the verse applied only to the People of the Book, meaning Jews and Christians.

The Shiʿa also used interpretation in explaining many Qurʾānic verses[^V3-222] in support of their belief in the Imamate of ʿAlī, his sons, and his descendants according to the accepted line of succession.

They argued that the verse about “those firmly grounded in knowledge” included the imams.

The imams, they said, were heirs to prophecy and therefore possessed the Prophet’s knowledge. For that reason, they too were among those “firmly grounded in knowledge.”

Some of the more extreme Shiʿi groups took interpretation much further.

The followers of Abū Hāshim ʿAbd Allāh ibn Muḥammad ibn al-Ḥanafiyya (d. 97 AH / 715–716 CE), for example, said that every outer meaning had an inner meaning, every person had a spirit, every revelation had an interpretation, and every example in this world had a true reality in the other world.[^V3-223]

We can notice here the influence of Plato’s theory of Forms.

They applied their “theory” of interpretation very widely in support of every part of their beliefs.

These included belief in the transmigration of souls and the claim that the prophetic mission of every prophet ended one hundred years after his call.

They supported this through their interpretation of the verse:

> “Or consider the one who passed by a town that had fallen into ruin. He said, ‘How will God bring this back to life after its death?’ So God caused him to die for a hundred years, then raised him again.”
> —Qurʾān 2:259[^V3-224]

We should notice something important here.

Among the followers of Abū Hāshim, we already find an idea that would later enter the theory of Islamic Sufism: going beyond prophecy and removing revelation as the intermediary between God and human beings.

This, in turn, led them toward another idea that would later become part of Sufi theory: setting aside religious law.

Here too, the followers of Abū Hāshim used Qurʾānic interpretation.

They interpreted the verse:

> “There is no blame on those who believe and do good for what they eat, so long as they remain mindful of God...”
> —Qurʾān 5:93

They argued that anyone who reached the imam and truly knew him was freed from blame in everything he consumed, and had reached perfection and fulfillment.[^V3-225]

The same idea appeared again among many of the groups described as the “extremists,” because of the extreme claims they made about ʿAlī or one of his descendants.

One example was the Khaṭṭābiyya.[^V3-226]

Abū al-Khaṭṭāb, the leader of this group, claimed that “the imams are prophets, and then gods.”[^V3-227]

His followers interpreted the verse:

> “When I have formed him and breathed into him of My spirit, fall down before him in prostration.”
> —Qurʾān 15:29

They claimed that al-Ḥusayn ibn ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib was Adam, and that they—that is, the followers of Abū Hāshim—were his children.

They also worshipped Abū al-Khaṭṭāb himself.[^V3-228]

Several groups later branched off from the Khaṭṭābiyya.

One of these was the Muʿammariyya,[^V3-229] which went even further than the Khaṭṭābiyya.

They claimed that:

> “The world will never come to an end. Paradise is whatever good, comfort, and well-being people experience, while hell is whatever evil, hardship, and suffering they experience. They declared wine, fornication, and all other forbidden things lawful, and they abandoned prayer and the other religious duties.”[^V3-230]

We find many examples of ideas like these—ideas that will later appear in similar forms among the Sufis, although Sufi versions would take a different form and would be built into philosophical systems with their own deliberate logic and structure.

But before we return to the main line of our argument, we cannot simply pass over the view we have just encountered among the Muʿammariyya: the apparently materialist idea that “the world will never come to an end,” together with the earthly meaning they gave to paradise and hell.

We cannot pass over this because it brings us directly back to our main subject.

It also opens a window onto a more realistic understanding of the revolutionary element hidden within some parts of the Sufi theory of knowledge.

Ideas of this kind among the groups called “extremists” are, without doubt, distorted forms of reflection.

But these forms are always in the process of developing.

The philosophical theories of “Islamic Sufism” about knowledge and its related questions, and the theories of the other, rationalist current in Arab philosophy, are themselves stages in the continuing development of these forms of reflection.

This development takes place as consciousness itself develops.

And consciousness develops because real existence develops—above all, because social reality develops—together with the relative growth of scientific knowledge.

Scientific knowledge can uncover the objective truths of the universe more and more deeply, provided it remains part of a continuing movement between theory and practice, between abstraction and reality.

Among the teachings of the groups called the “extremists,” we find many ideas similar to the Muʿammariyya claim that the world does not end and to their earthly understanding of paradise and hell.

If we look beyond the labels used by historians of Islamic sects—labels such as “extreme Shiʿa” and “heretics”—and if we also look beyond the confusion, contradictions, impossible claims, and wild personal ambitions found in the teachings of these groups and among some of their leaders and followers, another picture begins to appear.

If we examine the less obvious meaning of their ideas through a scientific method, we can see in them simple forms of a much wider human effort: the attempt, throughout the history of human consciousness, to discover the real relationship between the conscious human subject and the object that consciousness tries to understand.

Among the Islamic “extremist” groups, this effort appeared through their ideas about the relationship between God and the human being.

That was because the relationship between God and humanity formed the framework through which social reality was reflected in Islamic thought.

Islamic thought was not isolated from the wider history and heritage of human thought.

It was part of that history and an extension of it.

But it was also a part with a certain character of its own, and an extension that took a particular form.

That distinctive character came from the specific historical features of Arab-Islamic society during those centuries.

So the materialist elements that appear within the idealist, supernatural, and often contradictory beliefs of many Islamic sects—sects branded as examples of “Shiʿi extremism” or heretical “innovation”—belong to the kind of spontaneous materialism that appears when people still have only a limited understanding of the material world around them, at a time when the natural sciences are still relatively undeveloped.

We can now return to the development of philosophical Sufism. Our discussion of outward and inward meaning, and of the practice of interpretation that grew from this distinction, has shown us that both had historical roots going back before the rise of philosophical Sufism itself. This may help us understand the general direction that Sufi philosophical thought took during a new stage in the development of Arab-Islamic society.

---

##### Third — The Position on the Shariʿa

Philosophical Sufism’s position on the shariʿa was shaped by its theory of knowledge.

Once the Sufis adopted a view of knowledge—knowledge of God and of his oneness—that allowed the human being to reach God directly through inward “vision,” they removed the need for any intermediary between God and the individual. This included even revelation and prophecy.

They therefore broke with the idea of God’s absolute transcendence found in official Islam. That view of divine oneness places a decisive separation between God and the human being. God is understood as so completely transcendent that the human being can have no will alongside God’s will, and no independent ability to grasp God’s oneness.

Once the Sufis rejected intermediaries and rejected this absolute separation, the result was necessarily a rejection of the shariʿa—or, at the very least, the removal of its role as the intermediary between God and the human being.

The mystical experience itself made such an intermediary unnecessary. By its very nature, and according to the Sufi theory of knowledge, it was an experience of direct contact.

So what need was there for an intermediary?

At first sight, however, there seems to be a contradiction in the Sufi position on the shariʿa.

Their theory of knowledge leads them to reject the shariʿa as the intermediary between the human being and God. Yet they also accept the religious texts in which the shariʿa is contained, and they devote great effort to interpreting those same texts.

There would be no point in interpreting a text if they did not first accept the text itself.

The contradiction is real. But we have already explained why it arose. The Sufis were objectively bound by a historical reality they could not escape: Islam remained both the starting point and the framework for their thought and their conduct.

This is why they used interpretation as a way of expressing their rejection of the shariʿa while still appearing to acknowledge it.

In the end, however, this contradiction is mainly formal. The Sufi idea of interpretation is itself part of their theory of knowledge, and that theory rests on another formal duality: the distinction between the outward and the inward.

This is also the distinction between the shariʿa and the truth.

The outward is the shariʿa. The inward is the truth.

And since the Sufis accept the inward rather than the outward, this means that what they really recognize is the “truth” that lies behind the shariʿa and gives rise to it.

That “truth” is God.

So when the Sufis entered into this distinction between outward and inward, or between shariʿa and truth, they were really following a path imposed on them by their historical circumstances—a path that eventually led them to reject the distinction itself.

The real question for them was therefore not whether the outward meaning, or the shariʿa, was needed.

The deeper issue was their rejection of the very basis on which official Islam had built its idea of divine oneness: the idea that God and the human being are absolutely separate.

Once that relationship was defined in this way, official Islam itself was defined as belonging entirely to the outward realm—that is, to the shariʿa, which stands as an intermediary between two separate sides.

This also means that whatever “inner meaning” official Islam allows must remain the inner meaning of the shariʿa itself. Any interpretation of that inner meaning must therefore remain subject to the shariʿa from beginning to end.

This is expressed in the Qurʾanic verse mentioned earlier:

> “O you who believe, obey God, obey the Messenger, and those in authority among you. If you disagree about anything, refer it back to God and the Messenger, if you believe in God and the Last Day. That is better and leads to the best interpretation.”
> — Qurʾan 4:59

The kind of interpretation mentioned in this verse is fundamentally different from Sufi interpretation.

For the Sufis, the inward meaning stands against the outward meaning—the shariʿa—and takes its place. In the verse, by contrast, the “inward” meaning, if we can describe the ambiguous passages[^V3-231] of the Qurʾan in that way, remains alongside the outward meaning and stays subject to its guidance.

The Sufis were therefore trying to tear down the “official” wall separating God from the human being.

And because the shariʿa formed that dividing wall in the clearest and most decisive way, it became their real target.

The shariʿa was also the foundation of the official concept of divine oneness. It helped define the nature of official Islam itself as a religious law governing the relationship between the head of the state—the caliph—and the society he ruled, in a way modeled on the relationship between God and the human being.

The same idea therefore appears in both theology and the theory of the caliphate.

In theology, God possesses absolute transcendence, unity, and will.

In the political order, the caliph, as the “shadow of God on earth,” is likewise presented as possessing a single, supreme authority and will. Society is expected to obey that will absolutely, just as it is expected to submit to the absolute unity and transcendence of God.

In reality, however, the direction of influence was the reverse.

The image of God as completely transcendent above humanity and the universe was itself modeled on the social reality of the caliphal system. The social order was projected upward into the world of metaphysics.

By transferring this image from society into the metaphysical realm, the caliphal social system gained a kind of religious protection against both intellectual doubt and social revolt.

Seen in this light, the Sufis’ adoption of a gnostic theory of knowledge—direct knowledge of God without an intermediary[^V3-232]—was the philosophical side of their attempt to tear down the wall between God and the human being.

But that was not their final aim.

Behind it lay another, deeper motive: to tear down the wall separating one human being from another—the wall between the absolute will of the ruler, the caliph, and the will of the people he ruled.

That wall was the social system embodied in the shariʿa.

It was this system that gave the ruler his absolute will and surrounded him with a divine immunity that placed him beyond the will and understanding of the people he governed.

To challenge this system, the Sufis developed another idea of divine oneness.

Yet whether they intended it or not, this new idea ultimately led them toward the destruction of divine oneness itself.

The reason is that the mystical experience reaches an ultimate goal that is basically the same across the different schools of Sufism, even though those schools describe and name it differently.

For some, it is the human being’s “union”[^V3-233] with God.

For others, it is God’s “indwelling”[^V3-234] in the human being.

For others still, it is a “oneness of existence”[^V3-235] between God and the world.

Among the earliest Muslim philosophical Sufis, this goal was usually described in terms of a stage they all recognized: “annihilation” in God.

Some then spoke of a still higher stage called “subsistence”[^V3-236], meaning the continuation of that annihilation.

However much their descriptions differed, the basic idea remained the same.

What all these views shared was the attempt to break the principle of absolute separation between God and the human being.

The gnostic experience, in both its theoretical and practical sides, challenged that principle directly.

In its place, it introduced the opposite principle: direct contact between God and the human being.

This appeared in the form of “annihilation,” which, in their understanding, involved a kind of mutual disappearance: God in the human being, and the human being in God.

This is precisely what Abū Yazīd al-Bisṭāmī meant in his famous ecstatic statement[^V3-237]:

> “I am God...”[^V3-238]

And Farīd al-Dīn al-ʿAṭṭār expressed the same idea when he wrote:

> “When union is achieved, all reference to both the Sufi and God disappears.”[^V3-239]

Al-Ḥallāj gives us another clear picture of this mutual annihilation between God and the human being in his well-known doctrine of “He is He.”[^V3-240]

In one prayer to God, for example, he says:

> “O He, I am I, and I am He. There is no difference between my ‘I’ and your ‘He’ except that I came into being, while you are eternal.”[^V3-241]

With Ibn ʿArabī and his theory of the “oneness of existence,” the relationship between God and the human being is described as “the flowing of the Real through existing things.”[^V3-242]

This makes the idea of mutual annihilation even clearer, because here the other side of the process—the disappearance of God into the human being—is stated more directly through the idea of this “flow.”

The Sufi challenge to the official doctrine of divine oneness—the doctrine of absolute separation between God and the human being—was therefore built on the principle of a direct relationship with God.

But this produced a concept of divine oneness that carried within itself a contradiction with divine oneness.

This is what we meant when we said that the Sufi concept eventually led toward the destruction of the very idea of divine unity.

The kind of direct relationship and mutual annihilation they imagined does not fit easily with the idea of divine transcendence, even though the Sufis themselves continued to affirm that God is transcendent.

The logic of this relationship leads in one of two directions: either toward making God human, or toward making the human being divine.[^V3-243]

Both contradict the idea of absolute transcendence.

That idea can only be maintained if divinity is understood in completely abstract terms, with God placed entirely above the whole universe, and therefore still more completely above the human being.

Such a view leaves no room for any direct connection with God.

Some Sufis seem to have recognized this problem and tried to solve it.

Sufi sources contain several versions of this attempt. But in all of them, despite their differences, we can see a sense of uncertainty and confusion. They reveal how difficult it was for the Sufis to resolve the contradiction they had created.

Al-Junayd al-Baghdādī, who died in 298 AH/910 CE, expressed this problem clearly. He is often described as the first person to formulate Sufi ideas systematically in writing, and he taught Sufis in private houses and underground rooms.[^V3-244]

Al-Junayd said:

> “When the minds of the wise reach the furthest point of divine oneness, they reach bewilderment.”[^V3-245]

When he was asked about “the divine oneness of the elect”—meaning the Sufi understanding of oneness—he answered:

> “It is for the servant to become like a mere figure before God (...) carried along by God’s ordering within the workings of His power, in the depths of the seas of His oneness; annihilated to himself, to the claims of others upon him, and to his own response to them; absorbed in the reality of God’s existence and oneness, in the reality of nearness to Him, until his sensation and movement disappear and God alone acts through him according to what He wills from him. The end is that the servant returns to his beginning and becomes as he was before he existed.”[^V3-246]

We find something similar in Dhū al-Nūn al-Miṣrī, who died in 245 AH/859 CE.

He too is described as one of the earliest Sufis to study mystical knowledge in a precise and theoretical way,[^V3-247] as the first to make philosophy part of Sufism,[^V3-248] and as someone who “acquired knowledge of the inward and became familiar with much philosophy.”[^V3-249]

Yet this important figure in the history of Islamic Sufism shows the same uncertainty and bewilderment over divine oneness that we find in his contemporary al-Junayd.

When asked about divine oneness, Dhū al-Nūn replied:

> “It is to know that God’s power is present in things without admixture, that he makes things without needing any process, that the cause of every thing is his making, and that whatever you imagine in your mind, God is other than that.”[^V3-250]

These two philosophers of Sufism from the third century AH—the ninth century CE—therefore give us almost the same kind of uncertain, perhaps even desperate, attempt to find a formula that could solve the contradiction in their understanding of divine oneness.

As we can see in the two passages above, they try to make God’s power the basis of that solution.

It is as though they are saying that a direct relationship with God does not cancel his transcendence. God still remains above the universe because his power exceeds every other power. That power acts without arbitrariness and creates things without relying on any previous model.

But does this solution, if we have understood it correctly, really reach the heart of the problem?

It seems not.

The contradiction remains between the Sufis’ commitment to God’s transcendence[^V3-251]—his being above the universe—and their idea of divine oneness as a direct relationship with God.

In fact, this “solution” may only make the problem more complicated, because it adds still more absolute abstractions:

> “Whatever you imagine in your mind, God is other than that.”

When the doctrine of “indwelling” appears in Islamic Sufism, especially with al-Ḥallāj, the attempt to solve this contradiction becomes even harder.

The principle of indwelling is itself the direct opposite of divine transcendence. It destroys the very separation that transcendence requires.

It is difficult to imagine a better picture of this impossibility than the famous line of poetry attributed to al-Ḥallāj himself:[^V3-252]

> He threw him into the sea with his hands tied, then said:
> Take care—take care not to get wet!

Yet al-Ḥallāj himself seems to move between the ideas of indwelling and union, at least in the texts that have reached us under his name.

Many of those texts can be understood in either way.

For example:

> You mingled your spirit with my spirit
> as wine is mixed with clear water.
> If anything touches you, it touches me;
> for you are I, in every state.[^V3-253]

The idea of “mingling” belongs to the language of indwelling.

But the result of that mingling—“you are I”—contains the idea of union.

Each side, the “I” and the “you,” seems to dissolve into the other.

Yet the image still does not take us all the way to complete union or absolute oneness, because the two spirits remain separate enough to be spoken of as two.

Whether al-Ḥallāj should therefore be described as a believer in indwelling[^V3-254] or in union, the same contradiction remains.

Both indwelling and union conflict with the divine transcendence that al-Ḥallāj also accepts.

We can see this in the passage known as the “Ṭā-Sīn of Understanding” in his *Ṭawāsīn al-Azal*:

> “The understandings of created beings do not reach the Truth, and the Truth does not belong to creation. Thoughts are ties, and the ties of created beings do not reach the realities. Reaching knowledge of the Truth is difficult, so how could one reach the truth of the Truth? The Real is beyond the truth, and truth is beneath the Real...”

In the same passage, al-Ḥallāj compares the “knowers” to moths and knowledge of the truth to the light of a lamp.

The “truth of the truth” is the heat of the lamp itself.

Actually reaching the lamp is the “real truth,” something the seeker cannot achieve.

But the “knower” is not satisfied with the light or with the heat. He wants to reach the lamp itself.

So he throws his whole being into it until he disappears, shrinking and scattering away, until he remains “without trace, body, name, or mark...”

Here divine transcendence appears in its most extreme and unreachable form.

But then the problem returns.

How can direct contact—whether through indwelling or union—still be possible while transcendence remains truly absolute?

If we put this contradiction in the Sufi concept of divine oneness to one side, there are still two important features of this early philosophical Sufism that we should not overlook.

The first is the point we discussed earlier: the attempt to bring God and the human being closer together.

Its importance lies mainly in its social meaning.

By rejecting the theoretical basis for the sacred status of the social order—the idea of God’s absolute transcendence, of which the shariʿa was seen as an expression—the Sufis also challenged the sacred status of that social order itself.

Philosophically, this attempt ran into the contradiction we have just described and could not resolve it.

What remains important, then, is its historical significance.

It was one of the earliest forms taken by philosophical Sufism in Islam. We will return later to its social meaning.

The second major feature of the third-century Sufi concept of divine oneness—that is, the concept found in al-Ḥallāj and his contemporaries—is its recognition that the external world has an objective existence.

In Sufi language, this external world is “creation.”

The ideas of annihilation, indwelling, and union all assume the existence of two sides between which annihilation, indwelling, or union can take place: God and the human being.

Both therefore exist.

Each has an existence independent of the other.

Does this make the Sufi position materialist?

The basis of materialism, as Lenin put it, is “to recognize the objective reality revealed to us by our sense organs.”[^V3-255]

The Sufis, however, do something very different.

Although they recognize an objective “truth” and the objective existence of creation, they place that truth outside the material world in the first place, outside human perception and the senses in the second place, and they say in the third place that it can be known only through supernatural intuition or through the knowledge of the “heart.”

It is therefore not enough that these Sufis recognize the objective existence of the world, or even an objective “truth.”

That truth, in their view, does not exist within the material world and does not follow its objective laws.

Nor can the human mind or the senses reach it.

Still, their recognition of an objectively existing world remains important when compared with other Sufi schools that we will discuss later.

But that importance has clear limits.

At most, it separates them from subjective idealism and places them among the objective idealists.

Objective idealism remained the dominant tendency in philosophical Sufi thought until the time of Ibn ʿArabī and the spread of his doctrine of the “oneness of existence,” as we will see.

##### Fourth — Illuminationist Sufism

The problem of the relationship between God and the material world—and especially between God and the human being—continued for another period to dominate Arab-Islamic thought.

It was especially important in its two main branches: rationalist philosophy and Sufi philosophy.

There is an interesting pattern in the relationship between these two traditions.

The more they interacted and influenced one another, the more sharply they also disagreed over the central problem and over the solutions each side proposed.

We can explain this double relationship between rationalist philosophy and Sufi philosophy if we identify the common ground from which both began.

What was that common ground?

It was the same ground from which the central problem of Arab-Islamic thought itself arose: the relationship between God and the material world.

At its root, this was a problem of the social order.

It affected Arab-Islamic society as a whole.

In both rationalist philosophy and Sufi philosophy, the problem appeared in intellectual form as a reflection of that social reality, or as a theoretical expression of it.

But these were two different, and perhaps even opposing, responses to that reality in ideological terms.

We return to this point because it matters for the subject of Illuminationist Sufism.

In this kind of Sufism, we will find something different from the forms we have discussed so far.

A new combination appears.

It includes concepts, categories, and terms that had not formed part of third-century Sufism.

Some of them may already have appeared in one form or another, but they had not yet developed into full concepts and categories within philosophical Sufism.

The same is true of the fourth century AH.

Some Sufis of that period used expressions resembling those of the later Illuminationists—for example, the word “light.”

But they did not yet mean by it the special philosophical concept that it would later acquire.

They used “light” to describe what Sufis claimed to experience during mystical states: flashes of “secrets in their hearts.”[^V3-256]

That is something very different from the Illuminationist concept of “light,” which later came to express an entire metaphysical system in the philosophy of the Illuminationists.

Our study of Illuminationist Sufism will therefore examine the Ismaʿili form of Illuminationism, the doctrine of Suhrawardī the Executed, and the material basis of Illuminationism in general. We will also try to identify its revolutionary element.

### A — The Ismaʿili Form of Illumination

#### 1 — Changes within Sufi Thought

Sufi ideas began to change once an Illuminationist movement appeared within Islamic philosophical Sufism.

That movement eventually found in Suhrawardī the Executed[^V3-257] the thinker who organized it into a complete theory.

Before Suhrawardī, however, its main ideas and tendencies had already appeared in different forms within several schools and doctrines, especially among the Ismaʿilis.[^V3-258]

During the third and fourth centuries AH, Sufism had described the relationship between God and the human being mainly through direct “unveiling,” without an intermediary.

Now a different network of ideas began to develop.

The relationship with God became connected to a series of illuminating intermediaries that lifted the “knowers” upward through successive ranks until they reached “knowledge” in the Sufi sense.[^V3-259]

Among the Ismaʿilis, this hierarchy appeared in two parallel forms.

The human form consisted of seven imams and twelve *ḥujjas*.

The cosmic form mirrored it, with seven celestial spheres and the twelve signs of the zodiac.[^V3-260]

This complex Ismaʿili system appears to have developed through a blending of two ideas: the ancient Pythagorean belief in the sacred significance of the number seven, and the Shiʿi Islamic doctrine of the imamate.

The Ismaʿilis taught that the imamate passed from Ismāʿīl to his son Muḥammad.

They called Muḥammad the “seventh and complete imam,” because he completed the cycle of seven imams.[^V3-261]

This is why the Ismaʿilis were also called the “Seveners.”[^V3-262]

Their system of seven was based on comparisons with “the seven days of the week, the seven heavens, and the seven planets.”[^V3-263]

The philosophical version of this idea of intermediaries also appears in Ismaʿili thought.

Al-Shahrastānī[^V3-264] describes it in roughly the following way.

God first brought into being, by command, the First Intellect, which is already complete in actuality.

Through the First Intellect, God then brought into being the Soul.

The Soul is not yet complete. Its relation to the Intellect is like the relation of a result to what produces it.

Because the Soul lacks the completeness of the Intellect, it needs movement to take it from incompleteness to completeness.

Movement, in turn, requires something through which movement can take place.

This is how the celestial spheres came into being.

Under the direction of the Soul, these spheres moved in a circular motion, and from that motion came the simple natures.

They then also moved in a straight motion, again under the direction of the Soul.

From this second movement came composite things: minerals, plants, animals, and human beings.

Individual souls—the souls of individual people—then became connected to bodies.

Human beings were distinguished from other things by a special ability to receive the outpouring of these lights.

The human world thus came to stand as a counterpart to the universe as a whole.[^V3-265]

#### 2 — Divine Manifestations on Earth

To explain the relationship between the human world and the higher world, the Ismaʿilis also developed the idea that divine manifestations appeared on earth through the imams.

Their system worked as follows.

If the higher world contains a Universal Intellect and a Universal Soul, then the human world must also contain an individual form of intellect and an individual form of soul.

This individualized Intellect is still a universal reality in the sense that it represents a whole kind.

It therefore appears in the form of a person who has reached perfection.

The Ismaʿilis call this figure the “Speaker,” and it is represented by the prophet.

The human Soul is also a universal reality, but it is like a child that has not yet reached perfection and is moving toward it.

They call this the “Foundation,” and it is represented by the prophet’s successor, the imam.

This Soul reaches perfection when it rises to the level of the Intellect and unites with it under the influence of the movements of the celestial spheres.

Just as the celestial spheres and the natural world are moved by the Universal Intellect and Universal Soul, individual souls—or individual people—in our world are moved by the prophet and his successor in every age.

This cycle proceeds in groups of seven until the final cycle arrives.

Then comes the time of resurrection, religious obligations come to an end, and inherited customs and religious laws disappear.[^V3-266]

#### 3 — An Ontological and Epistemological Scheme[^V3-267][^V3-268]

We have described this Ismaʿili system so that we can better see the changes taking place within the gnostic movement—gnosticism, or *ʿirfān*—that had begun to appear within Islamic Sufism.

As these changes continued, they would eventually meet an Illuminationist Sufi movement in which Islamic, Pythagorean, Hermetic, and Neoplatonic elements came together in the philosophy of Suhrawardī.

Before turning to Suhrawardī, however, we should return to the Ismaʿili system described above.

Some of its main features contain an important attempt to explain the material world.

And within that attempt, despite its idealist and supernatural framework, we can also find certain materialist tendencies.

The first thing that stands out is that this system presents both an ontological scheme—a scheme of existence—and an epistemological one—a scheme of knowledge.

Existence and knowledge arise through the same process.

The process that brings things into existence is also the process through which the lights of “knowledge” flow outward.

God brings the First Intellect into being.

The First Intellect produces the Soul.

The Soul then moves toward perfection, and from its movement the celestial spheres arise.

The circular movement of these spheres produces the composite beings—that is, the material beings: minerals, plants, animals, and human beings.

The whole process ends with the human being in two different but connected ways.

First, it brings the human being into existence.

Second, it pours the lights of “knowledge” into the human being.

Human beings differ from other creatures because they have a special ability to receive this outpouring of knowledge.

A second important point is that the First Intellect appears here as eternal.

God brought it into being through his command—that is, through his creative will.

But God’s will is eternal because God himself is eternal.

The First Intellect must therefore be eternal as well.

And this leads, in turn, toward the idea that the world itself is eternal, because the world is the product of the eternal First Intellect.

Here, then, we find a spontaneous materialist tendency.
