The description of the First Intellect as “complete in actuality” supports this conclusion.

In theological and Illuminationist philosophies, this kind of complete actuality is normally attributed only to something eternal.

A third important feature of the same system is its serious attempt to explain the origin of matter.

In its ontological picture of the world, the simple natures form the basis from which material beings arise.

These material beings are what the system calls “composites.”

At the same time, the existence of the simple natures is linked to movement: the circular movement of the celestial spheres.

The transformation of these simple natures into composite material beings is also linked to movement: in this case, the straight movement of the spheres.

Both forms of movement are directed by the “Soul,” meaning the “Universal Soul.”

Within this system, the Universal Soul represents the general kind of which individual souls are the separate members or individual examples.

Does this suggest a Stoic influence?

Stoic natural philosophy held that the world is material, but also that it is always in motion.

Movement exists within the world’s matter, not outside it.

That matter was understood as made up of “souls” in constant movement, moving in an endless circular rhythm between contraction and expansion.[^V3-269]

The Ismaʿilis, judging from the system we are examining, seem to have drawn something from this Stoic idea.

But they did not adopt the whole Stoic theory of existence, nor the Stoic theory of knowledge.

Whether the Ismaʿili idea of a material world in motion actually came from Stoicism or not, the important point for us is that their theory contains a materialist tendency in its understanding of movement, even though that tendency exists within a metaphysical framework.

We can also put the matter the other way around.

Their explanation of the world takes a metaphysical form, but it begins from certain materialist assumptions.

The first is its recognition that the material world exists objectively.

The second is its recognition of movement as a basic principle of that world’s existence and as one of its essential features.

Circular movement produces the simple natures.

Straight movement transforms those simple natures into the “composite” beings of the material world.

Can we therefore see, within this metaphysical picture of a world constantly coming into being through movement, the beginnings of an idea that movement is the way matter exists?

Or would that be forcing too much into the evidence?

There is also a third basis for a materialist starting point in Ismaʿili thought.

Movement is treated as a general principle applying to the different forms of material existence, and in this sense as a principle of the unity of the world.

But it is also clear that this materialist view of movement remains tied to an idealist and purpose-driven view of the universe.

The Universal Soul needs movement because it needs to reach perfection.

Its movement is therefore directed toward a goal.

### B — Suhrawardī’s Illuminationism

#### 1 — The Interweaving of Philosophies

The Illuminationist theories of Yaḥyā Shihāb al-Dīn al-Suhrawardī the Executed, who lived from 549 to 587 AH/1153 to 1191 CE, provide a vivid example of the interweaving we mentioned earlier between rationalist philosophy—especially the Peripatetic tradition—and Sufi philosophy.

They also draw on several other sources of Illuminationist thought: Zoroastrianism, Pythagoreanism, Platonism, and Hermeticism.[^V3-270][^V3-271]

Islam, however, remained the broad framework within which these different philosophical and Illuminationist traditions came together in Suhrawardī’s thought.

We should stress once again that the basic ground on which these ideas interacted and combined into a complete Islamic Illuminationist Sufi system was Islamic society itself.

This will become clearer as we continue.

Suhrawardī’s own writings suggest that he had a strong awareness of the universal character of philosophical history: the way philosophical thought appears in different forms and expressions across different societies and historical periods.

With this awareness, he built his Illuminationist Sufi system from elements whose different origins, historical development, relationships, and mutual influences he seems to have understood quite well.[^V3-272]

What we do not find, however, is an understanding of how each of those elements was connected to the particular society and historical period in which it first appeared, or why it took one form in one society and another form somewhere else.

We should not expect to find such an understanding in Suhrawardī.

Human knowledge needed many more centuries before a more advanced scientific method appeared—one capable of uncovering these objective relationships.

That is the main reason.

But there is also another reason: the limits built into Suhrawardī’s own “method.”

His method is metaphysical[^V3-273] in its foundations, its concepts, and its structure.

For that reason, it separates the whole world of thought from the world of material reality—which, in Suhrawardī’s philosophy, is the world of darkness.

#### 2 — The Main Features of the Age

The period in which Suhrawardī’s Illuminationist philosophy appeared was marked by two major developments.

The first was the growing intensity of intellectual repression.

This had begun to dominate philosophical life when *kalām* moved from its Muʿtazilite phase into its Ashʿarite phase during the fourth century AH, the tenth century CE.

The trend became stronger in the fifth century AH, the eleventh century CE, when al-Ghazālī emerged[^V3-274] as the leading voice of official Sunni Islamic thought and took up the defense of Ashʿarism.

At the same time, he used the tools of philosophical logic themselves as weapons against philosophical doctrines.

By the sixth century AH, the twelfth century CE, many more people were carrying forward this intellectual campaign in both the eastern and western Islamic worlds.

Intellectual authority had increasingly passed into the hands of conservative jurists.

They became the main ideological support of the ruling groups at a time when those same ruling groups were suffering intense internal political and ideological conflict across the wide territory occupied by competing Islamic states.

The second major development was the appearance of a new kind of contradiction within the political and ideological conflicts of the eastern Islamic world.

The first great wave of European colonial invasion, known as the Crusades, was moving forcefully into the region.

Yet the eastern front that should have been confronting this major threat was itself divided by conflict.

On one side, the Shiʿi Fatimid state was at war with the Sunni Ayyubid state.[^V3-275]

Then, in 567 AH/1171 CE, the Fatimid state collapsed under the attacks of its Ayyubid opponent Ṣalāḥ al-Dīn.

His state gained control of Egypt, Palestine, and most of Syria.

At the same time, conflict continued between the Ayyubids and the Turkish Seljuk state, whose control had by then shrunk back toward Asia Minor.

Other struggles followed between the Khwarazmian state and its enemies across the vast area between the Ganges in India and the Tigris in Iraq.[^V3-276]

Meanwhile, the Abbasid caliphs in Baghdad were struggling against successive sultans to preserve the increasingly symbolic remains of religious authority in their own hands.

They finally lost even that in the catastrophe of the Mongol invasion under Hülegü in 655 AH/1258 CE.

These two developments—intensified intellectual repression and the new political conflicts produced by the Crusades—shaped the political and intellectual life of Islamic society from the sixth century AH onward.

But we must also add the continuation of the wider crisis that had existed since the third century.

This was a crisis of the social system as a whole.

It appeared most clearly in the weakness and stagnation of the dominant form of production and the relations built around it.

This continuing crisis repeatedly produced scattered popular uprisings and different forms of struggle.

Yet all of them were defeated without achieving their aims.

Once we add this wider social crisis to the two developments already described, it becomes easier to understand why these social, political, and intellectual conditions found their way into philosophical thought—and especially into Sufi philosophy.

They also shaped the ideological forms taken by oppositional thought.

Both the form and content of that thought reflected a growing despair over the possibility of changing a reality filled with material suffering for the overwhelming majority of people in that society.

This despair did not find a path toward a positive revolutionary outlook.

Instead, it turned into a negative, nihilistic kind of “revolutionary” rejection of the material world itself.

Some forms of this nihilistic rebellion eventually went so far as to deny that the material world had any true existence at all.

In its place, they turned toward a metaphysical form of “existence.”

Earlier world philosophies offered Islamic philosophical and Sufi thought many different models of such a realm.

These models attracted thinkers who were looking for another kind of order—a heavenly order in which people crushed by earthly conditions might find what they had lost in their own social system.

But their ideas of this supernatural order were necessarily shaped by their historical relationship to Islam, to its picture of the universe, and to its particular metaphysical ideas.

All of this was a search for “salvation.”

The idea of “salvation,” first in its Christian form and later in its Sufi form, is fundamentally an echo of the longing of oppressed masses to escape the different kinds of social injustice they have suffered since the beginning of class society.

This was the main real-world dimension of the Sufi Illuminationist movement when the conditions for its appearance finally came together in a complete philosophical system under Suhrawardī in the sixth century AH.

We will see this dimension appear in several parts of the system he created.

#### 3 — A System of Existence and Knowledge

We should begin by saying that Suhrawardī’s Illuminationist universe, like the Ismaʿili esoteric system we discussed earlier, is first and foremost a system of existence—an ontological system.

Its theory of knowledge grows directly out of that structure of existence.

Here, “light” is the basic principle of the existence of the whole universe.

This “light” flows from the highest source, the “Light of Lights,” and descends step by step through lower levels.

Every stage in this outflow is a form of “luminous” existence.

But at each lower level, the amount or intensity of light decreases.

When it finally reaches the level of physical bodies—the material world—the light becomes so weak that it almost disappears.

This is how the “dark” level of existence comes into being.

Suhrawardī’s Illuminationism therefore does not accept a true dualism between light and darkness of the kind found in Zoroastrianism and Manichaeism.[^V3-277]

For Suhrawardī, there is only one principle of existence: light.

Darkness is simply the lowest level of luminous existence—the point at which the radiance of existence has almost disappeared.

Broadly speaking, this is how the Illuminationist system understands existence, and its theory of knowledge is built into the same structure. “Light,” as the principle of existence, is also the principle of the mystical Illuminationist “truth” that knowledge seeks to reach. That is the first point.

The second is that one of the basic principles of Illuminationist philosophy brings together two acts: “illumination” and “direct vision.” They move in opposite directions but share the same double function. Illumination moves downward, while direct vision moves upward. Yet both take part in bringing things into existence, and both also provide a path to knowledge. This is how Suhrawardī defines both “the process of bringing things into existence and the process of knowing.”[^V3-278]

#### 4 - The Problem of Divine Transcendence in the Illuminationist System

Here too, we have to return to the great problem faced by philosophical mysticism: how to combine two opposites—God’s transcendence above the world, and the rejection of that transcendence.

The contradiction appears especially clearly in the Illuminationist picture of the universe.[^V3-279] The presence of the “Light of Lights” (God) extends through every level of this system, even its lowest levels. Suhrawardī describes this presence in two ways.

First, “light” flows downward in a chain of emanations, beginning with its highest source, passing to the “first emanation,” and then continuing from one level to the next until it reaches the world of dark physical bodies.

Second, the “Light of Lights” shines directly on every level in this chain of existence, without any intermediary.[^V3-280]

In both forms, this presence clearly rejects the idea that God is completely separate from the world.

Yet at the same time, Suhrawardī returns to a position taken much earlier by the Muʿtazilite theologians. He accepts their famous teaching on the absolute unity of the divine essence: God’s attributes are identical with God’s essence. This was also known as the “denial of the attributes.”[^V3-281] Among the different positions developed in Islamic theology, this was one of the strongest possible expressions of divine transcendence.

So with Illuminationist mysticism, we are facing the contradiction within mystical philosophy in one of its clearest and most explicit forms.

Perhaps we can see in Suhrawardī an attempt to solve this problem by moving toward the idea of the “unity of being.” Taken in its metaphysical sense, the logic of this idea leads to the conclusion that all existence is contained within the reality of the divine essence alone. Suhrawardī’s own structure of existence is so tightly interconnected that it sometimes comes close to this same idea.

But if this really was an attempt to solve the problem, it certainly failed.

It failed for two reasons. First, Suhrawardī himself rejects any assumption that would lead to the full meaning of this “unity.”[^V3-282] Second, even those who openly taught the “unity of being”—above all Ibn ʿArabī—did not actually deny the existence of things so completely that only one being, God, remained. In many of Ibn ʿArabī’s texts, created things still have a real existence of their own.[^V3-283] At the same time, Ibn ʿArabī does not separate their existence from God’s existence.

The problem therefore remains.

#### 5 - A Platonic View of the Material World

Illuminationism takes a Platonic view of the material world, and this ultimately leads it to deny that the material world has true existence of its own.

To make this clearer, we need a quick look at the Illuminationist structure of existence as a whole. We can then see how Suhrawardī builds his system from its highest principle, the “Light of Lights,” all the way down to “dark” physical bodies.

We should begin with the basic principle behind the entire Illuminationist picture of the world: **from one, only one can proceed**.[^V3-284]

The world therefore begins with a single first emanation. This is the first level produced by the flow of existence from the Light of Lights. It is a “pure light,” standing between the first principle of existence—the Light of Lights—and all other beings.

This first light contains no multiplicity. It is not a body. It knows itself and knows its first principle. Suhrawardī says that “nothing nobler than it is possible, and it is the highest of all possible beings.”[^V3-285]

He also calls it the “first creative light,”[^V3-286] and the “nearest light.” In some of his mystical writings he symbolically calls it “the Shaykh,”[^V3-287] and elsewhere “Beauty.”[^V3-288]

In his other works, which are more closely connected to Islamic Peripatetic philosophy, he calls it the “First Intellect,” just as Plotinus[^V3-289] and Ibn Sīnā[^V3-290] had done.

In Aristotelian metaphysics, we find a similar intellect described as the “first moved mover” (*Primum Mobile*). For Aristotle, this is what moves the outermost celestial sphere, or the “first heaven.”[^V3-291] Aristotle, as we know, describes the movers of the heavenly spheres as “the intellects of the planets.” The “first moved mover” can therefore also be called the “First Intellect” within the Aristotelian system.[^V3-292]

The Light of Lights first shines upon this “first emanation.”

Suhrawardī says that this radiance does not separate from the Light of Lights, because separation and contact are properties of bodies. Nor does the radiance move from one place to another, “because what moves cannot be a substance.”[^V3-293] The radiance itself is not a body either.[^V3-294]

From this first emanation, light then flows to the level below it. From there, further emanations continue downward, creating a whole system of existence through an increasingly complex chain of luminous beings.

But all these beings are, for Suhrawardī, “immaterial lights.” In other words, they are intellectual or spiritual beings with no bodily element at all.

He therefore divides them into two kinds.

The first has no relation to bodies at all.

The second has a relation to bodies only in the sense that it governs them. These are intellectual lights that direct physical bodies.

The rational soul belongs to this second kind. It is the human soul.[^V3-295] In Suhrawardī’s Illuminationist system, the human soul proceeds from what he calls the “Lord of the Human Form”—the archetype of the human species in the world of light.

To understand what Suhrawardī means by these “Lords of the Forms,” and to see why they matter to our argument, we need to look more closely at his division of the first kind of “immaterial lights,” which he also calls “intellects.”

He divides these into two groups.

The first is arranged vertically in a descending order. Suhrawardī calls these the “Mothers.”[^V3-296]

The second group comes from this vertical order, almost like branches growing from it. These form a horizontal level.

The first group—the vertical order of “Mothers”—contains the higher lights that do not produce physical bodies.

The second group contains the lights from which physical bodies do arise. These lights are not ranked above and below one another. They exist horizontally, on an equal level.

It is these horizontal “branches” that produce physical bodies.

Suhrawardī says that both groups—the vertical and the horizontal, the “Mothers” and the “branches”—are finite. Even so, they are so numerous that they cannot be counted.[^V3-297]

We can now return to the “Lords of the Forms.”

Where do they belong in this division? What exactly does Suhrawardī mean by them? And what is their philosophical importance?

From his texts on this subject,[^V3-299] we can see that what he calls the “Lords of the Species-Forms” belongs to the second group just described: the horizontal “immaterial lights,” or intellects.

These horizontal lights are the “Lords of the Species-Forms.”

They are themselves divided into two groups. One is produced through “direct vision,” while the other is produced through “illumination.”

Those produced through direct vision are the lords of the species found in the **imaginal world**—the non-sensory world that Suhrawardī also calls the “suspended world.”[^V3-300]

Those produced through illumination are the lords of the species found in the sensory world.

Why the difference?

Because, Suhrawardī says, “the lights of direct vision are nobler than the lights of illumination, and the imaginal world is nobler than the sensory world.” The imaginal world must therefore proceed from direct vision, while the sensory world proceeds from illumination.[^V3-301]

From the “Lords of the Species of the Sensory World” come, first, the lords of the celestial forms and, second, the “lords of the talismans of the simple and compound elements.”[^V3-302] In other words, these are the spiritual types from which the basic elements, and the compounds made from them, arise in our earthly world.

We can now summarize the picture.

There is a spiritual world made up of two systems, one vertical and one horizontal.

In the first, each level proceeds from the level above it through emanation. The chain begins with the “nearest light,” or “First Intellect,” and continues down through the “Lords of the Species of the Imaginal World,” the suspended world. Suhrawardī calls this the world of the “Mothers.”

In the second system, the different beings do not proceed from one another because they are equal in rank and stand beside one another horizontally. These are the lords of the species. Each produces a particular kind of being, which in turn becomes the source of either one of the heavenly bodies or one of the earthly elements.

The conclusion is clear: everything in the material world has a corresponding “type” in the heavenly, spiritual world.

And this “type” is simply another form of the Platonic Idea.

The material world therefore has no true existence of its own. Its existence is secondary. It is a shadow of the Lords of the Species—the Platonic Forms.

This is not merely our own conclusion. Suhrawardī says it openly:

> “Everything in the bodily world, whether substances or qualities, consists of effects and shadows of intellectual forms and luminous configurations. When the movements of the heavens and the positions of the planets prepare the elemental kinds for some substantial or accidental event, the separate intellect that is the lord of the prepared kind pours out the intellectual forms suited to that bodily and luminous preparation... In short, everything strange and wonderful in the world of bodies comes from the luminous, imaginal world.”[^V3-303]

If we take this position toward the material world—Suhrawardī’s denial that it has objective existence—and place beside it another part of his philosophy, the result becomes even clearer.

His Illuminationist system is not only a theory of existence. It is also a theory of knowledge.

For Suhrawardī, knowledge ultimately comes through supernatural illumination rather than through the senses or rational thought.

Taken together, these two positions show the general philosophical direction of his system. They place it clearly within the current of subjective idealism.

#### 6 - The Primacy of Essence

This question is directly connected to idealist philosophy. It is one of the issues that, in Muruwwa’s framework, helps determine where a thinker stands between the two main philosophical tendencies: idealism and materialism.[^V3-304]

Once Suhrawardī had built his Illuminationist system on a denial of the real existence of material reality, it was natural for him to give **essence** priority over existence.

This was consistent with his idealist outlook.

He denied that a physical body is an objectively existing substance. For him, the body exists only as something considered by the mind. And if the body exists only in this mental sense, then real existence cannot be one of its properties, much less something more basic than its essence.

In the first part of *The Philosophy of Illumination*, where he discusses many aspects of Aristotle and the Islamic Aristotelians—usually called the Islamic Peripatetics by historians of philosophy—Suhrawardī treats essence alone as the true reality.

Actual existence, by contrast, is secondary. It depends on essence, or is merely an accidental addition to it.

He also criticizes Aristotle for rejecting the Platonic Forms.

On this basis, Suhrawardī sees physical bodies as dark matter that prevents the radiance of divine light from passing through them.[^V3-305]

The human soul must therefore free itself from the darkness of the body. It must break free of bodily desires and strengthen itself through spiritual virtues. As the power of the body grows weaker, the soul becomes able to connect with its holy father and receive knowledge from it.[^V3-306]

Among the rationalist philosophers, however, the primacy of essence rested on a different argument.

They began from the belief that essences are fixed and do not change, while the things we experience through the senses are always changing. What is fixed, they argued, has a stronger claim to true existence and comes before what changes.

They therefore made essences the proper object of certain knowledge.

The changing objects of sense experience, by contrast, could not provide certainty. Because they constantly changed, they could not serve as the basis of knowledge that was itself fixed and certain. Only essences, which they regarded as unchanging, could do that.

This argument came from their acceptance of Aristotle’s formal logic, especially his theory of “definition.”

According to this theory, a proper definition is built from two fixed elements: the **genus**, or broader class to which something belongs, and the **differentia**, the feature that distinguishes it from other things in that class.[^V3-307]

Because this fixed structure makes definition a matter of defining essences, these philosophers also accepted the idea that essences themselves are fixed.

This theory of definition created a contradiction in Aristotle’s own philosophy.

When Aristotle speaks about existence itself, he gives existence priority. But his theory of knowledge leads him to give essence priority when it comes to what we can know.

This also shows the difference between the Peripatetic philosophers and Suhrawardī on the question of essence.

Suhrawardī begins mainly from a theory of existence. The Peripatetics begin from logic and the theory of knowledge.

Yet their own reasoning about existence—or at least that of some of them—eventually leads them in the opposite direction, toward the primacy of existence.

#### 7 - Visual Perception and Illumination

Suhrawardī’s theory of sight also follows from the subjective idealist direction of his Illuminationist philosophy.

He develops an account of sensory vision in which even the physical function of the eye depends on spiritual illumination.

This removes the basis of sensory knowledge even at the level of one of its most important physical organs.

It also cuts off the path to rational knowledge.

Rational knowledge begins when the mind observes individual things through the senses. It then separates these things from the particular sensory details connected with them. Through this process of abstraction, experience is turned into concepts, categories, and general ideas.

But if sensory knowledge is rejected as genuine knowledge, then the whole process of rational abstraction loses its starting point as well.[^V3-309]

Knowledge is then left with only the supernatural path offered by Suhrawardī: illumination and direct vision.

Suhrawardī prepares the ground for his theory of sight by distinguishing between two meanings of “light.”[^V3-310]

The first is the light we actually see, such as the light of the sun and stars. Suhrawardī denies that this light is a substance. He treats it as an accidental quality.

The second is Illuminationist light, which he calls “pure light.” This cannot be physically pointed to at all, because it is purely intellectual light.

If visible light is only an accidental quality and not a substance, what, then, is the underlying reality connected with it?

We find Suhrawardī’s answer in the way he explains the act of seeing.

His explanation depends, first, on his philosophical view of the material world and, second, on his belief that the way we know things exactly matches the way things are brought into existence.

We have already seen his view of the material world. He denies that it has true existence and treats it instead as something whose existence is only apparent.

This means that sensory knowledge cannot be the route through which the soul receives its perceptions.

For Suhrawardī, those perceptions come through powers that exist within the human soul itself. And the powers responsible for perception are themselves forms of Illuminationist light.

This is what connects the soul to the physical organs of sense.

The human eye therefore sees things through the radiance of pure intellectual light.

Suhrawardī writes:

> “All the immaterial lights can see. Their sight does not come from their knowledge. Rather, their knowledge comes from their sight. Everything in the body is a shadow of the regent light, while the bodily frame is its darkness.”[^V3-311]

For this reason, Suhrawardī rejects the material explanations of vision that were common in the Middle Ages.

The Peripatetics, for example, explained sight by saying that something is emitted from the visible object and reaches the pupil of the eye.

The mathematicians proposed the opposite. In their view, when the eye looks at an object, a cone-shaped ray comes out of the eye. Its broad end spreads over the visible object, while its point remains at the pupil.

Suhrawardī rejects both theories and replaces them with his own Illuminationist account.

When the seeing subject directly faces the visible object, he says, “the soul receives direct Illuminationist knowledge of the object before it, and the soul apprehends it through direct vision.”[^V3-312]

This direct knowledge, or direct illumination, means an immediate connection between the soul and the object being known—in this case, the thing being seen. This is what Suhrawardī calls “direct vision.”

The only condition is that nothing must stand in the way and block this kind of seeing.[^V3-313]

#### 8 - Prophecy, the Prophet, and Religious Law

We now need to ask how Suhrawardī’s Illuminationist philosophy relates to Islam as a religion and an ideology.

We can approach this from two sides.

The first concerns the oneness of God.

The second concerns prophecy in general, the Prophet of Islam in particular, and then the place of religious law as a distinct system.

Where does Suhrawardī stand on these questions?

On the first question, the Illuminationist system faces the same basic contradiction that appears in other forms of philosophical mysticism.

On one side is absolute monotheism.

On the other is the connection between the “Light of Lights”—the one God—and all the other levels of light that extend down to the human soul.

These levels form both the Illuminationist system of existence and its system of knowledge.

The connection operates through two paths: illumination and direct vision.

But this connection conflicts with the idea of absolute divine transcendence, which requires complete separation, as we have explained more than once.

We have already discussed Suhrawardī’s position on this problem.

The second question is prophecy and religious law.

Here, Suhrawardī’s position does not seem to us to agree with Islamic religious ideology.

We do not find a clear place for prophecy or the prophets within his chain of Illuminationist intermediaries between God and humanity.

Instead, the prophets seem to occupy the same level as the sages, without any special status that clearly sets them apart.[^V3-314]

For example, in his Persian treatise *The Cry of the Simurgh*,[^V3-315] Suhrawardī describes the stages through which the “knowers” travel on their spiritual path toward knowledge of God.

He first gives four stages. These are followed by a fifth and final stage: the “stage of absolute unity.”

At this point, “the one who arrives receives the sacred lights in the divine presence.” It is here, Suhrawardī says, that we find “the station of the foremost prophets and sages.”[^V3-316]

At this final stage, then, prophets are simply people who have reached one of the highest levels of “knowledge,” just like the sages.

Is that all Suhrawardī has to say about them?

Here we find nothing about a special “function” of the prophet, either in the heavenly world or in the earthly one.

In *The Philosophy of Illumination*, however,[^V3-317] Suhrawardī does refer to the Prophet Muḥammad. He calls him “the bearer of this religious law,” without giving his name or any other identifying title.

The reference appears while Suhrawardī is describing one of the highest levels of light that sages can reach as they rise through the Illuminationist spiritual path.

Anyone who reaches “this most exalted station,” he says, becomes “as though placed within the light that surrounds everything.”

He then says that a small number of people who reached this level described the experience themselves: Plato, Hermes, and great sages such as Empedocles and Pythagoras.

He adds:

> “This is also what the bearer of this religious law described, along with a group of those who had stripped themselves of the bonds of ordinary human existence.”[^V3-318]

So here again, “the bearer of this religious law” appears simply as one of those who said that they had reached this spiritual station.

He is presented as one among the great sages and among the mystics and Illuminationists who had freed themselves from “the bonds of the body.”

Nothing else is mentioned that clearly distinguishes him from the others.

Suhrawardī does not even directly say that he reached the station. He says only that he himself reported having reached it.

There is, however, a passage in Suhrawardī’s *Intimations* (*al-Talwīḥāt*)[^V3-319] that deals directly with prophecy.

He writes:

> “Know that the first condition of prophecy is that a person be commanded from heaven to reform humanity.”

He also says that among the qualities belonging to the rank of prophets are that “most of their knowledge comes to them without human teaching” and that they possess “a powerful connection with the Active Intellect.”[^V3-320]

He adds that “the animals of the world obey them,” and that they can bring about extraordinary events involving “earthquakes, movement, and stillness.” They can also warn of or report hidden things and particular events, whether in the past or the future.

Then he says:

> “The first is the essential condition. The other three qualities may also be found together among the Brethren of Detachment”—that is, among the mystics.[^V3-321]

This passage could be used as evidence that Suhrawardī recognized prophecy as a form of mediation between God and humanity. If being “commanded from heaven” is understood as a reference to revelation, the passage might seem to support that reading.

But we know that three of Suhrawardī’s books—*The Intimations*, *The Oppositions*, and *The Conversations*—are largely presentations and revisions of Peripatetic philosophy. They do not fully represent his Illuminationist philosophy.

The central and complete statement of that philosophy is *The Philosophy of Illumination*.

So what do we find there?

If the passage in *The Intimations* really does amount to an acceptance of prophecy as a divine message sent to humanity, the passages in *The Philosophy of Illumination* do not support that conclusion. We can even say that they contradict it.

There are two relevant passages.

We have already seen the first. In it, Suhrawardī places “the bearer of this religious law” on the same level as sages such as Plato, Hermes, Empedocles, and Pythagoras, as well as the mystics who had “stripped themselves of ordinary human nature.”

The meaning is quite different from what the passage in *The Intimations* seemed to suggest.

Either “the bearer of this religious law” is simply a sage like those other sages, with no essential difference between them, or else those sages are themselves “prophets” in the same sense that he is.

In the second case, Suhrawardī would be using the idea of “prophecy” in a special sense that differs from its meaning in Islam.

The second passage in *The Philosophy of Illumination* says:

> “The world has never been without wisdom, or without a person who upholds it and possesses its proofs and clear evidence.”[^V3-322]

Who is this person who upholds wisdom and possesses its proofs and evidence?

Is it the prophet?

Elsewhere in the same book, Suhrawardī describes this person as “God’s vicegerent on earth.”[^V3-323]

Does “vicegerent” mean prophet in Suhrawardī’s terminology?

Certainly not.

He repeatedly uses this term with Illuminationist meanings and symbols that have nothing to do with the Islamic meaning of prophecy.

The human soul—the regent soul—is the vicegerent of the “Highest Light” in the earthly world. It is the “greater vicegerent.”

Fire is also a “vicegerent,” though only the “lesser vicegerent.”[^V3-324]

Suhrawardī removes any remaining possibility that the phrase “God’s vicegerent on earth” must mean the prophet.

Elsewhere in the same book, he uses the term in a passage where prophecy is clearly not what he means.

Speaking about fire, he writes:

> “Fire has the lesser vicegerency on earth, just as perfect souls have the greater vicegerency. God has a vicegerent in every world: the First Intellect in the world of intellects; the stars and their souls in the world of the heavenly spheres; and likewise in the imaginal world; human souls and stellar rays in the world of the elements; and fire as well, especially in the darkness of night.”[^V3-325]

Where is the prophet in this passage?

Suhrawardī makes the meaning still clearer when he explains what he means by a “vicegerent”:

> “The meaning of vicegerency is to take charge of those under one’s care by preserving and setting them right. This world is governed through souls. Fire has the lesser vicegerency because, on dark nights, it takes the place of the rays of the stars, makes food fit for use, and cooks what is raw. Its vicegerency is lesser because the immaterial light that governs the human being—the rational soul—acts upon fire, or upon its accidental light. Fire is therefore like an instrument of the human being. Through these two—fire and the soul—the two forms of vicegerency are fulfilled...”[^V3-326]

After all this, how can the phrase “God’s vicegerent on earth” be taken to mean the prophet, or even be thought likely to mean him?

If we insist on interpreting the phrase as referring to a human person, another passage in the same source suggests that Suhrawardī means the **Imam**, in something like the Shīʿi or Ismāʿīli sense—though perhaps his meaning is even closer to the Sufi one.

In this passage, he explicitly says that this Imam “is what all the Sufis call the Pole.”[^V3-327]

---

After all this, we can add one final point that is decisive for the question.

We return again to Suhrawardī’s *The Cry of the Simurgh* and its description of the different ranks of the “knowers.”[^V3-328]

At the end of this sequence, those who reach “the station of the foremost prophets and sages” arrive at a state in which, Suhrawardī says:

> “The words ‘He,’ ‘you,’ and ‘I’ disappear for them into the sea of annihilation. There, commands and prohibitions fall away.”[^V3-329]

In other words, the obligations of religious law no longer apply to them.

This is the decisive point we referred to.

The idea that religious law falls away for those who reach the highest stages of mystical experience helps define the philosophical and ideological position of mysticism in general, and Illuminationist philosophy in particular.

Ultimately, it also defines their basic view of prophecy, revelation, and religious law.

For Muruwwa, this view was fundamentally a theoretical expression of opposition to the existing social order.

For this reason, we cannot simply follow those historians who claim that Suhrawardī was killed because the ruling authorities gave in to pressure from Sunni jurists.

The logic of the situation suggests something different.

Suhrawardī’s trial and execution were more likely the result of a decision made by the political authorities themselves. The pressure from the jurists was then an expression of that decision, rather than its true cause.[^V3-330]

#### 9 - The Eternity of the World

We have pointed out more than once that idealist philosophies are rarely completely consistent.

The same was also true of materialist philosophies before Marxism.

This is why we rarely find a perfectly pure idealist philosophy. Many idealist systems contain some materialist elements.

There is a real basis for this.

The objective existence of matter forces itself upon thought, however much idealist thinkers may try to escape that reality, consciously or unconsciously.

This is true of Illuminationist philosophy and of the different sources from which its ideas were drawn.

In everything we have seen so far, Suhrawardī’s Illuminationism is idealist in its general direction because the basic principles on which it is built are idealist.

Yet the contradictions inside a philosophy of this kind can sometimes pull it, almost without intention, toward materialist positions.

We now have one example of this.

We find similar examples in Plato, Aristotle, and Neoplatonism. We will mention some of them later when we compare al-Kindī’s view of the beginning and fate of the world with the views of the ancient philosophers.

The question of whether the world has always existed is, in itself, a materialist question.

To say that the world is eternal means that matter is eternal, and this rejects the idea of creation.

So any philosophy that says the world has always existed is, on this particular question, taking an objectively materialist position—even if the claim appears within an idealist system or grows out of other ideas that are themselves idealist.

But it remains only a **partial** materialist position. It does not necessarily reflect the character of the philosophy as a whole.

This is what happens in Suhrawardī’s Illuminationist philosophy.

What is especially important, however, is that Suhrawardī seems to have understood his own position on this question.

He did not leave it for us to discover indirectly.

He stated it himself and gave it a philosophical foundation: the principle that reason requires a necessary connection between a cause and its effect.[^V3-331]

Let us look at how he develops this argument.

Suhrawardī begins by defining what he means by “the world.”

The world, he says, is everything other than God.

He then divides everything other than God into two groups: the eternal and the originated.

The eternal part includes the intellects, the heavenly spheres, the souls of the spheres, the universal forms of the elements, and the things that necessarily accompany them—eternal movement and time.

The originated part includes everything else.

This means the elements themselves and all individual beings: the particular things that make up the material world.

But the origination of this second part is not absolute.

It is only relative.

Individual material things are “originated” in relation to the first part of the world because that first part stands “above” them in Suhrawardī’s Illuminationist hierarchy.

Their origination is therefore only relative.

In the final analysis, they too are eternal, because they depend on the “higher” world and belong to the system of eternal Illuminationist lights.

As long as the flow of these lights has no beginning and no end, anything that receives even the smallest share of that flow must also exist without beginning or end.[^V3-332]

Using the language of modern dialectical materialism, we might say that Suhrawardī’s idea of the **relative origination** of the elements,[^V3-333] even though he ultimately regarded them as eternal, may have been a historical way of expressing their constant change and becoming.

Perhaps, through a kind of spontaneous philosophical insight, he arrived at the idea of “relative origination” by noticing the changing states through which the material world continually passes—even though he still concluded that the world itself was eternal because the “higher” world was eternal.

Can we therefore say that Suhrawardī came close to this truth spontaneously, in a simple or unclear form, as material reality made itself felt within philosophical consciousness?

There is also another problem.

Does his claim that the elements are at once originated and eternal not contradict his general position on whether they truly exist at all?

After all, he denies that they are objectively existing substances. He treats them instead as things whose existence is only conceptual or apparent.

To speak of something as beginning to exist, or as existing eternally, already implies some recognition that it objectively exists.

So how does Suhrawardī solve this contradiction?

Whatever the answer, the important point here is that Suhrawardī ultimately describes the world in both its parts—the heavenly and the earthly—as eternal, without beginning or end.

As we have said, this position is materialist in itself.

That remains true even though, first, it is only a partial position and does not change the generally idealist character of his Illuminationist philosophy.

It also remains true whether, second, Suhrawardī consciously understood its materialist meaning or was simply led to it by the logic of the principle on which he based the eternity of the world: the law of causality.

Still, it is clear that he understood the most important consequence of saying that the world is eternal.

It means rejecting the idea of “creation”—or, at the very least, rejecting the theological doctrine that the world was created out of nothing.

Suhrawardī says this directly:

> “Nothing can come from the Light of Lights and the dominating lights after previously not existing...”[^V3-334]

#### 10 - The Eternity of Movement and Time

Suhrawardī supported his belief in the eternity of the world with a belief in the eternity of movement and time.

In doing so, he moved one step closer to materialism.

The importance of this position is not simply that he regarded movement and time themselves as eternal.

More important is the way he connected them.

Suhrawardī saw an inseparable connection between the world and movement, and then another inseparable connection between movement and time.

There is no doubt that this way of thinking had earlier roots in the ideas of the ancient Greek philosophers.

But there was also a second and more immediate source: the progress made in the natural sciences in the Arab world during the four centuries before Suhrawardī.

This was especially true in astronomy, mechanics, and physics, where practical and experimental methods had increasingly become part of scientific investigation.

Islamic philosophers and scientists had reached important results by combining theoretical thought with practical experiment. It was natural that some of those results would eventually leave their mark even on the writings of mystical and Illuminationist philosophy.

Arab knowledge of the movement of the heavenly bodies, mechanical motion, and the many other forms of motion studied in connection with climate, sound, light, heat, and other subjects[^V3-335] could hardly have remained outside the intellectual world of a major thinker like Suhrawardī, who lived in the sixth Islamic century, the twelfth century CE.

Once this knowledge had become part of his intellectual formation, it was bound to affect the picture of the world that he developed, however far that mystical Illuminationist picture moved away from material reality.

We can see another sign of this influence in Suhrawardī’s strict commitment to causality.

He built his Illuminationist system on the idea that all things in existence are connected through cause and effect, and he treated this relationship as a necessary law of the world.

This commitment reflects, in another form, the results that had grown out of the meeting between philosophy and science—both theoretical and experimental—in Arab-Islamic society during the “golden age” of Arab intellectual life.

We saw in the previous section how Suhrawardī’s strict commitment to causality led him to accept the eternity of all existence, including material existence.

This happened even though it contradicted the overall direction of his idealist philosophy, which denied that the material world had true existence.

The same way of thinking also led him to see movement as something inseparable from the existence of the world itself—a necessary part of that existence.[^V3-336]

If the world is eternal, then movement must also be eternal.

And because Suhrawardī defines time as “the measure of movement,” time too must be eternal, with no beginning and no end.[^V3-337]

When we look at movement, Suhrawardī’s whole system—both the higher world and the earthly one—appears as a world in constant motion.

Every part of it moves continuously.

There is upward movement and downward movement, direct vision and illumination. Both direct vision and illumination operate through movement. In fact, each is itself a form of movement.

If this movement stopped, the whole system would break down both as a system of existence and as a system of knowledge.

In the elemental world especially, movement and heat go together.

This is why Suhrawardī says that fire possesses “greater movement and more complete heat than the other elements.”[^V3-338]

This constant movement is the backbone of the entire Illuminationist structure.

From one point of view, it reflects the movement of material reality itself.

But it reflects it in a form that makes movement appear to come from outside material reality.

In Suhrawardī’s system, movement does not arise from within things themselves. It comes to them through the successive radiance of the intellectual lights, whose first source is the “Light of Lights”—God.

Even so, we can say that the **content** of this picture comes from reality, while its **form** is metaphysical.

It can therefore also be understood as a real awareness of the inseparable connection between existence and movement, expressed through the language of mystical Illuminationism.

The sharp contradiction between the content of this insight and the philosophical form in which it appears has, at its deepest level, a social explanation.

It belongs to a wider set of intellectual developments that reflected, in their own complicated ways, the growing social and political contradictions within Arab-Islamic society in Suhrawardī’s time.

We have already discussed the historical conditions that brought these contradictions to maturity.[^V3-339]

Overall, Suhrawardī’s position can be understood in the following way.

When he imagined his Illuminationist system as a world of movement, he was working from the picture that the real world and its movement had left on his philosophical consciousness.

Movement is the form through which that real world exists.

For this reason, movement also became essential to Suhrawardī’s Illuminationist system. The system cannot exist without movement, and it cannot continue unless movement continues.

As he puts it:

> “The continual renewal of the illuminations depends on the continual renewal of movements, and the continual renewal of movements depends on the continual renewal of the illuminations.”[^V3-340]

On the social side, there is an internal contradiction between the system’s supernatural, heavenly Illuminationist structure and the material reality beneath it.

Directly or indirectly, this reflected the contradiction within society itself.

It also reflected the weakening of existing social and intellectual values as the contradictions within that society entered a severe crisis.

### C - The Material Basis of the Idea of “Intermediaries” in Islamic Mysticism

#### 1 - A Common Pattern

The picture we have examined at length in Illuminationist mysticism first appeared, in the form we described, among the Ismāʿīlīs.

We then found it in the Illuminationist philosophy that reached its most complete form in Suhrawardī’s system.

But some of its features, and other closely related ones, also appeared separately in many other schools and groups within the wider Islamic world, especially among those usually described as the **ghulāt**, or “extremists.”

One pattern became increasingly visible as the fourth Islamic century—the tenth century CE—advanced, and then became even more pronounced during the fifth Islamic century, the eleventh century, and after.

This was the growing number of celestial and “spiritual” intermediaries placed between human beings and God.

It also involved the idea that “divine lights” could pour into particular human beings and raise them to the rank of Imam or prophet—and sometimes even to divinity itself.

Was this development simply an internal process within mysticism?

Did it arise from the inner dynamics of Sufi or “gnostic” thought itself?

Or was it caused by mystical and Illuminationist philosophical currents entering Islamic thought from different parts of the world—Persia, India, Greece, and the Alexandrian school?

In other words, did all this happen independently of the changing social reality of Arab-Islamic society?

#### 2 - What Lay Behind the Pattern

To accept such an explanation would mean surrendering to idealist speculation without any foundation in objective reality or scientific analysis.

A scientific study points us in a different direction.

It shows that this phenomenon was, in its own special intellectual form, a historical expression of the broader social and political changes that had been developing since the end of the early Abbasid period.

We call it a **historical** expression because its particular form was tied to the time and society in which those changes occurred.

That is why a real social process could appear in a supernatural form.

The form was supernatural, but what it expressed was still rooted in reality.

So what was that reality? What were the changes taking place?

We saw earlier how philosophical Islamic mysticism began by rejecting the official “theory” that tied the relationship between human beings and God to religious law.

In its place, mysticism offered the idea of a direct relationship with God, without any intermediary.

We also saw that this expressed a rejection of the ideological content of the official theory.

That meant rejecting a social order built on complete separation between the ruling authority and the people under its rule.

At that earlier stage, philosophical mysticism took this form while the ruling social order was still highly centralized around the caliphate and the caliph.

The intellectual and ideological challenge to the absolute separation between ruler and ruled therefore took the form of rejecting theological mediation altogether.

The rejection of mediation became the theoretical expression of opposition to the separation itself.

But things began to change from the time of the caliph al-Mutawakkil onward.

Central authority began to break apart.

More and more political “intermediaries” appeared between the center of government and society.

Power was increasingly divided among princes, military commanders, and the holders of independent landed estates in different parts of the empire.

At the same time, the productive strength of the countryside weakened, while merchant and artisan groups grew in the particular historical forms found in that region.

All these changes increasingly exposed the instability and weakness of the existing social order.

Conflict became sharper in two different ways: as **class conflict** and as conflict between different groups within the ruling order.

The first was the basic conflict between those who controlled the system and the people they ruled.

The second was the conflict among the different political and landed groups within the ruling classes themselves.

These groups became more and more divided as independent regional states multiplied and broke away from the political center. They often fought one another violently.

#### 3 - The Revolutionary Core of Mystical Thought

These major changes were reflected in different ways in the forms taken by intellectual life.

It seems to us that their effect appeared especially quickly and clearly in mystical thought.

It is not unreasonable to say that, during this historical period, mystical thought was the most revolutionary current of thought, despite its idealist and supernatural character.

What made it revolutionary was the ideological meaning it carried at the time.

It rejected the theological ideas that gave theoretical support to the existing social system.

There is no need to explain this point again after everything we have already said about it.

But these social changes also seem to have produced a remarkable change inside mystical thought itself.

Earlier mysticism had insisted on a direct relationship with God and rejected the idea of “intermediaries.”

Now it began to accept many intermediaries.

Illuminationist thought marked the high point of this transformation.

As we have seen, it constructed an entire world crowded with Illuminationist intermediaries between human beings and the “Light of Lights.”

This is a world full of life and movement, joining earth to heaven and heaven to earth through a ladder of rising and descending levels.

Celestial “intellects” and human “souls” move among these levels until, in a sense, the heavens become humanized and the earthly world becomes divine.

This Illuminationist world tries to cover all its beings—even its eternal movement—with a metaphysical veil decorated with “divine” radiance.

But its real face, and especially its real social content, could not completely disappear behind that brilliant veil.

We find this change in philosophical mysticism closely connected to the historical changes just described: changes in the political structure of the ruling social order and in the structure of that order itself.

The point can be summarized like this.

Earlier mystical thought had insisted on a direct relationship with God as a form of opposition to the social order at a time when political power was strongly and absolutely centralized.

But once that central authority broke apart and political power was divided among many rulers, the form of opposition also changed.

Rejection and resistance were no longer directed at a single political authority.

They were now directed at several centers of power and at several groups within the same ruling class.

All of these groups, whether through force or negotiation, divided the benefits of the system among themselves at the expense of the exploited and oppressed working population.

The many levels in the Illuminationist chain, standing as intermediaries between earth and heaven, were the intellectual and “utopian” image of the kind of world dreamed of by the revolutionary thinkers of that age.

It was a brighter, more “luminous” world set against the darker powers of the real one.

In this sense, “light” and “darkness” in Illuminationist philosophy become abstract images of two opposing worlds.

One is the world of “spiritual illumination,” the world of the utopian dream.

The other is the concrete world of social reality, a world of oppression and injustice.

Light and darkness are therefore, in the final analysis, symbols: light for happiness in the Illuminationist world, and darkness for misery in the earthly one.

This was the new form taken by rejection and resistance in Illuminationist mystical thought.

And this, for Muruwwa, is the real historical background to Suhrawardī’s tragedy and death.

His execution followed a dramatic but largely formal religious trial through which the political ruler hid his own face behind the “mask of the jurists”—jurists who were carrying out orders, not giving them.

#### 4 - Revolutionary... but “Dead”

Yet this revolutionary spirit of rejection and resistance remained trapped within an impossible world of its own.

It carried within itself the proof of its own limitations.

It could not turn into a real and effective revolutionary force—a material power capable of putting its “revolutionary” rejection into practice.

The weakness was built into both the theory and the mystical experience itself.

It made no difference whether the relationship between human beings and God was imagined as direct “unveiling,” with no intermediary, or as a chain of illuminations passing through many intermediaries.

We have already explained the source of this internal weakness.[^V3-341]

It meant that the revolutionary impulse of mystical thought was, in this sense, **born dead**.

Even so, one historical fact remains beyond doubt.

In the history of social conflict within Arab-Islamic society, mystical thought alone carried the clearest sign of open resistance and rebellion against the official theological ideology.

Rationalist philosophy could rebel against that ideology only partially, cautiously, and often ambiguously.

It remained tied to theology by many visible and invisible threads.

Yet none of this ever protected the rationalist philosophers from persecution and repression by the defenders of that same theology.

# Chapter Two: Sufism

## “Unity of Being” and Ibn ʿArabī’s Doctrine

### 1 — The Term and Its History

In the history of Islamic Sufism, the idea of **“Unity of Being”** is closely associated with Muḥyī al-Dīn Ibn ʿArabī, one of the most famous Arab Sufis and one of the most influential figures in philosophical Sufism during the later Middle Ages.[^V3-342] The idea became associated with his name both as a term and as a doctrine.

The expression “Unity of Being” had appeared in Sufi teachings before Ibn ʿArabī, but it was only with him that it became an established Sufi term for a particular way of understanding God’s oneness. Ibn ʿArabī also gave the term a new meaning. We can see his theory as a new attempt by Islamic Sufis to deal with a problem that had long confronted them: how could God possess absolute unity while at the same time not being separate from the world?

It was a new attempt, and a bold one. Its boldness may be one reason it had such a powerful influence on Islamic Sufi literature from Ibn ʿArabī’s time down to much later periods.

Why was it bold?

Before Ibn ʿArabī, Sufi thinkers had always approached the idea of Unity of Being with extreme caution. At times they even rejected it.[^V3-343] This caution may have come from the practice of protective concealment, or *taqiyya*. It may have come from the influence of theological ideas. Or it may simply have been because the idea itself had not yet developed into a clear philosophical Sufi doctrine of the kind we later find in Ibn ʿArabī.

Most likely, all these factors played a part.

But perhaps the strongest reason was the sharp confrontation that emerged—or reached its height—in the age of Ibn Rushd and Ibn ʿArabī between the two main currents of Arab-Islamic thought.

One was the rationalist current, which in Ibn Rushd finally broke completely with Sufi tendencies.

The other was the intuitive Sufi current, which in Ibn ʿArabī made mystical “vision” the basis of knowledge.[^V3-344]

As we will soon see, the highly abstract form that the idea of Unity of Being took in Ibn ʿArabī’s doctrine may have been one of the clearest expressions of this confrontation between the two currents.

### 2 — The Meaning of “Unity of Being”

In our own age—the age of dialectical materialism—we naturally understand “Unity of Being” to mean the unity of material existence, or the material unity of the world. The real unity of the world, as Engels put it, lies in its material nature.[^V3-345]

Matter in motion is the one common source of everything in the world, however different the forms of matter and motion may be. This applies to nature as well as to society. Everything is connected through this single, all-embracing material source.

All these things exist objectively, outside and independently of our consciousness and sensations. Our consciousness and sensations, by contrast, do not exist outside this material unity. Human consciousness is itself a property of matter—one that has reached an extraordinarily complex and highly developed form within a material world whose variety is almost endlessly rich.

Idealist philosophies understand “Unity of Being” differently.

Different idealist philosophies, in different periods, have imagined this other kind of reality in different ways. It may be a world of “spirits”; a world of “Forms,” as in Plato; the world of the “Universal Intellect,” as in Plotinus; the world of Suhrawardī’s “lights”; the divine realm of theology; Hegel’s world of the “Absolute Idea”; and so on.

As for the material world in which we actually live, some idealist philosophies accept that it exists alongside one of these metaphysical worlds. Others deny that it has any real existence at all and place true existence entirely beyond it.

This produces different idealist versions of Unity of Being.

For thinkers who grant the material world some kind of objective existence, Unity of Being may mean that the metaphysical world and the material world are joined together through union, indwelling, or something similar. This is the position of objective idealism.

For others, there is only one true existence, and what we call the real world is no more than a shadow of a higher, metaphysical reality: God, the Forms, the Universal Intellect, the Idea, and so on. This is the position of subjective idealism.[^V3-346]

Materialist philosophies before dialectical materialism also agreed that nothing exists outside the material world. But they did not all understand the world’s unity in the same way.

Some held that the world is one because it is the only world we can conceive as existing.[^V3-347] In other words, its unity was determined by consciousness rather than by its objective existence or material nature.

Others held that the simple fact that this world alone exists is what makes it one.[^V3-347]

Dialectical materialism then went further. It argued that the basis of the world’s unity is not only that the world exists, but that it is **material**. The discoveries of the natural sciences, it says, continue to confirm this understanding of the unity of the world.

With Ibn ʿArabī, however, we are dealing with an idealist understanding of Unity of Being, of the kind that developed within Islamic Sufi thought.

The basic direction of Sufi thought is idealist. It imagines a metaphysical realm whose source—and, for those who accept other forms of existence, the source of all existence—is a supreme power absolutely beyond matter, motion, time, place, quality, and quantity.

We saw earlier that the different Sufi schools within Islam remained tied to the doctrine of God’s oneness that lies at the heart of Islamic metaphysics. They could not, or did not dare to, reject it openly.

Yet philosophically and ideologically, they moved away from it indirectly.

We also saw how this indirect break trapped them in a contradiction. It threatened to place them either completely outside Islam or outside the Sufi experience itself—whether we look at what that experience says about being and knowledge, or at its role as an oppositional ideology.

They insisted on holding two things together at once: the **absolute unity** of the divine essence and the possibility of **mystical contact** with that same divine essence.

But this attempt ran into an impossibility. It preserved neither the traditional doctrine of divine unity nor the full benefit of breaking with the ideology built around that doctrine.

Once we understand this dilemma, we can better understand why Ibn ʿArabī moved toward a new concept of divine unity that tried to solve the problem while remaining within the same Sufi and metaphysical framework.

That concept became his theory of **Unity of Being**.

Here we are analyzing the problem as it appears to scientific study. It does not matter whether Ibn ʿArabī consciously intended to solve the problem in exactly this way, or whether such a thought never entered his mind. His intention is not what determines our understanding.

What matters is what his texts actually say, and the direct or indirect meanings they contain.

So what does Ibn ʿArabī say about Unity of Being?

We will begin with his idea of **absolute unity**, or absolute divine transcendence. We will then ask how, in his theory, this absolute unity is related to the world.

Perhaps Ibn ʿArabī’s clearest statement of absolute unity appears in the *Treatise on Oneness*.[^V3-348] There he writes:

> “…He [God] is not in anything, nor is anything in Him, whether within or outside (…) His veil is His oneness, and nothing veils Him except Himself. His veil is the concealment of His existence within His oneness, without any manner or form. No one sees Him except Himself—not a messenger-prophet, not a perfected saint, not an angel brought near. His prophet is Himself, His sending is Himself, and His word is Himself. He sent Himself, by Himself, to Himself.”

Here the idea of divine transcendence becomes even more radically abstract.

So Ibn ʿArabī still accepts the same basic principle of absolute divine unity that earlier Sufis accepted. But he pushes it further than they did, into an even more extreme level of abstraction.

When we move from this text to his *Fuṣūṣ al-Ḥikam*, where he develops his theory of Unity of Being, we find that he still wants this principle to remain the foundation of the theory. He tries to preserve God’s absolute and abstract transcendence while at the same time insisting that God is not separate from the world.

This makes the contradiction even sharper.

Ibn ʿArabī therefore turns to another impossible move in order to deal with the original impossibility. He separates the divine **essence** from God’s **Most Beautiful Names**. In this way, the essence can remain absolutely transcendent, while the divine names become the point of contact with the world—the side through which Unity of Being is established.

This is where the theory begins.

Ibn ʿArabī writes:

> “When the Real, glory be to Him, willed—in respect of His Most Beautiful Names, which cannot be counted—to see their concrete forms, or, if you wish, to see His own self, in an all-encompassing being that contained the whole matter because it was endowed with existence, and through it to reveal His secret to Himself—for a thing’s seeing itself by itself is not the same as seeing itself in another thing that serves as a mirror (…) the Real, glory be to Him, had brought the whole world into existence as a shaped form without spirit. It was like an unpolished mirror.
>
> “Divine wisdom requires that whenever He prepares a form, that form must be capable of receiving a divine spirit, which is described as His breathing into it.[^V3-349] This means nothing other than that the prepared form becomes ready to receive the everlasting outpouring of divine self-disclosure, which has never ceased and never will.
>
> “All that remained was something capable of receiving it, and whatever receives can only come from His most holy outpouring. So the whole affair is from Him, from beginning to end, and ‘to Him the whole affair returns,’[^V3-350] just as it began from Him.
>
> “The matter therefore required the polishing of the mirror of the world. Adam became the very polishing of that mirror and the spirit of that form…”[^V3-351]

This passage gives us the basic outline of Ibn ʿArabī’s theory of Unity of Being.

If we take these ideas out of Ibn ʿArabī’s very compressed language and restate them more simply, we can read the passage as a whole in light of its title: **“Divine Wisdom in the Word of Adam.”**

So let us begin with that title.

God willed that His wisdom should reveal itself in a “Word,” and that Word was the human being.[^V3-352]

The human being is therefore the basis of the world’s existence and the link between God and the world.

But this self-disclosure does not come directly from God’s **essence**, because His essence remains absolutely transcendent and beyond the world. Instead, the source of God’s self-disclosure in the universe is His countless names.

God wanted to see the names He had given Himself embodied as concrete realities within existence. In that sense, He wanted to see Himself within them—to see His own secret revealed to Himself through their concrete existence.

For God’s seeing Himself directly is not the same as seeing Himself reflected in the mirror of existence.

So God brought the world into being through His names rather than through His essence.

At first, however, He produced it as a lifeless form: like a dull, unpolished mirror that could not yet reflect the images before it.

But the world in this lifeless state already contained the readiness and capacity to receive spirit when God breathed His spirit into it. In other words, it was ready to receive the divine outpouring: the eternal and never-ending self-disclosure of God.

When that self-disclosure took place, the human being appeared.

The human being became the clear and polished mirror of the world. In this mirror, God’s Most Beautiful Names appeared in concrete form, and God saw Himself reflected in them.

The human being became the spirit of the divine image.

The original lifeless form, capable of receiving the divine outpouring, came from God. The same form after receiving that outpouring and the breath of spirit also came from God.

Everything, then, is from Him: its beginning and its end. And “to Him the whole affair returns.”

---

What can we draw from this reading?

We should begin with the direct conclusions offered by the text itself. We can then use them as the basis for examining its less direct implications.

First, the text tells us that all of existence, beginning with the human being, is nothing more than a particular form of God’s existence.

There are not, in other words, two separate existences: one belonging to God and another belonging to the world.

There is only one existence and one reality: the existence and reality of God.

Everything else is simply a manifestation of that one reality.

Second, the relation between God and the world is ultimately the relation between God’s essence and His names.

The concrete existence of individual things, just like their existence as intelligible possibilities—their lifeless forms before receiving full self-disclosure—is simply the concrete existence of these divine names in the world, and especially in the human being.

It is nothing else.

Third, this dependent existence of the world—and above all of the human being—is something God requires as an end or purpose.

God’s Most Beautiful Names need this existence in order to appear concretely, so that God may see Himself in them and, through seeing them, reveal His own secret to Himself.

The world’s existence is therefore necessary. It is not passing or accidental.

Fourth, if the world exists so that God may know Himself through something other than Himself, then the world must also be eternal.

The world, therefore, was not created in the sense of being brought into existence from nothing.

One basic theological principle thus collapses within Ibn ʿArabī’s theory of Unity of Being, together with the related doctrine that the world began to exist at a particular moment.

Fifth, we can see Ibn ʿArabī’s view of the human being.

As we saw in our reading of the passage, the human being is the basis of the world’s existence and the point that connects the world with God.

Ibn ʿArabī emphasizes this idea and develops it further immediately after the passage quoted above. He explains why the human being stands above all other beings, beginning with the angels.

We should recall his statement:

> “…The matter therefore required the polishing of the mirror of the world. Adam”—that is, the human being—“became the very polishing of that mirror and the spirit of that form.”

He immediately adds:[^V3-353]

> “The angels were some of the powers of that form, which is the form of the world and which the people of this path”—that is, the Sufis—“call the Greater Man.”[^V3-354]

He then describes the human being as God’s **vicegerent**, meaning the representative of divinity in the world.

The human being, he says, was called *insān* because in relation to God he is like the pupil of the eye, through which sight takes place:

> “…Through him the Real looks upon His creation and shows them mercy.”

That is, through the human being God grants creation existence.[^V3-355]

The human being holds this extraordinary position not only because humanity is the purpose of creation, but also because “the world depends on his existence.”

God, Ibn ʿArabī says, “preserves His creation through him just as a seal protects a treasury.”

The continued existence of the world therefore depends on the continued presence of the human being within it:

> “The world will remain preserved for as long as this Perfect Man remains within it.”

The human being is “perfect” because he is “the decisive and all-embracing Word.” In human existence, “all the names contained in the divine image” appear together.

This is also why the human being stands above the angels. The angels do not possess what Ibn ʿArabī calls “the comprehensiveness of Adam”: they do not gather within themselves all the manifestations of the divine image.

Humanity alone reaches this level of comprehensiveness and gathers the whole of existence within itself.

### 3 — The “Perfect Man”

The term **“Perfect Man”**, however, also has a more specific meaning in Ibn ʿArabī’s theory. He gives this idea special attention in both *Fuṣūṣ al-Ḥikam* and *The Tree of Being*.

The basic idea is that individual human beings differ in their degrees of “perfection.” These degrees rise until they reach a level close to the divine.

The **Perfect Man** is the person who reaches that level.

But how does a person reach it?

The answer depends on which side of the idea Ibn ʿArabī is considering. There are three.

The first concerns the human being as a form of existence.

Here Ibn ʿArabī is speaking about the archetype of humanity that he described earlier as “the decisive and all-embracing Word,” whose human form contains “all the names contained in the divine image.”

From this point of view, the Perfect Man is the purely “divine” side of the human being—or, looked at the other way around, the existentially “human” side of God.

The second concerns the human being as the link between God and the world.

Here, the Perfect Man means the prophets.

But Ibn ʿArabī understands “prophethood” differently from traditional theology.

In his theory of the Perfect Man, the prophet is a form taken by God’s eternal self-disclosure. The idea is no longer tied simply to the individual human prophet as Islam normally understands him.

Instead, an eternal “divine reality,” hidden from eternity, reveals itself on earth from time to time in the form of a prophet, until it reaches its fullest manifestation in the **Muḥammadan Reality**, or the **Muḥammadan Word**.[^V3-356]

According to this view, the Prophet Muḥammad, as a “Word” of God, existed from eternity:

> “He was a prophet while Adam was still between water and clay.”[^V3-357]

The third way of looking at the Perfect Man concerns **knowledge**.

Here the Perfect Man is the person who becomes able to recognize his own “inner” humanity—that is, to understand humanity as the form in which God appears within existence.

Such a person understands that humanity brings together, on one side, the universal qualities of the world and, on the other, the divine qualities.
