For that reason, the human being stands at the center of the world and at the root of existence. Humanity is the **microcosm**, the “small world” that contains in summary all the possibilities of the greater world, or the “Greater Man.”

Anyone who reaches this understanding becomes the Perfect Man.

And by understanding himself in this way, he reaches the highest knowledge of God.

This is how Ibn ʿArabī understood the saying attributed to the Prophet Muḥammad:

> “Whoever knows himself knows his Lord.”[^V3-358]

From this third point of view—the point of view of knowledge—the Perfect Man is a possibility open to every human being.

Every person is, in principle, capable of reaching the level of the Perfect Man.

In actual reality, however, Ibn ʿArabī believes that only the prophets and the great Sufi masters, the spiritual “poles,” have achieved it.[^V3-359]

### 4 — The Human Being in Actual Life

But where does the human being as an actual, concrete person fit into this theory?

For now, we will continue to read Ibn ʿArabī’s texts directly, as we did in the previous two sections. We will return to the same question later when we move to our own conclusions.[^V3-360]

Under the theory of Unity of Being, there appears to be no basic difference between an ordinary person and the Perfect Man except their different degrees of “perfection.”

As we have just seen, every human being is potentially “perfect,” even though Ibn ʿArabī—and later al-Jīlī[^V3-361]—believed that only the prophets and the great Sufi masters had actually reached this level.

The passages in *Fuṣūṣ al-Ḥikam* that lay out the theory of Unity of Being place the idea of the Perfect Man within a broader discussion of humanity as a whole.

From the beginning, these passages speak of God’s self-disclosure in Adam, who “became the very polishing of the mirror of the world.” Adam here is a symbol of humanity in general, not of one special group of people.

The same passages explain why the human being is called both “human” and God’s “vicegerent”:

> “…As for his humanity, it comes from the universality of his constitution and the fact that he contains all realities. In relation to the Real [God], he is like the pupil of the eye, through which sight takes place… For this reason he was called human.”

The texts then describe humanity as:

> “the created and eternal human being, the ever-renewed and everlasting formation, and the decisive and all-embracing Word.”

All of this is said about humanity in general.

Every individual human being stands in the same relation to this universal humanity, and universal humanity stands in the same relation to every individual person.

Any statement made here about “the human being” is therefore general and unrestricted. It is not limited to a particular class or individual.

We can therefore conclude that the **real, individual human being** is included in these statements.

Even when the term “Perfect Man” appears in the same discussion, it first appears as part of this general description of humanity.

The more specific idea of the Perfect Man that we explained in the previous section appears elsewhere in *Fuṣūṣ al-Ḥikam* and throughout *The Tree of Being*.

But even this more specific form of the idea applies, in theory, to every person as a possibility. In practice, it applies only to those who manage to reach a very high degree of “perfection.”

This helps us understand Ibn ʿArabī’s earlier description of the human being as **“created and eternal, ever-renewed and everlasting.”**

The description brings together opposites: createdness, eternity, and endless renewal—that is, change and becoming.

And it applies not only to the prophet and the Sufi “pole,” but also to the human being who exists in ordinary reality.

### 5 — The Universal and the Particular

The relation between the universal and the particular is extremely complicated in Ibn ʿArabī’s texts, especially in the first chapter of *Fuṣūṣ al-Ḥikam*.

On one side, Ibn ʿArabī does not accept that **universals** exist concretely in individual things.

He limits their existence to the intelligible realm:

> “They are intelligible and are undoubtedly known in the mind.”

They therefore remain inward or hidden rather than existing as separate concrete things.

Yet he also says that universals have “authority and effect in everything that possesses concrete existence,” and that this authority and effect is nothing other than the concrete things themselves.

In other words, concrete, sensory things are produced through universals.

Here Ibn ʿArabī takes a clear position on one of the basic questions of philosophy: the relation between reality and consciousness.

His position here is openly idealist, and indeed subjectively idealist.

In the way he presents the matter, consciousness does not merely come before reality. Consciousness also **produces** reality.

But then, on the other side, he says that:

> “Universal things are outwardly manifest in the concrete realities of existing things, just as they are inward in their intelligible form.”

Taken by itself, this is a much more realistic way of understanding the relation between the universal and the particular.

Yet Ibn ʿArabī immediately returns to his earlier, subjectively idealist position when he says:

> “Every concrete existing thing therefore depends upon these universal things…”

He continues along this path, but eventually reaches a point where he comes close to recognizing a **two-way dialectical influence** between reality and consciousness, and between the universal and the particular.

He writes:

> “…Yet the universal itself receives a determination back from concrete existing things, according to what the realities of those concrete things require.”

In other words, concrete things now begin to affect the universals in return.

It is true that Ibn ʿArabī uses this realistic insight in a context that leads us into another contradiction in his thought: the claim that the human being—and therefore the world—is created in time.

Even so, the insight itself remains realistic and historically important. It shows an intellectual attempt to understand the dialectical relation between the universal and the concrete particular—the individual.

This becomes clearer when Ibn ʿArabī applies the same idea to the relation between **knowledge and life**, on the one hand, and **the knower and the living being**, on the other.

He says that knowledge and life are both intelligible realities.

When knowledge or life is attributed to the eternal knower or the eternally living being—that is, to God—it takes on the quality of eternity. Knowledge becomes eternal and life becomes eternal because of this relation.

But when knowledge or life is attributed to the human being, who is created and not eternal, it takes on the quality of createdness. Knowledge becomes created and life becomes created because of this different relation.

Here, then, Ibn ʿArabī is describing the influence of concrete beings on intelligible realities—the universals.

Yet elsewhere, as we have already seen, he also describes the influence of those universals on concrete beings.

From Ibn ʿArabī’s treatment of the universal and the particular within his theory of Unity of Being, we can therefore reach the following conclusion.

His general position is clearly one of subjective idealism.

But we should not ignore the more realistic insight that briefly appears within it: an awareness of the dialectical relation between the universal and the particular.

The problem is that this insight appears only faintly, through the thick fog of his metaphysics. It never manages to reach the real nature of the relation.

There is little doubt that Ibn ʿArabī’s decision to construct the whole idea of Unity of Being on a purely metaphysical basis prevented him from reaching the real dialectic between the universal and the individual, even after he had come so close to recognizing it.

### 6 — Drawing Further Conclusions from Ibn ʿArabī

Now that we have reconstructed the basic outline of Ibn ʿArabī’s theory of Unity of Being, we can return to it with a more analytical reading and draw out some of its wider implications.

We will focus on three aspects.

#### First Aspect: The Contradiction Within the Sufi Idea of Divine Unity

At first, Ibn ʿArabī seems to move one step toward solving this problem—within the Sufi idealist framework, of course.

He appears to have thought that the contradiction arose because the problem contained two separate sides: God and the world.

Ibn ʿArabī removes the second side.

The world, in the fullest sense, does not really exist. Only God truly exists.

So there are no longer two separate existences whose relation we need to explain.

We no longer need to ask whether God and the world are completely separate or somehow connected; how such a connection could take place; or how any connection could be reconciled with God’s absolute transcendence.

But even if we follow this line of reasoning, and accept that the whole Sufi problem of divine unity comes from having two sides, Ibn ʿArabī still does not completely “unify” existence simply by denying the world an independent existence.

A gap remains in the system.

And this gap adds a new impossibility to the original impossibility already built into the Sufi experience.

The heart of Ibn ʿArabī’s solution is his separation between God’s **essence**, on one side, and God’s **names and attributes**, on the other.

The divine essence remains absolutely transcendent and has no direct connection with the universe.

The connection with the world belongs only to the names and attributes.

The divine manifestations within existence are therefore manifestations of God’s names and attributes, not of the divine essence itself.

This creates two problems.

First, by placing God’s essence in a realm of absolute abstraction while connecting the world only with the divine names and attributes, Ibn ʿArabī breaks apart the very relation between God and the world that the Sufi experience had originally claimed was direct.

Second, he also breaks apart God’s essence from God’s own names and attributes.

Even within a metaphysical way of thinking, it is difficult to see how this separation is supposed to work.

Ibn ʿArabī wants to place a barrier between the divine essence and the divine names and attributes so that God’s absolute transcendence remains untouched. At the same time, he still wants to break through the wall created by that very transcendence by connecting the names and attributes with the world.

This is what we meant when we said that his solution adds a new kind of impossibility to the original impossibility within the Sufi idea of divine unity.

Strangely, Ibn ʿArabī says something in *The Meccan Revelations* that directly contradicts this position. He writes:

> “Glory be to Him who created all things and is those very things.”

Here the pronoun “Him,” used without qualification, refers naturally to the **essence** of God, not merely to His names and attributes.

If so, God’s essence itself is identical with things—that is, with the world.

It is not simply the names and attributes that are identical with the world.

Whatever we make of this contradiction, Ibn ʿArabī’s theory of Unity of Being as it appears in *Fuṣūṣ al-Ḥikam* does not solve the basic contradiction within the Sufi idea of divine unity.

Nor, within his own system, does it clearly explain the relation between God’s essence and His names and attributes.

One modern interpreter of Ibn ʿArabī’s theory[^V3-362] has tried to clarify the issue. He concludes:

> “Ibn ʿArabī’s view of the relation between the attributes and the essence stands midway between transcendence and resemblance. At the same time, he affirms God’s transcendence and also affirms that God possesses attributes of which all the qualities of the universe are only reflections and images.”[^V3-362]

But this interpretation does little more than restate the original problem in different words.

The relation between the divine essence and the divine names and attributes remains unclear.

Indeed, it still appears to be a relation of separation.

The essence remains absolutely transcendent, which means it remains completely removed from any direct connection with “the qualities of the universe.”

That connection still exists only between the qualities of the universe and God’s names and attributes—not His essence.

So the central question remains unanswered:

**What exactly is the relation between the divine names and attributes, on one side, and the divine essence, on the other?**

The interpretation above does not answer this question.

Instead, it introduces another difficulty by describing the relation as “midway between transcendence and resemblance.”

But can we really imagine such a “middle” position while still preserving transcendence as genuine transcendence, in the sense Ibn ʿArabī requires?

#### Mullā Ṣadrā al-Shīrāzī’s Position

Among Ibn ʿArabī’s followers and the later thinkers who accepted the idea of Unity of Being, one appears to have noticed this gap in Ibn ʿArabī’s system and tried to close it.

This was the philosopher and Sufi Mullā Ṣadrā al-Shīrāzī.[^V3-363]

Instead of placing the connection with the world in God’s names and attributes, al-Shīrāzī restores the direct relation between existence and the **divine essence** itself.

He writes:

> “The cause that bestows existence absolutely is, in its being the origin, principle, and source, identical with its own essence. Once it has been established that the chain of beings, causes, and effects ends in a simple essence whose reality is luminous existence (…) and once it has been established that by His very essence He pours forth existence, by His reality He shines, by His identity He gives light to the heavens and the earth, and by His existence He brings forth the world of creation and command—it then becomes clear that all beings have one origin.
>
> “That origin is the Reality, while everything else consists of its modes. It is the Essence (…) and everything else is its names and descriptions. It is the origin, while everything else is its states and modes. It is the Existent, while everything beyond it consists only of its aspects and determinations.”[^V3-364]

Al-Shīrāzī’s meaning is explicit and needs little explanation.

For him, Unity of Being means a direct unity between the **essence of God** and the world, without the divine names and attributes standing between them.

For that reason, he explains and argues for this unity in a method very different from Ibn ʿArabī’s.

He relies on philosophical reasoning rather than on the evidence of Sufi “taste” or mystical experience.

He writes:

> “The essence of the producing cause—that is, the cause that brings something into existence—is identical with its existence, and the essence of the effect is identical with its existence.
>
> “It therefore becomes clear that what is called the effect does not, in truth, possess an identity separate from the identity of the cause that bestows existence upon it…
>
> “The effect produced through the simple act of bringing into existence has no independent reality of its own beyond its relation to its cause.”[^V3-365]

Al-Shīrāzī builds this philosophical argument on two propositions—or, more precisely, on one proposition with two sides.

The first is the **simplicity of the cause of existence**.

This leads to the second: whatever proceeds from a simple cause through the act of bringing something into existence must itself be simple.

God, as the cause of existence, is a simple cause. That means He is not made up of parts and is therefore eternal.

A simple cause has the characteristic that its essence is identical with its existence, while the essence of its effect is likewise identical with its existence.

But this does not mean that there are now two separate existences.

There is only one: the existence of the cause.

Why?

Because if the cause of existence is simple, then its act of bringing existence forth must also be simple.

A **simple act of bringing into existence** means that the cause does not produce something separate from itself.

The producer and the produced are therefore one.

If the thing produced were something genuinely different from the producer, the cause would no longer be simple. It would be composite, and the act of producing would also become composite.

But al-Shīrāzī considers this impossible, because the ultimate cause of existence—God—must be simple.

His argument therefore concludes that the effect produced by this cause—the world—is produced through a simple act of bringing into existence.

For that reason, the world:

> “has no independent reality of its own beyond its relation to its cause,”

so that:

> “the intellect cannot point to anything whose identity is separate from the identity of the one that brought it into existence,”[^V3-366]

and there cannot be:

> “two independent identities before the intellect: one that bestows existence and another upon which existence is bestowed.”[^V3-366]

This, then, is what **Unity of Being** means for al-Shīrāzī.

There is one further point we need to notice in his explanation of the theory.

In the passage quoted above, al-Shīrāzī describes the “simple act of bringing into existence” specifically in terms of **existence**.

The author of *The Theory of Substantial Motion in al-Shīrāzī*[^V3-367] correctly noticed the importance of this wording, and it matters for our discussion as well.

When al-Shīrāzī describes the simple act in terms of “existence,” he is saying that what is produced is **existence**, not **essence**.

The same point is reinforced by his statement that:

> “the essence of the producing cause is identical with its existence, and the essence of the effect is identical with its existence.”

He does not say that the effect is identical with its **essence**.

This wording is not accidental.

It follows directly from al-Shīrāzī’s doctrine that **existence is primary**, and from his rejection of the opposing view that **essence is primary**.

Those who defend the primacy of essence take the opposite position. They argue that what proceeds from an efficient cause through the simple act of production is the **essence** of the thing produced, not its existence.

On their view, the simple act produces only one thing: the essence of the thing itself.[^V3-368]

Here, then, we find another important difference between Ibn ʿArabī and al-Shīrāzī in their understanding of Unity of Being.

In the final result of Ibn ʿArabī’s explanations, he does not affirm the primacy of existence.

His statements about the existence of concrete things are sometimes inconsistent, as we will see later, but the dominant tendency in his thought is to deny every existence apart from the existence of God.

Al-Shīrāzī takes a different position.

In *The Divine Witnesses*, he rejects the idea that concrete, individual existence is merely something constructed by the mind.

For him, **existence exists in itself**, while essence exists only through existence.[^V3-369]

He also argues that nothing can actually exist unless it becomes individual and concrete, because actual existence is always individual existence.

Essence, by contrast, is a universal concept and does not exist independently in itself.

So whatever gives a thing its concrete individuality must itself already possess individuality in its own right.

For al-Shīrāzī, what proceeds from the cause must therefore be **existence**, not essence.

In his *Four Journeys*, al-Shīrāzī also rejects the idea that contingent beings—that is, everything that exists apart from God—have an existence that is merely conceptual. He explains that “each of them has its own particular effects and its own particular characteristics. By reality, we mean nothing more than something that produces an effect in the world outside the mind. And by multiplicity, we mean nothing more than what gives rise to different characteristics and effects. How, then, can a contingent being be nothing outside the mind and have no existence there?”[^V3-370]

He makes a similar point, just as explicitly, in his treatise *The Pervasion of Existence*.[^V3-371]

From what we have seen, al-Shīrāzī’s position on the “unity of being” can be summed up as follows. He openly accepts that the external world has a real, objective existence. At the same time, he also believes in a real divine existence. He then traces both forms of existence back to a single reality and a single identity, in keeping with the first passage we quoted several pages ago.

Ibn ʿArabī’s position on the same question is different. He does not accept that the external world has an objective existence of its own.

The difference between the two positions is not fundamental enough to amount to a real disagreement over the “unity of being” itself. But it does allow us to distinguish between two forms of idealism: al-Shīrāzī’s position is one of **objective idealism**, while Ibn ʿArabī’s is one of **subjective idealism**.

#### Second Aspect: Is the World Eternal or Created?

If we return to the passages from Ibn ʿArabī that we have used so far, we find him wavering between two positions: that the world is eternal, and that it came into being.

The first “outline” passage, which we discussed earlier in this volume, indirectly—but quite clearly—points toward the view that the world is eternal.

There, Ibn ʿArabī presents existence in very definite terms. Existence becomes actual, he says, because God wills to see Himself revealed in the mirror of the world. Islamic Sufis generally hold that this divine will is eternal. So the divine self-revelation—and therefore the realization of existence—must also be eternal.

That is the first point.

Second, in the same passage Ibn ʿArabī says that the world has an initial kind of existence. It exists as “a formed shadow without a spirit, like an unpolished mirror.” He describes this shadow-like existence as a state of readiness “to receive the outpouring of revelation.” What remains is simply something capable of receiving, and “the receiver can come only from His most holy outpouring”—that is, from God.

The meaning here is fairly clear. This first, shadow-like, receptive existence is another way of expressing what Aristotle called **potential existence**.

So before the final divine self-revelation, the world already exists potentially. It exists before, as Ibn ʿArabī puts it, “the matter required the polishing of the mirror of the world, and Adam became the very polish of that mirror.”

In the philosophers’ language, potential existence is eternal existence. So on this basis too, the world is eternal.

Third, even what we might call the final self-revelation is described by Ibn ʿArabī as “the continuous revelation that has never ceased and will never cease.” In other words, it has neither beginning nor end.

This is a direct statement of the endless continuity of existence.

Fourth, the beginning of the same passage tells us that the world exists for two purposes.

One is a purpose of existence: God sees Himself in the world.

The other is a purpose of knowledge: through the world, God knows Himself, and through the world, God is known.

Within this kind of metaphysical thinking, a world that serves these eternal purposes must itself be eternal.

But if we continue reading the same passage until Ibn ʿArabī begins discussing the human being as the “vicegerent,” the “Perfect Human,” and the “Muhammadan Reality,” we immediately run into signs of uncertainty and contradiction.

At one point he describes the human being as “a being who came into existence yet is eternal, a creation that endures forever.”

How can the same thing come into existence, be eternal, and continue forever all at once?

Some interpreters of Ibn ʿArabī say that the contradiction comes from the fact that the human being combines opposites. On the divine side, the human being is eternal. On the human side, the human being came into existence.[^V3-372]

But this explanation might work for someone who believes that concrete beings have an independent existence. It does not work for Ibn ʿArabī.

As we have seen, Ibn ʿArabī does not regard their existence as truly real. For him, there is only one real existence: God’s existence.

So how can we say that the human side “came into existence” if it has no independent existence in the first place? And how can we separate the human side from the divine side within the human being if every kind of existence, concrete or otherwise, is ultimately only a manifestation of God’s existence?

To divide the human being into a material side and a divine side, then say that the first came into existence while the second is eternal, would require us to recognize that the material side has an independent existence of its own, just as the divine side does.

But that is not Ibn ʿArabī’s position.

There is another interpretation. It says that “all these contradictions are resolved within the divine essence, which surrounds and contains all these opposites without being reduced to any of them.”[^V3-373]

This explanation can be given a real foundation if we place it within a scientific account of the many forms taken by matter and its movement, and of the many different phenomena, properties, and contradictions found within it. Despite all this variety, everything goes back to one common basis: **matter in motion**, which brings these contradictions together within a single reality.

But if we leave this explanation where it originally stands—in the world of pure abstraction or the purely supernatural—then its only real basis is as a supernatural echo of that material reality.

Still, if we look at what the explanation implies rather than only at what it says directly, we can see that it comes close to a scientific way of thinking. It tries to recognize the dialectical law of contradiction within the processes of the universe.

Seen in this light, we may also be able to explain why Ibn ʿArabī wavers between saying that the world is eternal and saying that it came into existence.

His uncertainty between these two positions reflects a deeper uncertainty between two ways of seeing the world.

One is an objective view that arises independently of his wishes. It reflects the real basis of the world and points him toward one of its basic truths.

The other is a subjective, metaphysical view. This second view alters the first and pulls it away from that real basis.

The result is that Ibn ʿArabī’s view of the universe is not cut off from reality, but neither does it accurately reflect reality.

His thought is filled with mystical and metaphysical visions. So the real basis of his thinking becomes coloured by those visions.

Even so, we should not forget that thought remains objectively tied to the external world. That connection is decisive even when thought produces supernatural ways of imagining reality.

Those forms of thought still reflect reality, but the further they move from its objective truths, the more distorted that reflection becomes.

#### Third Aspect: The Question of the Human Being

The question of the human being appeared in many different forms throughout the history of Islamic Sufi thought, from the time Sufism first became part of the developing Arab-Islamic society down to Ibn ʿArabī and the students, followers, and later thinkers influenced by him.

Before that, the same question had appeared among the Qadarites and then among the Muʿtazila.

At the same time, and with increasing force, it appeared in the teachings of the Islamic sects described as the “extremists,” or *ghulāt*. As we have already seen, the human being occupied the central place in the teachings of these groups.

Indeed, it was largely because of the status they gave the human being that they were described as extremists. They raised the human being so high that they placed him at the level of divinity—or, we might say, placed the crown of divinity on the human head.

The Sufis took a different path.

At first, they connected the question of the human being with the question of knowledge. In doing so, they broke through the rigid theological idea that God must remain absolutely beyond any direct relation with the human being. They established a direct relationship of knowledge between God and the human being.

Then they went beyond knowledge and spoke of a relationship in existence itself.

They taught the possibility of direct presence: the human being could encounter God immediately and directly. They did require the person to “strip away” material attachments first. But even this stripping away did not mean that the person stopped being human.

So the whole question still concerned the human being who lives here on earth, and no one else.

Ibn ʿArabī then raised the same question to a new level, higher than any it had reached before.

When we previously examined his basic texts on the “unity of being,” we saw the human being become the origin of the world itself. The whole world could therefore be called the “Greater Human,” while the human being could be called the “small world”—the microcosm—as the Brethren of Purity had already called him in the fourth Islamic century, the tenth century CE.

From here came Ibn ʿArabī’s idea of the **Perfect Human**.

As we explained earlier, this idea cannot be separated from the wider question of the human being. It gave the human being a new kind of “divine” dimension, while at the same time giving God a corresponding “human” dimension.[^V3-374]

About a century and a half later, this idea became even more firmly established in Islamic Sufi thought through al-Jīlī,[^V3-375] who devoted an entire work to it. That work became one of the main references in the literature of Islamic Sufism.[^V3-376]

So throughout this long history, the question of the human being appeared in many different forms. But they all shared one basic feature: they emphasized the importance of the human being and raised his place within existence.

This raises a serious question.

For centuries, from the earliest Islamic period until a late stage of the Middle Ages, this issue kept returning with great force and remarkable persistence. Was all of this simply an abstract intellectual process with no connection to social reality?

The question becomes even more important when we look specifically at the Sufi position.

The Sufis were, above all, thinkers of abstraction. Their entire way of life rested on a harsh and exhausting struggle to raise the human being above material needs.

Yet the human being remains human through his own particular existence. He remains an earthly being.

The Sufis never denied this.

And still, they placed this earthly human being within an extraordinary equation. They joined the human being to God so closely that the person could become God’s image, His face, a copy of Him, and the mirror of His existence in the world.

One might object that the Sufis applied all this only to the “spiritual” side of the human being, not to the human being as a material creature. Perhaps their aim was precisely to escape the material side and therefore reject the human being’s earthly existence.

But that explanation does not work.

If the Sufis had really meant only the purely spiritual side of the human being—if spirituality by itself had been what raised the human being until he became the origin of the whole world—then angels should have stood higher than human beings.

After all, in the Sufi view, angels are entirely spiritual beings with no material element at all.

Yet the Sufis gave the human being a higher place than the angels, and they did so decisively.

Ibn ʿArabī in particular wrote at length and very clearly about this superiority of the human being over the angels.[^V3-377]

So why did they take this position?

To explain it, we must first reject the idea that the long history of the human question in all the movements we have discussed had no connection with historical social reality.

Any picture of intellectual activity that cuts thought off from that reality is a picture drawn in empty space.

Such thought does not exist and cannot exist.

Nothing enters human thought, and nothing becomes an object of thought, without reflecting the external world in one way or another, whether that reflection is accurate or distorted.

This has become almost self-evident since dialectical materialism placed the question in its proper setting. It has also been repeatedly confirmed by the theoretical and experimental achievements that science continues to produce, almost every day, in both the natural sciences and the study of human society.

So the intellectual movement that developed through the major stages of medieval Arab-Islamic history was not an abstract process cut off from the living processes taking place in society itself.

It took many forms.

Among the Qadarites and Muʿtazila, it appeared as a defence of the human being’s independent will and freedom to choose his actions.

In Muʿtazilite *kalām* and in philosophy, it appeared as a defence of the human ability to understand the world through rational thought.

Among the Sufis, it appeared as an attempt to establish the human being’s place in the universe, his active role in existence, and his ability to move beyond the official theological relationship between humanity and God.

This was a long and active intellectual history. But it was never isolated from the equally active processes taking place in Arab-Islamic society during those same historical periods.

We need to state this again because it is the necessary starting point for explaining the position that the human question came to occupy throughout medieval Arab-Islamic thought.

From there, we must look for the relationship between this question in intellectual life and the living struggles taking place in society under the same historical conditions.

A scientific historical analysis shows that these were really two sides of the same question.

The Arab-Islamic human being first faced a real, living problem within society itself.

That same problem then had to appear, in one form or another, within intellectual life.

This is how the question of the human being entered the history of Arab-Islamic thought. From the beginning, it became one of the major questions running through that history in all its different forms: rationalist schools of *kalām*, philosophy, Sufism, illuminationist thought, schools of law and legal theory, and religious sects and movements.

Take the question of compulsion and free choice.

This was the first major issue through which the human question entered Arab thought. It became central to much of *kalām* and Arab philosophy, and it entered Sufi literature in several different ways.

The same happened with the question of knowledge: how far can human understanding go in knowing the things that exist?

And the same happened with the human being’s place in the universe, a question that received especially close attention in Sufi thought.

One might still say that all of this remains too general and does not yet point to anything specific.

So to make the argument more concrete, we need to return briefly to history.

At several earlier points in this study, we saw conflicts emerging from the material foundations of Arab-Islamic society.

On the surface, those conflicts appeared in “superstructural” forms that almost concealed their material basis. In fact, historians and researchers shaped by traditionalist thinking often failed to see that basis at all.

But there is an important pattern in this history.

The more severe the social and political contradiction became at a moment of crisis, the easier it becomes for a scientific researcher to see the hidden connection between these “superstructural” forms of conflict and the underlying class contradiction.

In other words, the ideological character of those intellectual forms becomes easier to recognize.

The clearest moments of this kind came when objective contradictions became especially sharp.

On one side, there was the contradiction between the productive forces of the countryside and feudal relations of production.

On another, there was the contradiction between the growing merchant and artisan groups in the cities and the feudal character of the ruling theocratic system.

But because of the particular historical conditions of medieval Arab-Islamic society, these class contradictions often appeared in other forms.

Sometimes they appeared in religious form, as conflicts between rival doctrines.

At other times they appeared in regional form, as struggles between the central power of the caliphate and social forces in the provinces that seemed simply to be rebelling against central rule.

All of this naturally had its ideological reflection.

Earlier in this study, we repeatedly saw clear examples of the ideology of the ruling class.

That ideology rested on a theology that presented the political and social system ruled by the economically and politically dominant class as the expression of God’s absolute will.

We have already explained this many times throughout the book.

But what about the exploited social classes and groups—people denied freedom, dignity, and the ability to determine their own lives?

It would be unnatural for them not to develop an ideology opposed to the ideology of the dominant social order.

And in fact they did.

It was expressed through thinkers with liberating tendencies, many of whom belonged, either directly or by origin, to these same oppressed classes and groups.

This gives us a real historical explanation for an important phenomenon.

Most of the Islamic thinkers who led intellectual movements against the ideology of the caliphal political order were either *mawālī*—non-Arab clients—or came from social and regional backgrounds that suffered class oppression in forms where ethnic or regional solidarity became mixed with class conflict.

This historical fact decisively refutes racial explanations of the phenomenon.

Reality and science both reject such explanations.

The real basis of the phenomenon was class, not race or ethnicity.

A racial explanation fails in practice because it fails to match reality.

How, then, did the ideology of these oppressed classes and social groups appear? How did it express itself?

The first and most basic form was the **question of the human being**.

In intellectual life, this became the broadest expression of their ideology.

It was the one form that remained present throughout the many centuries of social struggle carried out by these groups in medieval Arab-Islamic history.

Alongside this very broad formulation, we also find other, more specific forms. These were scattered and less stable, but some were more closely tied to direct political action and even armed struggle.

Examples can be found in the principles associated with the Bābakī revolt in the third Islamic century, the ninth century CE; the Qarmaṭian revolt in the third and fourth Islamic centuries, the ninth and tenth centuries CE; and the Ismāʿīlī movement in the fourth Islamic century, the tenth century CE.

But the broader and more basic form—the question of the human being—remained.

Even so, it never settled into a single intellectual form.

Nor, given the historical conditions of the time, was it based on a revolutionary theory that had freed itself from the influence of the “official,” theological ideology.

The closest exception may have been the Sufi “experience.”

At its starting point, that experience contained the seed of a theory that might have developed into a revolutionary expression of the ideology of the common people.

Its revolutionary potential lay in the fact that, as we explained earlier, it began with a bold attempt to tear away the theological foundation on which the ideology of the ruling system rested.

But Sufism never became that revolutionary theory.

As we also explained earlier, it carried within itself the means of its own destruction because the mystical experience it proposed could not actually be realized.

That also made it impossible for Sufism to become an effective revolutionary force capable of achieving the aims of the rural population or of the growing social groups in the cities, especially merchants and artisans.

None of this changes the basic historical fact.

The question of the human being moved from social reality into intellectual life and became the first broad sign of the ideology of the classes and social groups crushed under an absolute and despotic social order.

This appeared in several forms.

1. Among the extremist sects, the “human being” was raised from the ranks of ordinary people to the level of the imam, the prophet, or even the divine. Some were even granted immortality through reincarnation.[^V3-378]

2. Among the Qadarites and Muʿtazila, the “human being” possessed an independent will and freely chose his own actions.

3. Among theologians, philosophers, and natural scientists, the “human being” could discover the secrets of the universe and understand the things of the world through rational thought and practical sensory experience.

4. Among the Sufis, the “human being” could reach the rank of sainthood and perform extraordinary acts. He could reach God directly through intuitive knowledge, mystical unveiling and vision, or direct presence. He could even become the origin of the world itself, while the whole world could be named after him as the “Greater Human.”

Across several centuries, these different images of the human being appeared one after another in Arab-Islamic thought.

Together, they show repeated attempts by that thought to respond to the humiliation of human beings under the dominant despotic social order.

But there was a problem.

Although all these attempts took a positive position toward the oppressed human being, none managed to bring the intellectual image of the problem into even a reasonably close correspondence with the real nature of the problem in society.

Because of this, they were also unable to create an adequate ideology for the oppressed human being represented by the classes and social groups we have already described.

Why did they fail in both respects?

We believe the reason is that, to different degrees, they continued to move within the orbit of metaphysical and supernatural thought.

They never fully entered the real movement of change and development in society and nature.

When they did approach that movement, they did so only from the side, while remaining tied to fixed theological ideas—sometimes openly, sometimes in hidden ways.

This continued link with Islamic theology can perhaps be explained in the same way that Engels explained the link between the different forms of medieval ideology and Christian theology.

He writes:

> “The Middle Ages clothed all the other forms of ideology—philosophy, politics, and law—in theology, turning them into branches subordinate to it. Every social and political movement was therefore forced to take on a religious form. And if a movement wanted to affect masses who had been fed on nothing but religion, it had to present their own interests to them in religious dress.”[^V3-379]

We believe this explanation also applies to the intellectual movements that developed in Arab-Islamic society during the same historical period.

But one additional factor must be added.

We might call it a “subjective” factor from the point of view of the intellectual movements within Islam themselves.

Their attachment to Islamic theology did not come only from the fact that they had historically developed under theology, or from the fact that Muslim populations were deeply shaped by religious ideas.

The movements themselves also deliberately covered their more liberating ideas in religious language.

They did this partly to protect themselves from persecution by rulers who were extremely sensitive about defending their theological ideology.

They also did it to mislead conservative and reactionary religious scholars. These scholars sought favour with the authorities by denouncing independent thinkers, inciting the rulers against them, and taking part in campaigns of intellectual and physical persecution with extreme brutality.

We saw this in the killing of al-Ḥallāj and of Shihāb al-Dīn al-Suhrawardī, in the burning of Ibn Rushd’s works in the Maghreb, and in many other cases familiar from the history of intellectual persecution.

Al-Ghazālī also played a major and well-known role in this history.

## General Conclusions from the Study of Sufism

At the end of this study of Islamic Sufism, we will try to draw several general conclusions.

These do not concern the details of particular Sufi schools so much as the broader features and effects of Sufism as a whole within medieval Arab-Islamic society.

### 1 — Sufi “Fatalism”

Adam Metz correctly observed that Sufi doctrines were influenced by the doctrines of the Muʿtazilite theologians. He described this influence by saying that “the Sufis took questions and methods from the Muʿtazila.”[^V3-380]

But when Metz explained what he meant, he focused on only one question that both groups discussed: the problem of divine decree.

He then found that the Sufis took the opposite position from the Muʿtazila. In his words, the Sufis taught “compulsion without hesitation.”

On this reading, Sufi influence from the Muʿtazila went no further than taking up “one basic point of their doctrine,” while using a different method and reaching the opposite conclusion.

The basic observation about Muʿtazilite influence is correct.

But we approach one part of it differently.

By “Sufi fatalism,” we mean something broader than what Metz understood by the phrase.

In fact, the indirect meaning we find in the Sufi position may lead us to deny that Sufism was fatalistic at all in the philosophical or theological sense that directly opposes the Muʿtazilite idea of free choice.

Metz bases his idea of “Sufi fatalism” on the well-known practice of **trust in God** among ascetics, the earliest Sufis, and later dervishes.

They entrusted their livelihood to God’s will.

Metz then extended this idea to Sufi doctrine as a whole.

But trust in God does not by itself amount to philosophical fatalism.

It simply expresses the ascetic or Sufi believer’s trust in God’s mercy toward human beings.

That is a general Islamic religious belief shared by Muslims as believers. It does not define a distinct Sufi doctrine on the problem of human freedom and divine decree.

Metz himself seems to have recognized that it was wrong to make the claim too definite.

At the end of his discussion, he writes that “the Sufis did not construct a doctrine of fate,” and that “they never reached a firm and settled position in the views on fate that they expressed from time to time.”[^V3-381]

He supports this second statement by quoting one of the early Sufi authors regarded as an authority.[^V3-382]

A modern Arab researcher,[^V3-383] however, seems indirectly to accept the idea that Sufism was fatalistic.

He examines the power that Sufis attribute to the human being they call the “Pole” or the “saint”—the power to affect the structure of the universe.

But he removes this power from the earthly human being.

In his reading, Sufis give the qualities of the “Perfect Human” only to a purely metaphysical human being. In the end, human action is traced back to divine creation.

The earthly human being, on this reading, seems to have no will of his own.

This may amount to a form of fatalism.

Because such interpretations appear to be common among scholars of Sufi thought, we should explain our own understanding according to the method we have followed throughout this study.

Here we are speaking specifically about the doctrines of **philosophical Sufism**, not about the practices of ascetics, the earliest Sufis, or the dervishes.

If we look at the general direction of these philosophical schools—whether they speak of union, divine indwelling, or the unity of being—we find that they all give the human being an exalted position in his relationship with God.

In that relationship, the human being acquires a “divine” quality.

At the same time, God acquires a corresponding “human” quality.

The boundary between the human being’s “divinity” and God’s “humanity” becomes so faint that it is often difficult to see whether any boundary remains at all.

The Sufis themselves speak openly about this. No elaborate interpretation is needed.

We have already given enough examples and explanation throughout this study to support that conclusion.

Now add two other points.

First, in the last section we connected the human question in Sufi thought with the human question in social reality.

Second, we saw that Ibn ʿArabī’s description of the “Perfect Human” also includes the real human being who lives on earth.

From all of this, we can draw the following conclusion.

The Muʿtazila and the Sufis did more than simply discuss the same question of divine decree. They also reached essentially the same position on it.

Their difference lay in the method they used and in the way they reached that position.

The Muʿtazila approached the question rationally.

The Sufis approached it through “intuition,” or through what they themselves called “taste” or “mystical knowledge.”

The Muʿtazila reached the conclusion that the human being possesses will and freedom of choice through rational argument.

The Sufis reached a similar conclusion through the “divine” quality they gave the human being.

That quality grants the human being an active will not only in his own personal affairs but even in the “structure of the universe” and the fate of the whole world.

As one text puts it, “the world continues to exist as long as this Perfect Human exists within it.”[^V3-384]

And “for the Sufis, the saint is the Perfect Human, or the Pole.”[^V3-385]

But who is this “saint” or “Pole”?

Is he a spiritual being living in heaven?

Or is he a human being living on earth in flesh and blood?

If our conclusion is correct, then we can reject the idea that philosophical Sufism was a doctrine of fatalism.

The earlier parts of this study have already laid much of the groundwork for that conclusion, or at least for understanding it.

### 2 — Rational Thought in Sufi Philosophy

Here we return to Adam Metz’s observation from the previous section: that Sufi doctrines were influenced by the theologians, by which he meant the Muʿtazila.

This agrees with another observation of his that we mentioned earlier: Islamic Sufism shows a clear and close connection with rational thought.[^V3-386]

Metz made the first observation only briefly and somewhat superficially. He made the second almost in passing.

Even so, we should recognize that he noticed something fundamental about Islamic Sufism, something worth examining more closely.

The issue here is not simply the relationship between Islamic Sufism and philosophy.

That connection is already clear, and this entire study has shown it.

The question before us now is more specific.

It reveals a paradox—or a contradiction—inside the Sufi method itself.

As we know, the Sufi method begins by rejecting rational thought as a way of gaining knowledge.

It says that true knowledge can be reached only through mystical intuition: through what Sufis call “taste,” “unveiling and vision,” or direct inner knowledge.

How, then, can we also say that Sufis used rational thought, or were at least influenced by it?

That is the paradox.

But it is a paradox created by Sufism itself.

More precisely, it is one of the contradictions built into the Sufi “experience” from the beginning.

In reality, every school of philosophical Sufism in Islam relied heavily on principles and concepts that had already been used in rational inquiry by the Muʿtazilite theologians and later by Islamic philosophers.

These principles were used not only to establish particular arguments but to build the theoretical foundations and overall structure of Sufi doctrine.

Take the problem of unity and multiplicity.

Like theologians and philosophers, Sufis faced this problem whenever they discussed the oneness of God.

On one side, they were committed to the “absolute unity” of the divine essence—the source of creation, the source of illumination, the first principle of existence, or “true existence.”

At the same time, they also accepted a rational principle used by theologians and philosophers: **“from the One, only one can proceed.”**

In other words, a single first principle can directly produce only one thing.

But then they confronted a reality they could not escape: the enormous multiplicity and variety of things that exist.

So they found themselves facing a real feature of the universe that seemed to conflict with the very rational principle they had accepted.

Different Sufi schools tried to solve this problem in different ways.

But although their answers eventually moved beyond the logic of rational thought, the reasoning that led them there began from that same rational logic.

Ibn ʿArabī’s idea of the “unity of religions,” for example, rested on the principle that “from the One, only one can proceed.”

For him, all religions were ultimately one religion because it was impossible for true multiplicity to proceed from the one God.

He therefore regarded all prophets as branches of a single “tree” of light, while “the Muhammadan Reality is the root of this tree.”[^V3-387]

We find the same pattern elsewhere.

Sufi philosophy also used the categories of the “simple” and the “composite” when discussing the idea of “simple origination,” as we saw in the earlier discussion of the primacy of existence in Ibn ʿArabī and Mullā Ṣadrā al-Shīrāzī.

These were also categories used by rational philosophers.

We see the same thing in the classical categories that form the backbone of formal, rational logic.

In the illuminationist Sufi philosophy of its founder, Shihāb al-Dīn al-Suhrawardī, these categories became one of the starting points for constructing his philosophy of light.

He changed their number, however.

Instead of Aristotle’s ten categories, al-Suhrawardī used five: substance, quantity, quality, motion, and relation.

He treated action and being acted upon as forms of motion. He placed possession, time, place, and position under the category of relation.[^V3-388]

So rational logic entered directly into the foundations of this branch of Sufi thought.

Sufi philosophy also used the distinction between the **necessary** and the **contingent** when discussing whether the world is eternal or came into existence.[^V3-389]

Islamic philosophers, especially Ibn Sīnā, had used the same distinction.

The Sufis also used the law of causality in discussing the same problem, just as rational philosophers did.[^V3-390]

There are many other examples.

Al-Ghazālī himself pointed to a similar paradox among the Bāṭinīs.

He showed that when they tried to prove their doctrine of the infallible imam, they built their argument from a sequence of premises based entirely on rational judgments—even though the point of their argument was to deny the authority of reason.

The irony is that al-Ghazālī himself fell into the same paradox when replying to them.

He too tried to disprove the authority of reason by using rational arguments.[^V3-391]

Ibrāhīm Madkūr, one of the leading modern Arab scholars of philosophy, also commented on this phenomenon.

He suggested that when the Arab Peripatetic, or Aristotelian, tradition came under attack through its philosophers, it may have found refuge among the Sufis.

Madkūr therefore argues that the later history of Islamic philosophy cannot be properly studied without also studying what was written by Sufis and theologians.

This explanation is not far from the historical reality if we define the phenomenon broadly, as Madkūr does, as a “deep intermingling of Sufism and philosophy.”[^V3-392]

But our own definition is more specific.

Sufism did not merely discuss the same questions as philosophy.

It also used philosophy’s own logic, with all its rational structure.

Yet Sufism wanted to move beyond that logic and ultimately destroy the rational method.

In that sense, Sufism ended up undermining itself without destroying any principle of reason.

Something similar happened when Sufism tried to destroy the theological idea of divine unity.

It ended up destroying itself while leaving theological monotheism untouched in its absolute transcendence.

The similarity here concerns the form and result only.

The two cases are fundamentally different.

When Sufism turned to the principles of reason, it was responding to an objective reality that existed independently of Sufi wishes and supernatural beliefs.

Only this objective reality can truly explain the paradox we have been discussing.

We deliberately call it a **phenomenon**, because it expresses something fundamental about the relationship between thought and the external world.

To move beyond general statements, we can put the point more directly.

The real reason Sufis had to build their metaphysical systems on principles of rational thought, even while rejecting the judgments of reason, lies in the material nature of thought itself—or, more precisely, in the material basis of the relationship between thought and reality.

What we call the principles of rational thought did not appear from nowhere.

They have a history.

That history is itself the product of the relationship between thought and reality.

It is the history of human beings acting socially in the world.

These rational principles grew from human experience: from sensory experience, practical activity, and the general conclusions people drew from them throughout the long history of their social relationship with nature and with one another.

This does not mean that every rational conclusion automatically matches the objective truth of the external world.

It may fail to match it, sometimes by a great distance.

The degree of correspondence depends on how far scientific knowledge has developed in discovering the objective general laws that govern the movement of the external world.

But whether a particular idea accurately reflects reality or not, the relationship between thought and reality still remains.

In fact, rational generalizations that do **not** accurately reflect objective reality are themselves part of the necessary road toward more accurate knowledge.

That road may be very long.

But every step along it contains some part of the truth.

For this reason, every such step can be called a **relative truth**.

Objective truth itself is the only **absolute truth**.

---

From all of this, we can now see that what we called a Sufi “paradox” is really a paradox only from the Sufis’ own point of view.

In reality, there is no paradox.

Things developed exactly as their nature required.

The Sufis could not place even one stone in the construction of their abstract world from somewhere outside history—the history of thought, which is the history made by human beings with both their hands and their minds.

If we look again at the different forms of the abstract world the Sufis built outside the material world, with all its different levels and varieties, we find that every one of those forms reflects something from the material world.

Each one either reflects an image from that world, expresses some human experience within it, or makes use of human knowledge accumulated over earlier history.

The Sufis took the accumulated products of nature, society, and thought available in their own time and, in their own way, used them to create their abstract metaphysical worlds.

Even the way they arranged those Sufi and illuminationist worlds into ordered levels reflects structures that first existed in our material world.

The illuminationist hierarchy of “lights,” for example, can be understood as a kind of reflection of the social hierarchy found in the class societies known to human history up to that time.

This is how our method explains why the Sufis relied on principles of reason while trying to overturn the judgments of reason.

### 3 — The Persecution of Sufis and Sufism

Historical sources describe different forms of persecution directed against the Sufi movement during its early stages in Islam, before it changed from an intellectual movement into one centred mainly on organized Sufi orders.

The evidence available to us suggests that this persecution was not fundamentally religious in its causes.

Its main cause lay in the ideological content of Sufism as an intellectual movement.

Earlier in this study, we identified the clearest sign of that content.

In broad terms, Sufism created a new relationship between God and the human being, one that outwardly ran parallel to the prophetic relationship on which the entire Islamic system rested.

But the deeper aim of this new relationship was not simply to stand beside the prophetic one.

It was to displace it.

In outward form, this was a religious position.

In reality, it was an ideological one.

Its deeper meaning was an attempt to break free from absolute submission to the law of prophecy, because submission to that law had come to mean submission to a social order that used religious law both to express its ideology and to support its rule.

We do not need to repeat the details already discussed.

Our purpose here is to begin from this foundation and use it to explain several facts: facts about Sufi thought itself, about the attitude of the ruling social order toward Sufism, about the attitude of the religious scholars aligned with that authority, and about the attitude of the social groups ruled by that order through despotic power.

**A — Sufi Thought**

Here we find other signs that support what we have already said about the ideological content of Sufism.

One example is the Sufi idea of **sainthood**, or *walāya*.

Sainthood was one of the higher ranks of Sufi attainment.

A person who reached it was believed to acquire the ability to go beyond the laws of nature and perform extraordinary acts like the miracles that religions attribute to prophets.

We discussed this earlier.

But another feature of Sufi sainthood deserves attention because of what it implies.

Originally, *walāya* was associated specifically with the Shīʿī imams from the “People of the House.”[^V3-393]

The Sufis, however, made it universal.

In their view, any Muslim who met the necessary conditions could attain sainthood.

To remain within accepted Islamic tradition and avoid appearing to rebel against Muslim conventions, they also broadened the meaning of the term “People of the House.”

They attributed to the Prophet Muḥammad a saying in which he was asked, “Who belongs to the family of Muḥammad?” and replied: “Every devout person.”[^V3-394]

In this way, they moved the term away from a narrow meaning based on family descent and gave it a broader religious meaning.

“Family” came to mean Muslims in general.

The “house” came to mean Islam itself.

And rather than openly claiming this expansion as their own, they presented it as something established by the Prophet.

They supported it with another saying attributed to him about Salmān the Persian: “Salmān is one of us, the People of the House.”[^V3-395]

So the Sufis used the language and traditions of Islam itself to widen the human basis of the right to *walāya*.

They took this “right” away from the narrow circle of the Prophet’s family, where it implied a special distinction between that family and the rest of humanity, and turned it into something potentially open to people without distinction or privilege.

Ibn ʿArabī expressed the Sufi position in language that carries a clear sense of protest against limiting this right to one particular family.

Speaking about a Sufi saint from the Maghreb, he writes that “even if he is not from the house of the Prophet, he shares with him in a higher lineage”—meaning a lineage of spiritual elevation, not descent from ʿAlī.

“He belongs,” Ibn ʿArabī continues, “to His higher House”—that is, God’s house—“not to his lower house.”[^V3-396]

The same feeling of protest appears when Ibn ʿArabī speaks of the descendants of ʿAqīl ibn Abī Ṭālib and the descendants of Salmān the Persian:

> “I hope that the descendants of ʿAqīl and Salmān may also receive this care, just as it has reached the children of al-Ḥasan and al-Ḥusayn and their descendants, and the clients of the People of the House. For God’s mercy is vast, O saint.”[^V3-397]

We find a similar tone in al-Ḥallāj, who rejects any “monopoly” on descent from the Prophet when he says: “Muḥammad was not the father of any man.”[^V3-398]

Statements like these may belong to the Sufi practice of interpretation in which everything in Islam is said to have an “outer” and an “inner” meaning.

The Sufis consistently sought the inner meaning rather than the outer one.

This supported one of their most basic claims: religious law represents only the “outer” truth, while another, deeper “inner” truth exists beneath it, and that is the truth they follow.

In any case, the doctrine of *walāya* gives us a second important sign of the ideological character of the Sufi movement.

It deserves special attention.

By making *walāya*—a power capable of extraordinary acts—available even to ordinary people and servants, the Sufis were moving the ideological content of their thought away from a purely abstract discussion of divine unity and toward the real human beings living on earth.

They made the saints—people of flesh and blood—into spiritual powers capable of changing reality and breaking through the normal laws of nature.

And these saints did not have to belong to the Prophet’s family, to the caliphs, or to the ruling class.

It is almost as though the Sufis were expressing, in a partly direct way, their opposition to the idea of “privilege.”

At its core, that privilege was class privilege when it was used by those who held absolute political power and presented themselves as God’s representatives on earth.

In this way, philosophical Sufism brought its ideological meaning close to concrete social reality, despite the supernatural qualities it continued to attribute to its saints.

---

There is a third sign that this study should not ignore.

It concerns national or ethnic feeling, which under certain historical conditions can overlap and merge with class feeling before a clear class consciousness has fully developed.

We can see this in the Sufi movement by looking at the social environments in which Sufism spread most widely.

Historical sources show that many Islamic Sufis belonged both to non-Arab peoples—most often Persian—and to merchant and artisan groups within those same populations.

This pattern appeared not only in non-Arab regions far from Baghdad.

It also appeared in Baghdad itself, the capital of the Abbasid caliphate.

The capital attracted migrants from all these groups, especially people who came in search of work and a living.[^V3-399]

This gives us another possible source of the ideological direction we have already identified within Sufism.

We can see the effect of this social background even more clearly when we add the widespread poverty found in these same environments and the large number of Sufis among the poor.

The historian al-Muqaddasī, who died in 350 AH/961 CE, tells us that during a visit to Shiraz he wanted to learn about the local Sufi way of life.

He made contact with the Sufis there.

When he received gifts of clothing and purses of money, he would take them and then give them to the Sufis.[^V3-400]

This clearly suggests that he saw they needed such things because they were poor.

Poverty, in fact, followed the Sufis in almost every part of that society.

But their poverty was not caused by Sufism.

The opposite is closer to the truth.

Poverty was not the result of Sufism. It was one of the conditions that helped produce it.

Poverty was a social phenomenon affecting many lower and middle groups throughout different regions and environments.

So it is not enough to say that the Sufis were poor because they freely chose a life of asceticism and hardship in order to escape the “evils” of the world and the “impurity” of matter, whether for religious or philosophical reasons.

That explanation may apply to a small group, or to a few individuals.

It does not explain the great majority who formed the wider social phenomenon.

History tells us that during the third and fourth Islamic centuries—the ninth and tenth centuries CE—the urban masses experienced serious social unrest.

These masses included artisans, street sellers, owners of small shops, people living in extreme poverty, and the unemployed.

As their living conditions deteriorated, these groups formed organizations of their own.

Within each trade or group, these organizations had a cooperative side.

At the same time, they could also take violent action against the authorities, wealthy merchants, and large landowners.

Among them were groups known at the time as the **ʿayyārūn** and **shuṭṭār**.[^V3-401]

They attracted people from many sections of society who were angry at class inequality because they suffered some of the harshest forms of poverty.

Their members came from different ethnic backgrounds without racial distinction among them.[^V3-402]

Historians of the period give vivid descriptions of the decline in social conditions affecting these groups.

One example is al-Muqaddasī’s description of Iraq at the time:

> “It is the home of disorder and high prices. Every day it falls further behind, and injustice and taxation keep it in hardship and misery.”[^V3-403]

What matters for us here is the possible connection between these social conditions and another well-established historical fact: Sufi activity became especially intense during the same period in which these problems became severe.

We are not alone in seeing such a connection.

A leading modern Arab scholar writes that “these conditions may have had an effect on the growth of Sufi activity.”[^V3-404]

In reality, the strong growth of Sufism during this period can be seen as another form of the social groupings we have just described.

It brought together one section of the destitute poor.

What united them, besides poverty, may have been an intellectual bond, or perhaps a personal tendency toward dependence and withdrawal from the exhausting struggle to earn a living.

Historical sources give us several pictures of these gatherings of the “poor.”[^V3-405]

They describe people who longed intensely for good food and, when it became available without effort, threw themselves upon it with something close to greed—or perhaps with actual greed produced by deprivation.

Abū al-ʿAlāʾ al-Maʿarrī was therefore not simply inventing a fantasy when he mocked the Sufis’ enthusiasm for food:

> I see the Sufi clan as the worst of clans.
> So tell them—and how little such men deserve:
> Did God say, when you worshipped Him,
> “Eat like cattle, and dance for Me”?[^V3-406]

Nor was it without a real basis that “the eating of the Sufis” became proverbial at the time.[^V3-407]

Indeed, one of the leading Sufi authorities confirms that reality in a story told about him. It is said that he acquired loads of white sugar and called together a group of confectioners, until they built from the sugar a wall with battlements and prayer niches supported by columns, all of it carved from sugar. Then he invited the Sufis, who tore it down, smashed it apart, and carried it off.[^V3-408]

There were other features of Sufism in the third and fourth centuries AH that strengthen our view that its spread at the time was partly a form of social dislocation caused by widespread poverty, unemployment, and deprivation. These included the spread of sexual deviance among their ranks,[^V3-409] as well as the practice known as *kaldiyya*.[^V3-410][^V3-411] The fact that leading Sufi writers[^V3-412] strongly condemned these practices and called for Sufism to be cleansed of them only confirms that they existed. It does not prove the opposite.

What we want to show from this historical survey is that the decisive factor behind the growth of Sufism in this period was the social reality itself, not the conscious choices of individuals. From this, we can also trace the ideological direction of the Sufi movement back to its real social basis, independently of what the Sufis themselves consciously intended.

We are not claiming that the Sufis alone represented the outlook of all the broad and varied groups in their society who suffered under the class oppression and tyranny of the existing social order. But we do argue that the Sufis were the only ones who raised the ideological content of their movement to a theoretical level. At that level, it could stand face to face with the ideology through which the ruling system influenced and controlled society as a whole. That influence even reached the social groups whose material and spiritual misery had been caused by this “official” ideology—in fact, it reached those groups most of all.

With this social basis established, we can now turn to the second side of our discussion of the persecution of the Sufis: how the different groups in society responded to the Sufi movement in Islam.

**B — The Attitude of the Ruling System:** It should now be easy to understand why the despotic caliphate used persecution against the Sufi movement. In fact, we may not need much more explanation. What we do need is to give some examples of this persecution, because they support the central point of our study: that the Sufi form taken by Arab-Islamic philosophy, alongside its other, rationalist form, was in its essential content one expression of the ideological struggles of the Middle Ages.

If the Sufi movement had not carried this kind of content, it would not have been “subjected to attack and retaliation.”[^V3-413] There is no solid basis for explaining its persecution as the result of purely religious motives.

Medieval Islamic history is full of blatant violations of Islamic law and teaching by the Abbasid caliphs in particular, as well as by the people around them.[^V3-414] Why, then, did religious feeling fail to prevent these violations? Why did the jurists not act as defenders of religion by condemning them, or by stirring up the public against those responsible, as they did especially against the Sufi philosophers? And why did “the Sunnis unanimously reject Sufism,”[^V3-415] when Sunni doctrine was almost always the religious and ideological foundation of Abbasid rule, apart from the period of al-Maʾmūn?

Claude Cahen points us toward the answer: “Islam rarely behaves intolerantly in cases where politics is not involved.”[^V3-416]

In reality, the ideological position of the Sufi movement did not always remain confined to philosophy. At times, it became practical action.

> “In the year 200 AH, a group appeared in Alexandria who were called Sufis. They commanded what was right and forbade what was wrong, and they opposed the ruler in his actions…”[^V3-417]

Likewise:

> “Those in power during the Abbasid period, as well as the followers of official Sunnism, had little sympathy for these Sufis, who showed contempt for high rank and condemned the moral corruption of members of the court. But the people in general respected them, because they criticized the rich and listened to the complaints of the destitute (…)”[^V3-418]

And:

> “The conditions prevailing in the country were turning people’s attention toward Sufism.”[^V3-418]

Another account tells us:

> “The Sufis were not content simply to give human feelings a divine character. They also wanted to give human will this same divine character, and on that basis they claimed that this supposedly divine will possessed divine power over everything. Through doctrines of this kind, they posed the greatest dangers to the peace and stability of the state. Around 300 AH/912 CE, the number of people accused of heresy increased noticeably. Then, in 309 AH/921 CE, al-Ḥusayn ibn Manṣūr al-Ḥallāj was put to death in a horrific manner. He was given a thousand lashes, his hands and feet were cut off, and he was burned…”[^V3-419]

The influence of the Sufis also meant that:

> “The way people understood the ideal Islamic life began to change. They came to see it from a perspective different from the one approved by the teachings of the Sunni schools. The Sufis therefore had a powerful influence on the masses who came under their sway, and people’s admiration for the military character of the heroes of Islam began to decline. As a result, toward the end of the ninth century CE, when an intolerant Sunni spirit became dominant in Baghdad, many prominent Sufis were hunted down and persecuted in a manner resembling the Inquisition.”[^V3-420]

There is further evidence that, among its best and most principled figures, Sufism could represent a revolt against unjust rulers and the exploiting class:

> “Hārūn al-Rashīd was once walking with his attendants,[^V3-421] servants, and slaves ahead of him when a Sufi shouted: ‘Hārūn! You have exhausted both the people and the animals.’ Another Sufi told him: ‘Every other person is responsible for himself, but you are responsible for everyone.’ The Sufis used many different ways to rebuke rulers and call them to account (…) Politicians tried to buy the Sufis’ religion and their consciences, offering whatever price was necessary to make them keep silent about injustice and corruption. The sincere refused. The professionals accepted.”[^V3-422]

For all these reasons, it has been said that Sufi teachings, “in their popular form, brought together great numbers of Muslims,”[^V3-423] and that “people became attached to the Sufis and sought their blessing.”[^V3-424]

This also explains why the vizier Abū Ḥāmid feared the anger of the public after al-Ḥallāj’s trial. He tried to distance himself from responsibility for the execution and spread that responsibility among everyone involved. He gathered the witnesses and jurists who had approved the sentence and instructed them to assemble around the place where al-Ḥallāj had been crucified and all cry out together:

> “Yes! Kill him! His death is for the good of the Muslims!”[^V3-425]

These, then, were the two sides: the attitude of the authorities toward the Sufis, and the attitude of the masses from the lower and middle social groups.

At their real social level, both positions arose from class interests, whether the people involved were conscious of this or not. The class dimension is an objective reality. It does not stop being real simply because people lack class consciousness.

The class that holds economic and political power, however, usually has a highly developed sense of its own class interests. Because it controls the means of power and domination, it not only protects its own class ideology but also tries to impose that ideology on the opposing class. At times, the oppressed can even become more zealous in defending that ideology than the people who produced it in the first place.

Yet in the case we are discussing, the ordinary people seem to have possessed something we might call a class “instinct” toward a social order that relentlessly oppressed and impoverished them. Perhaps it was this class instinct that allowed them to recognize which side to support in the conflict between the authorities and the Sufis.
