Their support, however, had little effect because it remained limited in scale. We can explain this weakness through two factors. First, the ordinary population remained economically, socially, and culturally underdeveloped. Second, Sufi thought itself was unable to give its ideological content a realistic form that could actually be put into practice. It therefore could not become a full expression of the ideology of those masses within the historical conditions of the time.

One question remains: the attitude of the conservative jurists toward the Sufis.

We have already touched on this during our discussion. In our view, the jurists followed the authorities and supported their ideology. Their position was not an independent one genuinely driven by a desire to preserve the beliefs of Islam and defend the *sharia*, as they themselves claimed and as later scholars often explain it.

This is why some researchers, especially modern ones, place the persecution of the Sufis on the wrong historical ground. They reduce the issue to a struggle between jurists and Sufis. One explanation describes the jurists’ hostility to the Sufis as something that:

> “did not appear until the Sufis had organized themselves into groups and communities, disciples had gathered around their shaykhs, and ordinary people had begun to flock to these circles. People started drinking from the spring of Sufi sainthood and seeking blessings from the great Sufi masters. The Sufis therefore became the focus of public attention and took a leading place among Muslims as a whole. Before this, it had been the jurists who held this position in Muslim society (…) The jurists consequently became resentful of the Sufis. They began accusing them of wrongdoing, plotting against them, and circulating their sayings and ecstatic utterances in ways that made them appear to be unbelievers who had strayed from the faith. This then made it lawful to kill them, burn them, and drive them from their homes.”[^V3-426]

Under this explanation, a tragic historical conflict becomes little more than a personal rivalry between two groups competing for leadership by trying to attract the ordinary population.

To shift the issue away from its real social basis in this way is, in the end, to strip the Sufi movement—and Sufi thought in particular—of its ideological content. We cannot say whether this interpretation is deliberate and follows from a particular ideology, or whether it simply comes from a shallow understanding of the issue.

The tragedy of the Sufis in medieval Islamic history deserves a much deeper explanation, grounded in the historical conditions of the time.

And this interpretation does something else that takes us just as far from reality. Just as it strips Sufi thought of its revolutionary content, it also strips the ordinary people’s support for the Sufis of its revolutionary social meaning. Their position is turned into a purely religious one, supposedly driven by a desire “to drink from the spring of Sufi sainthood and seek blessings from the great Sufi masters.”

As though these people had no other problems. As though nothing else occupied their lives or demanded their attention except deciding where to drink from the spiritual spring and where to seek “religious blessings”: from the jurists, or from the Sufis!

### 4 — Sufism of the Orders

Finally, we should make clear that our study of Islamic Sufism, and the conclusions we have drawn from it, concern Sufism mainly in its philosophical form.

This means that our study is limited to the periods when Sufism was an intellectual activity built on certain theoretical foundations. Those foundations had an ideological direction that we have identified at several points in this study. This is why we have been justified in treating Sufism as one form of Arab-Islamic philosophical thought in the Middle Ages, though one with its own distinctive character and its own theory.

But Sufism also had another side: practical conduct and a particular way of life. During the periods we have been studying, this practical side existed alongside its theoretical and philosophical side without overwhelming it.

Later, however, Sufism began to turn into a one-sided movement. Physical and mechanical practices gradually came to dominate philosophical reflection. Eventually, these developed into systems, traditions, and purely formal rituals. The Sufi movement then split into different *ṭarīqas*, or orders, and separate shaykh-led groups, based on little more than these theatrical formalities.

At that point, the movement finally lost its ideological content. It even turned into a reactionary and parasitic movement that played a dangerous role in numbing society and serving the ideology of reactionary classes.

## Summary of Chapter Two

### 1

#### Why Study Sufism as Part of Philosophy?

I was led to do so because I found in the Sufi intellectual tradition discussions built on philosophical foundations, principles, and concepts. I also found that Sufi theory relied on philosophical ideas when dealing with the two central questions of existence and knowledge.

I therefore came to the conclusion that, in this respect, Sufi thought forms part of Arab-Islamic philosophy. But it is a distinct part, with its own character and its own theory.

There was another reason that convinced me of the importance of studying “Islamic Sufism” from an ideological point of view. When I reread this Sufi intellectual tradition while preparing the material for this book, I became increasingly aware of how closely Engels’s famous words applied to it:

> “Throughout the Middle Ages, revolutionary opposition to feudalism continued to appear. Depending on the circumstances of the time, this opposition took the form either of mysticism, of open heresy, or of armed revolt.”[^V3-427]

Before writing this chapter, I reread “Islamic Sufism” once again. It seemed to me that Engels, in these words, might almost have been speaking about “revolutionary opposition to feudalism” in the state of the caliphate, and about the very same forms through which this opposition appeared in Arab-Islamic society.

This made me even more certain that my work would be seriously incomplete if I left Sufism out of the study.

After finishing the chapter, rereading it, and comparing it with as many modern studies of “Islamic Sufism” as I could find, I felt the satisfaction of having done something new in this field of thought.

### 2

This chapter on Sufism in Islam is divided into two main sections.

The first section deals with Sufism as a form of practical conduct that did not yet depend on a theoretical foundation from outside Islam. This section is limited to the ascetic movement that appeared in the early Islamic period. That movement was influenced by the factional political struggle that broke out at the beginning of Islam among leading Companions of the Prophet over who should hold the caliphate after him.

Because this ascetic movement arose partly as a reaction to that struggle, and because ascetic withdrawal represented a definite position toward the political parties involved, I concluded that its main feature was that it expressed a **political position**.

I therefore gave the first section the title: **“Practical Sufism: A Political Position.”**

The second section deals mainly with Sufism as a body of theoretical thought. This is where Sufism in the full sense begins.

The theoretical foundation on which the Sufis now established themselves as an independent intellectual current in Arab-Islamic society involved a new understanding of the Islamic concept of *tawḥīd*, the oneness of God. This new understanding also contained a rejection of the theoretical basis of the ruling ideology of the Islamic social order, which rested on a system of absolute despotic government.

I therefore concluded that the main feature of philosophical and theoretical Sufism was that it expressed an **ideological position containing a revolutionary tendency**.

For this reason, I gave the second section the title: **“Theoretical Sufism: An Ideological Position.”**

The detailed plan of the two sections is as follows:

The first section contains eight parts:

1. **On Method.**
2. **The Ascetic Movement: Its Social and Political Origins.**
3. **Embryonic Sufism within the Ascetic Movement.**
4. **Summary, and the Logic Behind the Summary.**
5. **The Development of the Term.**
6. **Historical Continuity: The Ascetic Movement as a Way of Life.**
7. **Stones in the Stagnant Pond: Movement Toward Sufism.**
8. **On the Threshold of Sufism.**

The eighth part deals with two subjects: first, divine love and Rābiʿa al-ʿAdawiyya; and second, “knowledge” and Maʿrūf al-Karkhī.

The second section contains four parts and a conclusion:

1. **A Decisive Age.** This deals with the first Abbasid period. I argue that this period had two main features: the end of the Umayyad principle of ethnic group solidarity, or *ʿaṣabiyya*, and the strengthening of the “theocratic” character of the Islamic caliphate. Both developments appeared on two levels: the intellectual and the ideological.

2. **The Ideology of the Caliphate: What Were Its Foundations?**

3. **Another Introduction to Sufism.** This gives a methodological explanation of why we should study the Islamic Sufi tradition today. The purpose is to understand the place of that tradition in the present effort to rebuild the ideology of the progressive forces of contemporary Arab society.

4. **Sufism as Philosophy.** Here I begin the study of Sufism as philosophical thought and examine the foundations of Sufi philosophy. From a theoretical point of view, this is the most important of the four parts in the second section.

I divided the discussion of Sufi philosophy into five subjects:

**First: The Theory of Knowledge.**

**Second: The Philosophy of the Outward and the Inward.**

**Third: The Sufi Position on Islamic Law.**

**Fourth: Illuminationist Sufism.** This subject is divided as follows:

A. **The Ismaili Theory.**

B. **An Important Attempt to Explain the World: The Ismailis.**

C. **Suhrawardī’s Illuminationism.** This is discussed through the following subjects:

1. The Intermingling of Philosophies.
2. Features of Suhrawardī’s Age.
3. A Theory of Existence and Knowledge: The Illuminationist Metaphysical Structure.
4. Between Absolute Divine Transcendence and the Rejection of Transcendence.
5. The Material World: A Platonic Position.
6. The Primacy of Essence.
7. Sufi Vision and Illumination.
8. Prophecy, the Prophet, and Islamic Law.
9. The World: Without Beginning and Without End.
10. The Eternity of Motion and Time.

D. **The Real Basis of the Idea of Intermediaries Between God and Humanity.**

E. **Revolutionary—but “Dead”!**

**Fifth: The Unity of Being: Ibn ʿArabī.** This subject is divided as follows:

1. The Concept of the Unity of Being.
2. The Perfect Human Being.
3. The Real Human Being.
4. The Relationship Between the Universal and the Individual.
5. A Concluding Reading of Ibn ʿArabī.

This concluding reading dealt with three sides of the question.

The first: the basic contradiction within the Sufi concept of *tawḥīd*, together with Mullā Ṣadrā al-Shīrāzī’s position on the issue.

The second: Ibn ʿArabī between the eternity of the world and its creation in time.

The third: the question of the human being in Ibn ʿArabī’s theory.

The conclusion deals with three questions:

1. **Sufi Determinism.**
2. **Rational Thought in Sufi Philosophy.**
3. **The Persecution of the Sufis and of Sufism.**

The third question has two sides. The first concerns Sufi thought itself. The second concerns the attitudes taken toward it by the authorities, by the masses among the lower and middle social groups, and by the jurists. This final question casts one last light on the ideological character of Sufi thought.

These are the detailed outlines of the structure of the study. Now that we have seen them as a whole, we can give a short summary of their contents in the same order.

### 3

#### A — “Practical” Sufism: A Political Position[^V3-428]

##### 1 — On Method

In this section, I discussed the views of scholars who have studied Islamic Sufism and argued that all of them are one-sided.

They treat Sufism as a purely inward and personal movement, confined to an inner experience completely cut off from social reality. When they look for its origins, they find them only in outside influences such as Christianity, ancient Gnosticism,[^V3-429] Platonism, or Neoplatonism. They give no place at all to the social forces within Arab-Islamic society itself, where the Sufi movement actually arose. None of them discusses these forces.

I also criticized bourgeois psychologists who see Sufis only as “a type of mentally ill person,” as well as empiricists and rationalists who reject the phenomenon of Sufism because it does not fit the standards and rules of rational logic.

Despite their very different approaches, all these views begin from the same point: they see only one side of the subject.

What each of them overlooks are the historical conditions—economic, social, and political—in which the Sufi movement appeared.

I also discussed the claim that “Sufism is a universal phenomenon connected with every religion.” We have no objection, of course, to describing it as a universal phenomenon. What we reject is the idea that Sufism must necessarily be connected with religion, and that its universal character can be explained on that basis.

The universality of Sufism has a historical basis instead.

Bourgeois scholars have noticed similarities among the major forms of Sufism and mysticism found around the world. They usually limit these to Indian, Christian, and Islamic mysticism. But the similarities among them do not come from some necessary link with religion. They come from similarities among the historical conditions in which these three forms developed—in other words, similarities in social relations and in the general conditions surrounding those relations.

This similarity, however, is relative rather than absolute.

Our position is based on the view that mystical phenomena, wherever and whenever they appear, are fundamentally the products of particular social relations under particular historical conditions.

We therefore do not deny mystical phenomena or their universal character. What we reject is an idealist and metaphysical explanation of them.

Following the method of historical materialism, we explain them through the relationship between the individual human being and society, or between a person’s consciousness and social activity.

At the same time, we do not deny that human thought is a complex process. Thought is fundamentally shaped by social conditions, but it also develops an internal logic of its own. During its complex development, that internal movement can become relatively independent. It may sometimes move in a direction whose connection with immediate social reality is not obvious.

But the fact that this connection is not obvious does not mean that it does not exist.

##### 2 — The Ascetic Movement: Its Social and Political Origins

Here I described how an ascetic movement began during the first century AH, the seventh century CE, among a number of prominent Muslim figures in the fields of culture and law.

The movement began when these figures withdrew from social and political activity and devoted themselves entirely to Islamic religious practice. Their withdrawal was a negative reaction to the political struggles shaking the new Arab society, which was only then beginning to form as a society rather than as a collection of separate tribes.

These conflicts began to take openly violent and bloody forms after the killing of the third caliph, ʿUthmān. He was killed during a popular uprising protesting his favoritism toward senior Umayyad officials in the provinces, who were becoming extremely rich at the expense of the public interest and of the wider population.

His death led to political wars between ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib and his supporters on one side, and the Umayyads and their supporters on the other.

The ascetics withdrew from this political struggle as a form of protest against both sides. Their withdrawal was therefore itself a political position toward these events.

At first, however, it was a purely negative position: they withdrew rather than acted. Later, after the Umayyad state had taken shape, this position developed into open opposition to the social and political conditions produced by Umayyad despotism.

The study gives historical examples of this change in the ascetic movement and of ascetics siding with those who opposed social and political oppression.

Here we also find the view of the Hungarian Orientalist Goldziher, who supports our argument that “the tendency toward asceticism was connected with revolt against the existing authorities, and that many Muslims, in protest against a government and social order they rejected, withdrew into ascetic seclusion.”[^V3-430]

The ascetic movement at this stage had two main features.

First was its political content: a rejection of the social and political conduct of the Umayyad authorities.

Second was the form this rejection took in everyday life. The ascetics withdrew from all forms of social activity and devoted themselves to religious worship. They forced themselves to endure poor food and clothing, stayed awake at night in worship, and lived with a growing sense of sadness and fear over the consequences of imagined “sins.”

At times, this was accompanied by openly expressing opinions about the disturbing political conflicts around them, as al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī did later in his life.

Some ascetics even joined armed uprisings against the state. One example was Saʿīd ibn Jubayr al-Asadī al-Kūfī (45–95 AH/712 or 713 CE).

These features were found among the ascetics of Basra, Kufa, Egypt, and Syria, because the social and political conditions in all these Arab regions were broadly similar at the time. The study gives examples of ascetics from each of them.

##### 3 — Embryonic Sufism within the Ascetic Movement

But it would be wrong to treat this ascetic movement as a political movement in a simple and direct sense. It also had an indirect intellectual side.

Asceticism did not remain a matter of conduct alone, as it had been at first. Over time, it also developed an intellectual basis. We therefore have to consider the movement through the internal development of its ideas.

Seen in this way, the ascetic movement was already carrying the seeds of new ways of looking at life and the universe.

It also contained the beginnings of new ways of interpreting Islamic religious texts. These interpretations would eventually produce new positions on Islamic law, prophecy, the imamate, and other Islamic concepts. They also opened the way toward the idea of a new source of knowledge alongside divine revelation, and toward a new understanding of the human being’s place in existence.

These early tendencies later became the basis of Sufi thought.

Asceticism was therefore a preparatory stage for Sufism. Sufism would later become another form taken by Arab-Islamic philosophy and would play a noticeable role in the ideological struggles that followed.

How can we see the beginnings of Sufism within this ascetic movement?

Several statements by early ascetics already contain features of Sufi thought that would later develop into philosophical theories about knowledge and existence:

1. A text by ʿĀmir ibn ʿAbd Qays points toward a form of certain knowledge based on something other than simple faith.

2. This still-vague idea of certain knowledge within the ascetic movement became the theoretical basis of the Sufi idea of knowledge through what came to be called *unveiling* and *direct vision*.

3. The relationship between asceticism and Sufism was therefore not limited to the practical conduct of the ascetics. There was also an intellectual line running from asceticism into Sufism.

Alongside expressing a political position, asceticism also expressed a particular “theoretical” view of life that led the ascetics to turn away from worldly desires.

This “theoretical” position, in turn, led them to develop their own interpretations of Islamic texts in ways that matched their view of life. These interpretations became the beginnings of the method of symbolic interpretation later used by the Sufis. That method became a basis for their theories of the “outward and inward” meanings of things and of the dual nature of truth.

##### 4 — Summary, and the Logic Behind It

The appearance of asceticism around the middle of the first century AH was one result of the conflict breaking out within the new society.

This tells us that the phenomenon was also connected with what lay beneath the visible political conflict: the beginnings of class struggle were already stirring deeper within society.

The bloody political struggles at the surface were expressions of class tensions building up at the social base. One important factor was the enormous growth in the wealth of the Umayyad elite, beginning when they formed the circle around Caliph ʿUthmān and served as his representatives in the provinces.

These political and social struggles were then reflected in intellectual life. We see this in the ascetic movement, the Qadariyya, the Jabriyya, the conflict in Islamic law between those who relied on reason and those who relied on transmitted reports, and other movements.

The ascetic movement can therefore be described as having social, political, and intellectual sides. When we look at all of them together, we can see within asceticism the first embryonic form of something that later historical conditions would bring to life under the name of “Sufism.”

This conclusion about the ascetic movement follows a particular logic.

The first form taken by asceticism in this society was produced by the specific conditions of the society while it was still being formed. But the process of its formation was also a process of internal conflict. Society was developing into a class society.

We should therefore understand this internal upheaval not as the breakdown of society, but as the turbulence of a society growing from within. It was moving away from the remains of primitive social organization and a subsistence economy, first toward a fully feudal society and later toward a feudal-commercial one.

During a development of this kind, negative phenomena do not remain simply negative, and positive phenomena do not remain simply positive. Their roles can change as society itself changes.

This is why the ascetic movement gradually moved away from its original negative form of withdrawal and became a more active form of opposition to political despotism and social injustice. Ascetics increasingly found themselves in a position that attracted people suffering from social and political oppression.

We can put the same point another way.

Sufism began as a withdrawn and negative form of ascetic conduct. It then developed into an intellectual position that contained two kinds of opposition: religious, or ideological, opposition and political opposition, expressed in the rejection of social injustice and political despotism.

##### 5 — The Development of the Term

Historical evidence shows that the connection between asceticism and Sufism in ideas and conduct was accompanied by a connection in terminology.

The word “Sufi” was already being used for some ascetics before the ascetic movement had fully developed into Sufism in the historical sense of the term. There are examples of this in the Sufis’ own sources as well as in early and modern historical works.

There was therefore undoubtedly continuity between asceticism and Sufism in the use of the term over a period approaching a century, between the first and second centuries AH—the seventh and eighth centuries CE.

But during this period, the meaning of “Sufism” was still different from the philosophical and theoretical meaning it would acquire later. Historical evidence helps us identify what the term meant at this earlier stage.

##### 6 — Historical Continuity

We find that during the second century AH, the eighth century CE, asceticism largely kept its traditional character. It had not yet developed into theoretical Sufism.

Why did the movement remain in this form for so long?

Looking at its history, it seems that from the beginning of the second century AH the movement gradually became separated from the social conditions that had originally produced it.

It came close to losing its social dimension and taking on a mainly religious one instead. The outwardly religious form in which it had appeared began to overwhelm the social and political content that had originally given rise to it.

There were two reasons for this.

The first was that some ascetics withdrew so completely that they isolated themselves even from the ordinary affairs of daily life around them and immersed themselves entirely in worship. They became almost completely cut off from the movement of social reality.

The second was that social and political conflict in their society developed so far that it moved beyond the kind of political position represented by the ascetic movement under earlier conditions.

New historical conditions had pushed the conflict forward. Class divisions that had once been vague and undeveloped were now taking a more definite form, with the opposing social forces becoming somewhat clearer. The system was developing feudal features, while the demands of peasants and agricultural workers were also taking shape.

Opposition to the social order and its political authority therefore now required something more developed than the form of opposition represented by early asceticism. It required an ideological form of opposition.

But because the ascetic movement remained so closely tied to religion, it was not equipped to express an ideological opposition to the religious ideology of the ruling system.

It therefore fell behind the changes taking place in society.

The Qadariyya arose to meet the need for an ideological position under these new conditions. This new movement followed an intellectual direction opposite to that of the ascetic movement. The Qadariyya were rationalist, while the ascetic movement was moving away from rationalism.

##### 7 — Stones in the Stagnant Pond

After the middle of the second century AH, movement began to appear within the ascetic tradition itself, gradually pushing it out of its long period of stagnation.

We can see this new development in two ascetics: Dāwūd al-Ṭāʾī (d. 165 AH/782 CE) and al-Fuḍayl ibn ʿIyāḍ (d. 187 AH/803 CE).

Dāwūd al-Ṭāʾī began to move away from traditional asceticism in both his conduct and his ideas and closer to Sufi concepts. We can already see in him early signs of the idea of *walāya*, the special spiritual authority of the saint. As we shall see later, this idea would develop an ideological content opposed to the ideology of the ruling system.

The second figure, al-Fuḍayl ibn ʿIyāḍ, entered the ascetic life through a dramatic story that also contained an early form of another later Sufi idea: the *karāmāt*, or miraculous acts attributed to saints.

Other ascetics from Khurasan later entered the ascetic life through similar stories, among them Ibrāhīm ibn Adham al-Balkhī, ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Mubārak, and Shaqīq al-Balkhī.

This new phenomenon had several striking features. These men came from among princes, merchants, and the wealthy, and they travelled through many different parts of the Islamic lands.

They threw stones into the stagnant pond of asceticism and disturbed its stillness.

First, they brought back the idea of contact with society and reduced the exclusive concentration on religious worship. Second, they began to bring the question of knowledge into the meaning of asceticism itself. Both points are supported by the historical evidence and surviving texts.

Khurasan was the source of this new development, and that is significant.

We can explain this through two factors.

The first was cultural. It was connected with the historical character of the Persian people and their ancient intellectual heritage.

The second was that Khurasan had become a major center of struggle against the Arab conquerors. Class factors became mixed into this conflict as resistance grew against the social oppression imposed by the Umayyad state.

Together, these two factors help explain this different form of asceticism that emerged from Khurasan.

This form shows us much more clearly the social dimensions of the movement. We can see those dimensions reflected in the way asceticism was being pushed toward an ideological role. The movement was now becoming part of the historical process that would produce Sufism as a distinct force within both philosophy and ideological struggle.

##### 8 — On the Threshold of Sufism

By the end of the second century AH, the eighth century CE, asceticism stood on the threshold of Sufism.

Alongside the figures from Khurasan, new ascetics now appeared, bringing new forms of ascetic life that were much closer to Sufism itself.

Two figures are especially important here: Rābiʿa al-ʿAdawiyya and Maʿrūf al-Karkhī.

We have set aside the legendary stories with which historians surrounded both figures and concentrated instead on the core of the matter.

Rābiʿa, along with some of her contemporaries, was said to have introduced the idea of “divine love” into asceticism. Many sayings and stories were also attributed to her. We have reason to doubt them, because they do not fit the conditions of the period in which she lived, and in particular they do not fit the known circumstances of her own life.

Many parts of Rābiʿa’s life remain obscure, and a serious researcher cannot simply accept the explanations offered by historians and Sufis because they have been shaped into exaggerated legends.

If we limit ourselves to what can reasonably be believed about her life, we are left with a tragic picture.

Rābiʿa spent her youth in extreme poverty and homelessness. She eventually became a slave and lived under a “master” who had acquired ownership of her through captivity and treated her harshly.

Then an important part of her life disappears from the record. We do not know how she escaped slavery or how she came to live a life of solitude and worship.

The sayings attributed to her suggest both a personal tragedy and an obscure story of love.

From all of this, we conclude that what is called her “divine love” was really a form of extreme emotional sensitivity produced by severe deprivation, a harsh life, and an intense psychological shock that she also suffered through an emotional relationship with another human being. All of this was transformed into a highly refined imagination and a strong capacity for abstract thought. (Evidence and references.)

In broad terms, one historical fact is clear. Through the figure of Rābiʿa al-ʿAdawiyya and others like her, one of the most important ideas in later Sufism came into view during the second half of the second century AH: the idea of **“divine love.”** But we doubt that this idea, in the form later attributed to it, really belongs to that historical period itself.

As for Maʿrūf al-Karkhī, historians of Islamic Sufism claim that he spoke about “knowledge” in the specifically Sufi sense, and that he was deeply versed in religious learning. Maʿrūf died in 200 AH (815 CE). Yet the Sufi idea of this kind of mystical knowledge did not appear in the form attributed to him until before the middle of the third century AH—the ninth century CE.

There is another problem as well. In Maʿrūf’s time, the rationalist tendency within the Qadariyya movement was still dominant. It was this tendency that was advancing to meet society’s need, at that stage of its development, for an oppositional intellectual and ideological position. What we know about Maʿrūf al-Karkhī suggests that he himself was not equipped to play the role later assigned to him, except in the legendary form that we reject from the outset.

Even so, despite all our doubts about Maʿrūf’s role, we cannot doubt that history placed this second-century ascetic at the boundary between traditional Islamic asceticism and philosophical Sufism. And this was not only a matter of chronology. It was also true intellectually and ideologically.

Nor do we deny that a number of features connected with Maʿrūf point in the same direction. Taken together, they help us see why history placed this major ascetic, one of the leading figures of the second half of the second century AH, on the border between two stages of Sufism: the stage of ascetic practice, and the stage of what came to be called the “science of direct, experiential knowledge”—that is, philosophical Sufism.

### 4

#### B — “Theoretical” Sufism: An Ideological Position

##### 1 — A Decisive Period

By this we mean the first Abbasid period.

During this period, Sufism moved from its first stage—ascetic practice, which had expressed itself mainly as a political position—to its second stage, where it began to deal with theoretical thought and to become an ideological project.

Sufism changed in a fundamental way on two levels.

On the intellectual level, it developed into a distinct way of interpreting reality in human consciousness. This meant building its own theory and its own philosophical concepts.

On the social level, it became the expression of an ideological position.

This is what makes the period decisive.

Chronologically, this period runs from 132 to 232 AH (749–847 CE). We call it decisive because it forms a distinct historical whole whose effects continued into later centuries.

Its importance begins with the Abbasid Revolution and its victory over the Umayyad state. The creation of the Abbasid state brought two major changes.

The first was the end of the Umayyad principle of Arab ethnic partisanship in the treatment of non-Arab peoples.

The second was the strengthening of the religious position of the Islamic state. The state became fully theocratic, grounding its claim to rule in the Abbasids’ family connection to the Prophet through al-ʿAbbās, the Prophet’s uncle.

This form of state placed the Abbasid caliph in the position of supreme religious ruler, someone Muslims were required to obey. He presented himself as the representative of the authority of the religious law and as God’s caliph on earth.

Both of these features had consequences at the intellectual and ideological levels.

**First, at the intellectual level.**

The disappearance of the old principle of ethnic partisanship from state policy opened the way for many different Eastern intellectual, religious and doctrinal traditions, both older traditions and those contemporary with Islam. Persian traditions were especially important.

These currents were able to develop with considerable freedom through most of the first Abbasid period.

There was, of course, a sharp conflict between the two main ethnic loyalties within the society of the caliphate: Arab and Persian. There was also a meeting between its two major cultures: Arab culture and Persian culture, with its rich and ancient heritage.

But these were two different kinds of encounter.

The political and ethnic conflict was hostile. The cultural encounter, by contrast, involved coexistence and exchange.

Indeed, as the first conflict became sharper, interaction between the two cultures often became deeper and wider.

This led to many different works of Persian culture being translated into Arabic, and the translation movement helped produce a genuine intellectual blending of the two cultures.

**Second, at the ideological level.**

Of course, we do not draw a hard line between the intellectual and ideological sides of this process. Cultural coexistence and interaction also had ideological effects.

But the decisive factor here came from the despotic nature of the political system and from the theocratic character of the state itself. Cultural interaction helped this process along, but it was not the main cause.

The second major feature of the Abbasid state—its strengthening of absolute theocratic rule—produced its own opposite.

Its despotic way of governing worked against the interests of the productive forces in society and against the interests of the lower and middle groups. The harder the state pressed on people’s livelihoods and freedoms, the more clearly these groups could see the nature of the system.

The ruling class tried to strengthen the theocratic character of its rule and, through it, stabilize its religious ideology.

But the attempt failed.

In fact, it helped activate the very forces that were beginning to build a new social ideology opposed to the ideology of the state—that is, opposed to the ideology of the ruling class itself.

This reality began to shake and fracture the official ideology.

##### 2 — What Was the Ideology of the Caliphate?

Once the Islamic caliphate became a state in the full sense of the word, it presented itself as the political authority of the social order and as the representative of Islam as a divine religion.

It therefore defined its relationship with society in a very particular way. It claimed to rule by a “divine” authority that could not legitimately be challenged, let alone rejected, and could not be changed, let alone overthrown.

It is clear that this claim to represent divine authority was a reflection of the reality of absolute political despotism—not the other way around.

Islamic religious law, the *sharia*, provided the theoretical foundation for this reality.

The theory of the *sharia* rested on the idea of **tawḥīd**, the absolute oneness of God: one God with no partner and no equal.

This idea formed the core of the ideology of the caliphate.

The absolute oneness of God, completely separate from every created thing, became the image in which the “oneness” of the absolute ruler—the caliph—was reflected. It also reflected the exclusive position of the ruling group, which allowed no other group in society to share its power.

The *sharia* was also presented as the only link between human beings and God.

This meant that the ideology of the state required the state itself to become the sole authority over that single link.

In this way, the state claimed the right to control what people thought and to determine the sources from which they were allowed to gain knowledge.

This is why the ideological and political role of thinkers who were trying, with a certain degree of spontaneity, to formulate a new ideology for their society became so important. They were trying to create something that could confront the dominant ideology of the state.

Such an attempt could appear only when social development had advanced far enough to make it possible—when intellectual development was moving alongside social development.

And during the first Abbasid period, as we have already seen, intellectual life was extraordinarily active.

The general intellectual climate was therefore capable, in one form or another, of taking on an ideological role.

##### 3 — Another Introduction to Sufism

Our purpose in studying Sufism is not to study Sufism for its own sake, nor is our purpose purely philosophical.

Our aim is to place Islamic Sufism in its historical position within the attempt to build a new ideology for Arab-Islamic society under those historical conditions.

But even this is not simply a historical goal.

To study the past as though knowledge of the past were enough in itself is to turn the past into something sacred. We reject that approach.

The past matters to us only if we see it as something alive and moving, always changing and continually becoming part of the present and the future.

From this point of view, we study Sufism not only to understand its place in the ideological transformation of the past, but also to understand its place in the process taking place in our own time: the effort to rebuild the ideology of contemporary Arab society.

### 5

#### 4 — Philosophical Sufism

The conditions that were maturing during the third century AH—the ninth century CE—and making ideological change possible were the same conditions that were preparing Arab philosophical thought to change its position in relation to *kalām*, or systematic theology. We explained this earlier in Chapter One.

These conditions were marked by increasingly visible forms of social conflict rooted in objective class pressures.

Such conditions were bound to produce new forms of consciousness, including new ideological forms.

Philosophy is, by its nature, one form of ideology.

It was therefore natural for these historical conditions to push philosophical thought out of its dependence on Muʿtazilite *kalām* and allow it to take an independent place among the forms of Arab thought of the period.

Once it became independent, philosophical thought could begin to work toward an ideological change suited to the needs of the historical moment. Philosophy freed from the theological foundations of *kalām* was better able to confront the official theological ideology.

The first expressions of this independent philosophical thought appeared in two forms: rationalist philosophy, represented by al-Kindī, and philosophical Sufism.

What, then, were the theoretical foundations of Sufi philosophy?

The following sections examine its main foundations.

##### First — Theory of Knowledge

The various forms of Arab-Islamic thought—*kalām*, philosophy, Sufism, jurisprudence, and the theory of jurisprudence—all had one thing in common.

They agreed that the ultimate object of knowledge was God.

At the center of this question stood the oneness of God, or *tawḥīd*.

They also agreed that human beings could not grasp the true reality or essence of God. A person could know that God exists, that God is one, and that in His oneness He is absolutely unlike anything else.

Sufism began from this same theological ground, just as the other forms of Arab thought did.

What made Sufism different was the way it interpreted these shared ideas.

On the surface, its interpretation did not reject them. But in practice, the path it followed ultimately led toward their rejection.

The Sufi theory of knowledge cannot, however, be explained only through these theological roots. Those theological roots themselves grew out of a particular social reality.

So we must ask two questions: What was the content of the Sufi theory of knowledge? And how was it related to the social reality in which it developed?

We can begin with the first Sufi who tried to express Sufism in philosophical terms: Dhū al-Nūn al-Miṣrī, who died in 245 AH (859 CE).

Dhū al-Nūn drew a clear line between three kinds of knowledge.

The first was knowledge based purely on faith.

The second was rational knowledge based on proof.

The third was Sufi knowledge.

He described this third kind as belonging especially to God’s saints—those joined to God, who “see” Him with their hearts, until the Truth, meaning God, reveals to them what He has revealed to no one else in the world.

In its basic philosophical direction, this is clearly an idealist view of knowledge.

Sufi knowledge, as we see here, is knowledge of the heart. It comes through the direct “vision” of God. It does not depend on reason or on the senses.

This is where the Sufi problem of knowledge begins.

But this kind of knowledge also has other features, and these are what reveal its ideological meaning.

First, those who possess this knowledge are the “saints of God.”

Second, the saints possess *karāmāt*—miraculous powers. In Sufi belief, they can perform acts that break the ordinary laws of nature, much as prophets are believed to perform miracles in religious traditions.

Third, God reveals to the saints what He has not revealed to anyone else in the world—even, in this view, to the prophets themselves.

The saints therefore rise above the prophets.

These points are extremely important for our subject.

They create a new relationship between the human being and God.

They place the human being in direct contact with God.

And this is where the ideological position begins to appear.

We have already described the official ideology of the state as placing the relationship between God and the human being inside the *sharia*. In official ideology, the *sharia* is the one mediator between humanity and God.

Sufi knowledge, by contrast, crosses over this mediator and establishes a direct connection with God.

##### Second — The Theory of the Outer and Inner Meaning: Interpretation

This question is a branch of the Sufi theory of knowledge, or perhaps a consequence of it.

It also provided Sufis with a way around the Islamic order under which they lived.

They wanted to remain within the world of Islam even though they had moved beyond one of Islam’s basic foundations: the *sharia*.

Their solution was to accept the texts of the *sharia* outwardly, then use that outward acceptance as the starting point for moving beyond the *sharia* itself.

They accepted the texts, but developed a theory of **outer and inner meaning**.

According to this theory, a religious text does not have to mean what its outward wording appears to say. Behind the outward meaning lies an “inner” meaning that may be very different.

And who decides what this inner meaning is?

The Sufis themselves.

According to their own theory of knowledge, they alone have the ability to uncover the hidden secrets inside the *sharia*.

But this process of discovery eventually led them to the conclusion that the *sharia* was not necessary for knowing God at all, since they believed they could know God directly.

The theory of outer and inner meaning also breaks apart the idea that there is one single religious truth.

In official ideology, truth is one: it is the *sharia*.

The Sufis, however, say that the *sharia* is only the outward truth. Behind it lies an inner truth, and this inner truth is the essential one.

It is the inner truth that they follow, not the outward one.

The final result, therefore, is again the rejection of the *sharia*.

To support their theory of outer and inner meaning, the Sufis argued that the division of Qurʾānic and prophetic texts into outward and inward meanings already had a basis in the Qurʾān, in the sayings of the Prophet, and in the traditions of the earliest Muslims and of the Shīʿa. (Texts and evidence.)

This gave them a way to defend themselves against conservative Muslims.

On this basis, they claimed the right to interpret Islamic texts and to make their “inner” meanings conform to Sufi ideas without embarrassment.

They were also helped by the fact that the idea of *taʾwīl*—interpretation beyond the plain meaning—had never been defined with complete precision in official Islamic terminology.

The Sufis used this idea of interpretation, together with the theory of outer and inner meaning, in much the same way that some of the more radical Shīʿī sects had used it before them.

They pushed it far enough to argue that the *sharia* itself could be set aside if this was where interpretation ultimately led.

Earlier, when discussing the positions of the Shīʿī sects, we noted something similar.

If we strip away the supernatural and subjective elements in these ideas, we can still see indirect signs of a historical human attempt to discover the correct relationship between human consciousness and the external world—even though that attempt took the form of supernatural images, fantasies and abstractions.

##### Third — The Sufi Position on the Sharia

The previous discussion of interpretation has already made this position fairly clear.

But now we need to define it more precisely on the basis of philosophical Sufi theory itself.

The philosophical Sufi position toward the *sharia* follows directly from its theory of knowledge.

Once Sufism makes the relationship between God and the human being direct, through an inward “vision” of the heart, every mediator becomes unnecessary—even the mediation of revelation and prophecy.

Sufism also moves beyond the official Islamic understanding of *tawḥīd*, which rests on the absolute transcendence of God.

In other words, it removes the idea of an absolute separation between God and the human being.

Within the official conception, this separation means that the human being has no will beside the will of God and no power to grasp the absolute oneness of God.

Once the Sufi theory of knowledge breaks with this separation, it necessarily breaks with the *sharia* as well, because the *sharia* is the mediator that preserves that separation.

We have already seen that this rejection of the *sharia* does not contradict the Sufi interest in interpretation, even though interpretation seems outwardly to accept the religious law.

As we explained, that acceptance was formal. It was a way of reaching rejection.

But the real meaning of this result goes beyond the rejection of religious law.

At its deepest level, it is a rejection of the social order itself.

In other words, rejecting the *sharia* was not the final goal of the Sufis. That was only the philosophical side of the process.

By breaking down the wall separating God from the human being—the *sharia*—they were, in reality, trying to break down the wall separating one human being from another: the wall between the head of the social system and society itself.

The *sharia* was the ideological foundation of that political order.

Using this foundation, the system ruled the people through absolute despotism, in the same way that it presented the authority of God as absolute.

The system therefore imagined God’s authority in the image of its own authority—not the other way around.

This, then, is where the Sufi theory of knowledge leads, despite the differences between the various schools that appeared within Islam under the name of philosophical Sufism.

This is the revolutionary ideological content of Sufi philosophy.

But the Sufis faced a serious problem.

When they rejected the official Islamic understanding of *tawḥīd*, they still held on to the idea of God’s absolute transcendence. Yet at the same time, their theory of knowledge required a direct relationship between the human being and God.

They therefore built their understanding of *tawḥīd* on a contradiction that could not be resolved.

Absolute transcendence contradicts any direct relationship with God, because any direct relationship breaks down that absolute transcendence.

All the later Sufi ideas of “annihilation” in God, “union,” “indwelling,” unity of being, and “illumination”—as we find them in the schools of al-Ḥallāj, al-Suhrawardī and Ibn ʿArabī—struggle with this same problem.

None of the solutions they proposed could finally resolve it. (Evidence and texts.)

Even so, there are differences between these schools in the way they understand the external world.

Al-Ḥallāj and his contemporaries, for example, still accepted the objective existence of the external world.

Their theories of divine indwelling and union required two existing sides—the world and God—and therefore recognized the existence of both.

### 6

#### Fourth — Illuminationist Sufism

In this form of Sufism, we encounter something different from what we have seen so far.

A new structure appears, using concepts, categories and terms that had not belonged to third-century Sufism.

If some of these ideas had appeared earlier in one form or another, they had not yet developed into clear philosophical Sufi concepts and categories. (Third-century texts in which still-undeveloped illuminationist expressions appear.)

##### 1 — In Ismaili Theory

Before discussing illuminationist Sufism in its fully developed form—that is, the philosophy of al-Suhrawardī—we first need to discuss the inward-looking, esoteric theory of the Ismailis.

A number of ideas and tendencies appeared in Ismaili thought that formed a second step toward the illuminationist philosophy later developed by al-Suhrawardī. (The historical and doctrinal origins of Ismailism.)

The main point shared by Ismaili theory and Suhrawardī’s illuminationism is the existence of several intermediaries between the human being and God.

Earlier forms of Sufism had understood this relationship as direct.

The Sufi was annihilated directly in God, or God “indwelt” existence or the human being, or the human being and God entered into direct “union.”

There was no chain of intermediaries.

Ismaili theory introduces something new.

The relationship with God now takes the form of a hierarchy, represented in two parallel systems.

The first is human: seven imams and twelve *ḥujjas*, or religious representatives.

The second is cosmic: seven heavenly spheres and twelve signs of the zodiac.

In both systems we can hear an echo of the ancient Pythagorean reverence for the number seven.

But the Ismailis gave this Pythagorean element an Islamic form.

They taught that the imamate passed from Ismāʿīl ibn Jaʿfar al-Ṣādiq, one of the Shīʿī imams, to Muḥammad ibn Ismāʿīl.

They called Muḥammad ibn Ismāʿīl the **“Perfect Seventh Imam,”** because with him the cycle of seven imams—from ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib to Muḥammad ibn Ismāʿīl—was completed.

They connected this system of seven with the seven days of the week, the seven heavens and the seven planets.

Their theory of the intermediaries between God and the world took the following form.

God brought forth the **First Intellect**, which is fully perfect in actuality.

Through the First Intellect, God brought forth the **Soul**, which is not yet perfect.

The Soul contains a movement from imperfection toward perfection.

This movement has an instrument, and through that instrument the heavenly spheres came into being.

The Soul governs a circular movement in the spheres, and through this movement the simple elements of nature came into being. These form the basis of natural existence.

The Soul also governs a straight movement, and through this movement compound natural beings came into existence, including the human being.

Through this same process, individual souls became joined to their bodies.

Human beings differ from other natural beings because they have a special capacity to receive the flow of spiritual light.

The human world corresponds to the universe as a whole.

Just as the higher world contains Intellect and Soul, the human world also contains an Intellect and a Soul.

The Universal Intellect of the human world is represented by the prophet, whom the Ismailis call **“the Speaker.”**

The Universal Soul is represented by his successor, the imam, whom they call **“the Foundation.”**

This Soul is like an incomplete child moving toward perfection. It reaches perfection when it rises to the level of Intellect and unites with it under the influence of the heavenly movements.

And just as the celestial spheres are moved by the Universal Intellect and Universal Soul, individual souls and human beings in our world are moved in every age by the prophet and his successor.

Time itself moves in cycles of seven.

Eventually the final cycle arrives: the age of the Resurrection.

At that point, religious obligations are lifted and the revealed laws fall away.

##### B — A Distinctive Attempt to Explain the World

From this outline of Ismaili theory, we can see that it presents an ontological and a theory-of-knowledge scheme at the same time.

Existence and knowledge develop through one and the same process.

God brings forth the First Intellect.

The First Intellect produces the Soul.

Through its movement toward perfection, the Soul produces the heavenly spheres.

The circular movement of the spheres produces the simple elements.

Their straight movement produces compound beings.

The process finally reaches the human being in two ways: first as the creation of a being, and second as the reception of a flow of spiritual light—that is, knowledge.

We can also draw another conclusion from this scheme.

The First Intellect is eternal because it came into being through an eternal divine will.

What comes from it must therefore also be eternal.

The world, then, is eternal.

This is a materialist tendency within Ismaili theory.

The scheme also gives us a view of the origin of matter.

The simple natural elements appear as the basis of material beings, and these simple elements are tied to movement.

Their transformation into compound beings is also tied to movement.

This scheme may have a Stoic origin.

In any case, we can see here a materialist tendency in the theory of motion, even though it is built within a metaphysical framework.

We might say that the explanation of the world is metaphysical in form but begins from a materialist basis.

This appears in its recognition of the objective existence of the material world and in its treatment of movement as a basic principle of that world’s existence.

Can we therefore see here an early, undeveloped view of motion as the way matter exists, even though this view remains enclosed within an idealist and goal-directed philosophy?

##### C — Suhrawardī’s Illuminationism

**The interweaving of philosophies**

The first thing we notice when studying the illuminationist philosophy of Shihāb al-Dīn al-Suhrawardī, known as “the Executed” (549–587 AH / 1153–1191 CE), is that it brings rationalist philosophy—especially the Peripatetic tradition—together with Sufi philosophy.

Second, it also draws on ancient Eastern philosophies and on Greek philosophy.

Yet all of these elements are brought together within a broadly Islamic framework.

Third, al-Suhrawardī had absorbed these different sources and was aware of the relationship between his own illuminationist philosophy and the traditions from which it drew.

This is what we have called a kind of **universal awareness of human thought**.

Even so, al-Suhrawardī did not understand how each intellectual tradition was connected to its own environment and historical period, or how that connection related to the idea of universality.

**Features of the age**

Al-Suhrawardī’s age was marked by two major developments.

The first was the continued flourishing of intellectual life, even after al-Ghazālī had directed much of that intellectual energy against philosophy and, more broadly, against open thought.

The second was the appearance of a new political conflict.

This came from the wave of European colonial invasion into the East—the Crusades—combined with the violent conflicts among the Arab-Islamic rulers themselves.

At the same time, the crisis of the Islamic social order was becoming more severe.

The dominant form of production and the relations built around it were increasingly weak and unproductive.

This repeatedly produced mass uprisings rooted in class conflict and gave rise to new forms of struggle under the pressure of the crisis.

Yet none of these struggles succeeded in changing the social order in the interests of the masses.

Every form of resistance was eventually broken without achieving its goal.

Among freer intellectuals and thinkers, this repeated failure produced a growing despair about the possibility of changing a social reality filled with material and spiritual suffering for large parts of society.

But this despair could not develop into a positive revolutionary outlook.

Instead, it turned into a negative and nihilistic kind of “revolutionary” thought.

It rejected the material world.

In some forms, it even rejected the real existence of that world altogether.

It turned instead toward an unseen existence and built a metaphysical world from elements drawn from earlier philosophical traditions from many parts of the world.

The exact forms and combinations varied, but they all expressed the image of another kind of order—an imagined substitute for the real social order these thinkers rejected.

This is the basic real-world background of the Sufi world of illumination.

That world reached its fully developed philosophical form in the thought of Shihāb al-Dīn al-Suhrawardī.

**A system of both existence and knowledge**

This is the first major feature of the illuminationist world imagined by al-Suhrawardī and turned into a philosophical system in the sixth century AH, the twelfth century CE.

In this respect, his project resembles the earlier Ismaili construction of an inward, esoteric world.

In al-Suhrawardī’s philosophy, **light** is the basic principle of the existence of the entire universe.

It first flows from the **Light of Lights**, meaning God.

From there it descends level by level until it reaches the lowest point.

Every level is a form of luminous existence, but each contains less light than the level above it.

When this descent reaches the level of bodies—the material world—the light becomes weaker and weaker until it almost disappears.

This is the “dark” level of existence.

But al-Suhrawardī does not believe in two independent principles, light and darkness, as Zoroastrianism and Manichaeism do.

For him there is only one positive principle: light.

Darkness is simply the absence of light.

The theory of knowledge is built into this theory of existence.

Light is not only the principle of existence. It is also the principle of **truth**, and truth is the goal of knowledge.

Within this illuminationist world there are two movements.

One moves upward: the **act of vision**.

The other moves downward: the **act of illumination**.

Both movements are at once movements of existence and movements of knowledge. (Sources.)

**Transcendence—and the rejection of transcendence**

The Light of Lights includes the whole system, with all its levels and movements.

It is present throughout the system.

That presence takes two forms.

The first is the chain of emanation descending from the highest level to the lowest.

The second is God’s direct illumination of each individual part of the system, without passing through the chain.

God is therefore present throughout the whole world, and His presence is constant.

Yet at the same time, al-Suhrawardī accepts the Muʿtazilite idea that the attributes of God are identical with God’s own essence.

He does this in order to emphasize God’s absolute transcendence and oneness.

So we return once again to the central Sufi problem.

There is a contradiction between the absolute transcendence of God—which means His complete separation from the world—and His constant presence within the world—which means His connection with it.

**His position on the material world**

Al-Suhrawardī’s position toward the material world appears to us to be Platonic.

In other words, he denies that the material world has true existence of its own.

We can see this in the elaborate classification of his illuminationist universe.

In simplified form, he describes only a spiritual world, divided into two systems: one vertical and one horizontal.

The vertical system is generated level by level through the emanation of light.

It begins with the **Nearest Light**, or **First Intellect**, and continues down to the “lords of the species” in the ideal world.

Al-Suhrawardī also calls this the **Suspended World** and the world of “idols” or forms.

The horizontal system is different.

Its parts do not generate one another because they are equal in rank.

It consists of “lords of species,” each of which gives rise to a particular type of being and becomes the source of either a heavenly body or one of the earthly elements.

Everything in the material world therefore has a corresponding “type” in the heavenly, spiritual world.

This heavenly type is another version of the Platonic **Form**.

The material world, on this account, has no true independent existence.

It is only a shadow and a dependent reflection of these higher forms.

If we add to this understanding of existence al-Suhrawardī’s purely mystical and supernatural theory of knowledge, we can identify the general philosophical direction of his thought as **subjective idealism**.

**The primacy of essence**

This point makes al-Suhrawardī’s subjective idealism even clearer.

It follows logically from the way he understands existence.

Once he denies that the material world truly exists in its own right, essence must come before existence.

In the texts we have relied on here, al-Suhrawardī denies the concrete, objective existence of the physical body.

He treats its existence as something constructed purely by the mind.

This leaves little room for doubt that he gives priority to essence rather than existence.

He also states this position directly rather than leaving us to infer it.

He does so while discussing the philosophy of Aristotle and the Aristotelians.

If we compare al-Suhrawardī with the rationalist philosophers on this question, we find that both ultimately give priority to essence.

But they begin from different places.

Al-Suhrawardī starts from a theory of existence.

The rationalists begin from a theory of knowledge.

Yet the rationalists’ own theory of existence ultimately points them toward the opposite conclusion: the priority of existence.

**Visual perception and illumination**

Al-Suhrawardī’s position here follows the logic of his subjective idealism.

He rejects sensory knowledge even at the level of its most obvious physical instrument: the eye.

He denies that physical vision is simply an act carried out by the biological organs of sight.

Instead, he treats sensory vision as dependent on spiritual acts of illumination.

By doing this, he also blocks the ordinary path toward rational knowledge.

Reason normally begins with particular things received through the senses. It then strips away their individual sensory features and turns experience into concepts, categories and general ideas.

Al-Suhrawardī’s theory cuts off this route.

**Prophecy, the prophet and the sharia**

How should we understand the position of al-Suhrawardī’s illuminationist philosophy toward Islam as both a religion and an ideology?

We can approach the question from two sides.

The first concerns *tawḥīd*, the oneness of God.

The second concerns prophecy in general, the Prophet of Islam in particular, and the *sharia* as a social system.

On the question of *tawḥīd*, we have already seen his position in the section above on transcendence and the rejection of transcendence.

It is the general Sufi position, built around the contradiction we have already described.

On the second question, however, his position does not appear to agree with official theological ideology.

There is no clear place for prophecy or prophets in his illuminationist system.

Indeed, sages and prophets often seem to stand on the same level.

We see this, for example, in his Persian work *The Cry of the Simurgh*.

In *The Philosophy of Illumination*, the Prophet Muḥammad appears under the title **“the master of this religious law.”**

His name is not mentioned.

The phrase appears while al-Suhrawardī is discussing the highest spiritual levels reached by sages.

The only thing he says here about “the master of this religious law” is:

> “...as the master of this religious law reported concerning himself.”

This does not give us a clear position.

If anything, it suggests a vaguely negative one, because Muḥammad is placed on the same level as Plato, Hermes, Empedocles and Pythagoras.

In *The Intimations*, al-Suhrawardī includes a passage that mentions prophecy more generally.

From it, we might conclude that he accepts prophecy as a mediator between God and humanity.

But this book does not fully represent his illuminationist philosophy.

It is mainly an exposition, revision and discussion of Peripatetic philosophy.

And there is also a passage in *The Philosophy of Illumination* that contradicts it.

There al-Suhrawardī refers to the **“vicegerent of God on earth.”**

But his other texts make it clear that the human soul itself is the vicegerent of the “Highest Light” in the earthly world. It is the **“greater vicegerent.”**

Fire is also a “vicegerent,” but the **“lesser vicegerent.”**

In another passage he says that:

> “The world has never been empty of wisdom, or of a person who upholds it and possesses its proofs and demonstrations.”

Is this person the prophet?

No.

When we compare al-Suhrawardī’s texts with one another, the answer is clearly no.

As for his position toward the *sharia*, we can understand it first from the Sufi concept of *tawḥīd*, which we have explained more than once.

But it is also confirmed by a statement in which al-Suhrawardī says that those who reach “the station of the great ones among the prophets and sages” eventually reach a point where:

> “The words ‘he,’ ‘you’ and ‘I’ disappear in the sea of annihilation. There, commands and prohibitions fall away.”

In other words, the obligations of the *sharia* no longer apply to them.

The idea that religious law falls away for those who reach the highest stages of Sufi experience helps define the philosophical and ideological position of philosophical Sufism in general and illuminationist philosophy in particular.

It also defines their view of prophecy, revelation and religious law.

At its deepest level, this view becomes the theoretical expression of a rejection of the social order represented by the ideas of prophecy, revelation and *sharia*.

**The world is eternal**

Here we find another example of the lack of consistency that has marked idealist philosophies throughout history.

Al-Suhrawardī ultimately argues that the world has no beginning and no end, even though his illuminationist philosophy is built, as we have seen, on a subjective idealist foundation.

What is especially striking is that al-Suhrawardī clearly understood what this position meant.

He gave it a theoretical foundation through the idea that reason requires a necessary connection between a cause and its effect.

In this discussion, he defines the world as **everything other than God**.

He then divides the world into two parts: one eternal, the other originated.

The first includes the intellects and the heavenly spheres.

The second includes everything else—that is, the elements themselves.

But he then explains that the elements are only relatively originated, not absolutely originated.

They are “originated” only in relation to the higher part of the world.

Because they depend on that higher world, they too are eternal in another sense.

If we strip this idea of “relative origination” of its metaphysical form, we may find underneath it a vague awareness of movement, change and becoming within the “elements”—that is, within the material world.

Al-Suhrawardī expressed this awareness, in the terms of his own philosophy, as “relative origination.”

But another problem appears here.

Does his discussion of whether the elements are eternal or originated not contradict his earlier denial of their real existence?

To ask whether something is eternal or originated is already, in a sense, to recognize its objective existence rather than treating it as an illusion.

Whatever the answer to that question, one thing is clear.

Al-Suhrawardī understood the deeper meaning of saying that the world is eternal.

It means rejecting the idea of creation—or at least rejecting the theological claim that existence was created from nothing.

He says this explicitly:

> “Nothing can come from the Light of Lights and the dominating lights after having not existed.”

We can therefore find some materialist positions inside al-Suhrawardī’s idealist illuminationist philosophy.

The lack of consistency between his general idealist direction and these materialist positions is something we find in most idealist philosophies, as we have said more than once.

**And the eternity of motion and time**

This is another step toward a materialist position.

Its importance does not lie only in al-Suhrawardī’s acceptance of the eternity of motion and time.

It also lies in the fact that he connects the world necessarily with motion, and motion necessarily with time.

There can be little doubt that this idea drew mainly from two sources.

The first was the earlier ideas of the ancient Greek materialist philosophers.

The second was the progress made in the natural sciences by Arab thinkers during the four centuries before al-Suhrawardī, especially in astronomy, mechanics and physics.

His debt to this second source appears especially clearly in his acceptance of causal relations between all existing things.

He built his metaphysical illuminationist system on this law of causality, as we have already seen.

This is another example of the interaction between natural science and philosophy.

For this reason, al-Suhrawardī’s illuminationist universe is a universe in constant motion.

Every part of it moves continuously, both upward and downward.

This is also why, in his system, fire has “more movement and more heat than the other elements.”

All of this leads us to conclude that the dynamic movement of his philosophical system reflects the dynamic movement of material reality itself.

But in al-Suhrawardī’s thought, that movement still appears to come from outside things rather than from within them—from something other than the nature of their own real existence.

The inner contradiction between the supernatural structure of illuminationism and its material basis in reality reflects, in our view, the contradiction within the society in which al-Suhrawardī lived.

It also reflects the breakdown of social, political and intellectual values produced by the deep crisis and sharpening conflicts of that society.

##### D — The Real Basis of the Idea of “Intermediaries”

We now need to return to an important question that appears both in Ismaili esotericism and in al-Suhrawardī’s illuminationist philosophy.

The same idea had earlier roots in many movements and sects within the Islamic world, especially among the more radical sects.

We mean the idea of several celestial and “spiritual” intermediaries between the human being and God.

As we have seen, this idea became more prominent as the fourth century AH—the tenth century CE—advanced, and then became still more prominent in the fifth century AH and afterward.

These intermediaries are said to pour “divine lights” onto human beings who do not occupy high social positions.

The lights then raise those people to the rank of imam, prophet, or even divinity.

In our method, the explanation of this phenomenon must be connected to the wider changes in the social and political structure of the period, despite its supernatural appearance and its seeming distance from external reality.

When philosophical Sufism first took shape in the Islamic world, it opposed official theological theory by making the relationship between the human being and God direct, without intermediaries.

Why, then, did philosophical Sufism later change?

Why did these chains of intermediaries appear, as we have seen in Ismaili thought and illuminationist philosophy?

We believe the change resulted from changes in the political structure of the caliphate itself.

Earlier, state power had been built on absolute centralization.

The ideological rejection of that system therefore took the opposite form: a complete rejection of mediation.

The theoretical rejection of intermediaries became a way of rejecting the separation between God and the human being.

But from the caliphate of al-Mutawakkil onward, this political centralization began to break apart.

More and more ruling intermediaries appeared between the central caliphate and society, in the form of independent or semi-independent rulers in different regions.

At the same time, agricultural productive forces weakened, feudal relations of production became more strained, commercial economic power grew, and craft organizations became increasingly important in their particular historical form.

These changes brought the structural weakness of the social order increasingly to the surface.

Class conflict intensified, as did conflict among different groups inside the ruling class itself.

All of these changes were reflected in forms of thought, especially Sufi thought.

In our view, Sufi thought was in some ways more revolutionary than other currents, despite being deeply immersed in supernatural ideas.

Its revolutionary character came from its ideological meaning: it rejected the theoretical foundation of absolute despotic rule.

Our conclusion is that the social changes we have described transformed Sufi thought itself.

The earlier vision of a direct relationship with God became a vision of a relationship built through several intermediaries connecting earth to heaven and heaven to earth.

This expressed a change in the form of ideological rejection.

After the social and political changes described above, opposition was no longer aimed at one political authority alone.

It now had to confront several political authorities and several groups within a ruling class whose power had become divided.

These different groups shared the profits of the system, whether through force or compromise, at the expense of exploited workers, the poor and the dispossessed.

The chain of illuminationist intermediaries between earth and heaven was therefore a utopian intellectual image of the world these thinkers dreamed of.

It served as an imagined replacement for the broken chain of competing earthly authorities that existed in their own historical and social reality.

##### E — Revolutionary... but “Dead”

There can be no doubt that philosophical Sufi thought, both illuminationist and non-illuminationist, contained an ideological theory that was revolutionary in its own historical setting.

But there was a basic weakness at the heart of this theory.

It could not be realized.

That impossibility drained it of living force and left it “dead.”

The surprising thing is that, despite this inner weakness, it still terrified rulers.

### 7

#### Fifth — Unity of Being

In the history of Islamic Sufi thought, the idea of **“unity of being”** is especially associated with Ibn ʿArabī (560–638 AH / 1165–1240 CE).

In his Sufi system, the idea became established both as a philosophical term and as a developed Sufi doctrine.

His philosophy also sharpened the confrontation between the two main currents in Arab-Islamic thought.

On one side stood rationalist philosophy, represented by Ibn Rushd, who after Ibn Sīnā finally freed rationalist philosophy from the Sufi tendency.

On the other side stood Sufi philosophy, represented by Ibn ʿArabī, who made supernatural “vision” the final basis of knowledge.

The highly abstract content of Ibn ʿArabī’s theory of unity of being was one of the clearest expressions of this sharp confrontation.

##### 1 — The Meaning of “Unity of Being”

We first need to distinguish between two different meanings of unity of being.

The first is the meaning accepted by dialectical materialist philosophy.

The second is the meaning found, in different forms, across idealist philosophies.

For dialectical materialism, unity of being means the **material unity of the one real world**.

For idealist philosophies, it means some kind of “spiritual” unity between two worlds: the material world of reality and a world beyond nature, a metaphysical world.

This is the position of objective idealists.

For subjective idealists, by contrast, “unity of being” means that there is only one true existence: God, or the world of Platonic Forms, or one of the many other idealist versions of the same idea.

In general, Ibn ʿArabī’s theory of unity of being belongs to the idealist understanding.
