It moves between objective and subjective idealism, though its strongest tendency is toward subjective idealism, as we will see.

In my view, Ibn ʿArabī turned to this idea—whether consciously or not—in an attempt to solve the internal contradiction within the Sufi idea of *tawḥīd*.

That contradiction had trapped Sufism in a dead end.

Either the Sufi had to move outside Islam, or he had to abandon the Sufi “experience” itself, both in its combined theory of existence and knowledge and in its revolutionary ideological meaning.

The problem was this.

The Sufis insisted at the same time on two opposites: the absolute oneness of the divine essence and direct Sufi contact with that same divine essence.

But these two claims destroy one another.

If God is absolutely separate, direct union is impossible.

If direct union takes place, absolute separation has already been broken.

The Sufis therefore preserved neither the official idea of *tawḥīd* nor the full revolutionary meaning of breaking with its ideology.

Starting from this view of the Sufi problem, I believe Ibn ʿArabī tried to create a new idea of *tawḥīd* that might escape this dead end.

He expressed it through the theory of **unity of being**.

His answer was that only one true existence exists.

If there is only one existence, there can be no question of connection or separation, because connection and separation require two sides.

If there is only one side, the problem disappears.

This is how the issue presents itself to systematic analysis, whether Ibn ʿArabī himself consciously intended it or not.

His intention is not what determines our understanding of the problem.

What determines it is what his texts actually say.

The basic logic of those texts can be summarized as follows.

God willed to reveal Himself through His names, rather than through His essence, by bringing the world into existence.

The human being was the first form through which God revealed Himself.

The human being is therefore the origin of the world.

Humanity came into existence as a mirror of God’s existence: a mirror in which God could see Himself and through which He could know Himself and be known.

But this divine existence in the human being passed through two stages.

At first, the world—the human being—was only a kind of shadow or blurred mirror. It did not reflect God’s existence clearly.

This shadow-like state was existence **in potential**: a state of readiness and possibility.

Then, in the second stage, divine existence became clear.

The mirror became polished when this shadow received the divine breath, which is the “constant and everlasting manifestation”—eternal without beginning or end.

Once this manifestation took place, the human being came fully into being and became the clear mirror of the world.

From this we can draw several conclusions.

First, existence beginning with the human being is nothing but a particular form of the existence of God.

Second, God’s relationship with the world is the relationship between His names and their effects, because created things are the outward existence of those names.

Third, this existence is itself God’s purpose.

Through it, God sees Himself, and through it His hidden reality becomes visible.

The world is therefore necessary rather than temporary.

Fourth, the world is eternal.

It was not created in the sense of being brought into existence from nothing.

One Islamic theological principle therefore falls away: creation from nothing.

Another falls with it: the claim that the world had an absolute beginning.

Fifth, the human being is the highest of all beings.

The human being is the first place in which the world becomes manifest.

The world itself is the **“Greater Human Being.”**

Humanity is also the purpose of divine manifestation and the force that preserves the world’s continuing existence:

> “The world will remain preserved for as long as this Perfect Human Being exists within it.”

##### 2 — The “Perfect Human Being”

For Ibn ʿArabī, and for later Sufis influenced by him—especially ʿAbd al-Karīm al-Jīlī, who died in 832 AH (1428 CE)—the phrase **“Perfect Human Being”** expresses the idea that human beings differ in their degree of perfection.

The Perfect Human Being is the one who reaches the highest, divine level.

He becomes the “comprehensive and decisive word,” meaning that his “human constitution” gathers within itself all the names contained in the divine image.

This is the human being considered in terms of existence itself.

It is the purely divine side of humanity.

If we look at the human being as the link between the world and God, the Perfect Human Being becomes the meaning of the **prophet**—not one individual prophet, but prophecy as a type.

The **Muhammadan Reality**, understood as a universal type rather than simply as the individual human person Muḥammad, becomes one face of the eternal divine Reality.

If we look at the question from the side of knowledge, the Perfect Human Being is the person capable of becoming aware of his own inner humanity—that is, of recognizing himself as a form in which God’s existence appears.

But these three sides of the Perfect Human Being overlap completely.

They cannot really be separated from one another.

##### 3 — The Real Human Being

Within Ibn ʿArabī’s theory of unity of being, there is no absolute difference between the real human being who exists physically in the world and the Perfect Human Being.

The difference is only one of degree.

The logic of Ibn ʿArabī’s texts suggests that every human being is a Perfect Human Being in potential, even though in reality only prophets and the great Sufi spiritual leaders, the *quṭbs*, are said to have reached that level.

On this basis, Ibn ʿArabī’s description of the human being as:

> “originated and eternal, a formation forever renewed and everlasting”

must also apply to the real human being.

The sentence deliberately brings opposites together: “originated” and “eternal,” “forever renewed” and “everlasting.”

The expression points toward constant change, or an existence that is continually renewed.

##### 4 — The Relationship Between the Universal and the Individual

Ibn ʿArabī is inconsistent on the relationship between universals and individual things.

On one side, he limits universals to the world of thought.

They are “inward,” as he puts it.

Yet he also says that they affect concrete individual existence, and that their effect on an individual thing is identical with that thing’s existence.

On this point, Ibn ʿArabī adopts a subjective idealist position toward the relationship between being and consciousness.

The universal exists in the mind, yet it produces the individual being.

Consciousness therefore does not merely come before the object. It also creates it.

But elsewhere Ibn ʿArabī says that “universal things” are:

> “outward in the concrete realities of existing things, just as they are inward in their intelligibility.”

This second statement gives us the basis for a more realistic view of the relationship between the general and the particular.

It even gives us, in Ibn ʿArabī’s thought, the beginnings of an understanding of a dialectical, two-way relationship between being and consciousness, and between the general and the particular. (Evidence and texts.)

Despite Ibn ʿArabī’s uncertainty on this question, we should still give him credit for this brief glimpse of the dialectical relationship between the universal and the individual.

It is a dialectical insight, even though it remains hidden behind the fog of supernatural ideas.

##### 5 — Conclusions from the Texts

After everything we have discussed, we can now draw conclusions from Ibn ʿArabī’s theory of unity of being from three different angles.

###### First: The Contradiction in the Sufi Idea of *Tawḥīd*

Ibn ʿArabī did not really solve the contradiction in the Sufi understanding of *tawḥīd*, even though he “unified” existence.

There is still a gap in his solution.

The divine **essence** remains absolutely transcendent because Ibn ʿArabī makes outward existence the existence of God’s names and attributes, not of God’s essence itself.

He therefore does not completely unify existence by denying the true independent existence of outward things.

We are still left with the need to explain the relationship between the divine “essence” and the divine “names.”

By placing the essence in absolute abstraction while saying that the world connects with God only through the names and attributes, Ibn ʿArabī breaks the relationship apart in two ways.

He separates God from the world, and he also separates God’s essence from God’s names.

The old Sufi problem of *tawḥīd* therefore returns inside the theory of unity of being, only in a new form.

###### The Position of Mullā Ṣadrā

Muḥammad ibn Ibrāhīm al-Shīrāzī, known as **Mullā Ṣadrā** and **Ṣadr al-Mutaʾallihīn** (980–1050 AH / 1573–1640 CE), noticed this weakness in Ibn ʿArabī’s theory of unity of being.

He revised the theory by making outward existence identical directly with the essence of God, rather than connecting it with God only through the divine names.

He also added rational philosophical argument instead of relying only on Sufi “taste,” or direct mystical experience.

So there is a methodological difference here as well.

Al-Shīrāzī argued that God is a simple cause of existence.

A simple cause can produce only a simple effect.

This means that what it brings about is existence itself: what exists through the simple cause is the existent itself.

Al-Shīrāzī calls this kind of production **“simple existential making.”**

The word “existential” is especially important here because it points to a major part of al-Shīrāzī’s philosophy.

He gives priority to **existence**, not to essence.

For him, outward existence is primary.

Essence is not.

This is another difference between Ibn ʿArabī and Mullā Ṣadrā.

It may even be correct to call the difference fundamental, because it leads to the philosophical recognition that the material world has a true and objective existence.

Al-Shīrāzī states this recognition clearly in one of his texts. (We cited the text and its source on p. 191.)

###### Second: Between the Eternity of the World and Its Beginning

Ibn ʿArabī’s texts are inconsistent over whether the world is eternal or had a beginning.

This is especially clear in his description of:

> “the human being, originated and eternal, a formation forever renewed and everlasting.”

Here he brings together both origination and eternity in the human being—and therefore in the world, because for Ibn ʿArabī the human being is the “small world,” while the world is the “Greater Human Being.”

Yet the rest of his system necessarily points toward the eternity of the world.

One modern researcher explains Ibn ʿArabī’s position by saying that:

> “All contradictions are resolved in the divine essence, which surrounds and contains all these opposites without being reduced to them.”[^V3-431]

If we strip this explanation of its idealist and supernatural shell, we can see in it an understanding of the law of the **unity of opposites**.

For that reason, we can also see in it an echo of the real foundation on which that law rests: the material world itself.

In the same way, Ibn ʿArabī’s own uncertainty between the eternity of the world and its origination reflects an uncertainty between two different ways of seeing reality.

The first is an objective view, independent of his own consciousness, in which the real material foundation is reflected.

The second is a subjective idealist view that distorts that reality.

This supports a point we have made elsewhere.

The objective and organic connection between thought and the external world remains decisive even when thought produces supernatural images.

Those supernatural images may distort reality, and the distortion grows as they move further away from objective truth.

But even then, thought cannot escape the world from which it takes its material.

###### Third: The Human Question

We have seen that the question of the human being holds an important and increasingly central place in the different forms of Arab-Islamic thought.

This was true from the age of the Qadariyya and Muʿtazila down to Ibn ʿArabī, his students, and later followers of his theory such as al-Shīrāzī.

Ibn ʿArabī raises the human question higher than any of these earlier traditions.

He makes the human being the origin of the world.

He even calls the world itself the **“Greater Human Being.”**

In my own view, one important point should be emphasized.

Despite the extreme abstraction of Sufi thought, the Sufis never entirely turn away from the real human being.

They do not deny that a human being is human only through actual human, material existence.

They do not mean a purely “spiritual” being.

This is why they place the human being above the “angels,” even though angels, in their view, are purely spiritual beings.

The final conclusion is that the concern of freer Islamic thinkers—including philosophical Sufis—with the question of humanity was connected to the actual condition of human beings in their society.

Their ideas were a particular reflection of that real human problem.

That much is obvious.

But how exactly did this happen?

The Islamic social order was a system of absolute despotic rule.

Economically, it was an agricultural and commercial system governed by feudal relations of production, in their own historically specific form.

Within this system, the human being who worked the land or practiced a craft, who worked in agriculture, trade, domestic service or minor government positions, or who performed singing, dancing and music for the entertainment of caliphs, court circles, great landowners and merchants, was neglected, humiliated and oppressed.

His living conditions, his abilities and his social position were all treated with contempt.

At the same time, the theological ideology of the system completely erased his will beside the supreme will—which in practice meant the will of the caliph and the leading figures of the state.

This was the human question as it existed in social reality.

The freer thinkers of different periods in the history of this society could hardly avoid reflecting this problem in revolutionary thought.

This is why the human being appeared in their thought in such supernatural forms.

They raised him to the level of **imam**, **prophet**, or even **divinity** as a compensation for the deprivation, humiliation and oppression he suffered in social reality.

The ideological content of Sufi thought that we have discussed throughout Chapter Three of this part was, in the end, another expression of this same human problem.

## Conclusion

In this conclusion, we can record several general results from our study of Sufism.

They concern four questions.

### 1 — Sufi Determinism

If we examine this question through the philosophical schools of Sufism, rather than through the behavior of early ascetics, early mystics or “dervishes,” we find that the general tendency of these philosophical schools is to give the human being an extremely high place in relation to God.

At times, they give the human being a “divine” quality.

At other times, they give God a “human” quality.

The boundary between God and the human being begins to disappear.

Now add another conclusion from our earlier discussion of the human question in Sufi thought.

And add to that what we saw in Ibn ʿArabī: the qualities of the “Perfect Human Being” also extend, in potential, to the real human being.

We can then reach the following conclusion.

On the question of human will, the Sufis agree with the Muʿtazila, though they arrive there by a different path.

The Sufis reach a position close to the Muʿtazilite view of human freedom through the “divine” quality they give to the human being.

That quality gives the human being an active will powerful enough to take part even in building the “cosmic order” and shaping the fate of the world:

> “The world will continue to exist for as long as this Perfect Human Being exists within it.”
> — Ibn ʿArabī

The Sufi **saint** and **quṭb** are not spiritual beings living in heaven.

They are human beings living on earth, with human flesh and blood.

If this conclusion is correct—and we believe that it is—then philosophical Sufism cannot be described as a philosophy of determinism in the way some Orientalists and modern researchers have described it.

### 2 — Rational Thought in Sufi Philosophy

The title may sound contradictory, but a close study of Sufi thought shows that the contradiction is real.

It is true that the Sufi method stands in opposition to rational inquiry.

But it is equally true that the Sufis actually used rational inquiry.

If there is a contradiction here, it belongs to the Sufis themselves.

In every philosophical system they built, we have seen them rely on the same rational principles used by philosophers working through reason during the classical period.

One example is the principle that:

> “From the One, only one can proceed.”

They used this principle to deal with the contradiction between the “oneness” of God and the great number and variety of existing things.

They also used the categories of the **simple** and the **compound**.

We have already seen Mullā Ṣadrā use the idea of **“simple making.”**

Al-Suhrawardī used Aristotle’s ten classical categories when building his illuminationist system, changing only their number.

The Sufis also used the categories of the **necessary** and the **possible**, which had already been used by the Islamic philosophers.

Al-Ghazālī noticed this contradiction among the esoteric thinkers and exposed it.

But he then fell into the same contradiction himself when he tried to disprove the authority of reason through rational arguments. This can be seen in his book *The Infamies of the Esotericists*.

The Sufis dealt with the same questions as the philosophers and used the logic of philosophy with all its rational structure.

Yet at the same time, they wanted to move beyond this logic and destroy the rational method.

In effect, they carried into their own method the very principles that undermined that method, while failing to destroy any of the principles of reason itself.

In my view, the explanation is that when the Sufis turned to the principles of reason, they were responding to an objective reality that existed independently of their intentions and independently of their supernatural outlook.

What we mean is the material nature of thought itself—or, more precisely, the material relationship between thought and reality.

If the Sufis were forced, despite themselves, to use rational principles, this was because those principles had grown out of the relationship between thought and reality.

The principles of reason have a history.

That history is the history of this relationship.

At its deepest level, it is the history of human beings acting socially in the world.

The principles of reason are the accumulated result of human observation, sensory experience and rational generalization across the long history of humanity’s relationship with society and nature through social practice.

Those rational generalizations do not always match objective reality perfectly.

Sometimes they even distort it.

Whether they match reality depends on the level reached by scientific knowledge in discovering the objective general laws governing the movement of the external world.

This is why we say that there was nothing strange about the Sufis relying on the principles of reason, even though doing so placed them in contradiction with their own “subjective” theory of knowledge.

The Sufis could not build even one of their supernatural and metaphysical worlds without taking the material from reality itself.

### 3 — The Persecution of Sufis and Sufism

This persecution is a historical fact.

But were its causes really religious, as bourgeois historians and modern bourgeois researchers have often presented them?

The revolutionary ideological content we have uncovered in Sufi thought suggests otherwise.

The persecution was driven by ideological motives expressed in religious language.

In this conclusion, I have drawn attention to several aspects of Sufi thought that support this argument.

I have also drawn attention to the positions taken toward Sufism by the ruling authorities, by religious jurists, and by the popular masses, because all of these positions support the same conclusion.

### 4 — Sufism as a System of Orders

Everything we have discussed so far applies to Islamic Sufism only in its earlier stages, when it still formed an organic part of the development of philosophical consciousness in its first emerging forms.

From the later Middle Ages down to the present, however, the Sufi movement became almost entirely a movement of organized Sufi orders.

It was emptied of its philosophical content and of its revolutionary ideological meaning.

It became a reactionary and parasitic movement.

It played a dangerous role in dulling the spontaneous revolutionary energies of the masses.

It helped the ideology of reactionary classes maintain control over those masses.

And Sufism became one of the forces contributing to cultural and social backwardness in Eastern societies generally, and Arab societies in particular.

# Chapter Three

## The Brethren of Purity Phenomenon

- Qualitative change built on quantitative change
- An encyclopedic gathering of human knowledge
- An early seed of evolutionary theory
- The search for the source of matter’s motion

### A — The Brethren of Purity: A Phenomenon of an Age, Not Just a Group

#### 1 — Introduction

Although my task in this book goes beyond that of a historian, I still feel the weight of the historian’s responsibility in dealing with this subject. It is both heavy and difficult: heavy because it raises a whole series of questions that demand answers, and difficult because the available answers do not satisfy the historian’s conscience or ambitions.

How did the Brethren of Purity appear in their own age and society, and when did they appear? Who were these people whom their age and society called—or who called themselves—the “Brethren of Purity”? How did they come together as a single group under this name and banner? And who wrote the vast “encyclopedia” attributed to them, which appeared as the *Epistles of the Brethren of Purity* in an age and society that had never before produced an encyclopedic work with anything like this range and variety?

Historical sources from the time of the Brethren of Purity, or from periods close to them, do not give historians clear answers to any of these questions. One historian from the seventh Islamic century, the thirteenth century CE,[^V3-432] was himself uncertain about both the group and the “encyclopedia” that bears its name. If he was uncertain, what can we expect of a modern historian?

As for the fourth Islamic century, the tenth century CE, which modern historians and researchers generally accept as the age of the Brethren of Purity, we have only one contemporary source: Abū Ḥayyān al-Tawḥīdī.[^V3-433] He tells us that the group was based in Basra and was familiar with “the different branches of knowledge and the different crafts.” Its members, he says, were brought together by companionship, united by friendship, and committed to purity, holiness, and mutual advice. They developed a doctrine and “composed fifty epistles covering all parts of philosophy, theoretical and practical. They made a separate index for them, called them the *Epistles of the Brethren of Purity*, concealed their names in them, distributed them among the booksellers and copyists,[^V3-434] and gave them to the people.”[^V3-435]

From the same source we also learn the names of some members of this group, though only through al-Tawḥīdī’s account. He lists “Abū Sulaymān Muḥammad ibn Maʿshar al-Bustī, known as al-Maqdisī; Abū al-Ḥasan ʿAlī ibn Hārūn al-Zanjānī; Abū Aḥmad al-Mihrajānī; al-ʿAwfī; and others.”[^V3-436]

Al-Tawḥīdī also tells us that Zayd ibn Rifāʿa came across this group while living “for a long time in Basra,” and that he “kept their company and served them.”

Yet apart from what al-Tawḥīdī tells us, we find no real information in the sources about these people whom he identifies as members of the group and authors of its “fifty” epistles. Even in the case of Zayd ibn Rifāʿa, all we learn from al-Tawḥīdī is that he had exceptional intelligence, a sharp mind, wide ability in poetry and prose, skill in arithmetic, rhetoric, and history, and a good knowledge of different schools of thought, opinions, and religions.

Yet al-Tawḥīdī denies that Zayd belonged to any particular school or group. He explains this by saying that Zayd was “constantly surging into everything and boiling over in every field.”[^V3-437] So even one of the people al-Tawḥīdī identifies as a member of the group remains surrounded by uncertainty.

We also find another piece of information in the work of the German Orientalist Carl Brockelmann. He mentions “Abū Sulaymān Ḥamd”—people called him Aḥmad and he allowed the name to stick—“ibn Muḥammad ibn Ibrāhīm al-Khaṭṭābī al-Bustī.”[^V3-438] Brockelmann describes this Abū Sulaymān as a scholar of hadith and a poet who wrote criticism and commentary in several fields of hadith. Toward the end of his life, he turned toward Sufism. He died in 386 AH/996 CE, or according to another report, in 388 AH/998 CE.

Could this be the same Abū Sulaymān al-Bustī whom Abū Ḥayyān al-Tawḥīdī counted among the Brethren of Purity?

There are similarities. Both are called Abū Sulaymān and al-Bustī, and both fit the period that modern researchers generally identify as the time when the group appeared.

But there are also reasons to doubt that they were the same person.

First, Brockelmann’s figure is Aḥmad ibn Muḥammad ibn Ibrāhīm al-Khaṭṭābī, while al-Tawḥīdī’s is “Muḥammad ibn Maʿshar, known as al-Maqdisī.”

Second, Brockelmann refers to many Arabic sources that discuss this al-Bustī, yet not one of them says anything that could suggest he helped write the *Epistles of the Brethren of Purity* or belonged to their association. Nor do they say that he lived in Basra, where, according to Abū Ḥayyān al-Tawḥīdī, the group came into being.

Modern Arab historians and researchers give us further examples of this historical uncertainty.

Some report the view that “the Epistles were written by the philosopher al-Majrīṭī of Córdoba. He brought the *Comprehensive Epistle* of the Brethren of Purity to al-Andalus and dictated it to his students in lectures, so they mistakenly believed that al-Majrīṭī himself had written the Epistles.”[^V3-439]

There is also the view that the Epistles were written by Jaʿfar al-Ṣādiq.[^V3-440]

In the introduction that Aḥmad Zakī Pasha wrote for the Cairo edition of the *Epistles* in 1347 AH/1928 CE, he quotes from a book called *Jalāʾ al-ʿAynayn fī Muḥākamat al-Aḥmadayn* by Ibn al-Ālūsī al-Baghdādī, which in turn draws on *Kashf al-Ẓunūn* and a commentary on al-Saffārīnī’s creed. According to this account, “the *Epistles of the Brethren of Purity* are the foundation of the Qarmaṭian doctrine,” and attributing them to Jaʿfar al-Ṣādiq was only meant to help spread them. They were, it says, “composed after the third Islamic century under the Buyid state. They were dictated by Abū Sulaymān Muḥammad ibn Naṣr al-Bustī, known as al-Maqdisī; Abū al-Ḥasan ʿAlī ibn Hārūn al-Zanjānī; Abū Aḥmad al-Mahrajūrī; al-ʿAwfī; and Zayd ibn Rifāʿa. They were all philosophers who gathered together and composed these Epistles according to a philosophy that departed from the path of the pure religious law.”[^V3-441]

Aḥmad Zakī Pasha then quotes Ibn Ḥajar’s legal opinions rejecting the attribution of the *Epistles* to Jaʿfar al-Ṣādiq. Ibn Ḥajar instead says that their author was Maslama ibn Qāsim al-Andalusī,[^V3-442] who died in 395 AH/1005 CE. This Maslama is the same person identified above as “al-Majrīṭī of Córdoba.”

We then come across yet another view from a modern Arab Ismāʿīlī researcher.[^V3-443] He attributes the *Epistles* to the Ismāʿīlī imam ʿAbd Allāh ibn Muḥammad ibn Ismāʿīl ibn Jaʿfar al-Ṣādiq.

But if this imam died in 299 AH/911 CE, this would mean that the *Epistles* date from the second half of the third Islamic century, the ninth century CE. The dominant view, both old and modern, is that they were written in the second half of the fourth Islamic century, the tenth century CE.[^V3-444]

If we accepted this Ismāʿīlī researcher’s view, we would have to move the appearance of the *Epistles of the Brethren of Purity* back by almost a full century from their generally accepted historical setting. Given the nature of the phenomenon represented by these Epistles, this is not convincing, as we will see later.

Faced with so much disagreement, the logic of historical investigation requires us to return to the source closest to the period that most competing views accept as the age of the Brethren of Purity and their Epistles: the fourth Islamic century, the tenth century CE.

That source is Abū Ḥayyān al-Tawḥīdī and the passage preserved in his *al-Imtāʿ wa-l-Muʾānasa*, which we referred to earlier. Al-Qifṭī later reproduced this same passage word for word from al-Tawḥīdī.[^V3-445]

On the basis of this source, and of other sources that relied on it and were also much closer to that period than we are, we must for now assume that the *Epistles of the Brethren of Purity* appeared in the fourth Islamic century, the tenth century CE, and that their authors were the group who came together in Basra around the principles explained in their “encyclopedia.”

We still have to consider the relationship between this group, its Epistles, and Ismāʿīlism.

Modern studies[^V3-446] are almost unanimous that such a relationship existed. We have already seen that one modern Ismāʿīlī researcher believed the Epistles were written by their imam ʿAbd Allāh ibn Muḥammad.

If we turn to the *Epistles* themselves and examine their contents, we find a clear relationship between the philosophical principles on which the Brethren of Purity based their group, the organizational methods they followed, and the principles and organization of the Ismāʿīlīs.

But there is also room to argue that the relationship never reached the point of complete identity. We should not simply treat the Brethren as a purely Ismāʿīlī organization.

Ismāʿīlism existed long before the Brethren of Purity. It had already developed an advanced organization that had worked actively, carefully, and persistently for a long time. Through that organization, it influenced political and ideological struggles connected with several revolutionary movements that appeared during the third and fourth Islamic centuries, above all the Qarmaṭian movement.

Other esoteric movements also existed during these two centuries, but the Ismāʿīlīs were especially distinguished by their organizational strengths. These gave them a vitality, influence, and ability to survive for more than two generations that the other esoteric sects did not possess.

These strengths were one factor behind the success of the Fatimid mission in reaching political power.

Later, however, internal divisions within the Ismāʿīlī movement led to its fragmentation as an organized movement.

Our conclusion is that the Brethren of Purity, in their organizational structure, directly reflected the successful organizational experience of the Ismāʿīlī movement.

That experience had provided movements with revolutionary intellectual and ideological aims with a historical model of organized practice. Its power and attraction were so great that its influence did not stop at organizational methods. It extended into principles and ideas as well.

We can now state our conclusion more precisely. The Ismāʿīlī character visible in the Brethren of Purity shows a real connection, but not one that amounts to complete identity. The Brethren had an existence independent of the Ismāʿīlī organization, despite the many points of contact between them.

Their independence does not rule out some basic doctrinal or intellectual link with Ismāʿīlī principles.

We are led to this view because the historical sources contemporary with the Brethren, and those written soon after them, contain no documents describing the Brethren as an active organized group. Nor do they show Ismāʿīlī missionaries, who were active throughout many parts of the Islamic world at the time, using the *Epistles of the Brethren of Purity* in their work.

The only documents we find in this connection are a few lines of poetry by Abū al-ʿAlāʾ al-Maʿarrī that may refer specifically to the Brethren of Purity—or may simply use the words “brethren of purity” in their ordinary moral sense. For example:

> If misfortune casts me aside, I will never
> cast aside my loyalty to the brethren of purity.[^V3-447]

There is one other piece of evidence that may throw some light on the question. The historian Macdonald says in his book *The Development of Muslim Theology* that when the Mongols captured the fortress of Alamūt,[^V3-448] they found it rich in copies of the *Epistles of the Brethren of Purity*.[^V3-450]

But this proves no more than the kind of connection we have already described between the Brethren and the Ismāʿīlīs.

There is nothing to prevent the Brethren of Purity from having existed independently while their Epistles also served as one of the theoretical sources used by Ismāʿīlī groups, especially since they contained many of the basic principles of the doctrine.

One further sign of the Brethren’s independence from the official Ismāʿīlī organization may be the reports in some sources that they enjoyed a degree of relative freedom under the Buyid state,[^V3-451] even though the Ismāʿīlīs themselves were opponents of the state in Baghdad.

The historical evidence therefore points toward one conclusion: the organizational life of the Brethren of Purity did not go much beyond the “organizational plan” written into the *Epistles*.

In other words, it remained largely theoretical. They drew up an abstract model for themselves, inspired by the successful organizational experience of the Ismāʿīlīs.

Their practical activity seems instead to have been concentrated on producing their great encyclopedic work. This appears to have taken a very long time and may have absorbed all their energy, leaving them little opportunity to put the “plan” written into the *Epistles* into practice.

Otherwise, why do historians from their own time, or periods close to it, leave no record of such activity? Why, for example, do al-Shahrastānī or Ibn Khaldūn say nothing about them?

The only activity outside their writing that anyone from their own time appears to mention is the one already described by Abū Ḥayyān al-Tawḥīdī: “They composed fifty epistles... concealed their names in them, distributed them among the booksellers and copyists, and gave them to the people.”

They clearly wanted their encyclopedic knowledge to spread through society after the enormous effort they had put into collecting and organizing it. There was undoubtedly a definite purpose behind this ambition, which we will explain shortly.

They must also have made an effort to distribute this knowledge through booksellers and copyists. But beyond this, as we have said, we know of no other practical activity by which they tried to spread it among the wider population.

As for how they chose the name “Brethren of Purity and Loyal Friends” for their “organization,” it may well be true that they took inspiration from the expression “brethren of purity” in Ibn al-Muqaffaʿ’s translation of *Kalīla wa-Dimna*, in the chapter “The Ring-Dove.”[^V3-452]

The meaning of the name also fits the bond that brought these people together around a common task and a shared goal. Abū Ḥayyān al-Tawḥīdī’s account, quoted earlier, contains a hint in this direction.[^V3-453]

#### 2 — Strategy and Tactics

We can allow ourselves to use the language of our own age here because the ideas behind that language are already present in the encyclopedic work of the Brethren of Purity: the *Epistles*.

We can also describe the work in modern terms as an almost complete “program,” even though it lacks, first, full coordination in its composition and, second, full scientific precision.

This program-like character is clear in the overall structure of the fifty *Epistles* and in the *Comprehensive Epistle* that crowns the whole work and brings the group’s “strategy” to its highest point.

We can even say that the first fifty Epistles contain the “tactical plan,” while the *Comprehensive Epistle* gathers together the broader “strategic plan” that runs through the collection as a whole.

So what, first, was the group’s strategy?

Here we return once more to the earlier passage by Abū Ḥayyān al-Tawḥīdī, a contemporary of the scientific, philosophical, and ideological phenomenon we call the Brethren of Purity.

Speaking of the group, al-Tawḥīdī says:

> “They said that the religious law had been stained by ignorance and mixed with errors, and that there was no way to wash and purify it except through philosophy, because philosophy contains both doctrinal wisdom and practical benefit. They claimed that when Greek philosophy and the Arab religious law were joined together, perfection would be achieved.”[^V3-454]

These are the words of a major intellectual who lived through the experience of his age with bitterness and anxiety and who himself helped enrich the culture of that same age—the age of the Brethren of Purity.

Al-Tawḥīdī would hardly have said this unless he knew enough about both the group and its time to be confident, or at least close to confident, that the group’s own *Epistles* supported what he was saying.

As we will see, they do.

The Brethren accept the idea of humanity’s “original sin” from the time of Adam, broadly as it appears in religions that teach this doctrine.

But they also believe that humanity escaped the grip[^V3-455] of this sin after Adam repented and obeyed God’s second command and second instruction.

Those who succeeded Adam on earth by God’s command then followed this path and made God’s instruction a lasting word among their descendants.

This “word” is “the succession to prophecy, the kingdom of the message, and the imamate.”

Anyone who violates this divine command and rejects this instruction, then tries through his own ambition and effort to become God’s deputy and govern creation, cannot truly succeed. And even if he does gain power, “he is only a deputy of Satan, because his rule is based on trickery, plotting, deceit, transgression, seizure, injustice, aggression, betrayal, tyranny, and disobedience.”[^V3-456]

Look at this chain of accusations, pouring out one after another with the force of an angry flood.

Look especially at the final ones: “seizure, injustice, aggression, betrayal, tyranny, disobedience.”

It does not take much effort to see the target of this attack.

The person who has managed to become “caliph” by violating God’s command and rejecting his instruction to Adam, and who has therefore become a “caliph of Satan,” is clearly the person called “caliph” in the age of the Brethren of Purity.

And they do not mean one particular caliph. They mean everyone who holds the official office of caliph in the Islamic state of their time: the Abbasid caliphate.

This aim becomes even clearer elsewhere in the *Epistles*, where the Brethren refer to the Abbasids in language that leaves little doubt about whom they mean.

They accuse them of killing “the friends of God and the children of the prophets.”[^V3-457]

They repeatedly condemn unjust rulers. They denounce the man who sets himself up as God’s deputy on earth, takes power over people’s lives, and extends his hand across the land supposedly to protect the oppressed from the oppressor—only to become the oppressor himself and the one who abuses the weak and poor.[^V3-458]

They even develop what they present as a scientific and astronomical rule for predicting changes in political dynasties according to the cycles of conjunction and separation between the planets.

On this basis, they predict what they call the “middle conjunction.” According to them, this happens when Saturn and Jupiter move from one triplicity to another, once every 240 years.

When this happens, they say, “kings are replaced, power passes from one dynasty to another, from one people to another, and from one house to another.”[^V3-459]

Their figure of 240 years may be understood as marking the end of the first Abbasid era, counted from the foundation of the Abbasid state in 132 AH.[^V3-460]

If so, this is a prediction—or promise—that the caliphate will pass to a new state, to another group of people, and to another ruling house.

So what is this new state? Which people will rule it? Which house will replace the Abbasids?

We find the answer elsewhere in the same Epistle, before the passage in which they make this prediction.

There they also say that the astronomical cycle will eventually reach Scorpio. According to the Brethren, this cycle lasts 330 years and four months. At that point, rule will pass to the “state of the Brethren of Purity,” which they say will last 159 years.[^V3-461]

We find further clarification elsewhere in the *Epistles*, where they again speak of the cyclical rise and fall of rule and political states, passing in each cycle from one nation to another,[^V3-462] from one ruling house to another, and from one land to another.

They then add features that clearly match the Brethren themselves and those who join their cause.

They describe their expected state as the “state of the people of goodness.” This state will begin with “a group of learned and wise men, the best and most virtuous, who gather around one opinion and agree on one doctrine and one religion.”[^V3-463]

Among the qualities of the people of this “state of goodness” is that they understand religious matters, know the secrets of prophecy, and have been educated in the philosophical sciences.[^V3-464]

If this is who they are, we might expect their “one religion” to differ from the religion officially followed in their own society and age.

And in many passages of the *Epistles*, they tell us openly that it does.

They declare themselves fully open to all religions, schools of thought, and sciences, without reservation. They tell their companions “not to oppose any branch of knowledge, abandon any book, or show fanaticism toward any school, because our view and our doctrine contain all schools and bring together all sciences.”[^V3-465]

They then tell each of their “brothers”:

> “Strive, my brother, to show the truth to every follower of a religion or school from what he already possesses or holds to, and remove the confusion that has entered his thinking... Do not simply cling to the religion and doctrine you already follow. Search for something better. If you find it, you have no right to remain with what is lower. You must take what is better and superior and move toward it. Do not occupy yourself with listing the faults of other people’s doctrines. Instead ask: do you yourself have a doctrine with no faults?”[^V3-466]

This is an open call for a critical attitude toward religion—every religion—and for people not to cling unquestioningly to inherited religions and doctrines.

This critical attitude also extends to Islamic law, though the Brethren approach it cautiously. Like the Sufis, they distinguish between an outward and an inward meaning.

The outward law is suitable for the general public and treats weak and sick souls.

Strong minds, however, are nourished by the deeper wisdom drawn from philosophy.[^V3-467]

What is suitable for “the select who have reached maturity in wisdom and are deeply grounded in knowledge” is “to examine the secrets of religion, the inward meanings of hidden matters, and their concealed mysteries.”[^V3-468]

Worship, therefore, has “two kinds and no third: one is religious and legal worship... and the second is philosophical and divine worship.”[^V3-469]

Any acceptance of the rules, laws, and rituals of the religious law therefore becomes outward and formal, because in the end they are subjected to an “esoteric” interpretation reserved for those who are “deeply grounded in knowledge.”[^V3-470]

As with the Sufis, who counts as “deeply grounded in knowledge” depends on the concepts used by each group in its esoteric interpretations.

For example, in the Brethren’s interpretation, the pilgrimage to Mecca commanded by religious law does not, in its inner meaning, mean what those who are not “deeply grounded in knowledge” understand from its outward form.

Its “inner meaning and true reality,” they say, is “an image of the human being’s journey around the earth.”[^V3-471]

Their esoteric understanding of “prophecy” is likewise based on an astronomical influence.

They say that “a spiritual power flows out from the body of Mercury and spreads through the whole body of the world and all its parts. Through it come knowledge, perception, thoughts, inspiration, revelation, prophecy, and all learning.”[^V3-472]

Mercury is one of the planets, and all the planets, they say, are inhabited by good souls, which are the angels.

All these souls once lived in bodies for a period of time. They purified themselves, gained insight, left their bodies, became independent in themselves, achieved salvation, and began to move through the spaces of the celestial spheres, “rejoicing, happy and delighted for as long as the heavens and earth remain.”[^V3-473]

Evil souls, by contrast, remain “settled in the depths of these bodies, drowned in the sea of prime matter, and submerged in the structures of these generated beings.”[^V3-474]

This whole interpretation of prophecy, angels, and demons is completely at odds with the concepts of religious law.

It denies the existence of a separate world of spirits apart from the world of celestial bodies and the earthly world, contrary to what religions teach.

This interpretation also shapes the Brethren’s ideas about paradise, hell, and resurrection.

They do not believe in the resurrection of bodies after death, nor do they understand paradise and hell as the religious law describes them.

Paradise, in their view, is “a spiritual form, without bodily matter, pure life, rest, pleasure, and joy.”[^V3-475]

Hell is the world of generation and corruption below the sphere of the moon,[^V3-476] and “the inhabitants of hell are the souls attached to the bodies of animals, which suffer pains and illnesses unlike the other beings in the world.”[^V3-477]

This interpretation also contradicts the religious view of paradise, hell, and resurrection.

Indeed, it comes close to an explanation that contains a materialist starting point within an idealist and metaphysical framework.

---

From this broad overview, we can now form a general picture of the Brethren of Purity’s strategy.

In short, the group begins from a position of opposition to the Abbasid caliphate and to the religious-law-based ideas on which the official ideology of that state rested.

The Brethren therefore look toward building a new state and a new society based on a new philosophy—one open to every source of knowledge, to all religions and schools of thought, to all philosophies, and to different peoples, languages, and human groups.[^V3-478]

This philosophy would become the ideology of the new society on a different basis.

The social content that would support this ideology will be the subject of the next section.

In their encyclopedic work, the *Epistles*, the group laid out a broad program built around two main tasks.

First, they defined their position toward the existing state and its ideology.

Second, they defined the alternative to both that state and its ideology.

But this alternative belonged to a distant stage in the future.

That is what we mean here by “strategy.”

Within this same two-sided strategic process—the theoretical process of “destruction” and “construction”—the group also worked out how it would move toward its tasks and goals.

In other words, while writing its “program,” it was also carrying out a careful “tactical” operation. We might even say a consciously planned one.

The group introduced its teaching in stages, moving from one level to the next, and gave each stage a method suited to the level of the person being taught.

The *Epistles* begin with a stage of caution and maneuvering in dealing with people at the lowest level of understanding.

The Brethren are extremely careful with such people. They take into account the inherited beliefs already formed in their minds, the positions they have acquired from existing religious schools and official ideology, the nature of the prevailing Islamic political system, the dominant social relations, and the effects of keeping the mass of ordinary people ignorant and cut off from the cultural development that was then at its height.

The group’s awareness of this reality led it to include in its program something like an internal organizational code.

Above all, it required that young people be chosen for the first meetings of the organization because, as the Brethren put it, the young are “like a clean white sheet on which nothing has yet been written.”[^V3-479]

By this they meant that young people’s minds were relatively free from inherited beliefs and acquired ideas, or at least that these ideas had not yet become deeply rooted.

The “tactical” plan for dealing with these young recruits was to begin by giving them basic knowledge about the cause.

This knowledge was meant to awaken doubts about whatever religious, sectarian, or official ideological beliefs they had already absorbed.

At the same time, it was meant to awaken curiosity and encourage them to seek knowledge beyond these first lessons.

The method of “education” then advanced step by step as the recruits themselves grew older, until they reached the stage beyond the age of fifty.

At that point they could enter the fourth and highest rank. Caution could finally be relaxed, the teaching could become open and direct, and they could be shown all the secrets of the “organization.”[^V3-480]

#### 3 — The Social Content of the Brethren’s Ideology

To uncover this social content, we first have to clear away a considerable accumulation of supernatural ideas mixed with idealist philosophies—Pythagorean, Platonic, and Neoplatonic—as well as Ismāʿīlī and Sufi esoteric symbols.

Yet beneath these tangled layers, we find a social content different from that of Sufi philosophy.

What is new here is the much closer approach to social reality.

More important still, the Brethren increasingly describe things in a way that combines speculative thought with an almost clear picture of society’s material conditions.

This is an important step.

It shows that the objective relationship between the material development of society and its cultural development had become strong enough to move from a hidden quantitative stage into a visible qualitative one.

Abstract speculative thought could no longer remain isolated from social reality or avoid being directly affected by it.

We will show this through concrete evidence.

This raises a basic question that concerns not only our present subject but Arab-Islamic philosophy across its whole history: the relationship between this philosophy and the philosophies of the ancient world, especially Greek philosophy.

We see this relationship very clearly in the philosophy of the Brethren of Purity. It helped give their philosophy its encyclopedic character.

Indeed, this broad openness to other traditions, and the very idea of philosophy as encyclopedic, became a principle for the Brethren.

We have already seen them say:

> “Our view and our doctrine contain all schools and bring together all sciences.”[^V3-481]

Modern studies of Arab philosophy, and especially of the Brethren of Purity, whether written by Orientalists or by non-Marxist Arab scholars, usually explain this phenomenon in terms of “eclecticism,” “reconciliation,” or “syncretism.”[^V3-482]

This is an easy and comfortable explanation, but it is also based on an idealist view.

It makes the whole matter seem like a matter of personal choice.

A philosopher, or a group of philosophers, supposedly looked through the doctrines and philosophies available to them, chose whatever suited their own preferences or “temperament,” and rejected the rest.

In reality, the question is very different from this kind of subjective and speculative explanation.

This was a broad and objective historical phenomenon, not a set of isolated personal choices.

It was one way of dealing with the body of human knowledge that Arab culture had been able to encounter by that time.

Objective social conditions made this particular way of dealing with those different cultures necessary.

Those cultures differed from one another, and they all differed from the reality of Arab culture itself.

The source of these differences lay in the different historical forms of social relations found among the peoples that produced them.

Arab-Islamic philosophy’s engagement with Eastern philosophies, Greek philosophy, and Greco-Roman philosophy was shaped by the historical form of social relations in medieval Arab-Islamic society.

This is why, for example, Plotinus’s theory of emanation changed when it entered Arab philosophy.

Although it had an enormous influence on Arab philosophy as a whole, it was reshaped there in ways that eventually altered both its content and its original direction.

It was not simply an “eclectic” choice that one or another materialist tendency appeared in the philosophy of the Brethren of Purity, al-Fārābī, or Ibn Sīnā.

Nor was it a matter of personal choice that speculative thought became linked with experimental tendencies, that metaphysical ideas became connected with society’s material conditions, or that beneath layers of supernatural thought there appeared an ideological content relatively connected with the aspirations of working social groups in the countryside and cities and opposed to the ideology of the existing despotic social order based broadly on feudal relations of production.

When the theory of emanation appeared in Plotinus’s philosophy, it carried an ideological meaning very different in kind from the one it later acquired in Arab philosophy.

Plotinus’s own philosophy grew from a social reality radically different from that in which Arab philosophy emerged.

Plotinus separated thought from reality and the heavenly world from the earthly world very sharply, while looking down on the material world.

This reflected the deep separation between the class of “masters” in the declining Greek slave society and the class of slaves, who in that society had been deprived of all human rights, including the right to participate in intellectual and cultural life.

Plotinus expressed the ideology of the class of “masters.”

His close connection with Emperor Gordian is historically well known. It was because of this connection that he was able to accompany the emperor on his unsuccessful campaign against Persia in 242 CE.[^V3-483]

Given this social and political position, Plotinus’s ideas about a fixed metaphysical necessity governing the world[^V3-484] and about a complete harmony within the single order ruling life[^V3-485] reflected his defense of the despotic slave system represented by his emperor.

The situation of Arab philosophy and Arab philosophers, including the Brethren of Purity, was very different.

Arab philosophy appeared under social relations that were broadly feudal, while urban commercial and craft groups were growing under conditions that allowed them a degree of social development.

That is one difference.

A second is that many of the people who represented this philosophy came from peasant backgrounds, commercial or craft groups, or poor and propertyless sections of society.

Intellectual activity was no longer restricted, as it had been in slave society, to the circles of “masters” and aristocrats.

Quite the opposite.

Most of those who engaged in intellectual life now came from popular social backgrounds.

Third, more than six centuries of human history had passed between Plotinus and the age of medieval Arab philosophy.

During those long centuries, human knowledge of the world had gradually advanced.

People continued trying to understand the world and discover some of the laws of nature and society, whether or not their explanations fully matched objective truth.

In addition, most Arab philosophers of the period combined speculative philosophy with work in the natural sciences based on experiment and practical observation.

As they practiced these sciences, their methods improved.

In a dialectical process, better methods helped expand scientific knowledge and uncover new laws of nature, as far as the historical conditions of the time allowed.

The logic of all these facts leads us to expect the Arab philosophers of this period to have seen the world differently from Plotinus and from the Greek philosophers who came before him.

Arab philosophy in this period was therefore the objective result of a necessary blending of two things: the accumulated knowledge of earlier human history, and the historical state of knowledge and society during the Arab cultural renaissance, especially in the third and fourth Islamic centuries.

When we analyze the question this way, on the basis of historical reality, we are logically led back to our earlier conclusion.

The relationship between Arab philosophy and ancient philosophy—especially Greek philosophy, and above all Neoplatonism—should not be explained as mere “eclecticism” or “reconciliation.”

Such an explanation comes from a subjective idealist view that our own age can no longer accept.

We live in an age of expanding scientific discovery, which continues to build a scientific method that sees an objective connection between the laws of thought and the laws governing the movement of nature and society.

In light of this analysis, we can now return to the ideological content of the philosophy of the Brethren of Purity and compare it with the ideological content of Sufi philosophy.

We should begin by noting one element the two share: the esoteric interpretation of religious law.

As we have seen, this leads in both cases to the same broad result.

It rejects the theoretical foundation of the social order represented by the caliphal state.

In the state’s own understanding, religious law is precisely that foundation. It is treated as the one fixed link between God and humanity.

Both the Sufis and the Brethren of Purity rejected the idea that this one form of mediation was absolute and unchanging.

We will not return to the Sufis here, since we have already studied their position and its causes.

With the Brethren of Purity, however, this position appears in several parts of their philosophy.

First, they reject absolute imitation of the outward form of religious law.

Instead they demand “investigation and proof in order to uncover the secrets and hidden indications within it,” just as “those at an intermediate level in every science and craft are not satisfied with imitation when they are capable of investigating and discovering things through proof.”[^V3-486]

Second, they explain prophecy in a way that begins from a material conception.

For them, prophecy is an astronomical effect coming from the body of the planet Mercury,[^V3-487] as we noted earlier.

This directly undermines the official religious understanding of both law and prophecy.

Third, they believe that religious law and religion are periodically renewed according to the movements of the heavenly bodies as they approach and move away from one another.

They describe three kinds of astronomical events that produce three kinds of events on earth.

The first is what they call the “great conjunction,” when Saturn and Jupiter meet once in every cycle of 960 years.

When this conjunction occurs, they say, major changes take place on earth: a new messenger appears, and religious laws and systems change.[^V3-488]

As far as we can tell from their words, they do not mean only the renewal of religions in the past.

They describe this conjunction as a cosmic law that continues to operate regularly whenever its natural time arrives.

In this way, the Brethren of Purity also undermine the Islamic principle that the Prophet Muḥammad is the final prophet and that Islam is therefore the final revealed religion.

The ideological meaning of this position is that it also undermines the idea that the social order of the Islamic caliphate can last forever simply because the religious law supporting it is supposed to last forever.

Fourth, there is their theory of the imamate.

In their view, an imam should possess forty-six qualities.

Taken together, these qualities place the imam on the level of prophecy, or even above it.

They then say that if all these qualities cannot be found in one person, but are instead distributed among a group, then this group should “unite around one opinion and join their hearts in love for one another.”

They continue:

> “If you”—addressing each of their brothers—“are determined to seek the good of religion and the world, then come: let us gather with a group of virtuous brothers...”[^V3-489]

We should add to this their rejection of the view held by some Shiʿis that “the awaited imam is hidden because he fears his opponents.”

The Brethren insist instead that he is “present among them, knowing them while they fail to recognize him.”[^V3-490]

From all this, we can conclude that they saw themselves as the group in whom the qualities of the imam had been distributed—the group that had “united around one opinion and joined their hearts in love for one another.”

This means, quite clearly, that their own group represented the office of the imam.

That is why they said the awaited imam was not hidden but already present among the people.

In this sense, the Brethren of Purity placed themselves in direct opposition to the political system of the Abbasid caliphate, not only ideologically but politically as well.

They aimed to destroy that system, replace it with their own, and regard themselves as the holders of the legitimate imamate.

This is so if we take the word “imamate” in its ordinary outward sense.

Otherwise, it may simply be a symbol for their political doctrine.

#### 4 — The Social Side of the Brethren’s Philosophy

The first three points above represent the ideological element shared by both Sufi philosophy and the philosophy of the Brethren of Purity.

But the Brethren also possess another element that marks a clear advance beyond the Sufis in the ideological struggles of their time.

This is their direct connection with social reality.

Because of that connection, ideological struggle in their thought moved beyond the level of abstract ideas and acquired an immediate social content.

The Sufis entered this arena of ideological conflict in the same age as the Brethren of Purity.

Why, then, did the Brethren move further than the Sufis despite living under the same historical conditions?

In our view, there are two reasons.

First, the *Epistles* show that the Brethren had a real connection with the applied sciences. This mattered.

Second, the Sufis were isolated from the main fields of social activity, and speculative thought absorbed almost all their energy.

The social content of the Brethren’s thought appears in the following parts of their philosophy.

**1 —**

They reject the idea that a person can pursue survival and happiness in the afterlife while remaining isolated from social activity aimed at improving earthly life.

Instead, they make such activity part of the foundation of that pursuit.

They clearly recognize the social character of all human activity.

That is why they repeatedly tell their brothers things such as:

> “You cannot live alone except a miserable life, and you cannot live well except through the cooperation of the people of a city and by living under a law.”[^V3-491]

**2 —**

Because they emphasize the social character of human activity, they also stress the value of work and the social character of its goals.

They therefore see the division of labor as a basis for society’s progress and well-being, and material incentives as a basis for carrying out work well and improving it.

On the value and division of labor, they say that a good life requires “the mastery of many different crafts, and no single human being can master them all, because life is short and the crafts are many.”

For this reason:

> “Divine wisdom and providence required one group to devote itself to mastering crafts, another to trade, another to building, another to political administration, another to mastering and teaching the sciences”—notice here the link between practical activity and knowledge—“and another to serving everyone and attending to their needs.”[^V3-492]

On material incentives for work, they say in the same context:

> “As for their agreements concerning measures, weights, prices, and wages, these are matters of wisdom and organization, so that people will be encouraged to work hard in their jobs, crafts, and cooperation, and so that each person will receive a wage according to the effort he puts into his work and his energy in practicing his craft.”[^V3-493]

This passage has another social meaning connected with the historical position of labor.

It brings out the difference we noted earlier between Greek slave society, represented by Greek philosophy and especially by Plotinus, and the feudal-commercial society represented by Arab philosophy, including the philosophy of the Brethren of Purity.

The difference lies in the disappearance of the old contempt for manual and productive labor and for the social groups that perform it.

That contempt belonged to slave society.

With the Brethren, we find the opposite.

They place these productive groups on the same general level as political, scholarly, and educational groups.

That is one point.

There is another important point in their statement that “each person will receive a wage according to the effort he puts into his work and his energy in practicing his craft.”

Can we understand this as implying that the reward for labor should match the value of the labor itself, without workers being exploited or deprived of what they have earned?

Or would such an interpretation go beyond what people of that age were able to conceive?

**3 —**

The Brethren of Purity designed the encyclopedic knowledge gathered in their fifty-one Epistles[^V3-494] so that it would be available, in all its different kinds and levels, to different sections of society without discrimination.

They tried to spread this knowledge as widely as possible.

Within the *Epistles* themselves, they even described ways of bringing knowledge to everyone, including oral methods for people who could not read, through organized meetings with planned content and fixed times.

This is important because the subjects of the *Epistles* were not all propaganda or political agitation.

In fact, scientific subjects make up about three quarters of the collection.

These include mathematical and educational Epistles, physical and natural-science Epistles, and Epistles dealing with psychology and the intellect.

From this we can conclude that spreading purely scientific knowledge among the wider public was one of the Brethren’s goals alongside their political aim.

Even mass education in doctrinal and political subjects would itself carry a clear ideological and social meaning.

To spread scientific knowledge throughout society—knowledge about nature, humanity, history, society, and the state—using concepts independent of official doctrine was itself an ideological act.

It directly challenged the ideology of the ruling order in two ways.

First, the official ideology helped keep scientific knowledge away from the mass of ordinary people and restrict it to the upper levels of society and to other groups loyal to the state and its dominant social order.

Second, it helped strengthen the despotism of that order and support its absolute power as something not open to objection or debate.

It also banned ideas that broke free from the assumptions and theoretical certainties of “official” thought, and used accusations of “heresy,” “unbelief,” and religious deviation as weapons to frighten and persecute those who held independent ideas—even to the point of physically destroying them.

History gives us the Brethren of Purity as one of the clearest concrete examples of this.

Their organization was secretive and obscure.

The *Epistles* themselves were written with extreme caution.

Their ideas were often wrapped in symbols and protective language, or expressed through familiar religious slogans that could be understood in more than one way.

They were driven to all this by two pressures at once: fear for their own lives and for the survival of their ideas, and a desire to open a path for their ideology and knowledge into the minds of the widest possible public.

The Orientalist De Boer understood something of this side of the Brethren’s situation when he concluded from their views that they “appear to represent the doctrine of a persecuted group whose political tendencies become visible from time to time. Through some of what the authors of these Epistles suffered, we can see the pains they endured, the struggles they carried out, and the oppression to which they and their predecessors were subjected.”[^V3-495]

But De Boer, as his words suggest, treats this as a special problem of the Brethren of Purity and their predecessors—perhaps meaning the Ismāʿīlīs.

We see it instead as a broader problem and one of the defining questions of their age.

From this point of view, and from others we will discuss later, we regard the Brethren as a phenomenon of an entire historical period.

**4 —**

In their social philosophy, the Brethren begin objectively from a spontaneous class perspective.

Their own class position is perhaps not difficult to identify once we take the historical situation into account.

We mean the historical form of consciousness in the Middle Ages, in a society ruled by a social system whose theological ideology dominated the consciousness of different classes and social groups.

Even thinkers who opposed that system and its ideology could not entirely escape its influence.

If we fail to take this historical form of general consciousness into account, we will not be able to identify the Brethren’s class position through the social philosophy presented in the *Epistles*.

We may even mistakenly conclude that there is a contradiction between their class outlook and the ideological content of their philosophy as a whole, or between that outlook and the general direction of their “strategy.”

Since we are speaking about an ideological position and arguing that the Brethren clearly had one, it is only natural that the class structure of their society would be reflected in their philosophy and in their ideological position as a whole.

The problem is that some of the passages in which this class structure appears seem, on the surface, to show the Brethren accepting it and confirming the form that suited the ideology of the ruling social order.

Yet, according to our earlier conclusions, they were thinking and working toward the destruction of that order and the theoretical foundations of its ideology, and wanted to replace it with another system based on a different ideology.

For example, when they imagine the ideal society of their city—the city on which the “state of goodness” is to be built—they describe its class structure in the following way.

It consists of four ranks, or, we might say, four classes or social groups:

> “First, the rank of those connected with the four elements, the people of crafts. Second, the rank of those who hold offices of leadership. Third, the rank of kings who command and forbid. Fourth, the rank of the divine men who possess will and choice.”[^V3-496]

This is essentially the same class structure as that of the state they oppose, except for the fourth group: the “divine men,” meaning the sages.[^V3-497]

It follows the traditional social hierarchy.

At the bottom are the people of crafts—the productive forces.

Above them are those who hold administrative positions: governors of regions, provinces, towns, and other officials.

Above them are “the kings who command and forbid.”

At the top are the divine men, who possess “will and choice.”

On the surface, then, the Brethren accept monarchy and allow kings to command and forbid.

So how does their “state of the people of goodness” differ from the existing order in class terms?

That is the first question.

The second is: where do the Brethren themselves stand within this class structure?

To answer the first question, we must return to historical conditions.

We cannot demand that the Brethren, or any other thinkers, step outside their age or move beyond the historical limits of their society.

The societies of their time were class societies.

The historical form of their class structure was the same form reflected in the Brethren’s thought.

We cannot expect them to imagine some completely different form.

The political system based on monarchy was also an inseparable part of the same historical reality.

Again, we cannot demand that they imagine another political system that had not yet entered the horizon of those ages.

The objective historical conditions themselves were not yet ready to place such an idea in the consciousness even of revolutionary thinkers.

What distinguishes the Brethren’s class and ideological picture is something else.

And this same difference helps us identify their own class position.

It appears in two parts of the picture.

The first concerns the place occupied by kings in the social hierarchy and political system.

As we saw, kings stand below the “divine men.”

Above the kings’ power to “command and forbid” stands a higher authority: the “will and choice” that the Brethren give to the divine men—the philosophers.

This is a revolutionary ideological step that deserves serious attention.

Here we suddenly find the absolute power of medieval Islamic rulers being taken away from the kings and the highest power of will and decision being placed in the hands of philosophers and sages.

They are given effective authority to oversee what the “kings”—and here we should read “caliphs”—do.

This is directly connected with the ideological confrontation we discussed above.

The second part concerns the Brethren’s attitude toward the “people of crafts”[^V3-498]—the class base of society, or its human productive forces.

We should first notice their phrase when classifying the ranks of society: “the first is the rank of the people connected with the four elements, the people of crafts.”

What do they mean by “the people connected with the four elements”?

We understand this when we continue reading and see how they explain the relationships between the “ranks of the people of the city,” meaning the classes of society.

They write:

> “The direction of the people of crafts should flow through those below them as light flows through air, and as the power of growth flows through the four elements: fire, air, water, and earth. The government of those who hold positions of leadership should flow through the people of crafts as colors flow through light, or as animal power flows through the power of growth. The command of the kings who possess authority should flow through the leaders who govern as the power of sight flows in the perception of colors, and as the rational power flows through the animal power. And the will of the divine men should flow through the kings who possess authority as intellect flows through intelligible things, or as angelic power[^V3-499] flows through rational power. If the affairs of the city are organized according to these conditions, then this is the noble and good way in which the people of the city should deal with one another.”[^V3-500]

From this passage we can first conclude that the Brethren describe the “people of crafts” as “the people connected with the four elements” because they see the producers of material goods in the same way they see the four elements—fire, air, water, and earth—as the basic first foundations of life in material beings.

Notice their statement that the direction of the people of crafts flows through those below them “as the power of growth flows through the four elements.”

From this, we can also conclude that the Brethren draw a parallel between the ranks of social classes and the stages in the “development” of material living beings.

In the first stage of their “development,” living beings possess the “power of growth,” or plant-like power, formed through the basic elements of nature.

They then “develop” toward animal power, then rational or human power, and finally angelic power.

In the same way, the ranks of social groups rise from the lowest to the highest: from the people of crafts, to those in positions of leadership, and then to the divine men—the sages and philosophers.

If we set aside the mechanical nature of this parallel between social classes and the Brethren’s stages in the “development” of living beings, we can still draw two conclusions from it.

The first is that the people of crafts occupy the lowest rank in society.

This reflects the historical situation that, as we said earlier, must be taken into account when identifying the Brethren’s class position.

It was an objective historical reality that necessarily appeared in their thought.

We cannot expect them to have moved beyond it, because under those historical conditions that was not possible.

This hierarchy remained part of history even into our own age, the age of socialism, and will continue until classless society prevails throughout the world.

The second conclusion is that the Brethren see the social groups that create society’s material needs as a basic force that makes society possible in the first place.

They are a necessary condition for the proper organization of “the affairs of the city,” as the Brethren put it.

We can see this when they describe the “direction of the people of crafts,” meaning their productive work, as something that flows “through those below them as light flows through air, and as the power of growth flows through the four elements.”

We see more in this than a simple application of their “law of cosmic correspondence” between different ranks.

In our view, it also emphasizes the socially necessary place of the groups that produce society’s material needs.

Their role in the functioning of society is compared to the role of light in air and to the power of growth in the development of living beings.

If this conclusion is correct, we have reached the point where we can try to identify the Brethren of Purity’s class position.

We now need to connect this view of the importance of the “people of crafts” with their other ideas.

These include their view of the social character of human activity, the social goals of work, the division of labor as a basis for society’s progress and well-being, and the idea that each person deserves a wage according to the effort he puts into his work.
