When these theoretical positions are brought together, we can see the ideological starting point behind them.

In our view, they represent one historical expression of the ideology of the stable but oppressed social groups living under the social order of the Abbasid caliphate.

If the Brethren were expressing the ideology of these broad and socially diverse groups, they must themselves have had roots in one or more of them.

By the time they produced their philosophical encyclopedia, they had become intellectuals.

Their place in society, and the intensity of their intellectual work, may have moved them away from the actual ranks of the class or social group from which they came.

But what matters is not whether they physically remained inside their original class or social group.

What matters is whether their ideological loyalty to it continued, and whether they avoided moving into a new class whose ideology opposed it.

Their secretive and obscure situation, together with the cautious, fearful, and heavily disguised “tactics” used in writing the *Epistles*, is evidence that this loyalty did continue.

It also shows that they suffered the same persecution as the social groups whose ideology they expressed.

We should therefore not judge them for what, on a superficial reading, looks like acceptance of ruling-class ideology when they place these social groups at the bottom of society.

Once again, this belongs to the historical situation we have repeatedly said must be kept in view.

#### 5 — Fate and Human Will

During the age of the Qadarites and Muʿtazilites, in the first and second Islamic centuries—the seventh and eighth centuries CE—and into the early third Islamic century, the ninth century CE, the question of fate was one of the main subjects of ideological struggle between the caliphal state and independent thinkers.

Later, the scope of this struggle widened.

As we saw in our discussion of Sufism, it came to include the question of God’s transcendence.

With the Brethren of Purity, it also came to include social questions directly.

Even so, the problem of fate and its relationship to human will remained a major subject in Arab philosophy and kept its earlier place within the ideological struggle.

From this angle, the Brethren’s position on fate can be understood as another expression of their broader ideological position.

They continue the Muʿtazilite position on fate and openly reject determinism in the *Comprehensive Epistle*.[^V3-501]

This is the least cautious and guarded of their Epistles and the one that states their “strategy” most openly.

Before giving their position, they begin by distinguishing between the concepts of *qadar* and *qaḍāʾ*.

For them, *qadar* means that once things exist, their forms are arranged in their proper places within the order of existence “according to God’s measure.”

Put another way, it is “placing a thing in the position suited to it and having it exist in a place where its existence is fitting.”[^V3-502]

*Qaḍāʾ*, by contrast, refers to God’s prior knowledge that people will only be commanded to do what lies within their ability.

If they burden themselves with something else that God has forbidden, they deserve punishment because through such action they have “departed from his judgment and command and turned away from his instruction. To depart from his judgment is disobedience.”[^V3-503]

This interpretation of *qaḍāʾ* and *qadar* places a seed of human choice at the heart of both ideas.

*Qadar* concerns only the order in which things are placed where they belong.

*Qaḍāʾ* means only that people are commanded to do what matches their ability.

In other words, the Brethren assume that human beings already possess the ability to act before any command is addressed to them.

After this introduction, which clearly affirms human ability and choice, they answer “the determinists who say that the source of evil is the source of good”—that is, God—“and that he wills evil to be evil just as he wills good to be good.”[^V3-504]

Their answer rejects the determinist claim that evil actions are forced rather than chosen.

But if the Brethren allow human beings free choice in acts of evil, does this mean they deny the same freedom in acts of good?

No.

Their interpretation of *qaḍāʾ* and *qadar*, as we have just seen, makes that clear.

In their Epistle devoted to the natural and sensory sciences, they make the same position even clearer.

There they reject the view of some theologians that all actions come from God.

They say this view is wrong because it comes from ignorance of nature.

Here the Brethren directly answer the theologians.

They argue that natural events and processes, as well as human actions, are all “acts of individual souls, all of which are powers of the universal angelic soul.”

They continue:

> “Whatever among these acts is good is attributed to the good individual soul, and whatever is evil is attributed to the evil soul. Reward and punishment fall upon it accordingly.”[^V3-505]

In the Brethren’s terminology, the “individual soul” is the essence embodied in a particular being that exists in the external, material world.

They therefore explain the actions of nature directly through the laws of natural movement, and human actions directly through human beings themselves, even though they indirectly trace both back to the universal celestial soul.

But because the heavenly bodies in their theory are themselves part of nature and are physical bodies inhabited by the universal soul,[^V3-506] the final result is that all events in the world ultimately return to nature.

This position should therefore be recorded as a starting point for a materialist tendency in the philosophy of the Brethren of Purity, as well as something consistent with their ideological position.

They do not stop at adopting this view as their own philosophical position.

They go further and require the same principle from the “lawgiver.”

In the sixth Epistle of the group dealing with religious and legal sciences, they say that the lawgiver must accept several principles.

The fifth requires the lawgiver to believe that “each existing being is independent in itself, and nothing improves or corrupts it except what belongs to it through the evil of its actions, the corruption of its opinions, the badness of its character, or the accumulation of its ignorance.”[^V3-507]

The sixth principle requires the lawgiver “to believe that when the Creator commands people to do something, he has made it possible for them and removed the obstacles. Some obey his command, while others violate his prohibition.”[^V3-507]

The sixth condition does not concern us much here because it only confirms what we have already discussed.

The fifth, however, is especially important.

It presents the idea in a new and daring form.

It rejects supernatural outside intervention in human actions.[^V3-508]

It affirms human independence in what people do and do not do, and says that their improvement or corruption comes through their own independent will and choice.

We do not think the Brethren mean by the independence of the individual that a person is completely cut off from external conditions or from the general laws governing change and development.

We know from other parts of their philosophy that they take the opposite view.

They emphasize, for example, the relationship between human character and the natural environment,[^V3-509] and between a person’s temperament and the movements of the planets,[^V3-510] along with many other kinds of relationships.

Their purpose here must therefore be more specific.

They are denying divine intervention in human actions, behavior, and choices.

That is what they mean by human independence.

But they do not dare say this more openly than they do here—and even this wording is, in our view, relatively bold.

#### 6 — Their Attitude Toward Reason

We can understand the Brethren’s attitude toward reason from two sources:

1. Their explicit statements about relying on reason.
2. Their overall method for dealing with the questions raised in the *Epistles*.

If we look first at their explicit statements, we find that they place reason in the position of “leader” of their group.

Every group that wants its affairs to be sound and its conduct rightly guided, they say, must have a leader “who leads it, brings its members together, and preserves the order of its affairs.”

The Brethren therefore accept reason as the leader of their group and make it the judge between them.

Reason, they say, is “what God has appointed as leader over the virtuous among his creation.”

They are so determined to follow the requirements of reason’s leadership that they add:

> “Whoever refuses the conditions of reason and the conclusions of its judgments... will be punished by our withdrawing our friendship from him, renouncing our loyalty to him, refusing to rely on him in our affairs, avoiding his company in our dealings, refusing to speak to him about our sciences, hiding our secrets from him, and advising our brothers to keep away from him... Know that virtuous and intelligent people, when the power of the lawgiver is added to their reason, have no need of a leader to command them, forbid them, restrain them, and judge them, because reason and the lawgiver’s power take the place of the leader and imam.”[^V3-511]

The meaning is so clear that it needs little explanation.

We only need to add that, in our view, the reference to the lawgiver here is probably meant as a cover or disguise—*taqiyya*.

We also think the Brethren may use the term “lawgiver” here in a hidden sense that refers to themselves.

In any case, we have before us an explicit document that places the judgment of reason in the position of the group’s highest authority.

Nor is there any basis in this passage for claiming that by “reason” they mean the “First Intellect” or the “Active Intellect” used in their theory of creation or emanation.

Everything here concerns human reason.

That is why they say: “when the power of the lawgiver is added to **their reason**.”

We should also pay attention to the final words of the passage: “they have no need of a leader to command them...” and so on.

Is this not a fairly transparent suggestion that the Brethren believe the judgments of reason can replace the judgments of religious law—or that “philosophical worship” can replace “legal and religious worship”?

The matter, as we see it, is clear.

The second source for understanding their attitude toward reason is their method in the *Epistles*.

This appears in several ways, despite the Sufi precautions that sometimes surround it and the Greek terminology that appears at other times.

A few examples will be enough.

**First:** they define “human reason” as “the power of distinction that belongs to each human individual and not to the other animals.”[^V3-512]

They mean that reason belongs to every human being.

If reason is the basic quality that distinguishes every human being, can someone who states this so clearly and generally then ignore reason and refuse to make it a guide to knowledge, to discovering what is unknown, and to investigating the truths of existence?

**Second:** they reject miracles, and reason is the basis of this rejection.

Reason discovers the law of causal connection between things and events and their objective causes.

The Brethren rely on this principle when defining “cause” and its relation to “effect.”

In their Epistle on “Definitions and Descriptions,” which serves as their terminological and methodological guide for the encyclopedic project as a whole, they say that a cause is what brings another thing into existence, while an effect is something whose existence has a cause.[^V3-513]

On this basis, they reinterpret the miracle stories found in religious books.

They bring them under the rules of cause and effect and strip them of their supernatural theological aura.

For example, they interpret the story of Christ raising the dead by saying that “death” here means ignorance, and that the “dead person of ignorance” is brought back to life through knowledge.

They interpret his healing of the blind by saying that blindness here means blindness of the heart toward truth, and that healing comes through guidance toward truth.[^V3-514]

**Third:** they reject imitation in religion unless inherited beliefs can be supported by proof.

Because they take reason as the standard, they warn against the person who refuses to leave “a religion or doctrine in which he was raised, with which he became familiar, according to which he has worshipped for many years, and which he received from his fathers, teachers, and masters, without first becoming convinced that it is false.”[^V3-515]

Here we have a method that connects a person’s beliefs with the real conditions that produced them.

The judgments of reason become the main authority in this realistic approach.

**Fourth:** in their theory of knowledge, after recognizing the value of sensory knowledge,[^V3-516] they say that the judgments human reason draws from rational principles are the highest form of knowledge.

This is deductive knowledge that reason either reaches by itself or acquires through its own activity.[^V3-517]

We can also see this in the enormous care they take to explain the process of thought through its different bodily organs, to analyze the function of each one, and to show how they relate to and cooperate with one another in producing rational knowledge.[^V3-518]

**Fifth:** most of their encyclopedic attempt to explain the world relies more heavily on mathematical and natural-science investigations than on theological knowledge.

It is true that these investigations contain many illusions and naive conclusions.

But this does not cancel the scientific direction of their thought,[^V3-519] which tries to understand the objective truths of the material world through rational and experimental methods.

That is the first point.

The second is that many things we now see as illusions or naive conclusions were accepted as science in their own age when judged against the level those sciences had then reached and against the available material tools for rational and experimental investigation.

We should also remember the continuing influence of ancient knowledge on mathematics and natural science, as well as the influence of theological thought.

Here too, we must keep the historical level of scientific consciousness in view.

#### 7 — The Theory of Knowledge in the Brethren’s Philosophy

Let us briefly return to the age of al-Kindī, when the first clear signs of Arab-Islamic philosophy appeared in its rationalist form.

This will help us measure how far the theory of knowledge had developed from its first major representative, al-Kindī, to the age of the Brethren of Purity—a period of roughly one century.

We saw earlier that with al-Kindī the subject of knowledge began to break out of the limits imposed on it by theological thought.

Before the third Islamic century, the ninth century CE, knowledge had been largely confined to knowledge of God and the relationship between God and the world.

In al-Kindī’s philosophy, however, the field widened.

Alongside theological knowledge, it now included knowledge of the world for its own sake, independently of theology.[^V3-520]

If we move forward to the period after the middle of the fourth Islamic century, the tenth century CE, we find the field expanding further in the philosophy of the Brethren of Purity, al-Fārābī, and the other philosopher-scientists of the age.

The expansion was not only wider.

It also became deeper.

The encyclopedic form of the Brethren’s *Epistles* is itself one expression of this development.

The *Epistles* may not represent every dimension of this phenomenon, especially its deeper ones.

But even if we looked at them only as an index of the fields of knowledge known in their time, that alone would be enough to show the major outlines of this new expansion.

More and more areas of the material world were becoming independent subjects of study, increasingly separated from purely theological investigation.

This expansion of rational knowledge cannot, of course, be separated from the development of social relations, the development of the natural sciences, and the effect of both on philosophy’s growing tendency to explain the world on the basis of its objective existence.

After this general introduction, we can now ask more precisely: what are the limits of knowledge in the Brethren’s encyclopedia?

We must also ask what sources of knowledge they recognize, and what principles and standards they use.

Their subject matter moves mainly in two directions.

The first is knowledge of God and God’s relationship to the world.

The second is knowledge of the world itself and for its own sake, with a degree of independence from the first.

Because these two directions differ, the limits of knowledge also differ, although the sources, principles, and standards change only slightly.

Let us begin with the question of knowing God.

The Brethren say that this kind of knowledge is “the noblest of the sciences and the highest of forms of knowledge.”

We should not expect them to say otherwise, whether we consider the historical age in which they lived or the conditions of the society they hoped to reach with their teaching.

They then define their method for seeking knowledge of God.

The first point is that this kind of knowledge cannot be approached in the same way as knowledge of particular sensory things or of “other existing beings, including bodies, accidents, qualities, things possessing qualities, and beings located in places and times, whether individuals, species, or genera.”

The second point is that they use the traditional categories to explain the difference between these two kinds of knowledge.

Knowledge of sensory things and universals requires us to ask questions about a thing’s identity, essence, quantity, quality, position, place, time, cause, and distinguishing features.

Knowledge of God, however, requires answers to only two of these questions:

Does he exist?

And who is he?

In other words, the questions concern his existence and his attributes.

From this it follows that human knowledge of God does not include knowledge of God’s essence.

But the Brethren do not say this is because such knowledge lies beyond the power of human reason.

In fact, they say something that suggests the opposite.

The “scholars” who sought this knowledge, as the Brethren call them, failed to reach it not because God’s secret was hidden, but because of “the intensity of his visibility and the greatness of his light.”

The Brethren therefore do not approach the question in the traditional way.

They do not declare the human mind incapable of knowing God’s essence.

Instead, they say that the question of God’s essence should not be asked because God is the creator of essences.

Nor should we ask about God in terms of quantity, quality, place, time, or cause, because God is the creator of all quantities, qualities, places, times, and causes.[^V3-521]

These categories do not apply to God because God is completely beyond bodily existence and all the conditions and attributes that belong to bodies.

So here too, in both the starting point and the method, we find something new in the Brethren’s approach to knowledge.

Now let us turn to the other side: knowledge outside theology.

We mean knowledge of the material world.

We should begin with a basic question.

Does the material world really exist as an object of knowledge in the philosophy of the Brethren of Purity?

This question can in fact be reduced to another, more fundamental one:

Do the Brethren of Purity accept the objective existence of the external, material world?

Although the full answer will be the subject of the next section, we can already say: yes.

They do recognize its objective existence.

It is therefore only natural that the external world should also become an object of knowledge.

Their earlier discussion of the knowledge of God makes this explicit.

We saw them distinguish between seeking knowledge of God and seeking knowledge of “particular sensory things and all universal beings whose particular instances exist within those very things.”

All of these refer to the external world.

We also saw them identify the categories on which knowledge of these things and their universals is based.

This distinction would make no sense, and neither would the use of these categories, if the external world were not accepted as an object of knowledge.

Without that external world, this knowledge would have no object at all.

In any case, most of the questions we will examine here in the Brethren’s theory of knowledge concern the external world itself.

And the largest and most important sections of their encyclopedia are precisely those devoted to questions about the material world.

So on this point, the answer is clear.

As for the sources of knowledge used by the Ikhwān al-Ṣafāʾ, their starting point is generally shaped by their rationalist outlook. Even in what they call the “divine sciences,” they do not move far from this basis. The same is true when they discuss matters that go beyond the natural world. Their ideas about the immaterial realm beyond the physical world, and about the movement and relations between these two realms, are based above all on reason, inference, and proof.

But reason is not always their only source of knowledge about the “spiritual world.” The caution that runs through most of the *Epistles* sometimes leads the group to cover rationalist and materialist conclusions with a supernatural veil. This is one reason their texts can be so ambiguous. Idealist scholars can use that ambiguity to obscure these tendencies, or simply fail to see them behind the supernatural covering—which is sometimes thick, but at other times thin enough to reveal what lies beneath.[^V3-522]

In the previous section, we saw their attitude toward reason in a very clear statement: they call reason the head of their group and make it the judge among them. They follow this principle in most of what they say about knowledge and the world, despite all their caution, concealment, dissimulation, and what we earlier called their “tactics.”

We already saw one example in their statement that “if good and rational people add to their reason the power of the lawgiver, they have no need for a leader to rule over them...” We suggested that adding the lawgiver to reason may itself be a form of concealment, if by the lawgiver they mean the Prophet. But we think it more likely that by “the lawgiver” they mean themselves, not the Prophet.[^V3-523] They leave the expression vague because that vagueness serves the same policy of concealment.

Their use of the word “revelation” when discussing knowledge of God may be another example. They say that “revelation continues to descend from heaven upon the prophets and the chosen saints... and through it God, glory be to Him, is truly known.”[^V3-524]

What do they mean by “revelation” here?

On the surface, the wording points to revelation in the usual Islamic sense: something that descends from heaven upon prophets. But the Ikhwān add “saints” as well. In the standard Islamic understanding, revelation belongs to prophets alone. Even the Shiʿa do not say that revelation descends upon the Imam.[^V3-525]

This suggests that the Ikhwān mean something different by “revelation,” something that does not carry its usual supernatural religious meaning.

We can see this again when they discuss the meaning of sending messengers and prophets. They say that prophets, “although they are human beings, are living, speaking, discerning, learned beings whose pure souls resemble the angels, and who truly know God...”[^V3-526]

These are the only qualities that distinguish prophets in this passage. And all of them are human qualities that other people could also possess. Being alive and able to speak—if these words are meant literally, with no hidden meaning—are qualities shared by every human being. As for knowledge, prophets share that quality with every learned person, even if we assume that “knowledge” here carries an esoteric meaning. The Ikhwān themselves belong to this class of esoteric scholars.

The same applies to resembling the angels through the purity of the soul. In the earlier passage, the Ikhwān place saints alongside prophets by saying that revelation descends upon both. Why, then, should those saints not also resemble them through the purity of their souls?

And as for truly knowing God, the Ikhwān say elsewhere that people other than prophets can also reach this same true knowledge. They call such people the “saints of God,” and say that they do not need prophets as intermediaries through whom they approach God.[^V3-527]

So where does this knowledge come from, if the saints of God can possess it without relying on prophets?

If its source is “revelation,” then it is already clear that revelation means something different for the Ikhwān from its usual supernatural religious meaning. It would also follow that even the revelation received by prophets is not some miraculous knowledge placed directly in them outside the normal laws of human understanding.

Could they instead mean Sufi mystical knowledge?

That seems unlikely in the case of the authors of the *Epistles*, whose work is encyclopedic and whose general outlook is strongly rationalist. The source of this knowledge must therefore be something more concrete and more in keeping with their encyclopedic and rationalist way of thinking.

They do not leave us in doubt for long. In the same *Epistle*,[^V3-528] they explain their way of knowing God quite clearly.

They describe two stages.

The first is a rational beginning rooted in the natural capacities of the soul.[^V3-529] This leads to a second stage: mastering “all the divine, natural, mathematical, rational, and sensory sciences and forms of knowledge. Once these sciences and branches of knowledge have been mastered, the souls then know Him”—that is, God—“with true knowledge...”

So this is the path to knowing God: reason and its first principles, followed by knowledge and science—and here science includes knowledge that comes through the senses.

There is no revelation here, no Sufi unveiling, and no mystical illumination.

---

If knowledge of God and of the entire “spiritual world” is based on reason, rational inquiry, and scientific knowledge, as we have just seen, then knowledge of the “physical world” must depend on these means even more.

Indeed, when the Ikhwān describe how thinking works, we can see a materialist tendency running through their ideas. It begins from a practical view that stays close to reality.

As we will see more clearly, this approach places them outside both pure abstract rationalism and extreme sensualism. Or, to use terms from medieval European philosophy, it places them outside both the “nominalist” and the “realist” camps. Instead, it brings them closer to a scientific view of knowledge, one based on understanding the internal relationship between the bodily organs involved in sensation and those involved in thought, and the way these systems work together to produce knowledge.

If we can support this claim with passages from the *Epistles*, we will have concrete evidence of how far the age of the Ikhwān al-Ṣafāʾ had advanced in two areas. The first is its understanding of the process of thought itself. The second is its ability to connect philosophical thinking with the results of the developing practical sciences.

This scientific development was itself linked to the growth of production in the towns, and the two influenced each other. We can see this relationship clearly in the Ikhwān’s repeated habit of mentioning crafts together with the natural sciences.[^V3-530] They even state that “the knowledge of all craftsmen falls within the natural sciences.”[^V3-531]

De Boer also noticed this close connection between crafts and sciences in their thought. He rightly concluded that they divided intellectual activity into two broad fields: the sciences and the crafts.[^V3-532] But he was unable to uncover the real social basis reflected in that division.

When the Ikhwān explain how thought works, they begin with three basic means by which human beings gain knowledge. These are arranged from the lowest level to the highest.

The first is the five senses. Because human beings share these with animals, the senses represent the lowest stage of human knowledge. They are also the only means of knowledge available to children.

The second is reason. Because reason is a specifically human capacity that distinguishes human beings from animals, it represents a higher stage of knowledge. It begins when a person reaches the stage of understanding after childhood.

By “reason” here, the Ikhwān mean a general grasp of the first principles of thought: basic truths that are self-evident, known in ancient philosophy as the “first principles.” One example is the recognition that two contradictory things cannot both be true at the same time.

The Ikhwān therefore regard this basic use of reason as a means of knowledge shared by all rational people. But reason in this general sense cannot produce science by itself. A higher stage is still needed.

That third stage is proof.[^V3-533]

Scientific knowledge therefore requires all three together, in this order: sensation, reason, and proof.

And for proof to produce complete scientific knowledge, the Ikhwān say that it must be based on the study of mathematics, geometry, and logic.[^V3-534]

These, then, are the means by which knowledge is acquired at its different levels.

But do these means work together as parts of one continuous process of thought? Or does each operate separately from the others?

The answer to this question reveals the real direction and the central features of the Ikhwān al-Ṣafāʾ’s theory of knowledge.

We find their fullest answer in what they say about the powers of the soul and the functions those powers perform in managing the body and its physical and mental organs.

If we read their many discussions of this subject together, a complete picture begins to emerge. The powers of the soul are divided into groups with different functions, but all of them form a single whole. Each completes the others, and the work of one is connected with the work of the rest.

The Ikhwān compare this arrangement to a well-organized society whose members divide their tasks among themselves in a coordinated way. The soul is like the king of this society. The different powers of the soul are like its members, arranged according to the social hierarchy imagined by the Ikhwān.[^V3-535]

They divide these powers into three groups.

The first contains the natural powers: the vegetative, animal, and rational or human powers. Each of these is then divided into further powers, which the Ikhwān classify according to the bodily functions they perform in plants, animals, and human beings.[^V3-536]

The second group contains the sensory powers. These are the five senses: hearing, sight, smell, taste, and touch. Here too, they describe the physical function of each sense.

These descriptions show how far biology had advanced during the Arab period in the wider history of the natural sciences. They also show how closely this scientific development was connected with practical experience and with the production of the things society needed.

The third group contains what the Ikhwān call the “spiritual powers.” By this they mean the powers involved in thinking.

There are five of them: imagination, thought, memory, speech, and production or craftsmanship.[^V3-537]

If we read their full analysis of each of these powers, both separately and in relation to the others, several conclusions of major importance emerge.

**1 — The Ikhwān understood that life, sensation, and thought are organically connected through the material body.**

We can see this clearly in a statement such as the following:

> “The soul is one in itself. These different names”—vegetative, animal, and rational—“are given to it only according to the actions that appear from it. When it performs nourishment and growth in the body, it is called the growing soul. When it produces sensation, movement, and movement from place to place in the body, it is called the animal soul. And when it performs thought and discrimination, it is called the rational soul...”[^V3-538]

**2 — This awareness of the organic connection between life, sensation, and thought was not unique to the Ikhwān.**

Their texts, like those of other Arab-Islamic philosophers of the same age, show that this idea had become part of the wider intellectual outlook of the period.

But we must distinguish this materialist position from their idealist position on the nature of the soul itself.

The Ikhwān accept a dualism between soul and body. According to this idealist assumption, a human being is made up of a physical body and a spiritual soul. These are two different substances, different in their qualities and opposed in their states, although they share in temporary actions and passing characteristics.[^V3-539]

The contradiction between these two positions comes from the limits of the science of their time.

The Ikhwān lived in an age that had made important advances in biology, in the study of bodily functions, and in the study of organic matter. But they were unable to use these scientific results as the basis for broader theoretical conclusions about life and the soul.

They therefore failed to foresee a scientific solution to the problem of the soul and its relation to the body. Despite the considerable achievements of their research by the standards of their age, they remained trapped within an idealist dualism.

**3 — Their description of thought, its stages, and the organs involved shows that they had begun to recognize its material basis.**

Their texts even show an awareness that the inner movement of thought is connected with the bodily organs of life and sensation.

But once again, they did not take this insight far enough to reject the idea that the human soul is spiritual and separate from the body.

This conclusion is not weakened by the fact that they call the powers of thought “spiritual powers.” That name should not mislead us about what they actually mean when they describe how these powers work.

In this context, “spiritual” is largely the language of their age for what we would now call **mental abstraction**. Sensory images are separated in thought from their immediate material and sensory setting. They then become mental images that follow the laws of thought, which has a relative independence from external reality.

We are convinced that this is what the Ikhwān really mean when they call the powers of thought “spiritual powers.” Their own analysis comes very close to saying so directly.

Let us see first how they explain the term, and then how they describe the process of thought.

They begin by distinguishing the “spiritual powers” of the human soul from the powers of sensation.

The spiritual powers, they say, perceive “things known in a spiritual way, without their matter,” while the sensory powers “cannot perceive sensible things except in matter.”

They then describe the process of thought:

> “When the imaginative power receives all the forms of sensible things and impresses them upon itself (...), its nature is to pass all of them immediately to the thinking power. When the sensible things are no longer present before the senses, those forms remain within it in a spiritual form...”

Each power then passes these images on to the next, until the images of sensible things, after moving through the whole process, finally become written thoughts.[^V3-540]

Consider what they are saying here.

The thinking powers grasp the forms of things without their matter. The imagination receives the forms of all sensible things and preserves them within itself. And when the sensible things themselves are no longer present to the senses, “those forms remain within it in a spiritual form.”

The meaning of all this is very close to what we now call mental abstraction.

If we remove the word “spiritual” from the language they use, what remains is a basically sound description of mental abstraction: the process that connects sensory knowledge with rational knowledge, or more precisely, the process through which sensory knowledge becomes rational knowledge.

This is also the key to understanding the real relationship between the universal and the particular, or between the general and the specific.

The Ikhwān came very close here to finding the key to the problem.

But they could not free themselves from their assumption that soul and body are separate. Because of this, the solution remained just beyond their reach, and the idea of a “spiritual” soul continued to dominate their thinking.

This same assumption shaped the way they understood mental abstraction. They had taken an important step toward understanding abstraction correctly, but because they still regarded the soul as a “spiritual substance,” they imagined that sensory images became “spiritual” once they entered the soul after being stripped of their connection with matter.

They had the key in their hands, but could not fully use it.

This left them standing between the two main directions in philosophy: materialism and idealism. They had one foot in one camp and the other foot in the other.

The same problem runs through Arab philosophy as a whole, from its first stage with al-Kindī to its highest stage with Ibn Rushd, although individual philosophers differ greatly in where they stand between these two positions.

Still, we must repeat one point. The “spiritual” covering around the Ikhwān’s account of thought should not prevent us from seeing the real content beneath it, in the form we have just described.

**4 — The places where the Ikhwān locate the different functions of thought provide even stronger evidence that they saw a material basis for the process of thinking as a whole.**

At the same time, these passages reveal even more clearly the contradiction created by their inability to build broader theoretical conclusions on the advances that they—or their Arab intellectual world more generally—had made in the study of nature and bodily functions.

The most important point here is that they understood the centers of thought and the centers of sensation to be organically connected.

They begin their explanation of the imaginative power at the point where it meets the sensory centers: the front of the brain.[^V3-541]

From there, they say, delicate and soft nerves spread outward and connect with the roots of the senses. They branch through the substance of the brain like a spider’s web.

When the qualities of sensible things act upon the sense organs and change their condition, that change passes into the nerves at the front of the brain, where all these nerves begin. In this way, “the effects of all sensible things come together at the imaginative power...”[^V3-542]

The imaginative power then sends these effects to the thinking power, whose center they place in the middle of the brain.

The thinking power distinguishes one sensible thing from another. It separates truth from falsehood, right from wrong, and what is useful from what is harmful. It then passes this material to the memory.

The memory is located at the back of the brain. Its task is to preserve knowledge until it is needed or recalled.

Then comes the power of speech, whose path runs through the throat to the tongue. This power expresses what is in the soul.

Finally comes the productive power, whose path runs through the hands and fingers. Through it, the activity of the soul appears in actions such as writing and all forms of craftwork.[^V3-543]

This picture of the means of knowledge as material organs, with an organic connection between the centers of sensation and the centers of thought, represents a materialist position on knowledge.

It also rejects the idea that thought can exist in complete independence from sensory reality.

In other words, thought is not simply an inward contemplation cut off from the world. Nor is it Sufi unveiling, mystical illumination, or supernatural inspiration coming from outside human consciousness.

We are not simply drawing this conclusion ourselves. The Ikhwān’s texts come very close to saying it directly.

Two examples make this especially clear.

The first concerns the crafts.

In their view, crafts are produced through the joint action of the thinking power and the productive power. The work of the hand and the work of thought therefore meet in a single intellectual activity.

This clearly points to a two-way relationship between intellectual activity and practical social activity.

In the same passage, they make the thinking power responsible for everything they call “thought, reflection, conception, consideration, synthesis, analysis, combination, analogy, insight,”[^V3-544] “omen-reading,”[^V3-545] divination, passing thoughts, inspiration, receiving revelation, and imagining dreams. They then explain each of these functions separately.[^V3-546]

But as we have already seen, the thinking power itself depends on the forms of sensible things passed to it by the imagination after those forms have moved through the front of the brain.

Its task is to examine those same forms and distinguish what is true from what is false. And this activity, according to the Ikhwān, takes place through the middle of the brain.

If that is so, then everything produced by the thinking power comes from the organic connection between the “spiritual powers”—the powers of thought—and the five senses.

In other words, it comes from the relationship between thought and the external world.

The second example comes from their discussion of the science of the stars in the mathematical part of the *Epistles*.

There they reject the belief held by “many people” that the science of the stars means claiming knowledge of the unseen.

What matters most here is the reason they give.

They write:

> “Knowledge of the unseen means knowing what will happen without inference, without causes, and without any means at all. No created being possesses knowledge of this kind—not an astrologer, not a soothsayer, not a prophet, and not an angel. Only God does...”[^V3-547]

They then explain that human knowledge falls into three kinds: knowledge of the past, knowledge of the present, and knowledge of the future.

Human beings also have three ways of reaching these kinds of knowledge: hearing or reports for what has already happened, sensation for what is present, and inference for what will happen in the future.[^V3-547]

This rejects knowledge of the unseen precisely because such knowledge would not be based on inference or causes.

In other words, it rejects any form of knowledge that is not based on proof or on a causal connection.

This theoretical commitment to inference and causation as necessary foundations of knowledge was itself one of the intellectual features of the age that produced these encyclopedic thinkers.

But what method do the Ikhwān use when they reason from evidence?

Is it the Aristotelian method based on the logical syllogism, where knowledge is derived from the formal arrangement of premises?

Or is it an inductive method, where knowledge is drawn from the movement of reality itself?

Everything we have found so far allows us to say that their method is inductive and goes beyond the Aristotelian method.

We have already seen that they connect rational knowledge with sensory knowledge, that they believe changes in quantity can affect changes in quality, and that they connect intellectual activity with practical activity.

All this moves them away from the Aristotelian approach, which treats qualities as fixed and static states unaffected by changes in quantity.

One significant example is their treatment of definition.

Traditional logic recognized five basic elements used in definition. The Ikhwān added a sixth, which they called the “individual.” Their six elements therefore became: genus, species, individual, differentia, specific property, and common accident.[^V3-548]

By “individual,” they mean concrete individual things: sensible beings, or the particular things from which genera and species are formed.

This addition is important.

It shows that these encyclopedic thinkers did not see definition as something confined to fixed concepts. They connected it with the movement of real existence and allowed it to move with that reality.

There is another important sign of the same approach.

As we noted earlier, the Ikhwān say that anyone seeking knowledge of “all particular sensible things” must answer nine questions. In their words, such a thing is “investigated through nine questions: Does it exist? What is it? How much is it? What kind is it? Where is it? When is it? Why is it? Who is it?”[^V3-549]

By asking these questions about things in the external world, the Ikhwān reveal their method of inquiry.

It is, as we can see, a dynamic method that follows things through their different aspects, states, and forms of change.

Some modern scholars have described these questions as a kind of program for scientific and philosophical research, and have used them as evidence of the scientific direction followed by some Arab scholars in their investigations.[^V3-550]

##### Is Knowledge Innate or Acquired?

Everything we have already seen in our discussion of the Ikhwān al-Ṣafāʾ’s theory of knowledge gives us a ready answer to this question.

For them, all knowledge is acquired through the senses, reason, and proof.

But they also answer the question directly.

They state that knowledge exists **potentially** in the human soul. To become actual knowledge, it requires a teacher and a guide.

They describe the soul of a learned person as knowing **in actuality**,[^V3-551] because that person has acquired knowledge and has thereby become learned.

They make the same point when discussing debates about the nature of God.

They warn against entering such debates through ignorance or mere imitation, but they are more tolerant toward people who, “when they hear questions of this kind, feel their souls drawn toward the answers and wish to understand their meaning.”

They excuse such people because “their souls are still sound. They have not yet been twisted by corrupt opinions, and they have not yet sunk deeply into the sleep of ignorance. Such a person therefore needs to lead them gradually along the path of instruction...”[^V3-552]

Knowledge, then, is not something a person is born possessing.

What a person is born with is the capacity to acquire it.

This is what it means to say that knowledge exists potentially in the human soul.

We can see the same idea in the way the Ikhwān divide people into three groups according to the kind of religious knowledge and moral teaching suited to them. Their division is based on differences in people’s current levels of knowledge and understanding.

But they do not close the path from the lowest level to the highest.

They tell a person at the lower level:

> “If you are able to reach the highest ranks and the highest degrees, do not be satisfied with what is lower. Work hard in your search. Those above you were once not at the ranks they now occupy, but then they worked hard in seeking...”[^V3-553]

This means that knowledge is not an inborn possession of some people but not others. Nor is it a privilege belonging to one person, group, or social class.

It is open to anyone willing to work to acquire it.

All people are equal in their basic capacity to receive and gain knowledge.

#### 8 — The Objective Existence of the External World

Our discussion of the Ikhwān al-Ṣafāʾ’s theory of knowledge brings us to another question:

**Do the Ikhwān recognize that the external world exists objectively, independently of our minds?**

The answer matters for more than simply identifying their philosophical position.

It also helps us understand the relationship between their philosophy and the philosophies of antiquity.

If their answer is yes, this would provide further support for a point we have repeatedly argued in earlier parts of this book.

Arab philosophy in its rationalist current was strongly influenced by Platonism and Neoplatonism, especially the Neoplatonic theory of emanation. But despite this influence, it developed an independent way of understanding the material world. It overcame the subjective idealist tendency of both philosophies and even used ideas taken from them as a starting point for positions that moved in a materialist direction.

This would also strengthen another argument we have made: that Arab-Islamic philosophy should not simply be described as “eclectic.”

So how does the philosophy of the Ikhwān answer the question?

Let us begin by returning to what they say about knowledge of God.

As we have just seen, they distinguish between seeking knowledge of God’s essence on the one hand, and seeking knowledge of “particular sensible things, universals, and all beings, including substances and accidents,” on the other.

They connect the knowledge of sensible things and other existing things with the full range of categories used to describe existence. But when it comes to God, they restrict the subject to His identity and attributes.

They are therefore speaking about two different objects of knowledge. Each is independent of the other, and one cannot simply be treated in the same way as the other.

Sensible things, rational universals, individual beings, species, and genera—in other words, everything that falls under the categories of quantity, quality, place, time, and position, and everything whose essence is drawn from its existence—all of these have an existence separate from the existence of God.

They are therefore objects of knowledge different from the object of divine knowledge.

That is what their words state, and that is how we understand them.

But what does this mean?

When we treat something as an independent object of knowledge, we are recognizing that it has an objective existence of its own.

So if the external world—particular sensible things, their substances and accidents, their qualities and the things that possess those qualities, the space and time in which they exist, and the individuals, species, and genera through which we describe them—is an independent object of knowledge, then it clearly has an objective existence.

That is the first point.

But it is not enough by itself.

It may not even be convincing, because the *Epistles* contain many ideas taken from Plato, basic elements from Plotinus, and a Pythagorean use of numbers to explain some features of existence.

We therefore need to examine these borrowed ideas more closely.

Did they remain philosophically unchanged when the Ikhwān adopted them? Or did they adapt to the intellectual climate of the *Epistles*, to their age and their society, and take on a different direction?

Let us begin with Plato.

There is no doubt that the *Epistles* show a clear influence from Plato’s theory of Forms.

The Ikhwān say that the world proceeds from God in stages.

At the first stages of this process there are four simple substances, arranged in the following order:

First, Intellect.

Second, the Universal Soul.

Third, Prime Matter.

Fourth, Nature.

They describe Nature as “the psychic form imposed upon the world of the celestial spheres, because it is one of the powers of the Universal Soul. It gives the celestial bodies the forms proper to them and places them in the locations suited to them...”

The Ikhwān say that each of these first four levels has a “counterpart” or “model” in the “lower world,” meaning the external material world.

Their reason is that everything below God exists in pairs, so that God alone remains the One, the Unique, the Eternal Refuge.

Intellect has its counterpart in this world, by which they mean the human intellect.

The Universal Soul has its counterpart in the lower world: the individual soul that extends over all living products of natural bodies.

Prime Matter, which is the matter of the celestial spheres, also has a counterpart in the lower world: the matter of the primary elements, meaning the four basic elements of matter.

And “the psychic form imposed upon the world of the spheres”—that is, Nature as a concept—has its counterpart in the lower world in the human form: the human being, which they regard as the perfection of natural bodies and sensible individuals.[^V3-554]

There is no doubt that these “models” and “counterparts” have a Platonic origin.

But there is equally little doubt that their resemblance to Plato is mainly formal rather than fundamental.

There are major differences between the Ikhwān’s theory and Plato’s.

First, Plato treats the Forms as separate causes of things in the sensible world.

Each Form is the direct cause of a particular thing, and that thing depends on its Form as a shadow or image depends on what produces it.

The causes—that is, the Forms—are therefore many and exist alongside one another, with no necessary relation between one Form and another.

In religious language, we could say that the Forms function almost like many separate lords or gods.

This conflicts, first of all, with the Ikhwān’s starting point of monotheism. For them, God is “the One, the Unique, the Eternal Refuge,” and He alone creates all existence and everything that exists.

It also conflicts with the ordered sequence through which things proceed in the Ikhwān’s system: Intellect, then the Universal Soul, then Prime Matter, then Nature.

No comparable chain connects Plato’s Forms to one another.

There is a second major difference.

For Plato, the Form and the sensible thing that depends on it do not possess the same kind of existence.

The Form alone has true existence. The sensible thing does not possess true existence in the same sense.

For the Ikhwān, however, the model and its counterpart, or one counterpart and the other, are equal in the basic fact of existence.

The very words “model” and “counterpart” point toward this equality.

A thing can only be a true counterpart of another if the two share something at the same level. And here the first thing they share is existence itself.

In other words, both have real, objective existence.

If we now turn to the relationship between the Ikhwān and Plotinus, or Neoplatonism, we again find a real connection.

Their idea of emanation undoubtedly has a Plotinian origin.

But did it keep its Plotinian character unchanged?

The answer is no.

In the philosophy of the Ikhwān, as in other forms of Arab-Islamic philosophy, the Plotinian theory of emanation changed both in form and in substance.

The formal differences are straightforward.

The stages of emanation differ in their terminology, order, and number. This is well known, and there is no need to go into the details here.

The deeper difference is more important.

Plotinus’s teaching ultimately leads to a denial of the material world as genuinely real. The Ikhwān, by contrast, recognize the real and objective existence of the material world.[^V3-555]

There is another difference between Plato and Plotinus on the one hand, and the Ikhwān on the other.

It concerns sensory knowledge.

Plato and Plotinus reject sensory knowledge—or, more precisely, they do not accept it as a reliable form of knowledge.

The Ikhwān, however, treat sensory knowledge as a path toward rational knowledge, as we saw in our discussion of their theory of knowledge.

We must also consider the relationship between the Ikhwān al-Ṣafāʾ and Pythagorean thought, because there is a widespread misunderstanding here that has become almost taken for granted in both Orientalist and Arab scholarship.

The Dutch Orientalist De Boer expressed this view when he wrote that the Ikhwān “explain things according to the system of numbers.”[^V3-556]

This common assumption originally grew out of the desire of some Western Orientalists to trace the Ikhwān’s treatment of number back to Pythagorean philosophy and its reactionary ideology.

Historically, Pythagoras—the philosopher who treated number as the original principle or first essence of existence—appeared in the history of philosophy as a defender of the ideology of the Greek aristocracy against Greek democracy within the slave-owning class. He was also the founder of the Pythagorean association that represented that aristocracy.

Earlier, when discussing the ideology expressed in the philosophy of Plotinus, we said that it called for withdrawal from the material world.

The same can be said here of the ideology expressed in the philosophy of Pythagoras.

---

This is because a slave society is built on a complete and absolute separation between its two classes, the “masters” and the “slaves,” with no intermediate class between them. Pythagorean philosophy was therefore based on the idea that number is the foundation of everything that exists and that, as Aristotle describes the Pythagorean principle, number exists separately[^V3-557]—that is, independently[^V3-558] and outside things themselves. Aristotle says that, for the Pythagoreans, the One exists in itself. The opposing view, by contrast, holds that the One exists only in things and cannot exist outside them.[^V3-559]

When Aristotle discusses this Pythagorean principle, he asks how numerical proportions—whether of number or magnitude—could be enough to explain changes in phenomena. Quantitative changes may be explained through number and magnitude, but qualitative changes cannot possibly be explained in this way.[^V3-560]

The Pythagorean principle, then, comes down to this: the essence of the world lies in something abstract and separate from it—number. This amounts to denying the material world and its objective existence.

Let us now compare the Brethren of Purity’s idea of number with this Pythagorean principle. This will allow us to see the real limits of the connection between the two, as it appears in the Brethren’s own texts.

One of these texts[^V3-561] says, in summary, that dividing numbers into four levels—units, tens, hundreds, and thousands—is a convention that the sages arranged by their own choice. They did this so that the order of numbers would correspond to the order of things in nature.

The Brethren explain this by saying that many things in nature come in groups of four: the four qualities—heat, cold, moisture, and dryness; the four elements—fire, air, water, and earth; the four bodily humors—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile; the four seasons; the four directions; the four winds; and the four kinds of generated beings—minerals, plants, animals, and human beings.

They then say that God’s wisdom required a correspondence between natural things and the spiritual things “above natural things.” These things above nature also exist on four levels: God, the active universal intellect, the universal soul, and prime matter.

They write:

> “God’s relation to existing things is like the relation of one to number. The intellect’s relation to them is like the relation of two to number. The soul’s relation to existing things is like the relation of three to number. And matter’s relation to them is like the relation of four.”

They then explain their own view of how numbers are put together. In short, they believe that every number, however large, is ultimately based on the first four numbers: 1, 2, 3, and 4.

Everything above four is made from them. Five is four plus one. Six is four plus two. Seven is four plus three. Eight is four plus one plus three. Nine is four plus two plus three. Ten is four plus one plus two plus three, and so on, however high the numbers go.

The only basis for their decision to treat the first four numbers as the foundation of all numbers is their belief that wisdom requires a correspondence between natural things and things above nature. Since natural things were arranged in groups of four, and since the first simple substances above nature were also four in number, the numbers from one to four were given this special status.

The same line of reasoning led them to say that there are twelve signs of the zodiac because twelve is the best of numbers. Why? Because it is the first “extra” number—that is, the first even number greater than ten. They continue in the same way, comparing divisions of numbers with divisions of the celestial spheres, which belong to the natural world.[^V3-562] They do, after all, recognize that all celestial bodies are physical bodies—that is, material.

The idea of **correspondence** dominates the Brethren’s view here. But it rests on something important that we need to keep in mind.

For the Brethren, there are two worlds: “one physical and the other spiritual.”

The physical world consists of the outermost celestial sphere and everything within it: the other spheres, the stars, the elements—the first components of matter—and the three kinds of generated beings: minerals, plants, and animals.

The spiritual world is the world of intellect. It includes the soul and forms that are not three-dimensional bodies. They write that “the spiritual world surrounds the world of the spheres, just as the world of the spheres surrounds the world of the elements below the sphere of the Moon.”[^V3-563]

We need to pay attention to this point because it gives us the key to the Brethren’s idea of correspondence.

First, their view assumes that there are **two worlds**, not one. This means that the physical world has a real existence of its own. Its existence is just as real as that of the spiritual world.

Second, the two worlds are not cut off from each other. They are connected in an “organic” way: the spiritual world surrounds the world of the spheres, and the world of the spheres surrounds the world of the elements.

Their concern with correspondence is therefore a way of stressing this connection.

The same concern explains the many other correspondences that the Brethren propose between different things in the world. This is not limited to number.

They also apply the idea to the shapes and movements of the celestial spheres. The spheres are round and their movements are circular because, in their view, the spherical shape is the best of all shapes and circular motion is the best of all motions.[^V3-564] This is similar to the way they rank some numbers above others.

They also apply correspondence to musical instruments and musical notes. They write that “the musical sages limited the strings of the lute to four—no fewer and no more—so that their compositions”—meaning the melodies they created—“would correspond to natural things.”[^V3-565]

They then describe the correspondence between each string and the natural element and quality that matches it. They also describe correspondences between the different notes played on those strings and the different “temperaments” of listeners.

They believe that this pattern of correspondence can be seen objectively between “the body of the world as a whole, with all its spheres, individual stars, four elements, and the way they are arranged one inside another,” and “the body of a single animal, a single human being, and a single city”—that is, a single society.[^V3-566]

They extend this idea beyond nature and living organisms to social phenomena as well. We have already seen the example of the city. They also apply it to language and poetry, which they treat as social phenomena. They see a correspondence between the number of letters in the Arabic alphabet and the number of stations of the Moon, and another between the metrical units of Arabic poetry and the different forms of melody in Arabic song.[^V3-567]

The Brethren use the same approach when they discuss **harmony** in compounds made from different things and materials. They explain harmony in terms of a balance between numerical proportions and proportions of magnitude.

Here, alongside the idea of correspondence, we can also glimpse a kind of dialectical view of the relation between opposites and their unity.

They speak of differences in the strength of the elements of matter, conflicts between natural qualities, differences between forms, and differences between places. They then describe how these conflicting elements can be brought together when they are combined in particular numerical proportions and amounts. This creates balance, and from that balance comes a unity of things that were previously in conflict.

They give several examples. A musician combines clashing notes into a musical composition. A painter brings contrasting colors together into a harmonious visual whole. A chemist combines different substances in carefully measured proportions to make medicines and remedies. A cook balances taste, color, smell, and quantity when preparing food.

They apply the same idea to the balance of proportions in the natural formation of minerals and in their chemical analysis, and to the relations between the substances that make up living bodies and their organic parts.[^V3-568]

We have gone into this at some length because we need to be clear about what happens when we seriously compare the Pythagorean view of number with the Brethren’s view.

The comparison reveals a basic difference between them, both in their theoretical starting point and in their philosophical direction.

On the theoretical level, nothing in the Brethren’s statements that we have quoted or summarized gives us the right to say that they “explain things according to a system of numbers,” that they “reduce everything to number and explain everything through it,” or that they regard number as the source of nature, its primary essence, or the origin of its existence in the Pythagorean sense.

In fact, what they say points in the opposite direction.

First, they say that the different levels of number are a matter of convention, arranged by the sages according to their own choice. This rules out the idea that numbers were arranged by God or contain “divine secrets” in the Brethren’s philosophy.

Second, in their account of correspondence, **natural things come first**. They are the original model to which the sages make the numbers correspond.

When the Brethren go on to speak about correspondence between natural things, things “above nature,” and numbers, the correspondence is no more than quantitative. And beyond that, it is simply one part of the larger cosmic system of correspondences that the authors of the *Epistles* propose.

That larger system includes the bodily shapes of the celestial spheres, their movements, the components of the physical and spiritual worlds, the relations between living bodies and the celestial spheres, and number itself.

That is the difference at the level of theory.

At the level of philosophical direction, the comparison leads us to another important conclusion. The difference is not only between the Brethren and the Pythagoreans. It also separates them from Platonism and Neoplatonism.

As we have seen from their own statements, the Brethren recognize that the material world really exists. In other words, they recognize its objective existence.

---

During this comparison, the Brethren of Purity’s idea of **correspondence** came clearly into view. Within that idea, we also encountered the role of numerical proportions and measurable quantities in the relations between things in the material world. The Brethren treat these proportions as one form of correspondence—or as one expression of the overall order they believe governs existence.

Their idea of correspondence reminds us of the idea of **harmony** that dominates Plotinus’s view of the world. In his case, harmony served as an ideological expression of his “justification” of the slave system.

Does this mean that the Brethren’s correspondence and Plotinus’s harmony are really the same thing?

Of course, we cannot say that the Brethren learned nothing from Plotinus’s idea of harmony. On the contrary, they were probably influenced by it. But the two ideas begin from different philosophical and ideological positions.

For Plotinus, harmony begins from a determinist outlook dominated by a sense that fate rules everything.[^V3-569] This reflects the ideology of the slave-based social order expressed in his philosophy.

In the Brethren’s idea of correspondence, by contrast, **human will and choice** become part of the picture wherever human activity is involved.

This too is connected with their ideology. Their ideas express social and class interests and ambitions that conflict with the principle of absolute necessity, or determinism. That principle lay at the heart of the ideology of the ruling caliphate, which presented its authority as something required by divine necessity—by God’s decree and predestination.

The role of will and choice appears in several areas of the Brethren’s thought.

We see it first in the way they present correspondence itself. When they discuss how conflicting things come together and form a unified whole, they do not say that this happens through an unavoidable necessity.

We may recall their statement in an earlier passage that hostile and conflicting elements “cannot come together unless someone brings them together.”

And this “someone who brings them together” is not a will that exists outside the world. Their own practical examples make that clear.

All of the examples involve deliberate human action. A musician takes clashing notes and turns them into a unified musical pattern. A painter brings contrasting colors together into a harmonious whole. A chemist combines different substances in measured proportions and produces a new substance in which those differences are united. A cook takes different ingredients, tastes, colors, and smells and combines them into a single dish in which the differences work together.

In every one of these examples, the combination is clearly produced through the will of the person doing the combining.

#### 9 — A Turn Toward Quantitative Rather Than Qualitative Analysis

There is something very important here that deserves our attention: the Brethren’s idea of numerical proportions and measurable quantities, and the effects these have on the way things change.

This idea points to a clear development among the scientists of their age. They were increasingly studying natural and social phenomena through **experiment** and through **quantitative analysis**.

In this respect, they moved beyond the tendency of ancient philosophy and science—that is, the metaphysical approach[^V3-570] that stopped at the fixed and static qualities of things and essences.

This move toward quantity was an important historical step toward explaining qualitative changes in the movement of matter through quantitative changes.[^V3-571]

These scientists and philosophers understood that quantitative proportions could produce new quantities of matter. At the same time, they had a relative understanding of the dialectic of opposing forces and of the way opposites can form a unity.

Together, these ideas represented an advanced step for their age in the development of scientific and practical thought. They also marked progress in attempts to explain the world by understanding the movement of matter.[^V3-572]

This point confirms something we argued earlier. The encyclopedic character of the Brethren’s work was a feature of an entire historical age. It was not simply the product of a limited Shiʿi or Ismāʿīlī sectarian movement confined within the boundaries of religious thought, as most Orientalists and many modern Arab researchers have claimed.

#### 10 — One Way of Understanding Necessity and Freedom

Let us return to the role of free will and choice in the Brethren’s system of correspondence. Another passage gives us what was, in relative terms, an advanced way of understanding necessity and freedom—perhaps even by the standards of their own age.

The passage appears in their discussion of the benefits of astrology:

> “Know, my brother, that there are many benefits in knowing the science of the stars. One is that if a person knows that some event will happen in the future, or after a number of days, he may be able to protect himself from some of its effects—not by preventing it from happening, but by guarding against it or preparing for it. This is what people normally do. They prepare for the cold of winter by gathering warm coverings, and for the heat of summer by seeking shelter.[^V3-573] They prepare for years of high prices by storing supplies, flee from places of unrest and keep their distance from them, and avoid travel when there is danger...”[^V3-574]

Even if we do **not** remove from their argument their belief in a regular causal connection between the movements of the stars and events on Earth, especially events in human life, and even if we do **not** remove the supernatural methods[^V3-575] they mention in this context for protecting people from the harmful effects of natural laws, the words quoted above still have a clear significance.

They show that the Brethren had some awareness of the relation between necessity and freedom, even though they did not know how to formulate the issue in the terms later used by the laws of dialectical materialism.

Their meaning is quite clear. They understood that necessity—to use our modern term—is not an unavoidable fate from which there is no escape. If human beings learn its laws—notice their words, “Know, my brother, that there are many benefits in knowing the science of the stars”—that is, if they learn the laws of nature, they can free themselves from some of their harmful effects.[^V3-576]

We do **not** have the right to go a step further and add that human beings can make those laws serve their own interests.

First, the Brethren themselves did not say this. Second, the historical conditions in which they lived were not yet developed enough for the idea to be expressed in this form.

To sum up, the place given to number and to numerical and measurable proportions in the Brethren’s philosophy represents one form of **correspondence**, which they see as a general system and not as something limited to number alone.

That is the first point.

Second, their treatment of these proportions represents a progressive intellectual position on the relations between things in the material world—a world whose real, concrete, objective existence they recognize.

The importance of this progressive element is not limited to the theoretical advances made in the natural sciences, including mathematics, among the Arabs of the Brethren’s age. It also points to progress in linking theoretical thought with experiment in these sciences.[^V3-577]

Our purpose in the comparisons made in this section has been to make clear the basic difference—in philosophical direction and ideological content—between the philosophy of the Brethren of Purity on the one hand, and Pythagoreanism, Platonism, and Neoplatonism on the other.

The difference is this: those three philosophies deny the material world an objective existence, while the Brethren’s philosophy recognizes that the material world exists objectively.[^V3-578]

Their philosophy also contains materialist positions in several areas. But these positions do not remove it from its overall idealist framework.

As we continue our discussion of the Brethren’s philosophy, we may encounter further evidence supporting this argument.

#### 11 — Early Seeds of a Theory of Natural Evolution

We have now seen several things about the Brethren of Purity’s view of nature.

They looked toward quantitative changes as a basis for qualitative changes in nature. They treated causal relations between things in the natural world as a basic condition of knowledge. They rejected miracles, supernatural events, and knowledge of the unseen even for prophets and messengers. In some of their texts,[^V3-579] they even rejected a supernatural basis for revelation, inspiration, and astrological prediction. And they treated the external, material world as something that really and objectively exists.

Once we have seen all this, it becomes easier to understand how their picture of natural formation could contain an early, embryonic seed of a theory of the evolution of species—a theory that did not enter the language of science until the middle of the nineteenth century.

Perhaps this step followed from the others. Perhaps it helped produce them. Perhaps the developments simply occurred alongside one another.

That is not the important question here.

What matters is that all these elements appear together. Taken as a whole, they show that the Brethren’s encyclopedic work, produced during the great scientific age of Arab civilization in the fourth Islamic century—the tenth century CE—recorded one of the major features of that age: a growing tendency to explain the world in materialist terms, driven underneath by ideological forces.

This was no doubt connected with the growing and deepening interaction between knowledge of nature and society on the one hand, and the production of material necessities on the other.

At the same time, class divisions were becoming sharper and productive forces were breaking through older limits within this society.

Taken together, these conditions inevitably found an expression in the world of ideas. Ideological conflicts developed between thinkers who came from peasant, artisan, or impoverished social groups and the state, which served as the political power of the economically dominant class.

These thinkers also needed weapons for their ideological confrontation with the state. The new scientific knowledge itself became one of those weapons.

So if our impression is correct that the Brethren had, in some form, arrived at an idea of the evolution of species, this would mean that they had raised against the official ideology the sharpest weapon known to the ideological conflicts of their age.[^V3-580]

But we cannot accept that impression without qualification.

The problem is that we have a number of texts preserved in their encyclopedia, the *Epistles*, and these texts have a meaning and content that we cannot simply ignore.

So let us read them carefully, while keeping our reservations in mind, and then ask what they are actually saying.

The first passage is directed against the theologians who claimed that God was the direct source of the actions of nature.

The Brethren write that these theologians:

> “Because they did not know what nature is, attributed all its actions to the Creator (...) and fell into great confusion, bewilderment, and doubt. They understood that every action must have an agent, and when they saw actions whose agent they could not see, they attributed them to the Creator (...) Then they examined these actions and found that some of them were evil and destructive, such as the deaths of children, the misfortunes of good people, the power given to the wicked, the destruction of animals and the injuries, pains, ignorance, and afflictions that befall them (...) We have already explained that all these are the actions of individual souls, all of which are powers of the universal celestial soul as its Creator brought it into being (...) Whatever is good among these actions is attributed to the good individual soul, and whatever is evil is attributed to the evil soul, which then receives its due reward or punishment.
