Know, my brother, that your soul is one of these individual souls. It is one power among the powers of the universal soul. It is not identical with the universal soul, nor is it separate from it, just as your body is one part of the body of the world: it is not the whole world, but neither is it separate from it...”[^V3-581]

What do we learn from this passage?

The direct cause of the actions of nature and of human beings is nature itself and the human being himself. The “individual soul” is the individual being—the particular thing or person.

We know this because elsewhere in the *Epistles* the Brethren say that the human being consists of the soul and the body together, “the combination of the two.”[^V3-582]

Their statement that “the individual soul is one power among the powers of the universal celestial soul” reflects another part of their view: the celestial world and the earthly world are connected by life, by corporeality—that is, material existence[^V3-583]—and by movement, the continuous and unbroken movement of change.

At the same time, each of these two worlds has an existence of its own.

This is what they mean when they say that the individual soul is not identical with the universal soul, but is not separate from it either, just as your body is part of the body of the world without being the whole world or being separated from it.

That concerns the actions carried out by nature and human beings.

But what about the original formation of nature, including the formation of the human being?

Let us read another passage.

After explaining how the world of the celestial spheres and the world of the four elements are formed from absolute body, the Brethren write:

> “Then the spheres revolve around the elements, causing them to mix with one another. From them emerge the generated beings: minerals, plants, and animals.”[^V3-584]

Now consider this passage alongside another:

> “Universal matter—that is, absolute body—passed through a long period of time before it went through a process of formation and its subtle part[^V3-585] became separated from its dense part; before it received the different spherical forms of the heavens, arranged one inside another; before the bodies of the luminous stars became round and took their positions; and before the four elements became distinct, took their proper places, and entered into their ordered system.”[^V3-586]

From these two passages, we can understand the celestial bodies, the elements of nature, and the minerals, plants, and animals that arose from them as having formed through an internal process that unfolded in stages.

The process began with absolute body. It took a long period of time before this body entered a new stage of formation. During that stage, a kind of “selection” took place: the subtle became separated from the dense.

This “selection” then allowed absolute body to take on its first material forms—the spherical forms of the celestial spheres.

The process then continued within these forms, with the spheres being “arranged one inside another.”

From there it passed into another stage, until “the bodies of the luminous stars became round” and “took their positions.”

At this point, another kind of selection took place. The four elements became distinct from one another, meaning that each acquired its own particular natural qualities.

Once those qualities appeared, the process continued by arranging the elements in their different levels and ordering the laws of their movement.

What happened next?

We find the answer in another passage.

In summary, the Brethren say that after the elements “mixed with one another,” they produced the “generated beings”: minerals, plants, and animals.

They describe the way these beings emerged in a kind of ascending sequence, from lower forms to higher ones.

Minerals appeared first. Plants came next, followed by animals.

Their explanation is that the lower form serves as a tool and material for the one that comes after it. Minerals provide the material for the formation of plants, while minerals and plants together provide the material for the formation of animals.

Animal life itself then develops from lower to higher forms until it reaches the human being.[^V3-587]

The human being is therefore the highest of the generated beings found on Earth.

Why, then, does the world of the simple substances begin with the highest and move downward—the active intellect, then the universal soul, then prime matter, then nature—while the physical world moves in the opposite direction, from lower forms to higher ones?

The Brethren answer that the luminous substances contain no composition, difference, or variation of the kind found in physical things. Their only differences come from their rank and from how close they are to God.

The physical world, by contrast, is subject to decay and transformation. It therefore begins with what is lower and ends with what is better.

That is why human beings appeared only after minerals, plants, and animals already existed. Human beings need and benefit from all three.

If human beings had appeared before them, the Brethren say, their creation would have been pointless, because they could not survive or live without them.[^V3-588]

This gives us a picture in which earthly beings are “generated” through an ascending sequence.

The lower form is “a tool used by what comes after it,” while animal life itself develops through different forms, rising from lower forms to higher ones until the human being appears.

Of course, the Brethren themselves do not use the words “evolution,” “selection,” “process,” or “species” in any of these passages. These words are our way of translating the picture they present in the language of their own age.

But the picture itself is not far from what those terms describe.

The idea of a **process**, which is the key point here, is especially close to being stated directly in their texts, even if they use different words.

And there are other passages that strengthen this impression.

One says that minerals, plants, and animals can transform into one another as their qualities change, as their quantities differ, as their structures vary, and as those structures change into one another.[^V3-589]

Another describes “the process of transformations that begins in the powers of the vegetative soul, then extends to the animal soul, and finally to the rational”—that is, human—“soul.”[^V3-590]

When we add these passages, and the many others like them scattered throughout the *Epistles*, to those already discussed, we have good reason to think that the idea of formation taking place through a “process” was not far from the Brethren’s thinking.

But an important question remains.

Was this process entirely internal? Or, in the Brethren’s view, did some force outside these generated beings intervene in its course?

This is the difficult point.

It is also exactly where our reservations begin.

The Brethren themselves seem to become cautious whenever they approach a sensitive idea of this kind.

Again and again, they circle around an idea at length. Just as they seem about to reach it—or to enter the point where its real “secret” lies—they suddenly become cautious. They pull back and begin circling around it again, until the idea disappears beneath a deliberately constructed mass of expressions with supernatural meanings.

Many an “idea” is lost in their historical encyclopedia beneath expressions of this kind. And many extremely important pieces of evidence about their real thinking could have been handled with far greater scholarly confidence if they had not wrapped their arguments in so much caution.

Take the question before us.

We read the earlier passages closely. Then we turn to the Brethren’s definition of the word **“living”** in their section on “definitions and descriptions,”[^V3-591] which is an important source for establishing the precise meaning of their philosophical concepts and terms.

This definition requires no close interpretation because it is perfectly clear.

A living thing, they say, is “that which moves by itself.”

Taken together, all this makes the movement of natural formation appear to be an internal process that follows its own regular course.

But then, suddenly, theological language appears between the lines and introduces a force from outside that internal process.

For example, the Brethren explain how earthly natural bodies are generated through the movement of the celestial spheres around the four elements and through the mixing of those elements as a result of that movement.

At this point, we can see the process unfolding internally. Minerals are the first things “generated” from the mixture.

According to the ascending law of generation they themselves have proposed—from lower forms to higher ones—we would then expect plants to be generated from minerals through the same internal process.

But precisely at this point, they tell us that plants appeared “when God decreed that plants should be food for animals.”

The process therefore no longer remains entirely within its own internal course. God’s decree enters as a factor in the creation of plants, and the process leaves the path of its own internal laws.

How should a sound method of research deal with this difficulty?

There are two possibilities.

Either the Brethren included statements of this kind because they genuinely believed them, or they included them as a precaution—to protect themselves from the authorities or to avoid provoking the anger of simple-minded followers.

Which explanation should we consider more likely?

The two possibilities do not carry equal weight.

Everything we have found so far in this study gives us reason to favor the second.

In many of their philosophical and ideological positions, these encyclopedic thinkers have shown a tendency to explain the world by starting from the natural sciences rather than from theology.

At the same time, we have seen that they had a consciously planned strategy of surrounding their ideas and aims with secrecy, keeping them hidden, and avoiding anything likely to provoke hostility.

We must also consider the broad and inclusive character of their message, which brings different religions, sects, and philosophies together in a single melting pot in order to produce one philosophy—not, we should stress, one religion.

All this gives greater weight to the second possibility.

Still, we stop at saying that it is **more likely**. We do not claim certainty.

There are two reasons.

First, the Brethren never move outside the general framework of idealist thought. The furthest they reach, by the standards of their age, is objective idealism.

Second, whenever we find materialist positions or tendencies in their view of the world, we also find opposing positions and tendencies surrounding them.

The clearest example of this contradiction is their belief in the duality of the two worlds. From this follows the duality of soul and body.

But could we really demand that they leap across several centuries and arrive, with Darwin in the nineteenth century, at the theory of the evolution of species for the first time?[^V3-592]

Let us finally assume that the first possibility is correct: that the Brethren introduced divine will into the process of natural formation because they genuinely believed it belonged there.

Even then, the historical importance of their picture of the natural species developing in an “ascending” direction is not reduced, even if their idea appeared only in embryonic form and remained wrapped in supernatural beliefs.

Many forms of human knowledge that later, after the European Renaissance and through the development of society, science, and technology, became scientific facts or practical social achievements—and some of which became absolute truths[^V3-593]—first appeared as “visions,” spontaneous ideas, religious images, or poetic forms.

In the case before us, however, we can go further.

The Brethren’s idea does not appear to have been simply a fantasy or a spontaneous intuition. It belonged instead to a kind of thought grounded in the relative social and scientific development of their age and connected with other ideas in their own encyclopedic work that had a scientific character.

One example is their idea that qualitative changes can arise from quantitative changes, along with the other ideas we have already discussed.

#### 12 — The Origin of the World

It may seem strange to move so suddenly from an idea with a materialist side in the philosophy of the Brethren of Purity to an idea that completely contradicts it.

The Brethren assume that the world **came into being**—that is, that it is not the cause of its own existence.

This assumption clearly conflicts with the idea that natural formation takes place through a self-driven “process.”

Their discussion of the world’s coming into being is different from the sensitive subjects we were examining only a few lines ago.

Here they speak with complete clarity. There is no room for ambiguity.

That is hardly surprising. The belief that the world came into being agreed with the official religious doctrine and with the dominant official ideology. There was therefore no need for the Brethren to be cautious.

Yet they seem to defend this position with a certain enthusiasm.

So what exactly are they defending so strongly?

Is it really the idea that the world came into being for its own sake? Or is some other concern hidden behind it?

As usual, we will first let the texts speak. Then we will decide how to answer.

Let us begin with the passage that addresses the question directly:

> “Know that our purpose in discussing the movements of the world, the movements of its universal and particular parts, and the many forms taken by those movements is to disprove the claim of those who say that the world is eternal. Different movements show that its states differ, and something that moves through changing states cannot be eternal. What is eternal remains in a single state: it does not change or transform, and no new state arises in it. Nothing that exists has this quality except the one and unique God. Nothing apart from God Most High can exist in this way.”[^V3-594]

There is no room for interpretation here. Their statement is too direct and explicit.

God alone is eternal. The entire world came into being.

The reason, they say, is that the whole world is in motion, in both its particular and universal parts. Whatever moves also changes. And whatever changes cannot be eternal.

This is a traditional theological equation. The Brethren have taken it over almost word for word, without changing anything in it.

But whom are they arguing against when they reject the eternity of the world?

They explain that those who believe in an eternal world imagined that “the world is at rest, and what is at rest does not change its states.”

But, the Brethren continue, the world is not at rest as these thinkers imagined. They have already shown this through the many movements of both its universal and particular parts—movements that no sound mind can deny.

These include the movements of the stars, the revolutions of the celestial spheres, the transformations of the elements of nature, and the formation of the generated beings—minerals, plants, and animals.

Let us continue with the same passage. They write:

> “Know also that every movement occurs in something that moves. It is a movement belonging to that thing, and it is a cause of something else. Whenever that movement ceases to exist, the effect caused by it also disappears.”

They then give a number of examples from ordinary sensory experience to show that when a thing loses the movement that causes its existence, the thing itself disappears.

They continue:

> “The same is true of the world. If the outermost sphere were to stop revolving, and the stars were to stop their journeys and movements, the courses of night and day, winter and summer, would also stop. Generation and decay would then cease, the order of the world would come to an end ... and the Great Resurrection would begin...”[^V3-595]

Now that we have read the main lines of their argument about the world coming into being, let us return to our earlier question and put it in a different form.

Is their main concern really to prove that the world came into being? Or is something else hiding behind that argument?

It seems to us that the Brethren would have had little reason for such a forceful defense if the world’s having a beginning were the central issue for them.

The theologians, the scholars of religious law, and all the theorists of the official ideology had repeated the same formula long before the Brethren did. Its premises and conclusion had become basic assumptions of theological thought.

Why, then, would this “encyclopedic group,” which had rejected so many of those assumptions both philosophically and ideologically, feel the need to record the same familiar formula in its encyclopedic work with such enthusiasm?

I believe the Brethren had another purpose.

We can begin to see it when we notice that the real force of their argument falls on the **second half** of the passage: their rejection of the claim made by supporters of the world’s eternity that the world is motionless.

The Brethren reject the idea of a static world.

They are deeply concerned to prove that movement fills the whole of it, from the outermost celestial sphere down to the smallest things on Earth.

They are equally concerned to show that the world’s movement is active and productive—that movement is a cause of things.

As they put it:

> “Every movement occurs in something that moves. It is a movement belonging to that thing, and it is a cause of something else.”

They also say that the continued existence of the whole world depends on movement:

> “Whenever that movement ceases to exist, the effect caused by it also disappears.”

Movement is therefore a basic cause that helps constitute the world’s existence.

We say **its existence** because if the cause of a thing disappears, the thing itself disappears. A thing exists or ceases to exist with its cause; otherwise that cause would not truly be its cause.

If movement were not fundamental to the world’s existence, it would make no sense for the Brethren to say that “if the outermost sphere were to stop revolving, and the stars were to stop their journeys and movements,” the cycles of night and day, winter and summer, and so on would also stop.[^V3-596]

This view of movement, appearing in an “encyclopedia” produced during the medieval Arab scientific age, grew out of the rise of scientific thinking among the Arabs at that time.

It was connected with the growing practical and experimental direction of the natural sciences, which developed in response to changing social needs and the needs of production, as we have said more than once.

The authors of this “encyclopedia” seem to have absorbed this scientific outlook very deeply.

For that reason, we can understand the Brethren’s belief that the world came into being as a **result** of their view of movement, rather than the cause of it.

In other words, their scientific conviction that the world is in motion—and that change and becoming, or “transformations” in their own language, are essential features of the world’s existence—led them to believe that the world must have come into being rather than being eternal.

They imagined that eternity necessarily went together with rest, and that rest necessarily went together with eternity.

So when they defend the idea that the world came into being, their real concern is mainly to defend the world’s movement, change, and becoming, and to show that these are inseparable from its existence.

If this interpretation of their position is correct, we can go one step further.

Their acceptance of the idea that the world came into being—assuming that they truly did accept it—would then be based on what they regarded as scientific reasoning rather than on religious faith.

Where did they get this mistaken connection between the eternity of the world and its being at rest, or between the world’s having a beginning and its being in motion?

It may have come from Aristotle’s idea of the **“unmoved mover.”** Aristotle argued that the chain of movements and movers in the world must ultimately end with a first mover that is itself unmoved: God.[^V3-597][^V3-598]

Since God is eternal and is a mover that does not move, the Brethren may have come to associate eternity in their minds with the absence of movement—with rest.

But this immediately raises another question.

If their connection between rest and eternity, or between movement and the world’s having a beginning, was influenced by Aristotle’s theory, why did they take only one side of that theory and not the other?

Aristotle also held that motion is eternal, which in turn means that the world itself is eternal[^V3-599]—that it has always existed.

With this question—or objection—we reach the obscure point in our discussion.

It is “obscure” because the Brethren themselves may deliberately have made it so.

In fact, it seems to us that this was exactly what they did.

If we can uncover what lies behind this point, and if we become convinced that our discovery reflects something real, it may give us a new key to understanding their tactical strategy.

In the passages discussed above, we saw that the Brethren do more than openly say that the world came into being. They also defend this position forcefully and try to prove it through the movement of change and becoming that, in their view, is inseparable from the world’s existence.

For the moment, however, we will put all of that aside. We will also put aside our own interpretation of their position.

Instead, we will turn to other passages in search of the “obscure” point we have just mentioned. In doing so, we can complete the picture rather than looking at the question from only one side and reaching a partial conclusion—which would violate the most basic rules of scientific research.

Not far from the passage in the *Epistles* where we just read their defense of the idea that the world had a beginning, and their clear rejection of the idea that it is eternal, we find the following passage:

> “…You should know that the soul existed for a very long time before it became attached to a body with dimensions. It lived in its spiritual world, its realm of light, and its living abode—that is, an abode of life, not of animals. It was turned toward its cause, the Active Intellect, receiving from it emanation, virtues, and good things, and it lived in pleasure, happiness, and joy. When it became full of these virtues and good things, it experienced something like labor pains and began to seek something onto which it could pour them. Before this, body was empty of shapes, forms, and patterns. So the soul turned toward matter, separating the dense from the subtle and pouring these virtues and good things upon it. When the Creator saw this, He gave the soul access to body and prepared it for the soul. From that body He created the world of the celestial spheres and the layers of the heavens, from the outermost surrounding sphere all the way to the center of the earth, placing the spheres one inside another…”[^V3-600]

The way body is described here amounts to an acknowledgment that it existed before the soul poured onto it what it had received through “emanation, virtues, and good things.” Is this not what they mean when they say that the soul existed “before it became attached to a body with dimensions”? And when they say that “before this, body was empty of shapes, forms, and patterns”? Do these two statements not suggest that body already existed, ready for the soul to be given access to it?

The Brethren themselves give us good reason to understand their words in this way. They say: “From that body He created the world of the celestial spheres.” And they themselves define the term “creation” as “the forming of each thing from another thing,”[^V3-601] while the terms “origination” and “invention,” in their terminology, mean “bringing something into existence from nothing.”[^V3-601]

The world of the celestial spheres was therefore created “from another thing”—in other words, from something that already existed. It was not created “from nothing.”

By “body” here, of course, they mean body in the absolute sense, not the physical body that we perceive through the senses. We therefore have enough evidence to conclude that the Brethren’s “absolute body” resembles Aristotle’s matter, or is essentially the same thing. In other words, this absolute body exists eternally in potential form, much as Aristotelian matter does.[^V3-602] In their theory, the world of the celestial spheres is the first form in which this absolute body, or this “matter,” comes into actual existence.

Once again, the Brethren themselves give us good reason to hold to this conclusion. They use the term “matter” in a sense that does not differ fundamentally from Aristotle’s, even though they go beyond some of Aristotle’s ideas about form.

In fact, for the Brethren of Purity, even the forms of things—not only their matter—have a potential existence before they exist in actuality. Consider what they say:

> “…All things, without exception, are forms and distinct entities[^V3-603] which the Creator—exalted be He—emanated upon the Active Intellect (…) and from the Intellect upon the Universal Celestial Soul, which is the soul of the entire world (…) and from the Universal Soul upon prime matter (…) and from matter upon the individual human soul (…) These are what people form in their thoughts as objects of knowledge after seeing them in matter”—meaning physical matter here—“through the senses. Anyone who wishes to know how the forms of things existed in the Universal Soul before they were emanated upon matter should consider the forms of things made by human beings: how those forms exist in their minds before they bring them into outward existence in the materials used in their crafts.[^V3-604] (…) And anyone who wishes to know how the forms of things existed in the Active Intellect before it emanated them upon the Universal Soul, and how the Universal Soul received those patterns and forms, should consider the forms of knowledge in the minds of scholars, how they pass that knowledge on to students, and how the students receive it. (…) And anyone who wishes to know how objects of knowledge existed in the knowledge of the Creator—mighty and exalted be He—before He emanated them upon the Intellect should consider number, and how it existed in the one that comes before two…”[^V3-605]

If we read these passages together rather than taking them separately, we can draw several important conclusions.

First, when the Brethren use the word “creation”—“from that body He created the world of the celestial spheres”—and define creation as “the forming of each thing from another thing,” this means that the world was created **from something else**. It was not brought into existence “from nothing.”

Here, then, they depart from one of the basic Islamic principles: that the world “came into existence from nothing.”

Second, both form and matter had some kind of existence—a potential existence—before they came into actual existence in the world of physical matter. This is what they mean when they say that “all things, without exception, are forms and distinct entities which the Creator emanated…” and then ask us to consider “how the forms of things existed in the Universal Soul” before being emanated into matter.

This conclusion means that the Brethren went beyond Aristotle. They did away with the separation between matter and form that Aristotle had placed at the foundation of his teaching, and established a connection between the two even outside the material world. They did this through the “process” of emanation, which in their thought took a different direction from its original Plotinian form.

Third, they connected the “spiritual world” and the “bodily world” through one continuous chain of existence. The forms of things pass through this chain from the Active Intellect to prime matter, and then to the world of the celestial spheres, which marks the beginning of the bodily world.[^V3-606]

Indeed, this same connection extends from God, the source of the “emanation,” through both the spiritual and bodily worlds.[^V3-607] This unbroken chain of existence from God down to the material world supports the view that Ibn ʿArabī took the philosophical basis of his theory of the unity of being from the Brethren of Purity.[^V3-608]

All three of these conclusions point toward the same result: the world is eternal, rather than something that came into being in time.

If these conclusions are sound—and all the passages we have quoted support them—then we have reached the heart of the “unclear point” in the Brethren’s position on this question. It would also mean that they were influenced not only by one side of Aristotle’s idea of the “unmoved first mover,” but by the other side as well: the idea of potential existence. This implies that the world is eternal and, as a necessary consequence, that motion is eternal too.

But if our investigation has led us to these conclusions, how are we to reconcile them with the Brethren’s explicit statement that the world had a beginning?

Do the two positions contradict each other, or can they somehow be reconciled? If they do contradict each other—and so far that is how it appears—how do we explain such a striking contradiction? Were the Brethren unaware of where these passages led? Or was this part of a deliberate and consciously planned strategy?

In any case, we now have two conflicting positions before us.

The first is explicit: they reject the idea that the world and motion are eternal and defend the view that both had a beginning.

The second is implicit: it accepts the eternity of both the world and motion.

Which of these two positions, then, truly defines the doctrine of the Brethren of Purity on this question?

At first, it seems more reasonable to accept their explicit position rather than an opposing position that we have reached by inference and that remains open to debate and revision. That is true.

But there is another side of the evidence that we also have to consider.

First, we should compare their position here with their positions on the other questions we have already discussed. We should take account, for example, of some of their materialist tendencies and of the scientific direction of their studies of nature.

Second, we should consider the ideological content of their encyclopedic project as a whole, along with the political direction of their movement.

Third, we should remember how careful they were to keep their activities secret and how cautious they were in presenting their philosophical principles.

Fourth, we should look again at several statements they make while explicitly defending the idea that the world had a beginning. They say that motion is the cause of things and that the continued existence of the world depends on the continued existence of motion. In the *Epistle on Definitions and Descriptions*, as we saw earlier, they also define a living thing as “that which moves by itself.”

Looking at the evidence from this side may shed enough light on the problem to help us find a solution.

In light of these four points, we are inclined to say that the Brethren’s explicit defense of the view that the world had a beginning does not express their true and decisive position on the question.

Their contradiction, as we have uncovered it, seems to have one of two possible explanations.

The first is that they were simply unable, at the level of thought, to reach a firm position. This left them wavering and uncertain.

The second is that their caution and secrecy became so extreme that they openly stated the opposite of what they really believed, while hinting elsewhere at what they did believe. They then covered those hints in deliberately obscure language that could be interpreted in more than one way.

We find it difficult, however, to believe that their caution went so far that it led them into conduct that could hardly be described as anything other than a kind of cowardice. We hesitate to accuse free thinkers such as these of cowardice, or of anything resembling it.

We can therefore accept the first possibility, together with the explanation we gave earlier: their defense of the idea that the world had a beginning was aimed mainly at defending the principle that the world is constantly changing and becoming through the motion inseparable from its existence. Their mistake lay in the assumed connection they made between motion and the idea that motion itself must have had a beginning.

There is still a third possibility. It would clear the Brethren of both contradiction and excessive caution: perhaps the conclusion on which all of this rests is simply wrong.

That possibility certainly exists, and we do not reject it.

Even so, we prefer to retain our conclusion as a methodological approach to the problem—one that treats its historical character consistently and, at the same time, avoids the one-sided and fragmentary approach taken by most modern researchers who study the intellectual heritage.

In any case, throughout this book we consider all our conclusions open to discussion and revision.

---

After all this, let us return to the beginning of the question and make a different assumption.

Let us assume that the Brethren’s real and decisive position is exactly the one they openly stated and defended: that the world had a beginning. Let us also assume that they had no other position, either openly or secretly, that differed from or contradicted this one.

We can make this assumption while keeping our earlier explanation of their position: that their defense of a beginning for the world was mainly a defense of the principle of change and becoming in the world—that is, of the necessary connection between matter and motion.

Starting from this assumption, we can examine their position from another angle.

We have understood them to mean that the world cannot both move—that is, change and transform—and also be eternal. From this we understood that motion itself cannot be eternal.

We also saw that this view of motion—the denial of its eternity—is where they part company with Aristotle, since his teaching rests on the eternity of motion.

Did the Brethren actually study Aristotle’s position, recognize a contradiction in his theory, and then deliberately try to solve it by denying the eternity of the world so that they could also deny the eternity of motion?[^V3-609]

We think this is unlikely.

If their position had been based on recognizing this contradiction in Aristotle, we would expect to find at least some indication of that connection. But we have found nothing in their writings that even hints at Aristotle in this context.

We therefore need to look elsewhere for the source of their connection between motion and the idea that the world had a beginning. We must look within the development of human knowledge itself: both in knowledge inherited from earlier periods and in the new knowledge produced by their own age and society, together with the distinctive intellectual and ideological positions created by the development of that society.

From this point of view, we believe that the development of theoretical and practical research among the Arabs, in both nature and society, strengthened among scientist-philosophers—or philosopher-scientists—the conviction that matter and motion are necessarily connected.

This conviction grew stronger through their direct, and sometimes indirect, contact with the reality of the society around them. That society was full of powerful social and political movements and upheavals, which were producing major changes in the forces that drove production in both the countryside and the cities.

Many texts written by these people—who worked in science and philosophy, and sometimes in politics as well—clearly show their conviction that matter and motion are inseparable. This conviction pushed them to search seriously for the source of matter’s motion.

They did not simply submit to the religious assumptions they had inherited on this question. Nor did they simply submit to the philosophical theories that reached them from various ancient sources, especially Greek ones.

We can see the effects of this serious search very clearly in their many different accounts of the “process” of emanation in the metaphysical world.

They received the “theory” of emanation from Greek and Alexandrian sources as a purely metaphysical idea. But they tried to understand it as a **cosmic process**. They tried to find a connection between the movement of this “process” and the movement of the material world, treating the two as parts of one cosmic movement with a single source.

In the same way, they tried to understand the cosmic order connected with this “process” as a single system. In this system, the world beyond nature and the natural world are joined through one continuous form of existence: real, objective existence.

We also find strong evidence of their serious search for the source of matter’s motion in the many passages where they make clear attempts to locate this source **inside matter itself**, rather than outside it.

This is why it is not simply a meaningless coincidence that, in the epistle where the Brethren define their philosophical and scientific terminology—the *Epistle on Definitions and Descriptions*—they define a living thing as “that which moves by itself.”

The idealist, piecemeal approach of bourgeois researchers does not merely lead them to ignore statements like these when they show a materialist tendency, or to strip them of their materialist meaning. Even when such researchers cannot avoid mentioning them, they try to separate them from other passages dealing with metaphysical questions—even when both kinds of passage appear in the same work by the same author.

The Western historian Vagda, for example, did exactly this when he drew a dividing line between the Brethren of Purity’s studies of natural science and their study of the “microcosm,” or human being.[^V3-610]

We reject this piecemeal approach not only because of its idealist character, but also because it conflicts with the method of scientific research.

A consistent, comprehensive method requires us to bring together two sides of Arab philosophical thought: their ideas about the movement of the “process” of emanation and their attempts to connect it with the motion of the material world, on the one hand; and, on the other, the passages in which they were beginning to search for the source of motion within matter itself.

When we bring these together and treat them as connected rather than separate, we can see that they form parts of one coherent attempt to discover the source of matter’s motion and to connect the entire cosmic order with that source.

We can also see that although these thinkers did not reach a final answer—and under their historical conditions they could not have done so—they added another link to the long chain of human knowledge that eventually led to the discovery that matter contains the source of its own motion.

From this broader point of view, we can explain the contradiction we found in the Brethren of Purity. On the one hand, they explicitly denied that motion is eternal and said that the world had a beginning. On the other, many of their statements contain either the idea that matter moves from within itself or the idea that the world is eternal.

This contradiction can be understood as one form of their historical inability to reach the objective truth about the source of motion.

That limitation led them to search for the source of the material world’s movement—the movement of change and becoming[^V3-611]—in the world of divine emanation. At the same time, however, they tried to remove any complete theological separation between the two worlds, and they placed a form of material existence—prime matter, or absolute body—within the world of emanation itself.

### Conclusion

Our main concern in studying the philosophy of the Brethren of Purity has been to uncover its most important neglected aspects. These are precisely the aspects that bourgeois researchers, Western and otherwise, have often obscured.

Such researchers have concentrated almost entirely on the idealist and religious-doctrinal sides of the Brethren’s thought. They have treated these sides in isolation from their relationship with social reality, on the one hand, and from the other philosophical elements that complete and explain them, on the other.

The Brethren of Purity were not simply representatives of one particular religious doctrine. In other words, they were not merely followers of Ismailism, as these researchers and historians often present them.

In reality, they represented one of the major features of their age and society: the rise of wide-ranging encyclopedic inquiry. They were especially concerned with gathering the broadest possible knowledge of nature at the level reached by the Arab philosopher-scientists of their time.

Because the Brethren represented this wider movement, their encyclopedic work was marked by a strong tendency to bring together and spread all the knowledge their age had accumulated about the laws of nature discovered up to that point.

And when we say “the knowledge of their age,” we mean a concentrated summary of the human knowledge that had reached them from many earlier periods, combined with the new forms that Arab thinkers had been able to give some of that knowledge through experiments prompted by the needs of their own society.

This broad and comprehensive character of the *Epistles of the Brethren of Purity* attracted the attention of some Western scholars who worked on translating them into European languages. One described the encyclopedia as a synthesis of all the sciences the world had developed over several centuries.[^V3-612] Bandalī Jawzī likewise described it as the first encyclopedia of sciences and knowledge to appear in the world.[^V3-613]

But this is not the only feature that should attract the attention of an objective researcher.

Other features become visible only when we try to understand the *Epistles* through their connection with social reality and with the particular historical conditions in which they appeared.

Seen from this angle, the *Epistles* confirm several important truths about how deeply scientific and social activity were connected during this dynamic period.

One of these truths is that the *Epistles* suggest the beginnings of a practical, dialectical unity between the natural sciences and philosophy.

The positive elements we have uncovered in this study show how the results of research into nature were influencing philosophy. They were pushing philosophical thought one or more steps away from supernatural explanations of the world and, to the same degree, closer to explanations that matched the ideology of the oppressed social groups of medieval Arab-Islamic society.

This also shows how the class struggle within that society was reflected in the developing relationship between natural science and philosophy. This is a second truth confirmed by the *Epistles of the Brethren of Purity*.

From this follows a third truth, which one modern Arab researcher mentioned only in passing and in a form that strips it of its real significance. He wrote that the collection—that is, the fifty-one epistles of the Brethren of Purity—is, for us, “a priceless source for discovering which parts of Hellenic science were available and capable of being absorbed by the Muslims.”[^V3-614]

By “the Muslims,” of course, this researcher meant only the authors of the collection and other highly educated people like them. His statement therefore loses much of its real significance. It loses still more when the knowledge contained in the Brethren’s collection is reduced simply to “Hellenic knowledge.”

The truth we have in mind—and which this statement unintentionally supports—requires us to rewrite the researcher’s claim in a different form.

The fact that this body of scientific and philosophical knowledge was assembled and made available to different sections of society shows that these different social groups were capable of absorbing it.

This was not because the knowledge was Hellenic. Nor was it because these social groups had reached such a high cultural level that they were able to absorb “Hellenic knowledge.”

It was because the knowledge contained in the Brethren’s collection had been transformed. It was no longer simply Hellenic knowledge in origin. It had become the knowledge of the age and society that the Brethren themselves represented.

And because the general direction of this knowledge answered to the interests of broad sections of that society, it also became an ideological expression of those broad social groups.

A fourth truth contained in this encyclopedic collection follows from the same point.

To the extent that the collection helped spread the knowledge of its age about the laws of nature, and to the extent that this knowledge expressed the interests of exploited and oppressed social groups in practice, it carried with it a serious drive toward objective knowledge of both nature and society.

This drive appears not only in the Brethren’s method of research or in the conclusions they reached. They also stated it directly and openly when they advised their fellow members “not to be hostile to any branch of knowledge, not to abandon any book, and not to be fanatical in favor of any doctrine…”[^V3-615]

We should also note the connection between this desire for objective knowledge and their search for the forces that shape human character.

They looked for these forces not in invisible worlds and not in the “tablets” of divine decree and destiny, but in people’s immediate material conditions.

They certainly went too far in the importance they gave to natural factors such as climate, geography, and the positions of the planets and stars. They did not recognize the decisive role played by social factors.

But this exaggeration does not erase the importance of their basic direction: they looked toward the material conditions of human life.

They strengthened this material basis when they denied any connection between knowledge of the unseen and astrology. They rejected the possibility of human knowledge about “what will happen without inference, without causes, and without any reason that could produce it.”[^V3-616]

Here, the causal connection between things also appears as a cornerstone of their method for seeking objective knowledge.

This is a fifth truth.

Our decision to concentrate on these truths does not mean that we intend to ignore the other sides of the Brethren’s philosophy, where its idealist character becomes clear.

It means only that these truths need our attention more urgently than the idealist sides of their thought.

Bourgeois researchers have already given those idealist elements more than enough attention, just as they have neglected and obscured the other truths we have discussed.

Yet these neglected truths are fundamental to understanding the overall direction of the philosophy of the Brethren of Purity—and, more broadly, of Arab-Islamic philosophy in their age.

Perhaps that is precisely why these truths have been so neglected and obscured by those researchers.

## Summary of Chapter Three

### The Brethren of Purity

### 1

This chapter is divided into twelve sections, followed by a conclusion:

- The first section serves as a broadly historical introduction to the emergence of the Brethren of Purity.
- The second, titled **“Strategy and Tactics,”** examines the group’s social and political aims.
- The third is titled **“The Social Content of the Brethren’s Ideology.”**
- The fourth deals with **“The Social Aspects of the Group’s Philosophy.”**
- The fifth, **“The Problem of Divine Decree and Human Will,”** examines their position on human freedom and responsibility for human actions.
- The sixth examines their position on reason.
- The seventh deals with **“The Theory of Knowledge in the Brethren’s Philosophy.”**
- The eighth is titled **“The Objective Existence of the External World.”**
- The ninth deals with **“The Tendency Toward Quantitative Rather Than Qualitative Definition.”**
- The tenth is titled **“One Form of Understanding Necessity and Freedom.”**
- The eleventh deals with **“An Embryonic Seed of a Theory of Natural Evolution.”**
- The twelfth examines the Brethren’s position on whether the world and motion are eternal or had a beginning. This section is titled **“The Principle of the World…”**
- The conclusion draws together the most important findings about the philosophy of the Brethren of Purity.

### 2

In the introduction, we faced several questions that required historical answers.

How did this group emerge in its particular age and society? Who were its members? How did they come together and form a group called the “Brethren of Purity”? Who wrote the *Epistles* in the new encyclopedic form they took in that society and period?

History gives us no clear answer to any of these questions.

We know only that Abū Ḥayyān al-Tawḥīdī named one member of the group, Zayd ibn Rifāʿa, and described the group as possessing knowledge of many branches of science and many kinds of craft. He also said that they “composed fifty epistles covering every part of philosophy, both theoretical and practical (…) concealed their names, distributed the epistles among the book dealers, and gave them to the people.”

Abū Ḥayyān did not mention any other member by name, and the matter remained unclear.

The historian al-Qifṭī, who died in 646 AH/1248 CE, added nothing that clarified the matter beyond what Abū Ḥayyān had already said.

Aḥmad Zakī, a modern Egyptian scholar, reported that some writers attributed the *Epistles* to the Qarmaṭians, while others attributed them to Jaʿfar al-Ṣādiq, the sixth Shiʿi Imam. One modern Ismaili writer, ʿĀrif Tāmir, attributed them to the Ismaili Imam ʿAbd Allāh ibn Muḥammad ibn Ismāʿīl ibn Jaʿfar al-Ṣādiq.

Modern researchers generally agree that the Brethren of Purity were connected with Ismailism.

In our view, however, their relationship with Ismailism was not one of complete integration. It was more a matter of similarities in organization and in certain general principles.

The Brethren retained their own intellectual and organizational independence. The historical sources contain no evidence that they formed an Ismaili organization, nor do those sources tell us that their intellectual activity was tied to the activity of the Ismaili movement.

The sources also mention no practical activity carried out by the Brethren of Purity.

All we can conclude is that what we know of their organization does not go beyond what is described in the written material of the *Epistles* themselves.

It seems that they carried out no activity outside their intellectual work and their attempts to spread the *Epistles* among the public through copyists.

### 3

We have used the modern terms “strategy and tactics,” as well as “program,” to describe the way the Brethren of Purity planned to spread their ideas gradually through different parts of society. They adjusted their message to the level of the people they hoped to recruit into their highly secret organization. Only those who reached the highest stage of initiation were told the true nature of their philosophical principles. Those in the first, second, and third stages received the same principles disguised in conventional religious language, though the degree of disguise varied from one stage to another.

The Brethren of Purity had a social and political strategy. On the one hand, they opposed the Abbasid state. On the other, they opposed the social order that this state represented. Their tactics were therefore designed to protect the secrecy of their movement and help them escape persecution by the authorities. We have already quoted texts that show all of this.

The passages we have cited also suggest that they interpreted religious law in a way that ultimately did away with it. Their openness to every religion and school of thought, and to knowledge from every source, was another expression of this position.

### 4

From their overall strategy, we can conclude that their basic principles had a social content that expressed the ideology of oppressed groups within society. Their openness to all religions and schools of thought was the outward face of this ideology, and also the face it presented in opposition to the social and political system of the caliphate.

For this reason, we do not think this openness should be described, as bourgeois scholars often describe it, as mere “eclecticism” or “syncretism.” It was an objective result of their interaction with the accumulated knowledge of earlier human societies. It was also an objective result of the social movements and overlapping social and political struggles directed against an absolute and despotic system. Their opposition to that system was reflected in opposition to the sharia, which provided its theoretical and ideological foundation.

This also helps us understand the relationship of Arab philosophy in general, and the philosophy of the Brethren of Purity in particular, to Greek and Eastern philosophies. Ideas taken from those traditions were adapted and transformed according to the needs of the social struggle and in ways that served oppressed and exploited groups. This adaptation and transformation arose from objective historical conditions. It was not simply a matter of personal choice.

We can see this in the way Arab philosophers, and especially the Brethren of Purity as representatives of a broader phenomenon of their age, used Plotinus’s theory of emanation. In their hands, the theory moved in a different direction from the one it had taken in Plotinus and later Neoplatonism. Through this new use of the theory, some materialist positions began to appear.

The basic reason for the difference was that the social relationships were different. Plotinus’s theory expressed the ideology of masters against slaves within a slave society. The direction taken by Arab philosophers, including the Brethren of Purity, expressed the ideology of groups that had no stake in feudal relations of production or in the theocratic political authority built upon them.

The ideological position of the Brethren of Purity can be summed up as a rejection of the theoretical foundation of the dominant social order. That foundation was the sharia, insofar as it made the caliph the sole intermediary between God and human beings and gave him absolute authority to rule in God’s name.

Their rejection of this idea appears, as it does among the Sufis, in their acceptance of more than one intermediary between God and humanity. We have examples from their writings that express this rejection in different ways.

The difference between the Sufis and the Brethren of Purity is that the Brethren went further in the ideological struggle because they were more closely connected to social reality. The Sufis remained more distant from that reality, and their ideological position remained largely confined to the philosophical meaning of their inner spiritual experience.

The Brethren’s connection to social reality can be seen in several ways:

First, they made social activity a basic part of the human search for happiness.

Second, they stressed the value of physical work and its social purpose.

Third, they made their knowledge of nature and society available to a wide public and worked to spread it among different social groups.

Fourth, their social philosophy began from a view that recognized class divisions.

These four points, together with the texts that support them, are discussed in detail on pages 372–377.

### 5

The Brethren’s writings show that they rejected determinism. They interpreted divine decree and predestination in a way that left human beings free to choose their own actions.

Their positive view of human free will can therefore be treated as part of the ideological content of their opposition to the ideology of the caliphate. The caliphate itself also relied on a deterministic interpretation of divine decree and predestination, because this helped present the existing theocratic social and political order as permanent and unavoidable.

### 6

The Brethren relied above all on reason. This is clear both from their direct statements and from the way they dealt with the questions discussed in their *Epistles*.

They say that reason is the leader of their community, and that they reject anyone who “does not accept the conditions of reason and the conclusions required by its judgments.” They also say that people who take reason as their leader have no need for any other leader above them.

This position was itself part of their ideology. By accepting the authority of reason, they were also freeing themselves from the authority of the sharia and from the political power built upon it—the state of the caliphate.

Their rational method can be seen in the following points:

- First, they define human reason as “the power of distinction that belongs to every human individual and not to other animals.”[^V3-617] If reason is the defining feature of human beings, then its judgments and conclusions cannot simply be ignored.
- Second, they deny miracles.
- Third, they reject blindly following religious authority unless there is proof.
- Fourth, they regard the judgments that human reason draws from rational principles as the highest form of knowledge. At the same time, they recognize the value of sensory knowledge as a path toward rational knowledge.
- Fifth, most of the principles they use to explain the world come from mathematics and the natural sciences.

### 7

In their theory of knowledge, the Brethren of Purity greatly widened the range of what could be known. Knowledge had once been largely confined to the world of theology. For the Brethren, the external world itself became a major subject of knowledge, including society, nature, and thought. In this sense, knowledge of the external world gained a degree of independence from theology.

When it comes to knowledge of God, the Brethren do not say that reason is powerless. Their position is that because God has no physical body, He cannot be known through the same categories we use to understand the external world. Knowledge of God is therefore limited to questions such as “Does He exist?” and “Who is He?”

Their approach to the external world, however, is clearly rationalist. It relies on organized reasoning, sensory knowledge, logical proofs based on syllogisms, scientific experiment, and social activity.

They even placed the celestial spheres and the spiritual world within the reach of rational and demonstrative knowledge.

Their “tactical” plan did sometimes force them to use theological language when discussing the spiritual world. But their overall direction gives us reason to see this language as a form of disguise. One example is their use of the idea of “revelation.” Yet their own writings interpret revelation in a way that moves it away from its usual theological meaning.

Their understanding of the thinking process also reveals a materialist position rooted in a practical view of knowledge.

They differ from thinkers who rely on pure abstraction and reject sensory knowledge. But they also differ from those who accept sensory knowledge alone. For the Brethren, sensory knowledge provides the basis from which rational abstraction develops.

In our view, this position reflects the advances their age had made in understanding how thought works and in connecting philosophy with the growing results of the applied sciences. This development itself arose from the interaction between the natural sciences and the growth of commodity production in the cities.

The Brethren also had a picture of the physical organs through which knowledge takes place, including the organs involved in rational thought. More importantly, they clearly saw an organic connection between the processes of life, sensation, and thought. They understood them as parts of one connected process. In this way, they revealed what they saw as the material basis of thinking.

Their method of reasoning also appears to go beyond Aristotle’s syllogistic method. They use an inductive approach, drawing knowledge from the movement of reality itself—that is, moving from particular cases to general conclusions.

For them, knowledge is not something we are born with. It is gained through seeking, learning, and experience.

### 8

By treating the external world as an object of knowledge, the Brethren laid the foundation for recognizing the real and objective existence of the material world.

Here we find another difference between them and Neoplatonism, despite their use of the theory of emanation.

Earlier in this chapter, we compared the philosophy of the Brethren of Purity in detail with the philosophies of Pythagoras, Plato, and Plotinus, especially in the way each of them understood the external world. That comparison led us to the following conclusions.

First, when we compare them with Plato, we find that Plato’s theory of the Forms does have a counterpart in their philosophy. But the Brethren turned this idea in a more materialist direction.

They believed that the Active Intellect, the Universal Soul, and prime matter each had a corresponding counterpart or analogue in the material world.

There are, however, several important differences between Plato’s Forms and these “counterparts and analogues.”

**A.** Plato’s Forms are causes of the sensory world. This leads to a multiplication of divine beings. The Brethren do not treat their counterparts in this way. They are neither causes nor gods, because God is one.

**B.** Plato’s Forms stand alongside one another without any necessary relationship between them. The spiritual powers described by the Brethren—the Intellect, the Universal Soul, and prime matter—form an ordered hierarchy in which each receives from another.

**C.** For Plato, the Forms alone possess true existence. For the Brethren of Purity, however, the material counterparts corresponding to the spiritual powers also have real, objective existence.

This is why they call them “counterparts and analogues” rather than “Forms.” A counterpart or analogue is something equal to that which it corresponds to. The equality here is an equality of existence: the spiritual being has its own independent existence, and so does its material counterpart.

Second, when we compare the Brethren with Plotinus, there is no doubt that the idea of the world proceeding from God through emanation comes from Plotinus. But both the form and the substance of the idea changed in the philosophy of the Brethren.

In form, the levels of emanation changed, as did their number, their relationships with one another, and the terms used for them.

In substance, the difference is even more important. Plotinus denies the real existence of the material world, while the Brethren recognize it. Plotinus also rejects sensory knowledge, while the Brethren accept it.

Third, the difference between the Brethren and the Pythagoreans is also fundamental.

For Pythagoras, number is the origin, or the first essence, of existence. For the Brethren, number is only a convention arranged by the sages as they chose.

When they compare relationships among numbers with the relationship between God and the world, they mean no more than an analogy. Just as God is one and multiplicity proceeds from Him, many numbers are contained within the first absolute number.

For the Brethren, that is all the comparison means. Their theory does not say that number is the origin of existence.

We have already shown why we reject the claim that the Brethren reduced everything to number.

What they actually believed in was a correspondence between things in the spiritual world and things in the material world. By “correspondence,” they meant resemblance. Its purpose was to present the universe as a harmonious order.

This resemblance was not limited to number, and number itself had no spiritual meaning for them and contained no divine secrets, contrary to the interpretation given by bourgeois scholars.

When the Brethren discussed this idea of correspondence, another idea also appeared: harmony within material things depends on a balance of numerical and quantitative proportions in their composition.

In this idea, we can already glimpse a kind of dialectical view of the relationship between opposites and of the unity between them.

The Brethren’s idea of harmony also differs from the harmony of Plotinus, even though they were influenced by him. The difference is both philosophical and ideological.

Plotinus begins from a fatalistic and deterministic position. The Brethren begin from a position that leaves room for free choice.

Each position has its own ideological basis. Plotinus’s deterministic harmony expresses the ideology of the ruling masters by presenting their social order as a necessary and perfectly harmonious system. The Brethren, by contrast, express the ideology of new groups within a feudal-commercial society. Their view makes room for freedom of choice because this better suited the interests of those groups.

The Brethren’s discussion of numerical and quantitative proportions in the balance of things also gives us a basis for seeing the beginnings of a quantitative approach to the study of natural phenomena.

This marked an important development beyond the mainly qualitative and metaphysical—that is, non-dialectical—approach of ancient philosophy.

Their growing interest in quantity also helped produce another philosophical development: the idea that quantitative changes can lead to qualitative transformations.

### 9

So far, we have found several positions in the philosophy of the Brethren of Purity that contain materialist tendencies:

1. Quantitative changes form a basis for qualitative changes.
2. Causal relationships are a basic condition of rational knowledge.
3. They deny miracles, supernatural events, and knowledge of the unseen even to prophets and messengers. They even deny a supernatural basis for revelation and inspiration.
4. They recognize the objective existence of the material world.

These positions help us understand the basis of their view that living beings develop from one another through a process of gradual evolution, or what Darwin later called the evolution of species.

We should not exaggerate this and claim that they reached Darwin’s theory. Our claim is much more limited: we find in their thought an early, embryonic seed of that theory.

In this sense, they reflected an important scientific and philosophical tendency of their age: the growing attempt to explain the world in material terms.

This tendency reflected the development of the natural sciences, which was itself connected to the growing production of society’s material needs. It also reflected the intensification of ideological conflict as social conflict became more intense.

If it is correct that the Brethren understood the development of living beings as an evolutionary process arising from within matter itself, then this gave them a powerful weapon in their ideological struggle against the official religious ideology.

Not all of their writings, however, describe natural development as an entirely internal process. Some passages point in this direction, while others introduce an external factor into the process.

We have therefore examined these texts both internally, by analyzing what they say, and historically, by considering the position of the Brethren of Purity in their own time.

Taken together, these analyses have convinced us that the passages introducing a divine factor into the process of development may have formed part of their policy of caution and concealment—their tactical plan.

### 10

Did the world have a beginning, or has it existed forever, according to the Brethren of Purity?

They explicitly say that the world had a beginning and is not eternal, because it moves and changes. Since motion itself is not eternal, they argue, anything that moves and changes cannot be eternal.

This was a traditional argument among Islamic theologians. It was also one of the theoretical supports of the ideology of the medieval Islamic caliphate.

We have not accepted this as the Brethren’s final and genuine position on the question for two reasons.

First, it conflicts with the materialist tendencies we have already found in their thought, and it also conflicts with their wider ideological position.

Second, other passages in their writings take a position that contradicts the one they state openly here.

There is also another point that deserves attention.

When the Brethren openly argue that the world had a beginning, they place great emphasis on the world’s movement and change—on its constant process of becoming.

They argue against those who believe the world is eternal on the assumption that eternity would mean that the world is static. But the world, they say, is not static.

The central issue for them, then, is the connection they draw between eternity and stillness. Because they reject the idea of a static world, they also reject eternity in order to preserve the world’s movement and constant change.

If we combine this observation with the two reasons given above, we may be inclined to think that the Brethren were not entirely serious when they openly declared that the world had a beginning.

At the same time, we do not think they would have gone so far as to state openly something they did not believe at all.

Our final conclusion, therefore, is that they were uncertain and conflicted on this question. Because of their historical situation, and because scientific development in their age was still relatively limited, they were unable to understand motion as something arising from matter itself.

### 11

In conclusion, our study of the philosophy of the Brethren of Purity has led us to the following points about their *Epistles*:

1. They were not simply representatives of a narrow Ismaili sect, as most modern scholars portray them. They were a broader expression of one of the most important intellectual phenomena of their age: the search for encyclopedic knowledge. Their *Epistles* therefore brought together the accumulated knowledge of earlier ages and the new scientific contributions made by the philosopher-scientists of their own time.

2. Their encyclopedia, the *Epistles*, contains early beginnings of what we would now call a “dialectical” relationship between the natural sciences and philosophy. Their philosophical positions in general suggest that the results of research into nature, both theoretical and practical, were beginning to influence philosophy and move it toward a more materialist explanation of the world, several steps away from supernatural explanations.

3. This last point also shows how social conflict was reflected in the dialectical relationship between the natural sciences and philosophy.

4. The Brethren’s attempt to spread their encyclopedic *Epistles* among broad sections of society suggests that these groups were capable of receiving the different kinds of knowledge contained in them. One reason may have been that the Brethren’s ideas responded to the interests of these social groups and objectively expressed their ideology.

5. To the extent that the Brethren’s encyclopedia brought together the knowledge of its age about the laws of nature, and to the extent that it expressed the interests of oppressed social groups, it also contained a serious drive toward objective knowledge of nature and society.

   One expression of this was their advice to their followers: “Do not be hostile to any branch of knowledge, do not abandon any book, and do not blindly side against any school of thought.”

   Another was their emphasis on the effect of material conditions on the formation of human character.

6. They rejected any form of human knowledge that was not based on reasoning and on causes and explanations. Here again, the causal relationship between things appears as an essential part of their method for seeking objective knowledge.

---

None of these points means that there were no idealist elements in the philosophy of the Brethren of Purity.

We have focused on these other features because they have been obscured in the work of other scholars, and in some cases this may have been deliberate. Yet these features are precisely what define the general direction not only of the philosophy of the Brethren of Purity, but of Arab-Islamic philosophy as a whole.

# Chapter Four

## The Dialectical Relationship Between Arab Philosophy and the Natural Sciences

> In his preparatory notes for *Capital* in 1863, Marx stressed that the natural sciences form the basis of all knowledge.[^V3-618]

### A — Scientific Knowledge of Nature and Social Activity

#### 1 — Natural Science in Historical Perspective

When Marx said that the natural sciences form the basis of all human knowledge,[^V3-619] he was stating a truth confirmed both by reality and by the historical development of human knowledge across different ages.

The reason is simple. The natural sciences are more closely connected to material reality than other forms of knowledge. They formulate what people discover, over time, about the laws of nature. In this sense, they are the theoretical form taken by our direct and concrete experience of the real world.

When philosophy was regarded in ancient times as the “science of sciences,” this did not mean that philosophy rather than the natural sciences formed the basis of other knowledge.

Ancient philosophy was not separate from the study of nature. The natural sciences were part of philosophy itself.

We can go further. The level of philosophical thought in ancient thinkers—the kind of overall explanation of the world they were able to produce—reflected the level of their knowledge of nature.

Even what Aristotle called “first philosophy” was, in one sense, a theoretical and conceptual form of direct knowledge about nature.

It may therefore be fair to say that the ancient idea of philosophy as the “science of sciences” was an early and spontaneous way of bringing together the two basic forms of knowledge: sensory knowledge and abstract thought.

It is no accident, then, that none of the major ancient philosophers lacked knowledge of nature at the level available in their own time.

The real difference from one philosopher to another, or from one generation to another, lay in how closely their knowledge of nature was connected to practical experience.

This is one area in which Aristotle stands out from the Greek philosophers who came before him. He was much more closely connected to experience.

Earlier Greek philosophy was either completely detached from experiment or connected to it only weakly.

The Greeks before Aristotle could analyze natural processes very well, but mainly when those processes were simple enough to observe directly. They had not yet learned how to create artificial conditions in which a natural process could be reproduced and examined repeatedly from different angles.

Aristotle was the first philosopher in ancient Greek history to take some steps toward the experimental study of nature.

He was able to do this because he inherited the accumulated experience of earlier Greek thinkers. His writings therefore bring together much of what his predecessors had learned.

This experimental tendency in Aristotle may also explain his opposition to Plato’s idea that concepts have an existence independent of the objects we experience through the senses.

Aristotle rejected the idea that concepts exist independently of sensible things. He also rejected the idea that concepts alone are the true realities behind the sensory world.[^V3-620]

The same starting point also produced the dialectical seeds that can be found in Aristotle’s logic.

Lenin described Aristotle’s logic as something that “raises the question of dialectics everywhere, at every step.”[^V3-621]

This also helps explain Marx’s well-known appreciation of both Heraclitus and Aristotle when he said: “We are developing what Heraclitus and Aristotle left behind.”[^V3-622]

#### 2 — In the Arab-Islamic Period

All of this confirms the basic importance of scientific knowledge of nature for every other branch of knowledge. That importance comes from its close connection to the external world.

Above all, this means a close connection to social activity.

Our task here is to examine the form that scientific knowledge of nature took in the Arab-Islamic world during the fourth century AH, or the tenth century CE: the age of al-Fārābī, the Brethren of Purity, Abū Bakr al-Rāzī, and Ibn al-Haytham. It was also the period in which Ibn Sīnā began his scientific work.

A quick comparison between the state of scientific knowledge in this period and the period before it reveals a major difference.

If we also compare the economic and social conditions of the generation of al-Fārābī with the conditions that came before, the reason for much of that difference becomes clearer.

In other words, a double comparison—between two stages of scientific knowledge and between two sets of economic and social conditions—shows us both how great the change was and how strongly social activity helped produce it.

To see this, we need to go back to the stage when the early Abbasid caliphs—al-Manṣūr, Hārūn al-Rashīd, and al-Maʾmūn—began seriously supporting the translation into Arabic of scientific knowledge from the other relatively advanced cultures of the time, especially those of the Greeks, Persians, and Indians.

By this stage, translation had become a broad movement, a general social and intellectual phenomenon. It was no longer simply a matter of individual personal interest, as it had often been under the Umayyads, with figures such as Khālid ibn Yazīd ibn Muʿāwiya.

Before this development, the various branches of knowledge remained largely confined to the picture of the world provided by Islamic religious sources.

Religious scholars were usually the ones who drew these ideas from theological sources and placed them at the service of the official ideology.

Everything concerning knowledge of the universe, nature, and the human being was interpreted within this same framework.

Even knowledge that reached intellectuals from outside theological sources tended to be drawn back into the same framework. This happened even among people who did not accept the ideology of the caliphate, or who openly opposed it, such as the Qadarites.

Their ideas were clearly influenced by knowledge coming from other sources. Yet in the end, and without intending to do so, they often remained within the same basic intellectual framework and could even help reinforce the ideology of the state, despite beginning from a position of opposition.

The dominant picture of the world therefore remained the one drawn by theological sources.

The theologians of *kalām*, including the Muʿtazilites, continued to work inside this overall picture. They were not yet able to make fundamental changes to it.
