Some of them, such as al-Naẓẓām and al-ʿAllāf, introduced new elements into parts of the picture. But their effect was mainly to unsettle certain parts of it rather than transform it completely.

For the same reason, al-Kindī—the first Arab philosopher to lay the foundations for making Arab philosophy independent of theological *kalām*—still preserved some of the most important theoretical foundations of the ideology of the caliphate, including creation out of nothing and the belief that the world had a beginning.

The situation in the Islamic world at this stage closely resembles what Engels wrote about Christian Europe in the Middle Ages:

> “The dominant mentality of the Middle Ages was theological. Indeed, the clergy were the only educated class. The doctrine of the Church therefore became the basis and starting point of thought, and the content of law, natural knowledge, and philosophy was made to agree with the teachings of the Church.”[^V3-623]

#### 3 — Meeting the Needs of Social Activity

This picture could change only when new conditions appeared that created a need for knowledge of nature capable of meeting the demands of social activity.

Only this could drive the development of that knowledge. And as knowledge of nature developed, it would in turn change the way people understood nature and eventually the world as a whole.

These new conditions did in fact appear after the establishment of the Abbasid state.

More specifically, they appeared after the founding of the new Abbasid capital, Baghdad, under Abū Jaʿfar al-Manṣūr.[^V3-624]

The choice of Baghdad’s location had major historical importance from an economic and social point of view.

One of the main aims was to give the young state a capital that could become a major international center for travel and communication by both land and water.[^V3-625] Baghdad would be connected directly to the outside world and to the great trade routes and commercial centers of the time.

The Abbasid rulers had been brought to power by “revolutionaries” from both Arab and Persian backgrounds, belonging to social groups with different class origins.

They wanted to build a new state on deep changes in the two main areas of the economy that shaped social relations in Arab-Islamic society at the time: agriculture and trade.

During the first stage of their rule, they therefore worked to renew irrigation systems and improve the use of agricultural land.

They also changed the tax system. In Iraq, they abolished the tax that had been placed on the total amount of land capable of cultivation and applied it instead only to land that was actually being farmed.

Outside Iraq, including in Khurasan and Syria, they surveyed taxable land in order to determine sources of state revenue.[^V3-626]

In trade, they worked to expand foreign commerce as far as conditions allowed. This included expanding the range of goods being traded, widening the geographical sources of those goods, and developing methods of monetary exchange and banking services.

They also paid attention to the internal market, expanding the exchange of agricultural and craft products between different parts of the Arab empire. Many of these regions had developed their own areas of specialization, either in agriculture or in crafts.[^V3-627]

At the same time, the Abbasid rulers made major changes to the system of government so that it would suit their new situation.[^V3-628]

The new general direction of government rested on three foundations.

The first was social. The Abbasids moved away from the Umayyad emphasis on tribal belonging and tribal loyalties.

The second was ideological. They placed the political system on an absolute theocratic foundation. The caliphate became the symbol of divine authority and of the sharia, while the caliph himself was presented as “God’s shadow on earth.”

The third was administrative. They built a strict central authority to which the different levels of administration, across the empire’s different regions, were tied.

These general policies of the early Abbasid caliphs grew out of the full range of social, political, and ideological conditions that had produced the “revolution” that brought them to supreme power.

Once their political and administrative system had become reasonably stable, the new conditions made another need increasingly clear: agriculture, trade, and craft production all had to develop.

These same conditions then created an objective need to search for better methods and tools that could support the new institutions, advance economic development, and raise the level of human skill needed to carry out these tasks.

All of this helped strengthen the material foundations of the new state and consolidate its absolute theocratic political system.

This is the historical explanation for the enormously important initiatives taken by the early Abbasid caliphs—al-Manṣūr, al-Rashīd, and al-Maʾmūn—which helped launch one of the great scientific movements of the medieval world.

We mean, first, their official efforts to bring physicians and scientists working at the Sasanian school of Jundishapur to their new capital during the reign of al-Manṣūr.

After that came the effort to bring to Baghdad as much of the inherited knowledge of the wider world as the power of the early Abbasid caliphate could obtain.

These initiatives answered an objective need.

The social system itself needed this knowledge in order to become stronger and continue developing.

At the same time, it also served the direct class interests of the rulers. It helped provide them, first, with large and reliable sources of wealth, and second, with more refined methods and technologies for aristocratic comfort and luxury.

In this respect, the situation resembled the interest shown by European nobles and great feudal lords in artists, writers, and major intellectual figures from the Middle Ages through the Renaissance.

#### 4 — An Objective Historical Paradox

This was how the process began in the early Abbasid period.

From there, economic and social activity on the one side, and the scientific knowledge directly connected to it on the other, began to influence each other in an ongoing process of development.

That process eventually ran into obstacles. The productive forces stopped growing as before, and rural relations of production in particular began to deteriorate from the second Abbasid period onward.

Scientific activity, however, remained connected to craft production in the cities and to commercial activity, which continued despite the difficulties created by political disorder and popular uprisings.

The powerful historical momentum created by the early scientific movement gave it enough force to continue on its own. New obstacles did not stop it, although they did reduce its scope and slow its pace.

The rate of scientific development during this period was remarkable.

Arithmetic, geometry, algebra, trigonometry, and astronomy advanced rapidly.

So did physics, chemistry, botany, medicine, pharmacology, and mechanics.

Practical work in all these fields also produced major advances in technology, including distillation, the preparation of medicines and drugs, chemical compounds, engineering and construction, astronomical observation, mapmaking, and the writing of geographical encyclopedias.[^V3-629]

As a result, Baghdad became a center of world culture less than fifty years after its foundation.[^V3-630]

The later history of humanity and of science shows that the effects of these Arab scientific achievements continued into the European Renaissance and became one of the influences that helped shape it.

Engels makes valuable references to this historical fact in both *Dialectics of Nature* and *Anti-Dühring*.[^V3-631]

#### 5 — Production and the Natural Sciences

The Abbasid state would never have shown such interest in scientific knowledge, nor would that knowledge have developed to such a remarkable degree by medieval standards, if the development of society had not made it necessary.

Social development created needs that scientific knowledge could help meet.

Those same objective needs also forced scientific knowledge into practical use and application.

In this sense, what Engels later said about science during the European Renaissance can also be applied here. If science suddenly rose with a force no one had dreamed of and developed with extraordinary speed, he wrote, “once again we owe this miracle to production.”[^V3-632]

History therefore repeatedly confirms the scientific principle that production is a major force determining the development of the natural sciences in every age and every society.

In ancient agricultural societies, for example, the need to calculate the seasons drove the development of astronomy. Astronomy, in turn, drove the development of mathematics, because it could not advance without mathematical knowledge.[^V3-633]

At another stage of agricultural development, and in particular places—such as Egypt, where water had to be raised for irrigation—the science of mechanics began to develop.

The growth of cities, large-scale construction, and handicraft production also created a need for mechanics. So did navigation and warfare.[^V3-634]

Marx gives us a concrete example from the history of machinery in modern times.

He argues that until 1825—the year of the great crisis—consumer demand was growing faster than production. Machinery therefore had to develop in order to meet the needs of the market.

From that same year onward, according to Marx, the invention and use of machinery in England also became a result of the struggle between workers and employers.

In other European countries, he argues, machinery was introduced partly because they had to compete with England in world markets.

In North America, machinery was introduced both because of competition with other countries and because of a shortage of labor, together with an imbalance between the size of the population and the needs of the economy.[^V3-635]

But this scientific principle does not treat the influence of production on science as absolute or one-sided.

The relationship between production and scientific knowledge is dialectical. Each affects and changes the other.

This is true of every relationship between forms of human consciousness and the objective world.

As Lenin said, consciousness does not merely reflect the objective world; it also creates it.[^V3-636]

This is exactly what happened in the Arab scientific period we are discussing.

Production helped determine the rise and development of the scientific movement and of the natural sciences.

But these sciences, through practical use and application in production and other areas of economic activity, also helped change production and those other activities.

This mutual influence was a historical form of the unity of thought and action, of theory and practice.

### B — Scientific Knowledge of Nature and Philosophy

Economic and social conditions in the early Abbasid period created a favorable setting for active interaction between craft and rural production, the commercial economy, and applied scientific knowledge.

It might therefore seem natural to expect the same kind of interaction to appear between scientific knowledge and philosophy—that is, in the philosophical picture of the world developed by Arab philosophers of the period.

But this did not happen in the same way or at the same speed.

The reason is that the relationship between the natural sciences and production is almost direct. It develops through immediate contact with living reality.

The relationship between the findings of the natural sciences and philosophy is different. Those findings have to pass through a process of thought, and thought has its own complex internal laws that are relatively independent of the immediate movement of reality.

This is why a gap still existed in the early Abbasid period between the philosophical picture of the universe and nature, on the one hand, and the advanced level already reached by the natural sciences and mathematics, on the other.

Those sciences had advanced because of their direct practical contact with the material world.

This does not make us forget what we established earlier: the natural sciences form a basis for other kinds of knowledge, including philosophy.

But that is one question.

How quickly and in what way the findings of the natural sciences make their way into other forms of knowledge, especially philosophy, is another.

That process is not automatic.

It is part of the wider process by which reality is reflected in thought. And that process is extremely complex. Its course is shaped by many interacting historical, social, intellectual, and ideological factors.[^V3-637]

In our view, these factors began to come together strongly enough to affect the process from around the middle of the fourth century AH, or the tenth century CE.

This was the period when class conflict became more visible and began to produce clearer forms of ideological conflict.

In the previous two chapters, we saw two very different ideological forms produced by classes and social groups whose interests conflicted with those of the ruling groups.

One appeared in the form of philosophical Sufism.

The other was more directly connected to social reality and more openly engaged with it. We found this second form in the ideological content of the philosophy of the Brethren of Purity.

#### 2 — A Wider Phenomenon of the Age

Comparing these two forms with one another gives further support to Marx’s idea that the natural sciences form a basis for other kinds of knowledge.

In the philosophy of the Brethren of Purity, we found a clear ideological position that remained close to the movement of social struggle and responded to it almost directly.

In philosophical Sufism, we found almost the opposite.

One of the main reasons for this difference was the Brethren’s rich, encyclopedic knowledge of the natural sciences, something that distinguished them from the philosophers of Sufism.

When we studied this encyclopedic side of their work, we argued that the Brethren were expressing a wider phenomenon of their age. They were not simply a limited “group” or a narrow sect.

We now return to that idea so that we can look for the same phenomenon beyond the Brethren themselves.

In other words, we want to see how the development of scientific knowledge of nature began to appear within philosophical knowledge and within the philosophical understanding of the universe and nature.

This became possible once the forces driving that process had accumulated and begun to work together according to the internal laws of thought itself.

Before we turn to concrete examples, however, we must avoid an overly mechanical or absolute view of the question.

We should not imagine that the influence of scientific knowledge on philosophy immediately transformed the philosophers’ picture of the universe so that it matched perfectly everything they had already discovered through scientific research and practical experiment.

Such an assumption has no place in a scientific method of research.

A sound approach has to take three things into account.

First, the results of the natural sciences themselves were limited in that period.

Second, the tools available for experiment and scientific investigation were also limited. These tools could not yet produce results much more advanced than those that belonged to that historical stage.

Third, as we have just seen, the process by which reality enters and reshapes thought is itself extremely complex.

Keeping these three points in mind protects us from treating the relationship in an automatic or absolute way.

It also helps us appreciate the historical importance of the relative connection that those philosophers did manage to establish between scientific thought about nature and philosophical thought.

Their scientific results were limited. Their experimental and investigative methods were limited. The relationship between science and philosophy was itself complex.

Yet the degree of connection they achieved still deserves the attention of modern scholarship.

We can see its importance even more clearly if we compare it with what some philosophers and scientists of the nineteenth century failed to achieve.

Those thinkers lived in the modern world, with great discoveries in the natural sciences, major technological advances, and far more precise tools of investigation. All of this should have given them the ability to reach philosophical conclusions about the universe that corresponded more closely to its objective reality.

Yet many of them did not.

#### 3 — Comparing Two Eras

Here we are reminded of Engels’s complaint about those nineteenth-century philosophers and scientists.

Despite all the discoveries made available to them after the Renaissance, they continued to look at nature in a metaphysical rather than a dialectical way.

They were unable to resolve the contradiction between the remarkable results of modern scientific discoveries and the traditional methods of thought they continued to use.

Engels believed that this failure explained the enormous confusion that still affected theoretical natural science in the nineteenth century itself.

It also explained, in his view, the sense of frustration and despair that affected teachers and students alike, as well as writers and readers.

The only way out of this contradiction, confusion, and despair was to adopt a dialectical way of looking at the world—one capable of understanding the universe in a way that matched the discoveries of science.[^V3-638]

A comparison between the age of the Arab philosophers and the age of the philosophers and scientists of the nineteenth century therefore gives us the right to see the materialist positions that appear in Arab philosophy as a major achievement for their historical time.

Their achievement lay in giving a philosophical and theoretical form to the results of their studies of nature, even though those results were limited and the methods used to obtain them were limited as well.

The philosophers and scientists of the nineteenth century were in a very different position.

By then, the entire history of science had opened the road toward dialectical materialism much more directly.[^V3-639]

Under those conditions, their continued idealist and metaphysical positions represented a step backward, just as Engels said of the Neo-Kantians in Germany and the supporters of agnosticism in England.[^V3-640]

### C — How Natural Science Appears in Arab-Islamic Philosophy

#### 1 — The Roots of Materialist Philosophical Tendencies in Knowledge

I hope the previous two sections of this chapter have placed the relationship between natural science, philosophy, and ideological struggle in the right dialectical framework. If so—and I hope they have—we can now look at some concrete examples of how this relationship affected particular questions in Arab-Islamic philosophy, both positively and negatively, among its leading representatives in the fourth Islamic century, or tenth century CE.

Our main purpose is to understand, in a scientific way, what forms of connection or unity may have existed between the philosophical view of the world and the scientific study of nature among these major Arab-Islamic philosophers. If we can identify that connection or unity, it may help us uncover not only the ideological roots of their materialist positions and tendencies, but also their roots in knowledge itself.

Earlier, we described the Brethren of Purity as representatives of a wider phenomenon in their age, the fourth Islamic century. But that alone is not enough to give us the full picture. Their *Encyclopedia* does not contain every detail, feature, or aspect of that phenomenon. What it gives us is its general direction, both in terms of knowledge and ideology.

For our purposes, the individual details therefore remain important. So do the specific ideas, opinions, and positions taken by one philosopher or another on a particular question. Taken together, these details can help us see the larger picture—its outlines, features, and meaning—whether those elements fit together smoothly or pull in different directions.

We should not expect complete harmony. Differences in objective circumstances, personal conditions, and ideological positions make that unlikely.

#### 2 — Existence and Essence

In my view, the question of existence and essence is the point where philosophical theories are most likely to meet scientific knowledge of nature and interact with it. The nature of that interaction can therefore tell us a great deal about a philosopher’s position, outlook, and level of thought.

The question of existence and essence is one of the most important problems in philosophy. A philosopher’s position on it is strongly affected by how closely he is connected to scientific knowledge of nature, by the historical level that knowledge has reached, and by how far that level makes it possible to draw broader philosophical conclusions from new discoveries about the natural world.

Philosophy has always had two broad positions on the question of existence and essence.[^V3-641] The difference between them is one of the major dividing lines between the two basic philosophical outlooks: materialism and idealism.

To say that **essence**—the concept—is primary, and that it forms the basis of the order of the universe, is to take the side of idealism.

To say that **existence** is primary—that existence comes before essence and forms the basic starting point—is a necessary condition for any materialist tendency.

This is because making essence primary means denying the objective existence of reality. It means treating essence as the fixed truth, rather than the particular thing that actually exists.

To make existence primary means the opposite. It means recognizing that reality exists objectively in its own right. That is the first step toward philosophical materialism.

Lenin writes:

> “The only property of matter whose recognition defines philosophical materialism is that it is objective reality, existing outside our consciousness.”[^V3-642]

##### In Greek Philosophy

In ancient Greek philosophy,[^V3-643] before Plato, the general direction from the time of Thales (621–550 BCE) was toward the primacy of existence.

Philosophers devoted much of their effort to discovering the original substance from which the world was made. Thales did say that the gods were the force through which things existed and moved. But he also said that this divine force was present *within* things themselves. In other words, the source of matter’s existence and motion did not lie outside matter.

After Thales, philosophers developed many different ideas about the original substance of the world and how it came into being. But the general tendency to treat existence as primary remained dominant.[^V3-644]

Then came Anaxagoras (500–432 BCE), who introduced what seemed to be an outside factor: **Mind** (*Nous*). He argued that this Mind was the force that set the infinitely numerous elements of matter—the “seeds”—in motion.

Did this mark a retreat from what had until then been the dominant tendency in Greek philosophy, namely the primacy of material existence?

Apparently not.

Some parts of Anaxagoras’s teaching may seem to give Mind a divine or deterministic character. But when we look more closely at his basic idea, that interpretation becomes much harder to sustain. At bottom, he treats Mind as a material force that acts on the interaction between the “seeds.”

Aristotle himself explains Anaxagoras’s position in much the same way. He says that Anaxagoras used Mind as a device to explain the origin of the world. Whenever he had difficulty explaining why a particular thing in the material world necessarily existed, he appealed to Mind. Yet elsewhere in his teaching, causes other than Mind also explain the existence of things.

Two points can be drawn from Aristotle here.

First, Anaxagoras appeals to the idea of Mind when he cannot explain the existence of something through natural necessity. That natural necessity was understood as the struggle between two opposing forces: the force of love, which brings things together, and the force of hate, which separates them, in accordance with the doctrine of Empedocles.

Second, even when Anaxagoras does appeal to Mind, he treats it as a material force that acts in its own particular way on the “seeds,” not as a divine spirit.[^V3-645]

Plato also confirms this materialist tendency in Anaxagoras in the *Phaedo*. Speaking through Socrates, Plato says that he had read everything he could find in Anaxagoras’s writings. But the more deeply he went into Anaxagoras’s ideas, the more disappointed he became. In practice, he found almost no real place for Mind. Instead, Anaxagoras explained the existence of things through water, air, and many other physical things.

This testimony from Plato himself supports the view that Anaxagoras had a materialist tendency.[^V3-646]

Ancient Greek philosophy remained predominantly materialist until Plato appeared.[^V3-647] By “materialist” here, I mean that it tended spontaneously to treat existence as primary.

This does not mean that idealist elements were absent from earlier materialist thought. They were present. But idealism in the full philosophical sense—as a coherent system, method, and theory—had not yet taken shape before Plato.

Plato’s position on existence and essence clearly sides with the primacy of essence.

This follows directly from his theory of the Forms, or Ideas. These Forms are fixed essences, or concepts, and only they possess true existence. The external material world, by contrast, is no more than a passing shadow of those essences, or a kind of image or ghost of them.[^V3-648]

Aristotle then changes the position he inherited from his teacher Plato.

Aristotle was one of the first major thinkers to develop the idea that existence is primary, and perhaps the first to give it a systematic philosophical form in ancient thought. This is the basis of the materialist side of his philosophy, even though his philosophy as a whole remains idealist—though not purely so.

The first clear sign of Aristotle’s movement toward the primacy of existence is his recognition that nature comes first.

For him, concrete individual things—the particulars—are the primary beings and have the strongest claim to existence. Universals, such as genera and species, exist within those individual things.[^V3-649]

This is why Aristotle calls individual things **primary substances**, while genera and species are **secondary substances**.

Put another way, Aristotle’s criticisms of Plato’s world of Forms clearly reject the separation of the world grasped by reason from the material world. He also rejects the metaphysical separation of essence from appearance, and the idea that the universal exists outside the particular.[^V3-650]

But there is an important qualification.

When Aristotle criticizes Plato, he thinks in a materialist direction. Yet when he develops his own general theory of form and matter, he moves back toward idealism.

He contradicts himself again in his theory of knowledge. After giving priority to individual things in the question of existence, he gives priority to universals when he discusses knowledge.[^V3-651]

In his theory of knowledge, Aristotle says that certain knowledge is concerned with the universal rather than the particular—with essence rather than with actually existing individual things.

His reason is that the universal is clearer and prior **in itself**, meaning objectively. The individual thing we perceive through the senses, by contrast, is clearer and prior **for us**. In other words, its priority belongs only to our consciousness and not to objective reality.[^V3-651]

If we follow ancient Greek philosophy as far as Neoplatonism, we find that Plotinus’s system of emanation rests on the idea that true existence belongs only to the world of the “Intellects,” or spiritual beings.

These Intellects are simply another form of Plato’s fixed essences. The system therefore rests on the primacy of essence.[^V3-652]

---

##### In Arab Philosophy

This review of the Greek philosophers’ positions on existence and essence was necessary before turning to Arab philosophy.

Whatever shaped the positions of Arab-Islamic philosophers on this question and others—whether ideological influences or developments in knowledge—Greek philosophy remained one important influence among them. It remained one of their sources of knowledge and one of the objective foundations from which their thinking developed.

At times they went beyond this Greek inheritance. At times they changed its form. At other times they opposed it. But none of these changes, revisions, or disagreements happened without some basis.

In my view, that basis generally came from two kinds of reality: an **ideological reality** and a **reality of knowledge**.

These two were never separate from one another. On the contrary, they were in constant, dynamic, dialectical interaction.

Scientific knowledge of nature was closely connected with experiment and with practical application through social activity and economic life—in agriculture, trade, and crafts. This strengthened the connection between scientific findings and the ideological positions that expressed the class position of many of those working in the fields of knowledge, both in the applied sciences and in philosophy.

This double foundation—ideological and knowledge-based—lies behind an important feature of Arab-Islamic philosophy.

We often find Arab philosophers taking idealist teachings from Greek philosophy and transforming them from within, so that they move toward materialist positions while still remaining inside a broader framework of objective idealism. This is especially clear in the way they dealt with Plato and Plotinus.

Modern bourgeois scholars, however, tended to see this internal transformation as nothing more than “interpretation.” Then, when they noticed how freely Arab philosophers treated Greek philosophy, they called it “eclecticism” or “syncretism.”

But what Arab philosophers were actually trying to do was give philosophical form to their own ideological positions in connection with the results of their scientific knowledge.

They borrowed much of their language and terminology from ancient Greek philosophy, but they did not bind themselves to any one Greek philosophical system or school.

This helps us understand, for example, how they could use Plotinus’s theory of emanation without accepting his system as a whole or accepting its rejection of the real existence of the material world. It also explains how they could combine the terminology of emanation with Aristotle’s recognition that nature has a real existence of its own.

The same ideological and knowledge-based foundation also helps explain something else we often find in Arab philosophy: the particular way Arab philosophers constructed the demonstrative syllogism they inherited from Aristotle’s logic.

They often—if not always—begin their reasoning with what is called the “minor term.”[^V3-653]

In other words, they build the proof from a particular proposition rather than from a universal one. Their reasoning begins with an external fact—the particular—and moves from there toward the general in order to reach a judgment or conclusion.

Some Western researchers have refused to recognize that this phenomenon really exists. Instead, they have tried to explain it through racial theories, tracing it back to what they call the “character of Eastern thought.”[^V3-654]

Our historical analysis points elsewhere. As we argued earlier, the phenomenon has particular roots in knowledge that are themselves connected with a particular ideological position.

We will use this same foundation as our guide in examining the position of Arab-Islamic philosophers on the question before us: existence and essence.

Broadly speaking, there were two positions toward the universe and nature in the Arab-Islamic world.

The first held to the idea of fixed essences existing outside nature. It then projected this idea of fixedness onto the ruling theocratic social order, claiming that this order came from the same world of fixed essences. In this way, it supported the ideology of the existing system and the claim that it was permanent and eternal.

This was, of course, the “official” position.

The second position held that the world is dynamic: that change and becoming belong to existence itself.

This position grew out of treating nature as an object of knowledge with its own independence and objective reality. It also came from the practical pursuit of knowledge and from the social—and therefore ideological—position of those who held it.

From this, it may seem obvious that the first position leads to the primacy of essence, while the second leads to the primacy of existence.

The first part of that conclusion is sound. But we should not treat the second part as an absolute rule or apply it mechanically.

We have already seen, in our discussion of Sufism, that the martyred Suhrawardī argued for the primacy of essence even though, ideologically, he did not belong to the first or “official” camp.

We may also find philosophers other than Suhrawardī who belong to the second camp yet still take the same position on essence, even though they were directly involved—unlike Suhrawardī—with the development of natural science and its practical results in social life.

If we do find such a lag or contradiction, it may be due to the complicated way reality is reflected in thought, as we discussed earlier.

For now, however, this is only a theoretical and methodological possibility. Whether it actually applies will have to be decided by the investigation itself in the chapters that follow.

## Summary of Chapter Four

### 1

We began this chapter with Marx’s statement that the natural sciences are the foundation of all knowledge.

That statement expresses the plan behind the chapter as a whole.

Our aim has been to bring out the philosophical features that developed among the Arabs through their study and practical use of the natural sciences, as well as through the valuable contributions they made to the world’s scientific heritage.

But the main purpose of this plan was to connect scientific knowledge of nature and philosophical knowledge with social activity, so that we could uncover the relationship between the development of all these forms of knowledge and the development of Arab-Islamic society in the fourth and fifth Islamic centuries, or the tenth and eleventh centuries CE.

Understanding this relationship helps us distinguish between two different kinds of influence on philosophy.

The first comes from social and ideological conditions.

The second comes from the study and absorption of ancient philosophy.

It also helps us see how far Arab philosophers reshaped the ideas of ancient philosophers to fit the social relations of medieval Arab-Islamic society.

### 2

The chapter is divided into three main sections:

A — Scientific Knowledge and Social Activity.

B — Scientific Knowledge of Nature and Philosophy.

C — How Natural Science Appears in Arab-Islamic Philosophy.

In the third section, we focused on one main question: existence and essence.

We used it as a starting point for examining philosophy in relation to existence and essence more generally. This allows the discussion to include every major side of philosophy, because the question of existence and essence lies at the center of them all.

### 3

In the first section of the chapter, we began with a theoretical introduction.

Our first purpose was to establish that there has been a relationship between natural science and philosophy since the earliest periods of philosophy.

Our second was to show the real and objective connection between the historical level reached by the natural sciences and the historical level reached by social relations.

We also reviewed the experience of the Greek philosophers in this respect.

We then compared scientific knowledge of nature in ancient Greece with the form it took during the period of Arab philosophy. At the same time, we compared the economic and social conditions of the two periods.

This comparison showed that scientific knowledge could not have changed as it did without changes in economic and social conditions. Those new conditions created a need for new knowledge of nature in order to meet the needs of a relatively more developed form of social activity.

At the same time, the development of this new knowledge itself helped to change those conditions.

We supported this argument with concrete historical examples.

These examples showed that the conditions surrounding the creation of the Abbasid state made it necessary to develop agriculture, trade, and craft production. That need, in turn, created a need for scientific research into more advanced ways of supporting such development.

The examples we used were taken from original historical sources. There are many such examples, and we selected those that most clearly supported the point.

We also reinforced the argument with a statement by Engels. He wrote that if the sciences rose suddenly and powerfully during the Renaissance, developing at a speed no one had imagined possible, then once again “we owe this miracle to production” (*Dialectics of Nature*).

We argued that history repeatedly confirms the scientific principle that production is a determining factor in the development of the natural sciences.

But this principle does not describe influence as moving in only one direction. The relationship between scientific knowledge and production is one of mutual influence. It is dialectical, like every relationship between forms of human consciousness and the objective world.

In the early Abbasid period, economic and social conditions created favorable circumstances for active interaction between craft and agricultural production and the commercial economy on one side, and practical scientific knowledge on the other.

It might therefore seem reasonable to expect the same kind of interaction to appear between scientific knowledge and Arab-Islamic philosophy during the same period.

But it did not happen in the same way or at the same speed.

The reason is that philosophy is more complicated.

The relationship between natural science and production is direct and closely connected. In philosophy, by contrast, this relationship passes through a process of thought that has its own relatively independent internal laws.

For this reason, there was a large gap between the philosophical understanding of the universe and nature and the historical level that the natural sciences and mathematics had already reached.

But this gap does not change the fact that the natural sciences form the basis of all human knowledge, including philosophy.

That is one question.

How that foundation is reflected in other forms of knowledge, and how quickly it is reflected—especially in philosophy—is another.

This process does not happen mechanically.

It forms part of the broader process by which reality itself is reflected in thought. And that process, as we know, is highly complex. It is shaped by many interconnected factors at once: historical, social, intellectual, and ideological.

By the middle of the tenth century, these factors were increasingly coming together as social conflict became more visible and, in turn, gave rise to more open ideological conflict.

Even so, the influence of scientific knowledge of nature on philosophy remained behind the level that scientific knowledge itself had reached during the tenth century.

There were three main reasons for this.

First, the findings of the natural sciences were still relatively limited.

Second, the available tools and techniques for experiment and scientific investigation were also limited.

Third, the process by which reality is reflected in thought is itself highly complex.

A comparison between the age of the Arab philosophers and that of nineteenth-century philosophers and scientists brings out an important difference.

Relative to their own period, Arab philosophers had made considerable progress in drawing philosophical conclusions from the results of their studies of nature, even though those results were still limited.

Nineteenth-century philosophers and scientists, by contrast, inherited a history of science that had already opened a broad and direct road toward dialectical materialism.

For that reason, their continued idealist and metaphysical positions amounted to a step backward. This is how Engels described the neo-Kantians in Germany and the supporters of agnosticism in England in *Ludwig Feuerbach and the End of Classical German Philosophy*.

### 4

In the third section of this chapter, we examined concrete examples of how the natural sciences appeared within Arab philosophy, especially in the work of its leading representatives up to the eleventh century: al-Fārābī and Ibn Sīnā.

Our main purpose was to understand the different forms of connection between a philosophical view of the world and scientific research into nature. This would help us uncover both the roots in knowledge and the ideological roots of the materialist positions or tendencies that we may find in these philosophers.

The question of existence and essence is particularly suited to this purpose.

It is one of the clearest points at which philosophical theories can meet scientific knowledge of nature and interact with it. The nature of that interaction can therefore serve as a measure of materialist and idealist positions.

For this reason, the question of existence and essence became our key to studying a wide range of philosophical problems among Arab philosophers.

We first examined the way philosophy had divided, from its earliest periods, into two broad positions on existence and essence: a materialist position and an idealist one.

We then examined how Greek philosophers approached the question before turning to the Arab philosophers.

We did this because Greek philosophy continued to influence Arab philosophers, alongside the influence of historical conditions and social relations within Arab-Islamic society.

Together, these two influences produced a double foundation in Arab philosophy.

But this was not a static division between two separate influences. The two interacted with one another, and that interaction produced important changes in philosophical conclusions about the world.

At this point, we rejected the claims of bourgeois scholars who argued that Arab philosophers had made no real changes to Greek philosophy and had merely “interpreted” it.

When these scholars found that the changes could not simply be denied, they called them “eclecticism” or “syncretism.”

In reality, Arab philosophers were trying to give philosophical form to their ideological positions in connection with the results of their scientific knowledge of nature.

They drew much of their philosophical language and terminology from Greek philosophy, but they did not bind themselves to any one Greek philosophical system.

This is how they were able to use Plotinus’s theory of emanation without accepting its ideological content as a whole or accepting its denial of the material world.

In the same way, they used Aristotle’s concepts of form and matter and adopted his tendency to recognize the real, objective existence of the material world.

On the same basis, they also used forms of demonstrative reasoning that began with the particular and moved toward the general conclusion.

On the question of existence and essence itself, Arab philosophy broadly contained two positions.

The first held to fixed essences existing outside nature.

The second held to the idea that the world is dynamic and is constantly changing and becoming—features that belong to existence itself.

The first position represented the official ideology of the state. The idea of fixed essences supported the idea of a fixed, absolute, despotic social order that was supposedly “incapable of change.”

The second position represented the ideology of social classes and groups that wanted to change that order.

In philosophical terms, the difference between the two positions is that the first leads toward the view that essence is the foundation of the world, while the second leads toward the view that existence is primary.

But this distinction is not absolute.

Among the Sufi philosophers, for example, we saw that Suhrawardī defended the primacy of essence even though he opposed the official ideology.

To turn this distinction into an absolute rule would therefore mean simplifying something that is, by its nature, complex.

[^V3-18]: The Zanj revolt was purely a class revolt, not a racial struggle between Black and white people. This is clear from its social and economic conditions, from the fact that its leadership was white—its first leader, ʿAlī ibn Muḥammad, and his associates ʿAlī ibn Abān, Yaḥyā ibn Muḥammad, and Muḥammad ibn Salm—and from the fact that large groups of Bedouin Arabs and non-Zanj peasants gave valuable support by supplying its armies. See ʿAlī, p. 114; al-Ṭabarī, vol. 8, pp. 91, 95; and ʿAbd al-Fattāḥ ʿUlyān, *Qarāmiṭat al-ʿIrāq*, p. 24, citing Nöldeke, *Sketches from Eastern History*, pp. 152–153.

[^V3-19]: The name “Qarmatians” most likely comes from the man who first organized the movement and spread it in the region of Kufa, from which it later expanded into the areas where history records its presence. His name was Ḥamdān Qarmaṭ. He was a poor agricultural laborer from a village near Kufa. Around 264 AH/877 CE he came into contact with an Ismaʿili missionary known as al-Ḥusayn al-Ahwāzī, who brought him into the movement and entrusted him with spreading its message and organizing its followers in that area. The Qarmatians themselves, however, did not call their community by the name by which history came to know them. They called themselves “the believers made victorious by God, the supporters of His religion, and the reformers of the earth.” See ʿUlyān, *al-Qarāmiṭa fī al-ʿIrāq*, pp. 30, 35, 76.

[^V3-20]: al-Baghdādī says of the people from the tribes of southern Iraq and the Syrian desert who answered the Qarmatian call that they were proverbial for their poverty and hunger (*al-Farq bayna al-Firaq*, p. 142). Abū al-Faḍl, *al-Ishāra fī Maḥāsin al-Tijāra*, Cairo, 1318 AH, p. 43, describes the craftsmen and ordinary people in the towns who joined the movement as the poorest among the free population.

[^V3-21]: al-Maqrīzī, Taqī al-Dīn, *Ittiʿāẓ al-Ḥunafāʾ bi-Akhbār al-Aʾimma al-Fāṭimiyyīn al-Khulafāʾ*, ed. Jamāl al-Shayyāl, Cairo, 1948, p. 104.

[^V3-22]: The Nabataeans were “a non-Arab people who lived between the two Iraqs. They were called Nabataeans because they drew out what the land produced; the word later came to be used for mixed groups of people and for the common population” (*al-Munjid*, entry “Nabaṭ”). Dr. ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz al-Dūrī says that the term refers to the ancient peasants of Iraq who spoke Aramaic. After the Persians weakened them, they were absorbed among the Persians and came to identify with them. They later converted to Islam and spoke a rough form of Arabic, but kept many of their pre-Islamic traditions. Some Bedouins also settled among them, and the whole population came to be known as people of the Sawād. See al-Dūrī, *Tārīkh al-ʿIrāq al-Iqtiṣādī fī al-Qarn al-Rābiʿ al-Hijrī*, Baghdad, 1948, p. 26.

[^V3-36]: One example is the revolt of Abū Ḥarb al-Mubarqaʿ al-Yamānī during the caliphate of al-Muʿtaṣim ibn al-Rashīd. Abū Ḥarb appeared in the mountains of Jordan and declared rebellion against the state, wearing a veil over his face during the day to conceal his identity. Many peasants in the region joined him. The state was unable to crush his movement until the planting season, when his peasant followers became occupied with working the land. Other examples include the revolt of the Alid al-Ḥasan ibn Zayd and his establishment of an Alid state in Ṭabaristān; the rise of the Ṣaffārid state in Ṭabaristān, 254–290 AH; and Aḥmad ibn Ṭūlūn’s establishment of an independent state in Egypt. See ʿUlyān, *Qarāmiṭat al-ʿIrāq*, pp. 125, 127.

[^V3-39]: When the Abbasid caliph al-Muʿtazz ibn al-Mutawakkil (252–255 AH/866–868 CE) could no longer tolerate the pressure placed on him by the Turkish troops and their demands, he tried to counter them by calling on the Maghribi troops—that is, the Arab soldiers in the Abbasid army. But al-Muʿtazz was unable to pay the soldiers’ wages, so the Turkish and Arab troops ended up united against the caliph himself and together took part in killing him. See Ibn al-Wardī, *Tārīkh*, p. 332. In other words, the Arab soldiers’ hostility toward the politically dominant Turks did not prevent them from joining their “class comrades,” the Turkish soldiers, when their shared material interests brought them together—even though the caliph they killed was himself an Arab.

[^V3-40]: One of the many ways ordinary people engaged in social struggle at the time can be seen in the following incident. After the killing of al-Muʿtazz ibn al-Mutawakkil, the Turks installed Muḥammad ibn al-Wāthiq as caliph and gave him the title al-Muhtadī (255–256 AH/868–869 CE). But al-Muhtadī disappointed them. He tried to reduce extravagance, improve the machinery of government, and improve the condition of those suffering injustice. This angered the Turks who had put him in power, and they began plotting to remove him or kill him, as they had done before. The “common people” sensed the beginnings of the plot and took a stand in defense of the caliph. They prepared leaflets and scattered them in the streets and mosques. The leaflets read: “Muslims! Pray to God for your just caliph, that God may give him victory over his enemy, protect him from his oppressor, and complete His blessing upon him and upon this community by preserving his life. The Turks have forced him to abdicate.” See al-Ṭabarī, *Tārīkh al-Umam*, vol. 7, pp. 571–572.

[^V3-41]: al-Baghdādī, *al-Farq bayna al-Firaq*, p. 142.

[^V3-42]: The Sawād of Kufa means its villages and countryside.

[^V3-43]: “The sciences of the ancients” was a term used by medieval Arab scholars, philosophers, and historians of philosophy for the ancient natural and philosophical sciences that were translated into Arabic and then became part of the Arab-Islamic intellectual heritage. The term refers above all to the sciences of ancient Greece, but also to Persian, Indian, and other ancient sources that contributed to Arab culture in the Abbasid period. We prefer this older term to the modern expression “foreign sciences” when speaking of medieval Arab culture. “The sciences of the ancients” reflects a scientific view of civilization as a historical succession shared among peoples, nations, and regions, without restricting it to one people or one geographical area. “Foreign sciences,” by contrast, implies an opposite and unscientific view of civilization. No science is “foreign” in relation to any people on earth.

[^V3-44]: Meaning “Avicennian,” after Ibn Sīnā.

[^V3-55]: We can say that modern existentialism contains a mystical element in its inward-looking view of the self, its emphasis on the individual, and its concepts of the self’s “existential realization” and of “freedom.” We can also see a mystical spirit spreading through some recent trends in Western literature and art, as well as in the work of some leading modern Arab poets. The “hippie” phenomenon and the “Betels” phenomenon may also be compared to the dervish-like behavior of Sufism during the period when Islamic Sufism was in decline and breaking down.

[^V3-57]: We find an example of this contradiction in Goldziher. On the one hand, he looks for the source of Muslim asceticism in what he calls “the Indian ideal of life” (*Introduction to Islamic Theology and Law*, p. 159). On the other hand, he says that “the tendency toward asceticism was connected with revolt against the existing authority,” and that many Muslims “withdrew into seclusion and asceticism as a protest against the government and social order they rejected” (ibid., p. 147). We find similar examples among modern researchers such as Muḥammad ʿAlī Abū Rayyān, *Tārīkh al-Fikr al-Falsafī fī al-Islām*, pp. 258, 270, and Kāmil Muṣṭafā al-Shaybī, *al-Ṣila bayna al-Taṣawwuf wa-l-Tashayyuʿ*, pp. 242, 243, 247. Both treat Islamic teachings themselves as the basic source of asceticism, while speaking of social and political factors only as secondary influences.

[^V3-61]: By “intellectuals” here we mean those who represented Arab-Islamic learning in the first Islamic century. They were mainly Qurʾan reciters, hadith scholars, and jurists.

[^V3-62]: Goldziher says that in this early Islamic period—the beginning of Umayyad rule—many examples of asceticism and self-denial can be seen in the lives of men who had distinguished themselves in the Islamic wars. See *Introduction to Islamic Theology and Law*, Arabic ed., p. 146.

[^V3-66]: al-Jāḥiẓ gives a precise description of al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī’s sadness in *al-Bayān wa-l-Tabyīn*, vol. 3, p. 154. He says that when al-Ḥasan approached, “it was as though he had just buried someone dear to him,” and when he sat down, “it was as though he were a prisoner who had been ordered to have his head struck off.” When Hell was mentioned in his presence, “it was as though it had been created for him alone.”

[^V3-67]: al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī was asked why he was so sad. He answered that a believer “can be nothing else, because he stands between two fears: a sin already committed, and he does not know what God will do with him because of it; and a life still remaining, and he does not know what dangers he may meet in it.” Abū Nuʿaym al-Iṣfahānī, *Ḥilyat al-Awliyāʾ*, Egypt, 1932, vol. 2, p. 132.

[^V3-72]: al-Ṭabarī, *Tārīkh*, London ed., 1879, vol. 1, p. 915. In the early Islamic period, the word *Sawād* referred to the countryside between Basra and Kufa and the villages and farms around them. This Sawād was the most fertile land in Iraq.

[^V3-73]: Saʿīd ibn Jubayr al-Asadī al-Kūfī (45–95 AH). When ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn al-Ashʿath rebelled against al-Ḥajjāj in Iraq during the caliphate of the Umayyad ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān, Saʿīd joined the revolt because of his anger at al-Ḥajjāj’s tyranny. After Ibn al-Ashʿath was defeated and killed, Saʿīd went to Mecca. The governor there, Khālid al-Qasrī, arrested him and sent him to al-Ḥajjāj, who had him killed. Aḥmad ibn Ḥanbal said: “al-Ḥajjāj killed Saʿīd when there was no one on the face of the earth who was not in need of his knowledge.” Muḥammad Asʿad Ṭalas, *Tārīkh al-Umma al-ʿArabiyya*, Beirut, 198, vol. 2, p. 126.

[^V3-74]: Kumayl served ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib during his caliphate in Hīt and ʿĀna, west of Baghdad, and in Āmid in Iran. al-Ḥajjāj killed him in 83 AH/702 CE. See Muḥammad Bāqir al-Khwānsārī, *Rawḍāt al-Jannāt*, Iran, 1370 AH, p. 537.

[^V3-75]: Among the ascetics of Egypt we may mention Ḥaywa ibn Shurayḥ al-Tujībī; see Ibn al-Jawzī, *Ṣifat al-Ṣafwa*, vol. 4, p. 282. See also al-Faḍl ibn Faḍāla, ibid., Hyderabad ed., 1355 AH, vol. 3, p. 47.

[^V3-76]: Ibid., vol. 4, p. 187.

[^V3-80]: See Ibn ʿArabī, *al-Futūḥāt al-Makkiyya*, Egypt ed., 1293 AH, vol. 3, p. 174. See also *Rasāʾil Ikhwān al-Ṣafāʾ*, Egypt ed., 1928, vol. 2, p. 303.

[^V3-81]: In Islamic usage, the “Companions” are the companions of the Prophet Muḥammad, and the “Followers” are the generation that came after them and followed the Companions.

[^V3-85]: One example concerns Bahlūl ibn Dhuʾayb, one of the Companions. He is said to have gone out to a mountain near Medina, dressed in wool, tied his hands behind his back with iron chains, and cried out: “Lord, look at Bahlūl dragging his chains and confessing his sins.” See Albert Naṣrī Nādir, *al-Taṣawwuf al-Islāmī*, p. 10. Another example concerns al-Rabīʿ ibn Khuthaym, one of the ascetics of Kufa (d. 67 AH/686 CE). When he tried to explain to his mother why he was sad and weeping, he told her: “I have killed someone—myself.”

[^V3-86]: See al-Sarrāj al-Ṭūsī, *al-Lumaʿ*, pp. 65–66.

[^V3-87]: In Arabic, *zahida fī al-shayʾ* or *zahida ʿanhu* means to turn away from something and leave it. Thus *zahida fī al-dunyā* means to renounce worldly life in order to devote oneself to worship.

[^V3-88]: al-Sarrāj al-Ṭūsī, *al-Lumaʿ*, p. 72.

[^V3-89]: They interpreted the verse, “Wealth and children are the adornment of the life of this world, but lasting good deeds are better with your Lord in reward and better in hope” (Qurʾan 18:46), as proof that worldly life itself was worthless and as a justification for despising and abandoning it in pursuit of “lasting good deeds.”

[^V3-90]: *Ṣifat al-Ṣafwa* (vol. 3, p. 131) says that ʿĀmir ibn ʿAbd Qays prayed to God “to remove the desire for women from his heart.” al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī is also reported to have said: “When God wants good for a servant, He does not occupy him with a wife or children.” al-Shaʿrānī, *al-Ṭabaqāt al-Kubrā*, vol. 1, p. 25.

[^V3-91]: Some historians attribute to Imām ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib the saying that the Qurʾan has an outward meaning and an inward meaning. —Publisher.

[^V3-92]: See Muḥyī al-Dīn Ibn ʿArabī’s Qurʾan commentary as an example of Sufi interpretation.

[^V3-93]: Adam Mez, *Die Renaissance des Islams*, vol. 2, p. 30, citing al-Hujwīrī, *Kashf al-Maḥjūb*, p. 383.

[^V3-94]: The contradiction between asceticism and Qadarism comes from the fact that asceticism depends on trusting in God’s decree and destiny, while the Qadarites rejected the deterministic meaning of that trust. The contradiction between the ascetics and the traditionalists comes from the ascetics allowing themselves to interpret Islamic texts in ways that fit their own way of life. The traditionalists rejected this because they insisted on the plain and direct meanings of the texts.

[^V3-95]: Goldziher, *Introduction to Islamic Theology and Law*, p. 97.

[^V3-112]: The Sufis claim that al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī was the first Sufi and the pioneer of Sufism. In reality, neither his conduct nor his thought went beyond the asceticism of his own time in either character or content. Indeed, in practice he may be counted among the more moderate ascetics, since he did not go to extremes in seclusion or worship. He is famous for saying: “An atom’s weight of sound piety is better than a thousand weights of fasting and prayer” (*al-Risāla al-Qushayriyya*, p. 54). Despite the simplicity and conventional Islamic meaning of this statement, later Sufis treated it as evidence that al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī was one of them, or even their pioneer. Yet ordinary Muslim believers say the same thing, as do the leading scholars of Sunni Islam and the jurists, especially—even when they are hostile to Sufism.

[^V3-113]: al-Ṭūsī says of the Sufis in *al-Lumaʿ*, p. 42: “They were named after the outward appearance of their clothing, not after any branch of knowledge or spiritual state by which they were known, because wearing wool was the practice of the prophets, peace be upon them, of the truthful, and the mark of poor worshippers.” In al-Kalābādhī’s *al-Taʿarruf li-Madhhab al-Taṣawwuf* (d. 380 AH/990 CE), Cairo ed., 1923, ch. 1, he says of the Sufis: “They were called Sufis because of their clothing and appearance. They did not wear, for the pleasure of the self, clothes that were soft to the touch or beautiful to look at. They dressed only to cover themselves, and so deliberately chose rough haircloth and coarse wool.” al-Kalābādhī also reports al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī as saying: “I met seventy men who had fought at Badr, and they wore nothing but wool.”

[^V3-114]: See al-Shaybī, *al-Ṣila bayna al-Taṣawwuf wa-l-Tashayyuʿ*, pp. 262–263.

[^V3-115]: al-Masʿūdī, *Murūj al-Dhahab*, Egypt, 1346 AH, vol. 2, p. 162.

[^V3-116]: *Washy* is fabric decorated with bright colors.

[^V3-117]: Ibn Khaldūn, *al-Muqaddima*, Dār al-Kitāb al-Lubnānī, 1956, p. 843.

[^V3-118]: These ascetics could become so isolated from society that one of them would never leave his house, or would dig out a cellar where he secluded himself even from his own family. al-Iṣfahānī reports this in *Ḥilyat al-Awliyāʾ* (6/345) about ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz al-Rāsī, one of the ascetics of Basra.

[^V3-119]: The picture al-Masʿūdī gives us of social conditions at the end of the Umayyad period may help show what we mean by the development of social and political conflict. He reports that after the fall of the Umayyad state, one of its elders was asked why it had collapsed. He answered: “We became so occupied with our pleasures that we neglected what we were obliged to watch over. We wronged our subjects, so they lost hope of justice from us. Our officials mistreated them, so they longed to be rid of us. We burdened the taxpayers, so they abandoned us. Our estates were ruined, and with them our treasuries. We trusted our ministers, and they put their own interests before ours. They carried out affairs without us and hid knowledge of them from us. The pay of our soldiers was delayed, so they stopped obeying us. Our enemy called them over, and they joined him in fighting us. Our enemies came after us, and we were unable to resist because we had so few supporters...” The words may not really have been spoken by an Umayyad, but the picture they paint accurately reflects the historical reality of the final days of the Umayyad state. See al-Masʿūdī, *Murūj al-Dhahab*, vol. 2, p. 194.

[^V3-120]: The ideological side of this practice lies in its preparing the way for what the Sufis later called *walāya*, or sainthood. The Sufis created this rank for those among them who reached the highest spiritual stations, and attributed to their “saints” divine wonders similar to the miracles of prophets. The real basis of this Sufi idea of sainthood was an attempt to take absolute religious authority away from the caliphs, whose monopoly on the title “God’s caliph and His shadow on earth” formed part of the official religious ideology of the caliphate. The creation of Sufi sainthood was therefore one of many different ways in which the ideology of the state was challenged.

[^V3-137]: By “for a time” we mean that after the third Islamic century, Sufis would raise the status of the “knowers” to something approaching divine rank, as we shall see.

[^V3-138]: Nicholson denies that this group of ascetics—al-Ṭāʾī, Ibn ʿIyāḍ, Ibn Adham, and Shaqīq al-Balkhī—were influenced by Christian sources in their mystical direction. See *Studies in Islamic Mysticism*, p. 3, Arabic trans. Abū al-ʿAlā ʿAfīfī.

[^V3-139]: See de Boer, *The History of Philosophy in Islam*, p. 12, n. 3, 3rd Arabic ed.

[^V3-140]: The Arabs conquered Khurasan by treaty in 30 AH/650 CE, during the caliphate of ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān. But revolts and other forms of resistance to Arab rule continued through almost the entire Umayyad period, and then returned under the Abbasids in many new forms, including intellectual and ideological ones. Under al-Manṣūr, the people of Khurasan revolted against him in 136 AH/753 CE. See *Murūj al-Dhahab*, vol. 2, p. 237.

[^V3-141]: Khurasan was one of the main centers of the Alids and later of Abbasid propaganda against the Umayyads. It remained an important center of Shiʿism throughout Islamic history.

[^V3-142]: During the caliphate of the Umayyad Hishām ibn ʿAbd al-Malik (105–125 AH/723–742 CE), for example, the people of Khurasan took an active part in the revolt of Zayd ibn ʿAlī ibn al-Ḥusayn ibn ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib in 121 AH/738 CE. See Ibn al-Ṭiqṭaqā, *al-Fakhrī fī al-Ādāb al-Sulṭāniyya*, p. 113. Historians have usually explained revolts like this purely in sectarian terms—as expressions of Shiʿism—while neglecting their social motives. Yet for many participants, the main motives were less sectarian than they were forms of resistance to social or political domination. This applies very clearly to the people of Khurasan at the time.

[^V3-143]: al-Manāwī, *al-Kawākib al-Durriyya*, vol. 1, p. 143.

[^V3-144]: Sufi sources tell us that Ibrāhīm ibn Adham lived in Basra, Alexandria, and Mecca and later in Syria. See *Ṭabaqāt al-Ṣūfiyya*, p. 30. They say that al-Fuḍayl ibn ʿIyāḍ went to Kufa and lived there; see Jāmī, *Nafaḥāt al-Uns*, p. 38. He died in 187 AH/803 CE; see Ibn al-Jawzī, *Ṣifat al-Ṣafwa*, vol. 2, p. 134. Ibn al-Mubārak left Khurasan for Kufa; see ibid., vol. 4, p. 109. The sources disagree over the journeys, beliefs, and fate of Shaqīq al-Balkhī. The author of *al-Kawākib al-Durriyya* (vol. 1, p. 121) says that he once set out accompanied by 300 poor worshippers and ascetics, and that he met the Abbasid caliph al-Maʾmūn and admonished him. *Nafaḥāt al-Uns* (p. 15) presents him as Sunni, while *Rawḍāt al-Jannāt* (p. 328) presents him as a Jaʿfari and says that he was martyred in Transoxiana on a charge of *rafḍ*, or Shiʿism. *Nafaḥāt al-Uns* (p. 50) says that he was martyred in the province of Khuttal, and so on.

[^V3-145]: Abū al-ʿAlā ʿAfīfī, *al-Malāmatiyya wa-l-Ṣūfiyya wa-Ahl al-Futuwwa*, Cairo, 1945, p. 31.

[^V3-146]: By the “social dimension” of Khurasani asceticism at the time, we mean that in its real historical basis it grew mainly out of resistance to official Arab institutions. That resistance mixed “national” feeling with reactions against social and political oppression.

[^V3-147]: The term commonly used by the early Sufis was *maḥabba*, “love.” In Sufi teaching and practice, love was counted among the “states.” They defined a “state” as “something that descends into the heart, or with which the heart becomes adorned through the purity of remembrance.” See al-Sarrāj al-Ṭūsī, *al-Lumaʿ*, p. 66.

[^V3-148]: See al-Shaybī, *al-Ṣila bayna al-Taṣawwuf wa-l-Tashayyuʿ*, pp. 298–299.

[^V3-149]: If we set aside the miraculous elements, we can draw the following picture from what ʿAbd al-Raḥmān Badawī cites from Farīd al-Dīn ʿAṭṭār’s Persian *Tadhkirat al-Awliyāʾ*—Nicholson ed., Leiden and London, 1905 and 1907, vol. 1, pp. 59–61—and from the Arabic sources: Ibn Khallikān, *Wafayāt al-Aʿyān*, vol. 1, p. 256, Cairo, 1858; Ibn Taghrībirdī, *al-Nujūm al-Zāhira*, vol. 1, p. 33, Cairo, 1929; al-Jāḥiẓ, *al-Bayān wa-l-Tabyīn*, vol. 3, p. 85, Cairo, 1332 AH; and al-Jāḥiẓ, *al-Ḥayawān*, vol. 1, p. 78, Cairo, 1907. Rābiʿa al-ʿAdawiyya was born into a very poor family in Basra and was connected to Banū ʿAdwa through clientage rather than descent. When she was still young, her father died and a famine struck Basra. She and her three sisters became separated and wandered without direction. A cruel man captured her and sold her for six dirhams to a man who worked her very hard. One day, as she was walking, she noticed a man looking at her with bad intentions and ran away. On the road she threw herself onto the ground and prayed to God. She then heard a voice say: “Do not grieve. On the Day of Judgment, those closest to God in heaven will look toward you and envy what you will become.” After hearing this, she returned to her “master’s” house, began fasting, continued serving him, and spent the whole night in prayer. See Badawī, *Shahīdat al-ʿIshq al-Ilāhī*, Cairo, 1962, 2nd ed., pp. 8, 12. Even this summary still contains a legendary element at the end of the story.

[^V3-150]: Badawī, the work just cited, pp. 15, 17, 19.

[^V3-151]: Ibid., same pages. See also *al-Risāla al-Qushayriyya*, the chapters on love and repentance; *al-Taʿarruf li-Madhhab Ahl al-Taṣawwuf*, Cairo, 1933, p. 64; *Maṣāriʿ al-ʿUshshāq*, Istanbul, 1883, p. 136; and Massignon, *Recueil de textes inédits de l’histoire de la mystique*, Paris, 1929, p. 8, citing Ibn Taymiyya, and so on.

[^V3-152]: See footnote 1.

[^V3-153]: Badawī, *Shahīdat al-ʿIshq al-Ilāhī*, p. 23, citing Shaykh al-Ḥarīfīsh, *al-Rawḍ al-Fāʾiq fī al-Mawāʿiẓ wa-l-Raqāʾiq*, Cairo, 1886, p. 117.

[^V3-154]: Ibid., p. 39, citing Ibn Taymiyya as quoted by Massignon in *Recueil de textes inédits de l’histoire de la mystique islamique*, p. 8, no. 8.

[^V3-155]: By these elements we mean sayings attributed to Rābiʿa that are also attributed to other people. For example, she is reported to have said: “I did not worship Him [God] out of fear of His fire, or out of desire for His paradise, for then I would be like a bad hired worker. I worshipped Him out of love for Him and longing for Him.” See al-Manāwī, *al-Kawākib al-Durriyya*, vol. 1, p. 109. The same idea, and sometimes the same wording, is often attributed to ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib, and also to his grandson ʿAlī ibn al-Ḥusayn Zayn al-ʿĀbidīn; see ibid. Rābiʿa is also said to have remarked: “How many desires have lost their pleasure while leaving their burden behind with me.” Ibid. Almost the same words are also attributed to the Kufan ascetic ʿAwn ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUtba al-Hudhalī, who died around 110 AH/728 CE; see Ibn al-Jawzī, *Ṣifat al-Ṣafwa*, vol. 3, p. 56. The same idea is also attributed to ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib; see *Nahj al-Balāgha*, vol. 3, p. 179.

[^V3-156]: One example is the idea of pilgrimage that we mentioned a few lines above.

[^V3-157]: See Abū Rayyān, *Tārīkh al-Fikr al-Falsafī fī al-Islām*, p. 282.

[^V3-158]: Abū Rayyān, ibid., p. 283. “Knowledge by taste” here is, as we will discuss later, a Sufi term for the knowledge they claim to obtain through inward revelation after long spiritual and physical discipline based on suppressing bodily desires and instincts and enduring the hardships of poverty and isolation.

[^V3-159]: Abū Rayyān, ibid., p. 282.

[^V3-160]: al-Shaybī, *al-Ṣila bayna al-Taṣawwuf wa-l-Tashayyuʿ*, p. 358. What is meant by “divinely bestowed knowledge” is inner knowledge, or the familiar Sufi idea of revelation: reaching “divine truth” through inward intuition after “purifying the soul” by spiritual discipline.

[^V3-161]: Ibid., citing Abū Ṭālib al-Makkī, *Qūt al-Qulūb*, vol. 1, p. 35. al-Makkī means that Maʿrūf al-Karkhī knew less about doctrine and religious law than Ibn Ḥanbal and Ibn Maʿīn, yet the two men still relied on his judgment to settle their disagreements in these matters.

[^V3-162]: Ibid., citing Abū Nuʿaym al-Iṣfahānī, *Ḥilyat al-Awliyāʾ*, vol. 10, p. 263.

[^V3-163]: Ibid. The eighth Shiʿi Imam referred to here is ʿAlī ibn Mūsā al-Riḍā (d. 203 AH/818–819 CE).

[^V3-164]: Kāmil Muṣṭafā al-Shaybī, ibid., p. 357.

[^V3-165]: Abū Rayyān, *Tārīkh al-Fikr al-Falsafī fī al-Islām*, p. 282.

[^V3-173]: In a sermon delivered in Mecca, Abū Jaʿfar al-Manṣūr said: “People! I am God’s authority on His earth. I govern you with His guidance, direction, and support. I am the guardian of His wealth. I act in it according to His will and command, and I give from it with His permission.” Ibn ʿAbd Rabbih, *al-ʿIqd al-Farīd*, Egypt ed., 1302 AH, vol. 1, p. 179.

[^V3-174]: See Aḥmad Amīn, *Ḍuḥā al-Islām*, 5th ed., vol. 1, pp. 36–37.

[^V3-176]: One of the strongest signs of this “coexistence” between the two cultures is the translation of the Zoroastrian *Avesta* into Arabic under the Abbasid caliphate, despite the Islamic and “theocratic” character of the state and despite the fact that Zoroastrian beliefs do not agree with Islamic doctrine, especially the doctrine of divine unity.

[^V3-178]: Jundishapur was a city in Khuzistan founded by King Shapur I, from whom it took its name. He settled Roman prisoners there. See Aḥmad Amīn, *Ḍuḥā al-Islām*, vol. 1, p. 268. Khusraw Anushirvan (531–579 CE) later established an institute there for medical and philosophical studies. See de Boer, *The History of Philosophy in Islam*, p. 20.

[^V3-185]: See Chapter One of this volume. We ask the reader to return to the analysis given there, because here we take it as an explanation for the rise of philosophical Sufism, just as there we took it as an explanation for the rise of an independent rational philosophical movement. The two developments share an ideological side.

[^V3-186]: See this analysis at the beginning of this volume, under “The Decisive Preconditions for the Independence of Philosophy.”

[^V3-187]: This began when Muʿāwiya ibn Abī Sufyān founded the Umayyad state in 40 AH/660 CE. Muʿāwiya made its political system an absolute monarchy and then made the monarchy hereditary within a dynasty. This political system continued through the later periods of the Islamic caliphate. Muʿāwiya seems to have chosen this system with a conscious understanding of what kingship meant in the civilization of the time, as shown by the fact that he himself took pride in being “the first king” in Arab-Islamic history. See Ibn ʿAbd al-Barr, *al-Istīʿāb*, vol. 1, p. 254.

[^V3-188]: Qurʾan 42:11.

[^V3-189]: Chapter One of this volume.

[^V3-190]: Chapter One of this volume.

[^V3-191]: One Western scholar of Arab civilization noticed, in his own way, this relationship between philosophy and Islamic Sufism. He wrote that “mysticism among Muslims also clearly shows its close relationship and kinship with rational thought, a relationship that can be demonstrated throughout world history, because mysticism too is a science with principles of its own, and its opposite is not theoretical scientific knowledge...” See Adam Mez, *Die Renaissance des Islams*, vol. 2, p. 14, 3rd Arabic ed. Mez means here that Sufism is itself a kind of “theoretical scientific knowledge,” rather than its opposite. This is the basis on which we study Sufism as part of the history of Arab-Islamic philosophy.

[^V3-192]: Abū Saʿīd Aḥmad ibn Saʿīd al-Kharrāz (d. 277 AH/890 CE), one of the major Sufis of the third Islamic century, says: “...Knowledge of the Truth [God] is knowledge of His oneness.” al-Sarrāj al-Ṭūsī, *al-Lumaʿ*, p. 56.

[^V3-193]: An example is Adam Mez’s claim that from the third Islamic century onward Muslims began to feel new religious needs, and that “the old religions that had always remained hidden behind an outward veil, especially Christianity—that is, the body of late Greek philosophy in its Christianized schools and forms,” by which he means Neoplatonism, “quickly came forward to meet these needs. The movement that changed the form of Islam during the third and fourth centuries was, taken as a whole, nothing but the result of Christian intellectual currents entering the religion of Muḥammad.” Mez then says, in the same context, that the idea of knowing God “as the highest religious ideal belongs to the ancient Gnostic doctrine. Among free thinkers it appeared as rationalism or scientific theology, and among others as mysticism.” On Mez’s view, then, the Sufi theory of knowledge comes entirely from two external sources: Christianity in its Neoplatonic form, and ancient Eastern Gnosticism. Similar views can be found among other Arab and Orientalist scholars. See, for example, Nicholson, *Studies in Islamic Mysticism*, Arabic trans. Abū al-ʿAlā ʿAfīfī, p. 14 ff.; and Ḥannā al-Fākhūrī and Khalīl al-Jurr, *Tārīkh al-Falsafa al-ʿArabiyya*, Beirut, vol. 1, p. 301, on the Sufi Dhū al-Nūn al-Miṣrī.

[^V3-194]: *al-Risāla al-Qushayriyya* says: “Abū al-Fayḍ Dhū al-Nūn al-Miṣrī, whose name was Thawbān ibn Ibrāhīm, and whose father was Nubian, from the land of Nubia.” Nicholson calls him “the true founder of Sufism” (*Studies in Islamic Mysticism*, Arabic trans. Abū al-ʿAlā ʿAfīfī, Cairo, 1947, p. 7). Philip Hitti calls him “the founder of Sufi doctrine” (*History of the Arabs*, extended ed., vol. 2, p. 524, Beirut, 1950). Albert Naṣrī Nādir argues that Dhū al-Nūn al-Miṣrī was “the first to make philosophy a part of Sufism” (*al-Taṣawwuf al-Islāmī*, Beirut, 1960, p. 42). We may not regard such exact claims as scientifically sound, since intellectual phenomena, like other phenomena, cannot be dated as though they suddenly came into existence through one person without earlier roots or preparation. Even so, these claims remain significant because they show the historical importance of Dhū al-Nūn’s thought within philosophical Sufism.

[^V3-195]: We took this passage from Kāmil Muṣṭafā al-Shaybī, *al-Ṣila bayna al-Taṣawwuf wa-l-Tashayyuʿ*, p. 363, translated from Farīd al-Dīn ʿAṭṭār’s Persian *Tadhkirat al-Awliyāʾ*, vol. 1, p. 108. Another translation of the same passage from the same source appears at vol. 1, p. 127, and reads: “True knowledge of God is not knowledge of His oneness, which all believers possess. Nor is it the demonstrative and speculative knowledge of the philosophers, theologians, and men of eloquence. Rather, it is knowledge of the qualities of oneness, a knowledge belonging especially to the saints, because they are the ones who behold God with their hearts, and He reveals to them what He does not reveal to His other servants.” See Albert Nādir, *al-Taṣawwuf al-Islāmī*, Beirut, 1960, p. 20. The clear difference between the two translations does not affect Dhū al-Nūn’s picture of Sufi knowledge, the knowledge of the saints. But it does affect his attitude toward the other two kinds of knowledge. In the first translation, he seems to recognize the knowledge of believers and the rational knowledge of philosophers alongside the knowledge of the saints, or Sufis. In the second, he appears to reject the other two and recognize only Sufi knowledge.

[^V3-216]: Among the “ambiguous” Qurʾanic verses are those whose surface wording seems to suggest that God has a physical nature. One example is: “Wherever you turn, there is the face of God” (Qurʾan 2:115). Taken literally, the verse would seem to mean that God has a face like a human being.

[^V3-220]: Abū Dharr al-Ghifārī was among the earliest Muslims and among the earliest Islamic ascetics. He supported ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib and opposed ʿUthmān and Muʿāwiya. In 30 AH, Muʿāwiya expelled him from Syria to Medina, and Caliph ʿUthmān later exiled him to the village of al-Rabadha near Medina because of his strong opposition to the caliph’s policies and to the enrichment of the Umayyads at the expense of the poor masses.

[^V3-221]: Qurʾan 9:34.

[^V3-222]: One example is the interpretation of the verse, “God only wishes to remove impurity from you, People of the House, and to purify you completely” (Qurʾan 33:33), as referring by “People of the House” to the Prophet, ʿAlī, Fāṭima, al-Ḥasan, and al-Ḥusayn. See Tafsīr ʿAlī ibn Ibrāhīm, p. 531. Another example is the interpretation of the verse, “We offered the Trust to the heavens, the earth, and the mountains, but they refused to bear it and were afraid of it. Man undertook it; he was truly unjust and ignorant” (Qurʾan 33:72), where “unjust and ignorant” is taken to mean the person who usurped ʿAlī’s right to the imamate. See ibid., p. 535. There are many other examples.

[^V3-226]: The Khaṭṭābiyya were named after Abū al-Khaṭṭāb ibn Abī Zaynab, as stated in al-Ashʿarī’s *Maqālāt*, Cairo, 1969, vol. 1, p. 76. Abū al-Khaṭṭāb was killed in 143 AH.
