[^V3-230]: See the two sources just cited, same pages. al-Ashʿarī adds that the Muʿammariyya “believed in transmigration of souls, and said that they do not die, but are raised bodily to the heavenly realm while bodies resembling their own are placed before the people.”

[^V3-231]: This refers to the Qurʾanic distinction between clear verses and ambiguous verses—that is, verses whose meaning is not limited to their outward wording. —Publisher.

[^V3-232]: The word *gnosis* means knowledge. For “mediator,” the better term is *médiateur*. —Publisher.

[^V3-233]: In Sufi usage, *union* means reaching such a degree of “connection” with God that the distinction between lover and beloved disappears and the two become one in essence and action. Once union is achieved, reference to either “the Sufi” or “God” disappears. See Abū Rayyān, *Tārīkh al-Fikr al-Falsafī fī al-Islām*, pp. 296–297. Abū Yazīd al-Bisṭāmī (d. 261 AH/874 CE) expressed this “union” in famous ecstatic sayings such as: “I am God. There is no god but me, so worship me.” See al-Shaybī, *al-Ṣila bayna al-Taṣawwuf wa-l-Tashayyuʿ*, p. 394, citing Farīd al-Dīn ʿAṭṭār’s Persian *Tadhkirat al-Awliyāʾ*, vol. 1, p. 116.

[^V3-234]: *Ḥulūl*, or divine indwelling, is represented by al-Ḥallāj—al-Ḥusayn ibn Manṣūr al-Ḥallāj (244–309 AH/858–921 CE). He expressed the idea in these lines:

    > Glory to Him who revealed His humanity,
    > the secret of His radiant, piercing divinity;
    > then appeared openly to His creation
    > in the form of one who eats and drinks,
    > until His creatures saw Him
    > as plainly as one eyebrow sees the other.

    See *Dīwān al-Ḥallāj*, ed. Massignon, Paris, 1955, p. 37.

[^V3-235]: Muḥyī al-Dīn Ibn ʿArabī (560–638 AH/1164–1240 CE) represents the doctrine of the unity of being, or pantheism, in Islamic Sufism. He holds that all existence is one: God is true existence, while the world is a mirror in which divine existence appears. See Ibn ʿArabī, *Fuṣūṣ al-Ḥikam*, ed. Abū al-ʿAlā ʿAfīfī, the section “A Divine Wisdom in an Adamic Word,” and see the discussion of “the unity of being” later in this chapter.

[^V3-236]: *Fanāʾ* (“annihilation”) and *baqāʾ* (“subsistence”) are two sides of one stage in Sufi experience. al-Qushayrī describes their levels as follows: “...annihilation from his own self and attributes, subsistence through the attributes of the Truth [God], then annihilation from the attributes of the Truth, then annihilation even from witnessing his own annihilation as he is absorbed in the existence of the Truth.” See *al-Risāla al-Qushayriyya*, p. 37. al-Sarī al-Saqaṭī described the Sufi in the state of *fanāʾ* by saying: “If he were struck with a sword, he would not feel it.” See *al-Lumaʿ*, p. 192.

[^V3-237]: al-Sarrāj al-Ṭūsī explains *shaṭḥ*—an ecstatic utterance—as “a word derived from movement, because it is the movement of the inner secrets of those overcome by ecstasy. When their ecstasy becomes intense, they express it in words that sound strange to the listener.” See *al-Lumaʿ*, p. 453.

[^V3-238]: See footnote [^V3-233] on the previous page.

[^V3-239]: See footnote [^V3-233] on the previous page.

[^V3-240]: al-Ḥallāj’s theory of “He is I and I am He” is based on the idea that God appears in the human being and that the human being is an image of divine manifestation. Through spiritual struggle, the human being can therefore uncover the divine image within himself. The name of the theory comes from one of al-Ḥallāj’s prayers: “...O You who are I, and I am You.” See *Arbaʿat Nuṣūṣ Tataʿallaq bi-l-Ḥallāj*, ed. Massignon, p. 59.

[^V3-241]: *Akhbār al-Ḥallāj*, ed. Massignon and P. Kraus, Paris, 1939, p. 21.

[^V3-242]: Ibn ʿArabī, *Fuṣūṣ al-Ḥikam*, “A Divine Wisdom in an Adamic Word.” See Albert Naṣrī Nādir, *al-Taṣawwuf al-Islāmī*, p. 129.

[^V3-243]: Or, as Mahdī ʿĀmil put it: “humanizing God by divinizing man, and vice versa.” See *al-Ṭarīq*, Beirut, November 1968. al-Ḥallāj brought both sides together when he said: “With my humanity I entered through You among creation—and were it not for my divinity, I would have departed from truth.” See *Akhbār al-Ḥallāj*, p. 83. He means that through his human nature he entered the world of divinity, and that without a divine element within him he would not have been truthful in making this claim. al-Ḥallāj has many other texts expressing this doctrine of indwelling without using the Christian terms “humanity” and “divinity,” such as: “I am the one I love, and the one I love is I—we are two spirits dwelling in one body.” See al-Ḥallāj, *Kitāb al-Ṭawāsīn*, Paris ed., 1913, p. 134. Here there are still two spirits—that is, a duality.

[^V3-251]: This commitment appears very clearly in al-Junayd’s own answer when he was asked about divine unity: “To single out the One by realizing His oneness in the perfection of His absolute unity: that He is the One who neither begets nor is begotten, denying opposites, equals, and likenesses, without comparison, asking how, picturing, or representing Him: ‘There is nothing like Him, and He is the Hearing, the Seeing’” (Qurʾan 42:11). See al-Qushayrī, the work cited above.

[^V3-256]: One example appears in the writings of ʿAmr ibn ʿUthmān al-Makkī, a Sufi of the third–fourth Islamic centuries. He says: “God causes flashes to appear in the clarity of the imagination like flashes of lightning, one following another. He reveals them to the hearts of His saints, without their imagining them through the original forms on which their hearts’ belief and faith in the unseen were based. The flashes of increasing light that appear to the hearts become such that the souls cannot imagine that light within the clarity of the imagination...” See *al-Lumaʿ*, p. 412. al-Ṭūsī explains these “flashes” as being “taken from flashes of lightning: when lightning flashes in the clouds, the thirsty person hopes for rain.” Ibid. So the terms “light” and “flashes” here do not yet refer to the “metaphysical” world later systematized by the Illuminationists and made the basis of their doctrine.

[^V3-257]: The standard sources give his name as Yaḥyā Shihāb al-Dīn al-Suhrawardī al-Maqtūl, “the slain.” The title *al-Maqtūl* became attached to his name after he was killed by the Ayyubid authorities in Aleppo in 587 AH/1191 CE, following a ruling by the Sunni jurists of Aleppo that he was an unbeliever. The ruling came after a debate between him and the jurists in the court of al-Malik al-Ẓāhir, son of Ṣalāḥ al-Dīn al-Ayyūbī. The main issue on which they based their judgment was his statement—after they deliberately maneuvered him into the question—that God was capable of creating a prophet after the Prophet Muḥammad. See Abū Rayyān, *Uṣūl al-Falsafa al-Ishrāqiyya*, 1st ed., Cairo, 1959, pp. 17–18.

[^V3-258]: The name Ismaʿiliyya has a historical origin in the followers of Ismāʿīl ibn Jaʿfar al-Ṣādiq, the sixth Imam in Twelver Shiʿism. Although Ismāʿīl died before his father, one Imami group still declared him the Imam and denied his death, saying that he would not die until he ruled the earth. See al-Nawbakhtī, *Firaq al-Shīʿa*, p. 58. Another group later declared Muḥammad ibn Ismāʿīl himself to be Imam. After that, however, the name “Ismaʿiliyya” came to be used for several esoteric sects. In this study, we mean the followers of an esoteric philosophical doctrine that gives divine qualities to its Imams and uses number symbolism, like ancient Pythagoreanism, to classify the levels of the metaphysical world as it imagines them. This reflects the hierarchical organization of missionaries and followers, and later the ranks of the political hierarchy under Fatimid rule. Ismaʿilism was the official doctrine of the Fatimid state (297–567 AH/909–1171 CE). See Claude Cahen, *Histoire des peuples musulmans*, p. 314, and al-Shaybī, *al-Ṣila bayna al-Taṣawwuf wa-l-Tashayyuʿ*, p. 910, citing Nāṣir-i Khusraw, *Jāmiʿ al-Ḥikmatayn*, p. 296, in Persian.

    Some historians consider the Druze community to have originated as a break from official Fatimid Ismaʿili doctrine, though this break enjoyed the protection of the fifth Fatimid caliph, al-Ḥākim bi-Amr Allāh. —Publisher.

[^V3-272]: See, for example, his text published in the work just cited, quoting H. Corbin in *Opera*, vol. 2, pp. 10–11. al-Suhrawardī has another passage in *Ḥikmat al-Ishrāq*, Tehran lithograph ed., 1313–1316 AH, p. 371, where he presents a “universal” view of philosophical thought. He sees a continuous lineage within the worldwide “family” of philosophy and places in this chain Empedocles, Pythagoras, Plato, Aristotle, Buddha, Agathodaemon, Hermes, Mazdak, and Mani.

[^V3-273]: In this study, we use the word “metaphysics” as the opposite of “physics,” meaning nature, not as the opposite of “dialectics” in the scientific sense used by dialectical materialism.

[^V3-274]: It may be an exaggeration to say that al-Ghazālī “took over” the leadership of Sunni thought. As the author says, al-Ghazālī was an Ashʿarī, but Ashʿarism did not represent all Sunni thought. —Publisher.

[^V3-275]: It may be more accurate to say that the Ayyubid state succeeded the Fatimid state, since no war was fought between them. —Publisher.

[^V3-276]: See Ḥasan Ibrāhīm Ḥasan and ʿAlī Ibrāhīm Ḥasan, *al-Nuẓum al-Islāmiyya*, Cairo, 4th ed., 1970, pp. 84–93.

[^V3-277]: Quṭb al-Dīn al-Shīrāzī, in his commentary on *Ḥikmat al-Ishrāq*, points to this side of al-Suhrawardī’s Illuminationism. He says that when the author—al-Suhrawardī—came across fragments of Persian books and found them consistent with matters of mystical unveiling and direct vision, he approved of them and developed them further. This became the Illuminationist principle of light and darkness. al-Shīrāzī then says that this principle “is not like the doctrine of the unbelieving Magians, who believe in literal light and darkness as two first principles, because they are polytheists, not believers in divine unity.” See the introduction to *Ḥikmat al-Ishrāq*, Tehran ed., p. 19. Here we can see the Islamic side of Illuminationist principles.

[^V3-278]: A detailed account of the relationship between illumination and direct vision can be found in al-Suhrawardī’s *Ḥikmat al-Ishrāq*, Tehran ed., 1313–1316 AH, section 4, article 3. al-Suhrawardī also uses two other terms for illumination and vision: *qahr*, “domination,” for illumination, and *maḥabba* or *ʿishq*, “love,” for vision. For him, domination is “the process of bringing into existence,” while love is “the process of perception.” Love is movement toward the completion of a perfection, whether imagined, rational, or otherwise, and God—or the Light of Lights—is the first beloved. See *Ḥikmat al-Ishrāq*, article 2, chapter 5. Through “love,” God orders existence upward; through “domination,” He orders it downward. All existence, al-Suhrawardī says, is contained within the domination of the Light of Lights, but in a hierarchy from top to bottom. Bodies lie under the domination of souls; souls under the luminous domination of intellects; intellects under the luminous domination of the first effect; and this last under the luminosity of the Light of Lights. See al-Suhrawardī, *al-Mashāriʿ wa-l-Muṭāraḥāt*, p. 464.

[^V3-279]: al-Suhrawardī says, for example: “The love that every lower light has for itself is overcome by its love for the higher light.” See *Ḥikmat al-Ishrāq*, article 2, chapter 6.

[^V3-280]: See *al-Mashāriʿ wa-l-Muṭāraḥāt*, p. 488.

[^V3-281]: See vol. 2 of this book, pp. 658–659.

[^V3-282]: In *Hayākil al-Nūr*, Egypt ed., 1957, second temple, al-Suhrawardī rejects those who “imagine that the soul is a part of Him,” meaning a part of God. He regards this as a false belief: “God does not admit division, so how could the rational soul resemble a part of Him?” There is therefore no unity of being between God and the soul. Since the soul is one link in the chain of Illuminationist existence, denying unity at one link means denying it throughout the whole chain.

[^V3-283]: As evidence, we can cite part of a passage from Ibn ʿArabī’s *Shajarat al-Kawn*, p. 7: “God brought beings into existence through His power over them, not because He needed them. The perfection of His wisdom lies in creation, and this was to reveal the nobility of water and clay.” This clearly treats “beings,” including water and clay, as existing with an existence different from God’s. In other words, they have an existence independent of His, even though it remains connected with Him.

[^V3-284]: See *Ḥikmat al-Ishrāq*, article 2, chapter 1, and *al-Mashāriʿ wa-l-Muṭāraḥāt*, lithograph ed., mashraʿ 3, chapter 15.

[^V3-285]: *Hayākil al-Nūr*, 4, chapter 3.

[^V3-286]: The treatise *Āwāz-i Par-i Jibrāʾīl* (“The Sound of Gabriel’s Wing”). See Abū Rayyān, *Uṣūl al-Falsafa al-Ishrāqiyya*, p. 148.

[^V3-287]: The treatise *Muʾnis al-ʿUshshāq*. See ibid.

[^V3-288]: See ibid.

[^V3-289]: See *Plotinus apud Arabes*, ed. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān Badawī, Cairo, 1966, p. 148, where Plotinus says of this intellect that in relation to what lies below it, it is first, and in relation to what lies above it, it is second.

[^V3-290]: Ibn Sīnā, *al-Najāt*, p. 455.

[^V3-291]: Aristotle: 1072.

[^V3-292]: There seems to be some confusion here between Plotinus’s terminology and Aristotle’s. Plotinus’s First Intellect emanates, whereas Aristotle’s First Mover does not move; it moves beings by making them long for its perfection and beauty. —Publisher.

[^V3-293]: al-Suhrawardī, *Ḥikmat al-Ishrāq*, article 2, chapter 2.

[^V3-294]: Ibid., article 3, p. 261.

[^V3-295]: al-Suhrawardī calls the unit of “pure light” that governs the rational soul the *ispahbad*, a term of Persian origin. See Abū Rayyān, *Uṣūl al-Falsafa al-Ishrāqiyya*, p. 177.

[^V3-296]: al-Suhrawardī uses the term “mothers” differently from the Peripatetic philosophers. For the Peripatetics, the “mothers” are the four simple first elements—the *stoicheia*: earth, water, air, and fire—which Ibn Sīnā calls “the four first things.” See Ibn Sīnā, *al-Shifāʾ*, *On Generation and Corruption*, Cairo ed., 1969, ed. Maḥmūd Qāsim, p. 155.

[^V3-297]: al-Suhrawardī’s claim that there are many “intellects” is a response to the theory of the “ten intellects,” first formulated in Arab-Islamic philosophy by al-Fārābī[^V3-298] and then adopted by Ibn Sīnā. When al-Suhrawardī says that their number cannot be counted, he appeals to the Qurʾanic verse: “No one knows the hosts of your Lord except Him” (Qurʾan 74:31). For this classification, see al-Suhrawardī, *al-Mashāriʿ wa-l-Muṭāraḥāt*, mashraʿ 6, chapter 8.

[^V3-298]: al-Fārābī was the first to formulate the theory in Arab-Islamic philosophy, but the term “ten intellects” is Plotinian. —Publisher.

[^V3-299]: See the source just cited, and *Ḥikmat al-Ishrāq*, article 3, chapter 3.

[^V3-300]: We find no mention of this “suspended world” in al-Suhrawardī’s Illuminationist metaphysical system outside *Ḥikmat al-Ishrāq*, where he calls it the world of “suspended forms” and “suspended images.” From the passages dealing with it, we can conclude that it is a world of images lying between the world of intellectual lights and the sensible world. It is neither luminous nor dark, and it cannot be perceived by the outward senses. al-Suhrawardī claims to have discovered this world through his own experiences, which showed him that there are four worlds: dominant lights, governing lights, barriers, and suspended forms, both dark and luminous, in which the wicked suffer and the blessed experience pleasure. He describes this fourth world as immense and infinite. It seems to us that al-Suhrawardī needed to insert these “suspended forms” into his Illuminationist system as a connecting link between his two worlds, the luminous and the dark, because without this link his system could not explain the relationship between them. Yet the link itself also fails to explain its own existence or how it can combine the opposites of darkness and illumination. See *Ḥikmat al-Ishrāq*, pp. 512–515, 518.

[^V3-301]: Abū Rayyān, *Uṣūl al-Falsafa al-Ishrāqiyya*, p. 178.

[^V3-302]: Ibid., p. 79. Here “talisman” has the sense of a Platonic Form: the model for each species in the sensible world. The term “idol” is used in the same sense.

[^V3-303]: *Ḥikmat al-Ishrāq*, article 3, chapter 3.

[^V3-304]: This issue will be discussed in the next chapter.

[^V3-305]: al-Suhrawardī does not explain how this dark “matter” came to exist. In fact, he contradicts himself. On the one hand, he treats bodies as “matter”; on the other, he says they are deprived of the honor of the highest light. But if they lack this light, then they also lack the Illuminationist principle that brings things into existence. In that case, they should not exist at all. Yet he speaks of them as existing, even if their existence is secondary and accidental in relation to essence. So where did their existence come from?

[^V3-306]: al-Suhrawardī, *Hayākil al-Nūr*, pp. 28–29, 32.

[^V3-307]: The standard example of a complete definition in Aristotelian logic is the definition of a human being as “a rational animal.” “Animal” is the genus, because it includes all animal species. “Rational” is the differentia, because it is the feature that distinguishes humans from the other things sharing the genus “animal.”[^V3-308]

[^V3-308]: “Genus” and “species” belong to what is known as the Porphyrian Tree, a hierarchical classification. Aristotle also adds what later terminology calls the “proximate genus” and the specific difference. —Publisher.

[^V3-309]: al-Suhrawardī’s rejection of the rational path to knowledge appears most clearly in his main work on Illuminationist theory, *Ḥikmat al-Ishrāq*. In his other works, however, he combines the rational path used by Islamic philosophers with the Illuminationist path.

[^V3-310]: al-Suhrawardī, *Ḥikmat al-Ishrāq*, rule 389, where he says: “...Once you understand that every light that can be pointed to by the senses is an accidental light, like the light of the sun and stars, then every pure light cannot be pointed to, does not inhere in a body, and has no direction at all.” By “pure light,” he means “spiritual” light. By saying that it “cannot be pointed to,” he means that it is not like sensible things that can normally be indicated through the senses.

[^V3-311]: Ibid., article 4, chapter 6. In al-Suhrawardī’s terminology, the *ispahbad* is the rational soul, meaning the human soul, while the “talisman” or “idol” is a simple natural element such as earth, water, or air.

[^V3-312]: Ibid., article 8, chapter 10.

[^V3-313]: Ibid.

[^V3-314]: In al-Suhrawardī’s terminology, the “sages” are the Sufis and the people of Illumination, not the philosophers. He claimed to have had a dream in which he saw the author of the *Theology of Aristotle*, whom he took to be Aristotle, as Muslims of his time still commonly did. In the dream, he has Aristotle say that Sufis such as Abū Yazīd al-Bisṭāmī and Abū Muḥammad ibn Sahl al-Tustarī are the true philosophers. The text of this dream appears in *al-Talwīḥāt*, section 55 of the metaphysics.

[^V3-315]: This treatise is one of eight works written in a symbolic narrative style with mystical content.

[^V3-316]: See Abū Rayyān, *Uṣūl al-Falsafa al-Ishrāqiyya*, p. 300.

[^V3-317]: al-Suhrawardī, *Ḥikmat al-Ishrāq*, p. 557.

[^V3-318]: *Nawāsīt* is the plural of *nāsūt*, meaning human nature. The word is Syriac. It is contrasted with *lāhūt*, meaning divinity, from which comes the term “theology,” the study of religious doctrine.

[^V3-319]: *al-Talwīḥāt*: resource 24, first *talwīḥ* in the *Tanbīhāt*, p. 95. See Abū Rayyān, the work cited above, pp. 305–306.

[^V3-320]: This is the last of the intellects separate from matter in the Plotinian theory of emanation. It gives form to matter—the “Giver of Forms”—and also governs our world, what Plotinus calls the sublunary realm. —Publisher.

[^V3-321]: *Ḥikmat al-Ishrāq*, p. 19.

[^V3-322]: Ibid., p. 2.

[^V3-323]: Ibid., pp. 422–423, 427, 435.

[^V3-324]: Ibid., pp. 422–423, 427, 435.

[^V3-325]: Ibid., article 4, chapter 2.

[^V3-326]: *Uṣūl al-Falsafa al-Ishrāqiyya*, p. 19.

[^V3-327]: *Ḥikmat al-Ishrāq*, pp. 20–24.

[^V3-328]: See vol. 4, pp. 33–48.

[^V3-329]: See Abū Rayyān, the work cited above, p. 300.

[^V3-330]: On al-Suhrawardī’s trial, we can add another point that supports our interpretation of his position on prophecy. According to ʿImād al-Iṣfahānī’s account of al-Suhrawardī’s killing, the jurists who tried him accused him of stating in his writings that God was capable of creating a prophet after the Prophet Muḥammad. He did not deny the accusation. Instead, he limited his defense to proving that God had the power to do so. See Abū Rayyān, *Uṣūl al-Falsafa al-Ishrāqiyya*, pp. 17–18, and ʿImād al-Dīn al-Iṣfahānī, *al-Bustān al-Jāmiʿ li-Tawārīkh al-Zamān*, Claude Cahen ed., 1937–1938. If this account, found only in ʿImād al-Iṣfahānī, is correct, then it shows that al-Suhrawardī believed Muḥammad’s prophethood had come to an end. This position has another social and ideological meaning, because saying that Muḥammad’s prophethood had ended is one form of rejecting the ideological system on which the social and political order of the caliphate rested.

[^V3-331]: al-Suhrawardī explains the matter by saying that “anything whose existence depends on something else exists whenever that other thing exists.” He then applies this to the relation between God and the world: “The world is an effect of God, and God is eternal; therefore the world is eternal.” We have already stated that this follows from the impossibility of an effect lagging behind its cause. For that reason, “there can be no before or after in relation to the Light of Lights and what proceeds from it.” See *Ḥikmat al-Ishrāq*, article 3, chapters 1 and 3.

[^V3-332]: Ibid. See also Abū Rayyān, *Uṣūl al-Falsafa al-Ishrāqiyya*, pp. 246–248.

[^V3-333]: We should note that al-Suhrawardī recognizes three “elements,” not four as in Aristotelian thought: earth, water, and air. Fire, in his system, is not an earthly element but belongs to the luminous world. Its “luminous” status is comparable to that of the soul, and he treats both as representatives of the higher light in the earthly world. The soul is the “greater representative,” while fire is the “lesser representative”: “God has a representative in every world.” Through the soul and fire, “the two representations, lesser and greater, are completed. This is why the Persians were once commanded to turn toward it”—meaning fire. See *Ḥikmat al-Ishrāq*, pp. 422–423, 427–435.

[^V3-334]: *Ḥikmat al-Ishrāq*, article 3, chapter 1. al-Suhrawardī also argues for this thesis through the law of causality. See footnote 58.

[^V3-335]: See, for example, Qadrī Ḥāfiẓ Ṭūqān, *al-ʿUlūm ʿinda al-ʿArab*, Cairo, 1956, pp. 31–66.

[^V3-336]: *Ḥikmat al-Ishrāq*, article 3, chapter 1.

[^V3-337]: Ibid.

[^V3-338]: Ibid., article 4, chapter 2.

[^V3-339]: See pp. 144–146 of this volume.

[^V3-340]: al-Suhrawardī, *Hayākil al-Nūr*, p. 70.

[^V3-341]: See p. 145 of this volume.

[^V3-342]: He is Abū Bakr Muḥammad ibn ʿArabī al-Ṭāʾī, named after the Arab tribe of Ṭayyiʾ. He was born in Murcia in al-Andalus in 560 AH/1164 CE. He lived there for about thirty-eight years and spent the rest of his life in the East. He died in Damascus in 638 AH/1240 CE. He was given the titles Muḥyī al-Dīn and “Sultan of the Knowers” because of his standing in Sufism and mystical knowledge. In the East he wrote some of the Sufi works in which he developed the idea of the “unity of being,” especially *al-Futūḥāt al-Makkiyya* and *Fuṣūṣ al-Ḥikam*. He and Ibn Rushd lived at the same time and met in Córdoba. Ibn ʿArabī wrote a fascinating account of the meeting in *al-Futūḥāt al-Makkiyya*, vol. 1, pp. 153–154, Cairo ed., 1329 AH.

[^V3-343]: One example of this rejection can be found in al-Sarrāj al-Ṭūsī, a fourth-century AH Sufi, who works hard to interpret every Sufi statement that seems to contain the idea of the “unity of being” in a way that removes that meaning. Examples include his interpretation of a statement on divine unity by Abū Bakr al-Shiblī (d. 334 AH/945 CE), and another statement on the same subject by Ruwaym al-Baghdādī (d. 303 AH/915 CE). See al-Ṭūsī, *al-Lumaʿ*, Cairo ed., 1960, pp. 50–51. Another example comes from al-Hujwīrī in the fifth AH/eleventh century, who describes Sufi *fanāʾ* as the annihilation of the servant’s will in God’s will, not the annihilation of the servant’s existence in God’s existence. See al-Hujwīrī, *Kashf al-Maḥjūb*, Nicholson’s English translation from Persian, Leiden, 1911, p. 245. al-Hujwīrī’s words clearly reject the idea of the unity of being.

[^V3-344]: In the *Risālat al-Aḥadiyya* attributed to Ibn ʿArabī, he says of knowing God: “...You must know Him not through knowledge, not through action, not through understanding, not through conception, not through sensation, not through perception...” See *Three Muslim Sages*, pp. 140–208.

[^V3-345]: See *Anti-Dühring*, p. 42, Russian ed.

[^V3-346]: The concepts of the Universal Intellect and the Idea are expressions of objective idealism. —Publisher.

[^V3-347]: See *Dialectical Materialism*, by “a group of Soviet professors,” trans. Badr al-Dīn al-Sibāʿī and a colleague, Dār al-Jamāhīr, Damascus, n.d., p. 120. In this source and the one cited above, see Engels’s discussion of these two ideas of the unity of being, or unity of the world, and his demonstration of their errors.

[^V3-348]: *Risālat al-Aḥadiyya* is also known as *Kitāb al-Ajwiba* and *Kitāb al-Alif*. Its Arabic original had not yet been published. A French translation by “Abd al-Hadi” appeared in *Le Voile d’Iris*, 34:15–17, January 1933, and 34:55–72, February 1933. An English translation by T. H. Weir appeared in the *Journal of the Royal Asiatic Society*. See Seyyed Hossein Nasr, *Three Muslim Sages*, p. 208, n. 56.

[^V3-349]: This refers to the Qurʾanic verse: “...When I have fashioned him and breathed into him of My spirit...” (Qurʾan 15:29 and 38:72).

[^V3-350]: Qurʾan 11:123.

[^V3-351]: Ibn ʿArabī, *Fuṣūṣ al-Ḥikam*, Abū al-ʿAlā ʿAfīfī ed., Cairo, 1946, ch. 1, “A Divine Wisdom in an Adamic Word.”

[^V3-352]: The word “Adamic” in the title refers to Adam, whom the monotheistic religions call the father of humanity. Ibn ʿArabī, however, uses it to mean humanity as such; in the text, “Adam” refers to the human species, not simply to the individual Adam. The use of “word” may be influenced by the biblical idea “In the beginning was the Word,” where “word” expresses the will to bring something into being. The same idea appears in the Qurʾanic verse: “When He decrees a matter, He only says to it, ‘Be,’ and it is” (Qurʾan 2:117). Ibn ʿArabī’s use of “word” may also point to his idea that the world exists in two stages, as in the passage above: first as “an unpolished mirror,” then as a mirror that “required polishing.” If this is what he means, the title compares the world’s coming from God to a spoken word coming from a human speaker. A word also appears in two stages: first in an unclear form in the speaker’s mind, then openly in speech. In the same way, the world comes from God first as “a shape without spirit,” then as the open “manifestation” of the “most holy emanation.” See the passage above.

[^V3-353]: Ibn ʿArabī, ibid. See the previous page. All the phrases placed in quotation marks in this explanation are from the same source.

[^V3-354]: For Ibn ʿArabī, the term “greater human” means the whole world. When he uses “small world” or “smaller world,” he means the human being. These terms were used before Ibn ʿArabī, from at least the fourth AH/tenth century onward. We find them, for example, in the encyclopedia of the Brethren of Purity, *Rasāʾil Ikhwān al-Ṣafāʾ*, vol. 2, p. 213, and *al-Risāla al-Jāmiʿa*, vol. 1, pp. 582–587, vol. 2, pp. 26–27. But the Brethren used the terms differently: they meant that the structure of the world resembles the structure of the complete human body.

[^V3-355]: For Ibn ʿArabī, existence in itself is mercy. Everything that exists in the world exists because “the flow of divine existence through it is a mercy from God to it.” He uses “mercy” in this sense in *Shajarat al-Kawn*.

[^V3-356]: Chapter 27 of *Fuṣūṣ al-Ḥikam* is titled “A Singular Wisdom in a Muhammadan Word.” Ibn ʿArabī begins by explaining what makes this wisdom “singular” in relation to the “Muhammadan Word”: “His wisdom—that is, Muḥammad’s—was singular because he was the most perfect being in this human species. For this reason, the matter began with him and ended with him...”

[^V3-357]: This sentence comes directly after the words, “For this reason, the matter began with him and ended with him.” Together, the two statements indicate that the “Muhammadan Word” is the first existent being—that is, the first divine realization within the human species. We have already seen that the human being is the foundation of existence and the beginning of the link between God and the world. Muḥammad, then, is the first existent being in an absolute sense. Similar ideas appear in some Shiʿi sects in relation to Muḥammad, ʿAlī, and his family, as in the Tradition of the Cloak.

[^V3-358]: Ibid.

[^V3-359]: *Fuṣūṣ al-Ḥikam*, ch. 1, “A Divine Wisdom in an Adamic Word.”

[^V3-360]: See p. 289 ff. below.

[^V3-361]: He is ʿAbd al-Karīm ibn Ibrāhīm al-Jīlī (d. 832 AH/1428 CE).

[^V3-363]: He is Muḥammad ibn Ibrāhīm al-Shīrāzī, known as Mullā Ṣadrā and Ṣadr al-Mutaʾallihīn (979 or 980–1050 AH/1573–1640 CE).

[^V3-364]: al-Shīrāzī, *al-Asfār al-Arbaʿa*, Tehran ed., 1387 AH, p. 300.

[^V3-365]: See al-Shīrāzī, *Shawāhid al-Rubūbiyya*. See also Hādī al-ʿAlawī, *Naẓariyyat al-Ḥaraka al-Jawhariyya ʿinda al-Shīrāzī*, p. 40.

[^V3-366]: Ibid.

[^V3-367]: Hādī al-ʿAlawī, ibid.

[^V3-368]: Ibid.

[^V3-369]: See ibid., p. 60.

[^V3-371]: See Shaykh ʿAbd Allāh Niʿma, *Falāsifat al-Shīʿa*, Beirut, n.d., p. 358.

[^V3-372]: From Abū al-ʿAlā ʿAfīfī’s notes on Ibn ʿArabī’s *Fuṣūṣ al-Ḥikam*, note 6 on ch. 1, “A Divine Wisdom in an Adamic Word.”

[^V3-373]: Seyyed Hossein Nasr, *Three Muslim Sages*, p. 141.

[^V3-374]: A clear picture of this reciprocal relationship between God and the human being appears in *Fuṣūṣ al-Ḥikam*, p. 104, where Ibn ʿArabī speaks of the purity of God’s shadow in the human being: “...This is a light extending from a shadow, and it is the very substance of glass. It is therefore a luminous shadow because of its purity. In the same way, in one of us who realizes the Truth [God], the form of the Truth appears more fully than it does in others. Among us are those for whom the Truth becomes his hearing, his sight, all his powers, and all his limbs, according to signs given by the revealed law that speaks of the Truth.”

[^V3-375]: ʿAbd al-Karīm ibn Ibrāhīm al-Jīlānī, or al-Jīlī (d. 832 AH/1428 CE).

[^V3-376]: As an example of what al-Jīlī says about the “Perfect Human,” we may cite the following: “...He—that is, the Perfect Human—is a copy of the Truth (...) God Most High is living, knowing, powerful, hearing, seeing, and speaking. The human being too is living, knowing, powerful, hearing, seeing, and speaking...” See al-Jīlī, *al-Insān al-Kāmil fī Maʿrifat al-Awākhir wa-l-Awāʾil*, Egypt ed., 1293 AH, vol. 2, p. 28.

[^V3-377]: In the first chapter of *Fuṣūṣ al-Ḥikam*, where Ibn ʿArabī lays out the foundations of the idea of the unity of being, he explains the status of the angels and the superiority of the human being over them. He says, for example: “The angels did not grasp what the formation of this vicegerent—that is, the human being—contained, nor did they grasp what the presence of the Truth requires in terms of worship rooted in the self. No one knows of the Truth except what his own nature allows him to know...” The angels do not possess “Adam’s comprehensiveness,” meaning the way the qualities of the whole cosmos come together in the human being.

[^V3-378]: In the history of Islamic sects and schools, we find figures such as Abū al-Khaṭṭāb Muḥammad ibn Abī Zaynab, whose followers first claimed that he was an “Imam” and then that he was a “prophet”; another man called Bazīgh; and a third called al-Sarī. The Manṣūriyya made similar claims for Abū Manṣūr al-ʿIjlī, an illiterate man. One branch of the Khaṭṭābiyya even took a man called ʿAmr as a god and worshipped him, while also believing in transmigration of souls, meaning their endless movement from one body to another. Examples of such sects are too numerous to count. See al-Nawbakhtī, *Firaq al-Shīʿa*, p. 38 ff., Istanbul ed., 1931; and al-Ashʿarī, *Maqālāt al-Islāmiyyīn*, vol. 1, p. 74 ff., ed. Muḥammad Muḥyī al-Dīn ʿAbd al-Ḥamīd, 1969.

[^V3-379]: Engels, *Ludwig Feuerbach and the End of Classical German Philosophy*, in Marx and Engels, *Selected Works*, Progress Publishers, Moscow, 1970, Arabic ed., vol. 4, p. 64.

[^V3-380]: Adam Mez, *Die Renaissance des Islams*, vol. 2, pp. 36–37.

[^V3-381]: Ibid., p. 40.

[^V3-432]: This is Jamāl al-Dīn al-Qifṭī (d. 646 AH/1248 CE), *Akhbār al-ʿUlamāʾ bi-Akhbār al-Ḥukamāʾ*, Egypt ed., 1326 AH, p. 80 ff. al-Qifṭī says of the Brethren of Purity: “They were a group who came together to compile a book covering the different branches of the ancient wisdom and arranged it as a series of treatises (...) Because its authors concealed their names, people disagreed over who had written it, and each group offered a guess or conjecture. Some said it was the work of certain Imams descended from ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib, may God honor him, though they disagreed over which Imam had written it and reached no certain conclusion. Others said it was composed by some of the early Muʿtazilite theologians...”

[^V3-433]: Abū Ḥayyān al-Tawḥīdī (d. ca. 414 AH/1023 CE), *al-Imtāʿ wa-l-Muʾānasa*, Egypt ed., 1953, vol. 2, pp. 3–4.

[^V3-434]: In medieval Arabic usage, a *warrāq* was a copyist of books. He performed much the same function that the printing press would later perform.

[^V3-436]: al-Shahrazūrī and al-Bayhaqī give slightly different versions of these names. See de Boer, *The History of Philosophy in Islam*, p. 125, n. 2.

[^V3-437]: Abū Ḥayyān al-Tawḥīdī made this statement while answering a question from Ibn Saʿdān, the vizier of Ṣamṣām al-Dawla al-Buwayhī. The vizier had asked him about Zayd ibn Rifāʿa. From the question, we understand that Zayd had some connection with the vizier, attended his gatherings, and discussed many matters of learning and knowledge with him, but the vizier did not know what school or doctrine Zayd followed. The dialogue that Abū Ḥayyān records here is important for our subject. It shows how much uncertainty surrounded Zayd ibn Rifāʿa even among the educated people of his own time, and the vizier Ibn Saʿdān was one of them. Abū Ḥayyān’s discussion of the Brethren of Purity also shows that these educated contemporaries knew little about the group itself, even during the period when it is said to have appeared.

[^V3-494]: In the introduction to the *Rasāʾil*, the Brethren say that there are fifty-two treatises, but only fifty-one are known. Jabbūr ʿAbd al-Nūr (*Ikhwān al-Ṣafāʾ*, p. 51) suggests that copyists may have merged two treatises in the fourth section, the section on divine law and religious legislation. The Brethren say that this section contains eleven treatises, but only ten survive, while the three earlier sections contain exactly the number they specify.

[^V3-497]: In interpreting “the divine ones” as “sages and philosophers,” we rely on the Brethren of Purity themselves. For example, in *Rasāʾil*, vol. 4, pp. 301–302, they speak of “divine philosophical worship.” In their view, true “divinity” means philosophical wisdom, and the true “divine person” is the philosophical sage.

[^V3-498]: We have already explained the historical meaning of the word *ṣanāʾiʿ* on p. 288, footnote 53. We can add that in the terminology of the Brethren of Purity, people of the *ṣanāʾiʿ* are workers who produce socially useful goods. The phrase does not mean owners of the means of production, such as land, workshops, and other productive tools.

[^V3-499]: *Malakiyya* here comes from *malāʾika*, “angels.” The Brethren of Purity believed that the rank of angels is the highest level that righteous human souls can reach. Since they saw it as an “evolutionary” development of righteous human beings, it corresponds to the highest stage in their ascending “development” of living beings: from the vegetative faculty, to the rational faculty, and then to the angelic. See *Rasāʾil*, vol. 2, p. 172. They also connect nature with the angels. In their thought, angels are in fact an expression of the nature present in the Universal Soul. See *Rasāʾil*, vol. 2, pp. 55, 108–109.

[^V3-500]: *Rasāʾil*, vol. 4, pp. 220–221.

[^V3-501]: *al-Risāla al-Jāmiʿa*, vol. 1, p. 66.

[^V3-503]: Ibid.

[^V3-504]: Ibid.

[^V3-505]: *Rasāʾil*, vol. 2, pp. 110–111.

[^V3-506]: In the Brethren’s treatise on “Definitions and Descriptions,” they define active nature as “a power of the Universal Celestial Soul flowing through the elements.” In their terminology, the “elements” are the primary elements of matter. See *Rasāʾil*, vol. 3, p. 363, section on psychology and intellect. Nature here is being defined as an abstract category.

[^V3-507]: *Rasāʾil*, vol. 4, p. 184. When the Brethren assign certain beliefs to the founder of the religious law and derive those beliefs from their own philosophical principles, this strongly suggests that the lawgiver they have in mind is not the Islamic Prophet, and that the “law” is not Islamic law. Rather, they seem to be trying to formulate a law of their own for the “State of the People of Good,” the state whose plan they outline in their encyclopedic *Rasāʾil*. We draw attention to this because it opens up several lines of inquiry. Our observation is strengthened by the fact that they also list twelve qualities that, in their view, the “founder of the law” must possess. All twelve are physical, moral, or intellectual qualities that could exist in any human being given healthy conditions of life. See *Rasāʾil*, vol. 4, pp. 172–173.

[^V3-508]: The wording speaks of “each existent thing,” which makes it seem as though the statement is not specifically about human beings. But the rest of the passage makes clear that it means an “existent” that acts consciously, voluntarily, and according to judgment. This applies only to the human being.

[^V3-522]: It is only fair to mention here the modern Arab scholar Jabbūr ʿAbd al-Nūr of Lebanon. He managed to see through this ambiguity in the Brethren’s texts and identify their real position in their discussion of the influence of the planets and celestial spheres on the material world. He writes: “At times they do not fail to say, by way of concealment and precaution, that God determines the fate of universals and particulars. Yet their real opinion is clear and can be seen by any reader in most of the *Rasāʾil*. They believe that the planets are the direct cause of natural generation, the appearance of matter and form, their development into mineral, plant, animal, and human forms, and the emergence of individuality within species.” See Dr. Jabbūr ʿAbd al-Nūr, *Ikhwān al-Ṣafāʾ*, Dār al-Maʿārif, Egypt, 1954, p. 28.

[^V3-523]: See footnotes 1 and 2 on p. 303.

[^V3-524]: *al-Risāla al-Jāmiʿa*, vol. 1, p. 302.

[^V3-525]: Here we mean the non-extremist Shiʿa, specifically the Imamis, especially the Jaʿfaris, or Twelvers.

[^V3-526]: First treatise on opinions and religions, vol. 4, p. 20.

[^V3-527]: Ibid. This appears in their classification of people into those who approach God through His prophets and messengers and through their Imams and successors, and those who “know God with true knowledge and therefore use no one else as an intermediary to Him.” This is the rank of the people of knowledge, who are God’s saints. These “saints,” then, do not use the prophets as a means of reaching God. They reach Him directly, without prophetic mediation. But they do not reach Him through “vision” or “Sufi unveiling.” They do so through a kind of knowledge that is not “gnostic” in the Sufi sense.

[^V3-528]: Ibid., p. 50.

[^V3-529]: *Jibilla* means nature or innate disposition. “The *jibilla* of souls” means their nature and inborn character.

[^V3-530]: See the fourth treatise in the section on revealed and religious sciences, *Rasāʾil*, vol. 4, p. 107.

[^V3-531]: The seventh treatise on crafts, mathematical section, vol. 1, p. 207.

[^V3-532]: de Boer, *The History of Philosophy in Islam*, p. 127.

[^V3-533]: This hierarchy of the human path to knowledge is close to Hegel’s three-stage account of coming to know the truth. First comes a primitive clarity gained through the senses. Then comes correct knowledge of a thing or proposition, such as knowing that 2 × 2 = 4; this is higher than the first stage. Finally comes the level at which knowledge uncovers a general law of nature or society, such as the law of gravity. This means that the Brethren of Purity—or science and philosophy together in their time—had an idea close to what we now regard as true: that human beings discover truth gradually, moving from simple and primitive knowledge to complex knowledge whose correctness can be established, and then to objective truth itself as it appears in the form of a law.

[^V3-534]: *Rasāʾil*, vol. 2, pp. 334–335.

[^V3-535]: Ibid., vol. 2, p. 203; vol. 3, pp. 64–65; and *al-Risāla al-Jāmiʿa*, vol. 1, p. 595.

[^V3-537]: A full description of the functions of the three groups of faculties—natural, sensory, and “spiritual” or intellectual—can be found in *al-Risāla al-Jāmiʿa*, vol. 1, pp. 594–602.

[^V3-538]: *Rasāʾil*, vol. 2, pp. 325–326.

[^V3-539]: Seventh treatise of the mathematical section, *Rasāʾil*, vol. 1, p. 195.

[^V3-540]: Tenth treatise of the physical and natural sciences, *Rasāʾil*, vol. 2, pp. 349–351.

[^V3-541]: This idea found among the Brethren, as among other Arab philosophers and scientists of their time, is one of the ideas that modern science has partly confirmed. One example is the discovery of the gray matter of the human brain, or cerebral cortex, which is now considered one of the most important organic instruments of knowledge.

[^V3-542]: *Rasāʾil*, vol. 2, p. 347.

[^V3-543]: See *Rasāʾil*, vol. 3, p. 195, and *al-Risāla al-Jāmiʿa*, vol. 1, pp. 603, 595.

[^V3-544]: *Firāsa* means discovering what is hidden by observing what is outward.

[^V3-545]: *Zajr* means driving off or startling a bird. If it flies to the right, the observer takes it as a good sign; if it flies to the left, as a bad sign. *Zajr* is therefore a form of divination about future events.

[^V3-546]: See *Rasāʾil*, vol. 3, pp. 240–241.

[^V3-547]: See *Rasāʾil*, vol. 1, p. 105. In this passage, however, the Brethren exempt “thoughts, revelation, and inspiration” from the need for evidence. They say that knowledge obtained through these does not come through acquired learning but is a gift from God. Yet in the earlier passage they made thoughts, revelation, and inspiration subject to the activity of the thinking faculty, which acquires its information from the sensible world rather than receiving it as a gift from God. Is this contradiction in the first volume of the *Rasāʾil* deliberate concealment and *taqiyya*—a kind of “tactic”? Or is it one of the contradictions that any thinker is bound to fall into when one foot stands on the ground of philosophical idealism and the other on the ground of philosophical materialism, whether consciously or spontaneously?

[^V3-548]: See *Rasāʾil*, vol. 1, p. 323.

[^V3-549]: First treatise in the section on opinions and religions, *Rasāʾil*, vol. 4, pp. 48–49.

[^V3-550]: See Qadrī Ḥāfiẓ Ṭūqān, *al-ʿUlūm ʿinda al-ʿArab*, pp. 84–85.

[^V3-551]: See *Rasāʾil*, vol. 1, pp. 198, 211, 317.

[^V3-552]: *Rasāʾil*, vol. 4, p. 77.

[^V3-553]: First treatise on opinions and religions, *Rasāʾil*, vol. 4, p. 48.

[^V3-554]: Everything said here about “examples” and “counterparts” is quoted from *Jāmiʿat al-Jāmiʿa* of the *Rasāʾil*, ch. 6, p. 76. The final phrase about “the human form, which is the perfection of natural bodies and sensible individuals,” clearly has an Aristotelian background. When this Aristotelian element is joined to the wider argument of the Brethren in this passage, the Platonic and Aristotelian elements merge into a new theoretical form independent of both Plato and Aristotle. This fits the general philosophical direction of the Brethren’s encyclopedia: a new form of objective idealism.

[^V3-555]: It may be said that the Brethren of Purity also looked down on the material world. They speak of “purifying” the soul of bodily needs and desires so that after death it will deserve to join the Universal Soul, where its paradise and eternal happiness lie; this is how they interpret paradise. But despising matter in this sense is very different from denying that the material world exists. The Stoics, for example, also looked down on the material world while at the same time recognizing its objective existence.

[^V3-556]: de Boer, *The History of Philosophy in Islam*, p. 128. See also Henry Corbin, *History of Islamic Philosophy*, Arabic trans. Naṣīr Muruwwa and Ḥasan Qubaysī, p. 211. Among Arabic studies, see ʿAbd al-Raḥmān Badawī, *Rūḥ al-Ḥaḍāra al-ʿArabiyya*, p. 14, on the Brethren’s interest in the mysteries of numbers; and Ḥannā al-Fākhūrī and Khalīl al-Jurr, *Tārīkh al-Falsafa al-ʿArabiyya*, p. 239, on their reliance on number, “reducing everything to it and explaining everything through it.”

[^V3-557]: Arab philosophers used the term “separate” to mean separate from matter. —Publisher.

[^V3-558]: Aristotle, *Physics*, 203, Arabic trans. Ḥunayn ibn Isḥāq, ed. Badawī, vol. 1, p. 203.

[^V3-559]: ʿAbd al-Raḥmān Badawī, *Arisṭū*, Cairo, 1964, p. 102.

[^V3-560]: Ibid., p. 110.

[^V3-561]: First treatise on number, the first treatise of the mathematical section, *Rasāʾil*, vol. 1, pp. 27–29.

[^V3-563]: Ibid., from the same passage.

[^V3-564]: Ibid., same passage.

[^V3-566]: *Rasāʾil*.

[^V3-568]: Sixth treatise of the mathematical section, *Rasāʾil*, vol. 1, pp. 189–192.

[^V3-569]: See Plotinus, *Fourth Ennead*, treatise 3, where he discusses unavoidable necessity in relation to the soul, section 13, Arabic ed., trans. Fuʾād Zakariyyā, pp. 195–196.

[^V3-570]: “Metaphysical” here means the opposite of “dialectical.”

[^V3-571]: The *Rasāʾil* contain more than one example of this advanced scientific tendency: the recognition that quantitative changes can have qualitative effects. One example appears in *al-Risāla al-Jāmiʿa*, vol. 1, p. 301, where the Brethren list the contents of the third treatise on the soul. Among other things, it discusses “the mothers of generated beings from which all existing things—minerals, plants, and animals—come into being, and how some change into others as their qualities differ, the numerical amounts of their quantities change, and their structures vary...” We should pay close attention to the phrase “change...as their qualities and numerical quantities differ.” Here “change” means transformation: a transformation in the qualities of beings, indeed “all existing things,” as the passage itself says.

[^V3-572]: In the laws of Marxist materialist dialectics, the unity and mutual relationship of quantity and quality is revealed as a principle of change. This is the revolutionary side of the Brethren of Purity’s thought.

[^V3-573]: In Arabic, *kinn* means something that shelters and protects. —Publisher.

[^V3-574]: From the third treatise of the mathematical section, on astronomy, *Rasāʾil*, vol. 1, pp. 108–109.

[^V3-575]: One example comes directly after the passage quoted in the main text: “There is another benefit as well: whenever people know events before they happen, they can ward them off before they occur through prayer and supplication to God...” Even these words point in the same direction: the Brethren reject a fatalistic and deterministic meaning of “necessity.”

[^V3-576]: The fact that Arab encyclopedists of this period grasped the law of necessity, even in an early form, confirms the Marxist thesis that practical necessity drives human beings toward knowledge—in other words, their basic material needs. This differs from pre-Marxist philosophies, which explained the drive to knowledge through wonder and amazement at nature. If Arab scientists of the tenth century understood this law, it was because they were connected with the movement of social reality and with the developing production of material necessities. From this they learned how society could protect itself against some of the necessities imposed by natural laws.

[^V3-577]: For examples of this progress, see, among others, the English Orientalist Rom Landau, *Islam and the Arabs*, Beirut, 1962, trans. Munīr al-Baʿlabakkī, pp. 245–249 on mathematics and pp. 258–281 on the natural sciences, chemistry, and medicine.

[^V3-578]: In the treatise on “Definitions and Descriptions,” where the Brethren define the technical terms used in the *Rasāʾil*, they define an “existent” as “that which one of the senses finds, or the intellect conceives, or evidence points to.” See the tenth treatise of the section on psychology and intellect, *Rasāʾil*, vol. 3, p. 35. This definition might seem to deny the objective existence of the world, because it defines what “exists” in terms of how it is known: through sensation, intellectual conception, or evidence. That would mean either that the Brethren fell into a contradiction or that they did not mean what we have taken the definition to mean. But two points resolve the difficulty. First, the textual evidence supporting our conclusion is explicit. Second, the definition itself says “that which one of the senses finds.” This wording suggests that the “existent” exists objectively before knowledge and that the three ways of knowing merely reveal its existence rather than creating it.

[^V3-579]: See pp. 321–322 of this volume.

[^V3-580]: See our earlier discussion of the class position of the Brethren of Purity.

[^V3-581]: Fifth treatise of the physical and natural sciences, *Rasāʾil*, vol. 2, pp. 110–111.

[^V3-582]: Seventh treatise of the mathematical section, *Rasāʾil*, vol. 1, p. 196.

[^V3-583]: The Brethren of Purity hold that the Universal Soul moves prime matter in length, breadth, and depth, producing absolute body. From this absolute body are formed the world of the celestial spheres and planets and all four elements, and so on. See *Rasāʾil*, vol. 4, p. 4. This means that the world of the celestial spheres is material just like the world of the earthly elements.

[^V3-584]: Ibid.

[^V3-585]: A “subtle” body is one that is not coarse or dense.

[^V3-586]: *Rasāʾil*, vol. 3, p. 331.

[^V3-587]: *al-Risāla al-Jāmiʿa*, vol. 1, p. 279.

[^V3-588]: *al-Risāla al-Jāmiʿa*, vol. 1, p. 280.

[^V3-589]: Ibid.

[^V3-591]: Tenth treatise, section on psychology and intellect, vol. 3, p. 359.

[^V3-592]: We should say here that natural science remained unable to explain the world through nature itself until the second half of the eighteenth century. This is why Engels, in the introduction to *Dialectics of Nature*, complains about the inability of scientists even at this relatively advanced stage, despite the large body of knowledge they had accumulated. He says that science was still deeply immersed in theology and searched everywhere for a “first principle,” only to find it in an external impulse that could not be explained from nature itself. Russian ed.

[^V3-593]: One example of what we can now call an absolute truth is the physical proposition that all forms of motion can be transformed into one another. Many ages passed between the first human discovery of the seed of this proposition as a relative truth and its later scientific form. Human beings first discovered that rubbing two pieces of wood together could produce fire. Much later, this discovery was expressed theoretically as the claim that friction, a form of mechanical motion, can be transformed into heat. On this basis, and with the later development of technology, science eventually arrived at the general law that all forms of the motion of matter can transform into one another—an absolute truth. The same applies to all human knowledge whose correctness has been established through scientific experiment.

[^V3-594]: Eighth treatise of the section on psychology and intellect, vol. 3, pp. 314–316.

[^V3-595]: Ibid. Here the “Great Resurrection” means the end of the world, while the “Small Resurrection” means the death of an individual, following the saying attributed to the Prophet: “Whoever dies, his resurrection has begun.” Since “the world is a great human being” and “the human being is a small world,” as the Brethren put it, they called the death of the world the “Great Resurrection” by analogy with the “Small Resurrection,” the death of the individual. In the passage just cited, they imagine the Great Resurrection as the separation of the Universal Soul from the Universal Body, or absolute body.

[^V3-596]: Ibid.

[^V3-597]: See Aristotle, *Physics*, 8, 257b.

[^V3-598]: Aristotle’s “First Mover” does not mean God as portrayed by the religions. Aristotle’s mover is not a creator from nothing, nor does it move nothingness. It moves a world that is as eternal as the First Mover itself. —Publisher.

[^V3-599]: See Aristotle, ibid.

[^V3-600]: *Rasāʾil*, vol. 3, p. 332.

[^V3-601]: *Rasāʾil*, vol. 4, p. 11. We should note that although the Brethren clearly distinguish *ikhtirāʿ* and *ibdāʿ* from *khalq*, “creation,” they do not use the three terms consistently. Their usage often breaks the distinction they themselves draw. They use “creation” and “origination” in the same context when speaking of the world and existing things. See the ninth treatise of the section on psychology and intellect, *Rasāʾil*, vol. 3, pp. 339–340.

[^V3-602]: Aristotle, *Physics*, 1029a, 19.

[^V3-603]: *Ghayriyya* means that each of two things is other than the other. Its opposite is *ʿayniyya*, the identity of each thing with the other.

[^V3-604]: The Brethren’s idea that the intellectual form of things exists before their material existence, as we understand this passage, fits what Engels says in *Dialectics of Nature*: as civilization advanced, people increasingly came to see the products of civilization as primarily the fruits of the mind. The role of the working hand disappeared from view, and all the achievements of civilization were credited to the activity and ideas of the brain. See *Dialectics of Nature*, Arabic ed., Damascus, 1970, pp. 246–247.

[^V3-605]: Tenth treatise of the mathematical section, *Rasāʾil*, vol. 1, p. 316.

[^V3-606]: This connection can be seen clearly in an earlier passage beginning: “...Know that a long age passed over the soul before it became attached to the body...”

[^V3-607]: This connection also appears in their comparison of the relation between the world and God to the relation between light and the body of the sun—light is not a part of the sun but proceeds from it—or to the relation between speech and the speaker. See the eighth treatise of the section on psychology and intellect, *Rasāʾil*, vol. 3, pp. 318–319.

[^V3-608]: See Abū al-ʿAlā ʿAfīfī, “From Where Did Ibn ʿArabī Draw His Sufi Philosophy?”, *Journal of the Faculty of Arts, Egyptian University*, May 1933.

[^V3-609]: By Aristotle’s contradiction here, we mean that he speaks of an unmoved First Mover while also affirming the eternity of motion. These claims appear to conflict. An unmoved First Mover means a mover that is fixed and motionless and is not preceded by motion. But if motion is eternal, then motion precedes it. How, then, can motion be eternal while the First Mover is not preceded by motion?

    (*) Aristotle’s problem arose from his view that the whole universe is a chain of moving things and movers, while a mover must itself be moving in order to move something else. He found no solution to this endless chain except to posit an “intellectual necessity” for a First Mover that does not move, with no temporal gap between it and motion. —Publisher.

[^V3-610]: G. Vajda, “The Caliph Yūsuf ibn Ṣiddīq and His Theology,” *Journal of Manuscripts on the Doctrinal and Literary History of the Middle Ages*, Paris, 1949, pp. 96–97.

[^V3-611]: In the history of philosophy, the ancient materialists, and all materialists before Marx and Engels, recognized motion as the main property of matter. But in general they reduced every form of motion to a single kind: the mechanical movement of bodies through space, or spatial motion—what Arabic philosophical texts call “motion by transfer.” Because their understanding of motion was so limited, they repeatedly explained motion as the result of an external push. Yet spatial motion is only the lowest and simplest form. From it, motion rises through higher forms: thermal motion, chemical motion, organic living motion, psychological motion, and finally social motion, the highest of these forms. Mechanical motion exists within every one of these higher forms, but the reverse is not true.

[^V3-612]: See the French philosopher Dugat, *Histoire des philosophes et des théologiens musulmans*, Paris, 1878, p. 159.

[^V3-613]: Bandali Jawzi, *Min Tārīkh al-Ḥarakāt al-Fikriyya fī al-Islām*, Dār al-Rawāʾiʿ ed., Beirut, n.d., p. 150.

[^V3-614]: ʿAbd al-Raḥmān Badawī, *Rūḥ al-Ḥaḍāra al-ʿArabiyya*, Beirut, 1949, p. 114.

[^V3-615]: Fourth treatise of the section on revealed and religious sciences, *Rasāʾil*, vol. 4, p. 105.

[^V3-616]: Third treatise of the mathematical section, on astronomy, *Rasāʾil*, vol. 1, p. 105.

[^V3-617]: *Rasāʾil*, vol. 3, p. 360.

[^V3-620]: On this point, see Aristotle’s *Metaphysics*: A, 1, 1199.

[^V3-624]: al-Manṣūr was the second Abbasid caliph, ruling from 136 to 158 AH/753–774 CE. He founded Baghdad in 145 AH/762 CE.

[^V3-625]: See Dr. ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz al-Dūrī, *Muqaddima fī al-Tārīkh al-Iqtiṣādī al-ʿArabī*, p. 59.

[^V3-629]: On Arab scientific achievements, see the following: Adam Mez, *Die Renaissance des Islams*, vol. 2, pp. 1–11, 298–343, 344–362, 363–440; Claude Cahen, *Histoire des peuples musulmans*, pp. 329–336; de Boer, *The History of Philosophy in Islam*, pp. 110–115; Rom Landau, *Islam and the Arabs*, pp. 245–287. One of the best studies of the connection between the development of science among the Arabs and production and social needs is an article by Professor N. Bammate, head of the Division of Philosophical and Humanistic Studies at the University of Paris, titled “The Status of Science and Technology in Islamic Civilization,” published in *Philosophy and Culture—East and West*, University of Hawaii, Honolulu, 1962, pp. 172–187, in English.

[^V3-630]: Rom Landau, *Islam and the Arabs*, p. 76.

[^V3-631]: In *Dialectics of Nature*, Arabic ed., trans. Usāma al-Qūtlī, Damascus, 1970, Engels says on p. 30 that the Arab discoveries in natural science during the Middle Ages were extremely important, though scattered and for the most part left without further development. On p. 31 he says that a vigorous spirit of free thought grew steadily among the Latin peoples during the European Renaissance, taken over from the Arabs and nourished by Greek philosophy. On p. 34 he says that the Arabs left the European Renaissance decimal notation, the foundations of algebra, modern numerals, and alchemy, while the Christian Middle Ages left nothing.

[^V3-632]: In *Anti-Dühring*, Arabic ed., trans. Fuʾād Ayyūb, Damascus, 1965, p. 27, Engels says that the foundations of exact natural science were first laid by the Greeks in the Alexandrian period and later by the Arabs in the Middle Ages.

[^V3-633]: *Dialectics of Nature*, p. 256.

[^V3-634]: Ibid., p. 255.

[^V3-635]: Marx, *The Poverty of Philosophy*, Arabic trans. André Bazni, Damascus, 1967, 2nd ed., p. 187.

[^V3-636]: Lenin, *Notebooks on Dialectics*, Arabic ed., p. 233.

[^V3-637]: On this point, Engels—following Feuerbach—says that it is true that scientific materialism based on natural science forms the foundation of human knowledge, but it is not the whole building. The reason, Engels says, is that we do not live in nature alone; we also live in human society. Society has its own history of development and its own science, just as nature has a history. See Engels, *Ludwig Feuerbach and the End of Classical German Philosophy*, Arabic ed., p. 30.

[^V3-638]: See Engels, *Anti-Dühring*, Arabic ed., p. 29.

[^V3-639]: Among the major scientific events of the nineteenth century that are described as revolutionary changes in the natural sciences, we may mention three examples: 1. the discovery of the cell as the basic unit of bodily structure, which helped establish the idea of development in nature; 2. Darwin’s discovery of the evolution of different biological species; 3. Robert Mayer’s formulation of the theory that different forms of the motion of matter can be transformed into one another. To these we can add Marx’s discovery of the material basis of spiritual life in society, meaning the economy as the foundation of society.

[^V3-640]: Engels, *Ludwig Feuerbach and the End of Classical German Philosophy*, Arabic ed., p. 24.

[^V3-641]: For the ancient philosophers and theologians, a thing’s *māhiyya*, or essence, is its universal reality—the general universal. What refers to the particular, individual thing they call *huwiyya*, or identity. They also use *huwiyya* for a thing’s external, material, concrete existence.

[^V3-642]: Lenin, *Materialism and Empirio-Criticism*, Arabic trans. Munīr Shafīq, Damascus, 1971, p. 260.

[^V3-643]: Here we limit our discussion to ancient Greek philosophy, not because we regard it as the only source of world philosophy. That would be both historically and methodologically wrong. We do so for two reasons. First, Greek philosophy was the main global source among the sources of Arab philosophy. Second, the ancient Greek philosophers were among the earliest pioneers in forming a view of the unity of existence and nature and in trying to explain the world dialectically, even though from a modern perspective their attempt was spontaneous and naive. This is the point Engels begins from in *Dialectics of Nature* when he says that almost every type of worldview appears in embryonic form in the different schools of ancient Greek philosophy—in the process of arising and taking shape.

[^V3-644]: We should except Pythagoras here, born around 580 or 570 BCE. An early idealist tendency appeared in his well-known idea of “number.” He believed in invisible spirits and emerged as a defender of the ideas of the Greek aristocracy and an opponent of democracy among the slave-owning Greeks.

[^V3-645]: See Aristotle, *Physics*, 203b16, 256b24, and 188a5, Arabic ed., ed. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān Badawī, Cairo, 1964, pp. 205, 849, 41.

[^V3-646]: We should add that Anaxagoras took an important step forward in addressing the problem of essence. Most of the Ionian philosophers had looked for the source of material things in a single concrete substance, while Empedocles looked for the basis of all existence in four roots, or elements. Anaxagoras then argued that the elements in the material world are infinite and cannot be counted. He also made a brilliant suggestion that points toward recognizing the hand as a basic factor distinguishing human beings from animals.

[^V3-647]: We have not included Socrates in this survey because the main task of his philosophy was not to investigate the basic principles of nature or the existence of the world. He limited his philosophy to questions concerning human beings, so it is fair to say that his central philosophical question was: How should a human being live? Socrates’s great contribution, however, was to develop a set of methods of inquiry that later made it possible to overcome the one-sided outlook of the philosophy before him, which had focused on the cosmos and nature while leaving the human being aside.

[^V3-648]: See, for example, Plato’s dialogues, especially the *Timaeus* (27d) and the *Theaetetus*, in the words of Socrates, together with Plato’s philosophical system as a whole.

[^V3-649]: See Aristotle’s criticism of Plato’s Forms in the *Metaphysics*, 1a and 991a.

[^V3-650]: The clearly materialist tendency in Aristotle’s criticism of his teacher Plato, especially at the foundation of the theory of Forms, is what Lenin had in mind when he said that the criticism is directed at idealism as idealism in general, not only at Plato. Lenin wrote this in his notes while summarizing Aristotle’s *Metaphysics* in the *Philosophical Notebooks*, and added: “When one idealist criticizes the foundations of idealism in another idealist, materialism gains from it.”

[^V3-651]: See Majid Fakhry, *Arisṭūṭālīs*, Beirut, 1958, p. 33.

[^V3-652]: Plotinus states this idea directly in the Fourth Ennead while discussing the nature of the soul: “True existence is the existence of the intelligible world.” See Plotinus, *Fourth Ennead*, Arabic ed., trans. Fuʾād Zakariyyā, Cairo, 1970, p. 167.

[^V3-653]: Aristotle’s demonstrative syllogism is built from three terms: the minor term, the major term, and the middle term. These are arranged into two premises and a conclusion. The two premises must be connected: a major premise, which is a universal proposition, and a minor premise, which is a particular proposition. The minor term appears in the minor premise. Using the traditional example employed by logicians, in the form adopted by Arab philosophers, the syllogism is: Socrates is a human being + every human being is mortal = Socrates is mortal. “Human being” is the middle term, and “Socrates” is the minor term. The first premise in this arrangement—“Socrates is a human being”—is the minor premise, a particular proposition.

[^V3-654]: See Professor N. Bammate, “The Status of Science and Technology in Islamic Civilization,” in *Philosophy and Culture—East and West*, University of Hawaii, Honolulu, 1962, p. 183, in English, while recognizing that the article contains many sound starting points.
